Science Fiction The Conservatory of Broken Seasons PDF

The Conservatory of Broken Seasons

T. K. Arven

No community may be used as another community's weather.

Act I — The Unnamed Debit

Prologue — A Dawn Without Light

Ressa put her heel against the chalk mark so no one could sweep it away by mistake.

The mark crossed the plaster at the height of her ankle, one short white bar beneath the address hall window. Hesh had drawn it after supper and written the promised time beside it in smaller figures. When the light came, Ressa could erase both with the damp end of the chalk. That part belonged to her because she had asked first.

“It will not fall off the wall,” Hesh said from the ledger table.

“Jori kicks things.”

“Jori cannot kick plaster off a heat wall.”

“He could try.”

Hesh considered this, then nodded. Jori could try most things. Ressa kept her heel where it was.

Everyone in Lorn Dusk had gathered early for the reflected window. The public schedule promised twelve minutes of dawn through the high glass, long enough to warm the seed trays and let children see their own shadows. Adults brought work that wanted pale light: thread sorting, seal inspection, the search for splinters in a glove. Two mechanics had opened the outer shutters and braced them with iron hooks. Beyond the glass waited a sky without morning, black except for a thin green smear above the radiator towers.

The hall lamps had been turned low. Under them, faces looked unfinished.

Ressa knew dawn from windows rather than horizons. It arrived as a bright square that moved across the floor, climbed the communal stove, and reached the wall of names before the mirror turned away. Once, when she was five, light had touched the copper button on her coat and made it too bright to look at. She had kept the button afterward, though the coat no longer fit.

Tonight's window had been printed on the clinic board, the school door, and the flour station. Her teacher called it a promised dawn. They had moved the first lesson into the address hall so the class could sketch shadows while adults checked the youngest greenhouse leaves for gray mold.

A minute remained.

Hesh crossed one entry in the bound ledger and sanded the wet ink. The pages recorded households, births, burials, heater failures, and light windows. Ressa could read the large headings. She could not yet read Hesh's close script, which leaned forward as if every word had somewhere colder to go.

“Ready,” called one of the mechanics.

Conversation stopped. People shifted away from the center of the floor. Ressa took her heel from the mark and held the chalk inside her fist.

The minute passed.

No white edge found the window. The greenhouse lamps remained yellow. Above the roofline, the aurora loosened into threads and gathered again.

“Mirror delay,” someone said.

The mechanics checked the shutter hooks, though everyone could see the opening stood clear. A woman near the stove lifted her smallest child onto one hip. The child had been woken for dawn and now tried to sleep against her shoulder.

Ressa watched the floorboards. She had chosen the seam where the bright square usually began. If the light arrived weakly, perhaps the wood would show it before the glass did.

Two minutes passed. Then four.

At six minutes beyond the printed time, the address terminal gave a soft administrative tone. Its screen brightened blue beside Hesh's elbow. Ressa expected the chime used for a delayed window, followed by a new time. Instead, a line of narrow letters appeared and remained there.

Hesh read them without speaking.

“What does it say?” asked the greenhouse keeper.

“Window withdrawn from active schedule.”

“For how long?”

“No duration.”

The keeper set down the tray she had brought. “Reason?”

Hesh touched the lower corner of the message. More text should have opened. Nothing did.

“Ask district weather,” one mechanic said.

“I did.” Hesh entered the request again. The terminal waited, then displayed a list of civic districts. Orison appeared near the top in green. Caldrift, Nacre Coast, and cities Ressa knew only from school maps followed it. Hesh moved through two pages. Their finger stopped, went back, and searched by name.

NO FORECAST DISTRICT FOUND: LORN DUSK.

Ressa read that line because the letters stood wide apart.

“We are here,” she said.

No one laughed. Hesh looked from the screen to her as if she had asked a serious question.

“Yes,” they said.

“Then why can't it find us?”

“The forecast uses a different list.”

The explanation did not help. Lists should contain what they listed. Ressa knew that from addresses. If someone moved from Heat Wall Three to the tower rooms, Hesh crossed out one room and wrote the next. Nobody vanished between pages.

Behind her, people began turning the hall lamps up. Yellow light strengthened over the tables. The mechanics released the shutters and drew the insulated panels together. Their iron edges met with a dull strike. A teacher told the children to take out their writing boards. The promised dawn had become an ordinary lesson before the scheduled twelve minutes ended.

Ressa opened her fist. Chalk dust filled the lines of her palm.

“Do I erase it?” she asked.

Hesh pulled the ledger closer. “Did the window arrive?”

“No.”

“Then the mark stays.”

“But it says the time.”

“The time belongs to what was promised.”

Across the room, Jori dragged his stool over the boards and came too close to the wall. Ressa pushed him away with one hand. He objected loudly until the teacher moved him to another table.

Hesh wrote the date, the scheduled duration, and the message from the terminal. Their pen scratched through the hush. A few adults came to read over their shoulder. The clinic porter dictated the heater reserve that had been used to prepare the treatment room. The greenhouse keeper reported six trays uncovered for expected light. One mechanic named the hours spent clearing frost from the outer shutter track.

None of those things changed the sky. They changed the page.

The teacher set a lamp on the floor for the shadow lesson. Every child received a black stick of charcoal and a sheet of rough paper. Ressa's shadow spread too softly beneath the lamp, with three edges instead of one. She drew the shutter hooks while the others drew hands, cups, or each other's profiles.

“What happened to the mirror?” Jori whispered.

“It went somewhere else.”

“How do you know?”

Ressa looked at the closed panels. She did not know. Perhaps a mirror could break. Perhaps it could forget an angle. Adults had said the Choir contained more mirrors than anyone in the hall could count without losing their place. One might have turned toward another greenhouse, another clinic, another room where children waited beside a wall.

She only knew the public schedule had named this window, then the forecast had failed to name Lorn Dusk.

When the lesson ended, the adults carried their unfinished light-work home. Seed trays went back under narrow lamps. The woman with the sleepy child tucked a warm brick beneath her coat. People touched the new ledger entry as they passed, not for luck but to show they had seen it.

Ressa stayed by the plaster.

Her chalk line no longer looked like the start of anything. In the stronger hall lamps it appeared blunt and dusty, no brighter than old marks above it. She counted those for the first time. Some had dates. Some carried small hooks at one end, Hesh's sign for a window that arrived late. Others stood plain.

“How many didn't come?” she asked.

Hesh capped the ink. “Enough that we write them down.”

“Will writing make the next one come?”

“No.”

Adults often softened a no by adding a later or a perhaps. Hesh left this one clean.

Ressa rubbed chalk from her palm onto her trousers. “Then why keep it?”

“So nobody can say we received what we did not receive.” Hesh closed the ledger around its new page. “And so nobody can say nobody waited.”

The answer fit inside her, though not comfortably.

At the door, the teacher called her to breakfast. Ressa bent and refreshed the white bar where her heel had blurred its lower edge. She copied the scheduled time more carefully beneath it. The figures leaned in different directions, but Hesh said they could still be read.

Outside, Lorn Dusk continued without dawn. Radiator hum passed through the floor. Green lamps shone over the high glass, and frost sealed the shutter seams the mechanics had cleared. The sky had kept nothing for her.

On the wall, the promise remained.

Chapter 1 — The Heat Ledger

Senn Vale held the hive door shut with her shoulder while six thousand municipal bees argued against her through the wood.

The box had warmed nine minutes ahead of schedule. A thin vibration traveled from the frames into her coat, purposeful as a pump beginning its cycle, and the temperature strip beside the latch showed a green line climbing toward release. Across the canal, Orison's eastern mirrors had not yet cleared the haze. Their light hung above the roofs in four pale columns, bright enough to silver the greenhouse glass but too weak to wake an apricot flower.

“Keep them closed,” called the bloom steward from the lower terrace.

“I had reached that conclusion.”

Senn leaned harder. The keeper beside her, a boy with a copper veil folded on his head, tried not to smile. He had been told that a senior seasonal auditor would inspect the first hive release. He had probably imagined a woman carrying a slate and asking ceremonial questions, not someone braced against a wooden box while the colony attempted an unauthorized spring.

Below them, canal gates opened in sequence. Water that had spent the night under insulated shutters slid into the terrace channels. Frost loosened from the stone in transparent scales. The public chimes sounded one low note, then another, calling gardeners to uncover the early beds.

None of it should have begun yet.

Senn took the probe from her pocket and pushed its blunt end through the hive's service port. Air temperature inside read 18.2 degrees. The bloom order authorized release at 18.5 after three continuous minutes of east-array light. No array light had reached the terrace.

“Did you preheat the box?” she asked.

The keeper's smile vanished. “No, Auditor.”

“Did your steward?”

The woman below heard him hesitate. “I did not,” she called. “The hive wall takes canal heat. If the canal woke early, so did they.”

Senn looked across the terraces. Along five tiers, workers stood beside closed hive boxes, their shoulders in the same awkward posture as hers. Orison had designed bloom to look effortless from the tram bridges. From inside the schedule, spring consisted of people holding doors against insects until a ledger and a sky agreed.

The third chime sounded. A bright edge appeared on the upper greenhouse roofs.

“Now,” the steward said.

Senn lifted the latch. Bees pressed into the narrow opening, paused in a dark knot, then spilled across the warming air. They did not rise toward the official light columns. They turned down toward the canal, following heat already present in the stone.

She recorded the direction of flight.

The keeper reached for the box record clipped under the eave. Senn stopped him before he could write release normal in the morning column.

“Record the internal temperature at first latch pressure,” she said. “Record canal surface temperature. Put my instrument number beside both.”

He looked down at the steward. The woman climbed the terrace steps with the care of someone approaching a dispute she expected to outlast. Soil darkened her knees. A ribbon of white petals clung to one sleeve.

“A warm box does not make a failed bloom,” the steward said.

“No.” Senn showed her the probe reading. “It makes a question about where the warmth came from.”

“It came from the canal.”

“Then the canal has the question.”

The steward read the strip and rubbed a thumb over the crease beside her mouth. Early warmth pleased growers until an auditor named it. An advance of fifteen minutes could bring one set of buds forward while another remained tight. Pollinators spent themselves against unopened flowers. Nursery managers compensated with extra lamps, then the next ledger treated those lamps as ordinary demand. A small shift could vanish into a turn's abundance while still charging someone for the correction.

Senn walked to the next box. Its keeper had already released the colony, but the empty hive held 18.1 degrees. The third measured 18.3. At the fourth terrace she crouched beside the canal and submerged the probe until cold water covered the metal stem. Heat moved strongest along the inner retaining stones, not the surface facing the new mirror light. Whatever warmed the channel had entered before dawn through the district return line.

“Do you want us to close the boxes?” the boy asked when she came back.

“No. Closing them now would punish the bees for our schedule.”

The steward gave a short laugh. “That may be the first sensible sentence an auditor has spoken on this terrace.”

“Put that in the remarks field.”

Senn copied five box temperatures, the gate sequence, and the direction of the bees' first flight. She also recorded what she did not do: no halted release, no crop alarm, no accusation against the keepers. A useful discrepancy needed clean edges. If every office could later claim she had mistaken ordinary bloom for an emergency, they would spend more attention defending spring than explaining its advance.

On the tram uphill, she watched the terraces pass under the growing light. Municipal hives opened one after another. Children on a school bridge counted the boxes and cheered each dark cloud of bees. Their teacher pointed toward the east array as if the mirrors alone had summoned them.

Senn kept looking at the canal stones.

By the time she reached the Conservatory, the first discrepancy had acquired three innocent explanations. Canal pumps might have retained heat from the prior rain turn. A greenhouse cooperative might have requested an unlogged nursery allowance. Mirror 117, whose edge corrections often painted more ground than intended, might have warmed the water before its measured light reached the roofs.

Senn preferred innocent explanations. They were cheaper to prove and usually came with someone embarrassed enough to fix them.

The heat ledger occupied a recessed desk on the Conservatory's second floor, where the old pressureworks windows faced starward through red glass. Even filtered, Varda's permanent glare left a copper bar across the slate. Senn set her coat over it before she opened Orison's account.

Four columns arranged the city's coming turn: light delivered, shade accepted, cloud water seeded, heat exported. Bloom allowances appeared in green. Winter obligations appeared in blue. Each quantity had a district, a source instrument, an authorizing schedule, and a balancing entry. Weather on Meral did not arrive. Someone angled it, seeded it, blocked it, or paid for its consequences elsewhere.

The canal reading sat within the light-delivery column as a correction of 0.08 percent. Small enough to disappear into routine tolerance. Senn traced it to mirror 117 and found a maintenance note signed D. Orro: EAST EDGE OFFSET TO HOLD CLINIC WINDOW. The note carried no district name.

She marked it for origin review and kept reading.

Orison's account did not merely close. It closed elegantly. The delivered bloom matched hospital heating, greenhouse demand, school-day extensions, and canal release within a fraction that would have pleased an examiner. The corresponding heat export flowed into darkward radiator capacity, where the planet could shed it into permanent night. No tower exceeded its listed tolerance. No district surrendered more than its traded share.

Yet the hive had warmed early.

Senn removed her coat from the copper bar and laid a transparent work sheet over the account. She copied the measured canal temperature, then reconstructed the preceding twelve hours from primary instruments rather than Conservatory summaries. Pumps 4 through 9 had started at the authorized time. The insulated shutters had opened correctly. Greenhouse discharge contributed less than a fifth of the excess. Mirror 117 explained part of what remained, but its offset could not have warmed five terraces.

She widened the query to the whole city.

The slate slowed. Orison carried too many bloom instruments for quick reconstruction: tram glass that turned reflective during high light, school roofs that banked warmth for evening lessons, hundreds of greenhouse skins, hospital reservoirs, canal stones, hive walls. The Conservatory condensed them into district totals because no one could govern a season by watching every surface.

Senn had once believed condensation meant clarity. Eight years earlier, she had reconciled storm losses for tenant growers in Caldrift. Her total had been exact. The compensation schedule had been exact. By the time the money reached the growers, twelve leases had already passed to landholders who could afford the delay. Every line had balanced. The people under the lines had moved.

She never again accepted a clean total before asking what it had made invisible.

The reconstruction completed. Eleven blocks of residual warmth appeared across Orison, each trivial alone. Together they formed a smooth advance in the bloom curve. The city had begun taking spring before its mirrors announced spring.

Senn checked the authorized schedule. It matched the public chimes. She checked the physical schedule. It matched the bees.

Then she found the second ledger.

Not a hidden file. Conservatory practice discouraged hidden files, which created legal risk. This one sat under Auxiliary Balancing, a category broad enough to contain harmless shade exchanges, emergency cloud seeding, and the slow redistribution of heat around a world with one day and one night. The category showed Orison receiving an eleven-day bloom extension over the coming turn.

The extension appeared nowhere in the public forecast.

Senn leaned back. Around her, the second-floor desks carried the quiet noises of ordinary climate work: styluses against slate, paper orders lifted from drawers, a technician dictating wind-shear numbers into a throat microphone. The Conservatory did not feel like a place concealing eleven days of spring. It felt like a place where concealment could wear the same gray cover as maintenance.

She opened the balancing side.

The account expanded into a diagram of transfers. On its left, Orison's added light entered as a broad green band. On its right, the Conservatory had divided the cost until no single line looked like payment. Industrial shade carried a quarter. Radiator duty carried nearly half. Wind-risk tolerance absorbed a narrow fraction whose legal meaning depended on the season. The remaining demand descended through three nested categories and ended at the unnamed debit.

Senn rebuilt the balance without the summaries. She converted lumen-hours into expected surface heat, subtracted crop uptake, canal storage, and outgoing air exchange, then compared the result with darkward radiator capacity. The arithmetic required the debit. Remove it and the account held a physical deficit large enough to alter the next shear forecast. Keep it and the city received warmth whose receiving cost had no civic address.

A yellow flag appeared beside one radiator figure. The tower reported continuous operation above preferred load but below emergency tolerance. A second tower carried the same flag. Senn opened their maintenance histories and found repeated bearing inspections, coolant replacements, and one request for residential heater priority during a clinic window. Policy accounting had rolled every item into infrastructure demand.

She called the archive index and requested the legal definition of occupancy exempt.

The index offered six current manuals and an emergency glossary older than Senn. Current manuals defined unoccupied zones by absence of domestic heat, medical reserves, schools, births, and local burials. The old glossary defined service occupancy as personnel temporarily present to maintain equipment. Neither definition fit instruments showing continuous homes.

Senn sent a narrow question to policy accounting: Which survey certifies continued occupancy exemption for allocation 9.7?

The response arrived before she could close the query. Certification inherited from originating order.

She asked for the originating order.

Access limited: policy classification.

She requested only its date.

This answer took longer. The slate displayed a turning circle while someone, or a permission system designed by someone, considered whether a date could reveal too much. At last it returned a year thirty-one years earlier.

No temporary service crew remained temporary for thirty-one years. Either the instruments had been assigned to the wrong allocation, or the classification had survived the facts it once described.

Every benefit required a cost. The eleven days drew on hospital need, staple-crop stabilization, and “urban continuity,” a phrase with no unit attached. The cost side listed shade acceptance at three unused industrial tracts, increased radiator duty, and a winter debit.

The debit had an authorization number. It had a duration, a thermal value, and a risk ceiling. It did not have a district.

Senn read the line again, more slowly.

WINTER DEBIT: SERVICE ALLOCATION 9.7 / LOAD-BALANCED / OCCUPANCY EXEMPT.

Most weather transfers named both places. Caldrift surrendered two rain hours to the western reservoirs. Orison accepted a cold morning so Nacre orchards could finish fruit set. Even uninhabited tracts had survey coordinates, because wind did not respect administrative vagueness. This entry named only an exemption.

She requested the source map.

ACCESS LIMITED: POLICY CLASSIFICATION.

She requested the instrument bundle.

The slate returned eighteen radiator feeds, atmospheric shear monitors, heat-wall consumption, medical reserve draw, school-cycle load, and domestic warming demand. Senn stopped at the last three.

An uninhabited service allocation did not have medical reserves. Equipment did not attend school. Domestic warming demand required homes.

Someone had not erased the people from the instruments. They had erased them from the category that decided whether winter could be imposed.

Senn copied the authorization number to paper. Digital annotations could be revised with an audit trail; paper made the act of carrying a number visible. She went down one floor to maintenance coordination.

Dael Orro stood inside a shade-kite frame with both arms raised, holding a strip of black fabric against a test lamp. He was broad through the shoulders and wore the short gray coat of a lattice engineer. Two fingers on his left hand did not straighten fully. The lamp cast a hard rectangle across his face.

“You signed an offset on mirror 117,” Senn said.

“Then it must have needed one.”

“Your note says a clinic window.”

Dael lowered the fabric. “Clinics generally prefer their windows to arrive.”

“Which clinic?”

The junior technicians at the edge of the frame found urgent reasons to examine their tension gauges.

Dael stepped out and switched off the lamp. In the softer red light from the windows, he looked tired rather than obstructive. “Did the offset breach tolerance?”

“No.”

“Did it compromise Orison's bloom?”

“It advanced canal heat by less than a tenth of a percent.”

“Then your bees survived the outrage.”

Senn placed the paper authorization number on the workbench. His amusement left without visible motion.

“Where does this debit go?” she asked.

Dael read the number. “That belongs to policy accounting.”

“The instruments include school-cycle load.”

“They include many things.”

“Medical reserve draw.”

His damaged fingers closed over the fabric strip. “You should ask the First Curator.”

“I asked the ledger.”

“And it refused you.”

“A ledger cannot refuse. A person selects the access rule.”

Dael looked toward the technicians, then motioned Senn to the narrow service balcony. Outside, Orison's central canal shone under the now-complete bloom array. Bees moved above the terraces as shifting brown dust. From this height, no one could see the workers who had held the boxes shut.

“The offset and the debit are not the same thing,” he said.

“I know. Your offset is too small.”

“Then leave it small.”

“Who receives the clinic window?”

Dael watched mirror light travel down the canal. “A clinic that loses more windows than it receives.”

The answer carried location without naming it: darkward, beyond the public district map, somewhere the Choir's reflected dawn could be withheld.

“Is the winter debit imposed on people?” Senn asked.

Dael's jaw tightened. “If I tell you yes, what do you do before the next schedule closes?”

“Open an origin trace.”

“If the trace freezes the balancing order, Orison loses light before anyone has modeled the shear response.”

“That sounds like yes.”

“It sounds like weather has momentum. Auditors forget that because numbers stop when you close a file.”

“I have not forgotten.”

“Then do not make innocence out of speed.”

Senn picked up the authorization slip. “Do not make safety out of silence.”

Neither of them moved. Below, the canal released another skin of frost. The public chimes announced that bloom had begun exactly on time.

Back at her desk, three messages waited. The bloom steward had filed the early hive heat as a harmless calibration drift. Maintenance had attached Dael's mirror note to routine tolerance. Policy accounting had answered Senn's source-map request with a meeting invitation for the following afternoon, no documents attached.

Every response tried to reduce the discrepancy to the size of the office answering it.

Senn reopened Orison's account. Freezing a weather ledger did not stop the machinery. It prevented new authorizations from inheriting an unexamined entry. Existing schedules would continue for thirty-six hours under maintenance authority. After that, the Season Table would require a named curator to renew the debit or remove it.

She checked the storm field. Stable for four days under the current schedule, uncertain after that. She checked hospital reserves. Adequate. She checked greenhouse floor temperatures. Vulnerable, but not immediately. The decision contained risk. So did leaving the line unnamed.

In the freeze form, a blank field asked for grounds.

Senn wrote: BALANCING ENTRY APPEARS TO CARRY HUMAN OCCUPANCY UNDER INFRASTRUCTURE EXEMPTION. ORIGIN AND STANDING UNVERIFIED.

The form asked whether she alleged fraud.

She selected NO.

It asked whether she alleged measurement error.

NO.

It asked whether the entry could affect a protected climate floor.

She selected YES.

A final notice warned that an origin trace would alert the First Curator, the affected district, and all engineers whose maintenance entries touched the allocation. No affected district appeared in the account, so the notice listed only Orison.

Senn added the authorization number, Dael's maintenance initials, and the hive temperature. Then she pressed her thumb to the seal.

The ledger dimmed around the winter debit. Its blue line remained visible, but a thin white boundary appeared on either side, preventing it from flowing forward unnoticed.

Outside the red windows, Orison brightened into the spring it had expected. On Senn's slate, the city kept eleven days no public forecast admitted it owned. Somewhere beyond the named districts, medical heaters and school lamps waited inside a category that claimed no one lived there.

The origin trace opened.

Chapter 2 — The Mirror's Small Lie

Senn lowered the light sensor into the canal until cold water covered the second calibration ring.

“Not the cable,” Dael said.

“I can see the cable.”

“Most people can. They still drown it.”

Dael Orro crouched on the retaining wall with one boot braced against a greenhouse buttress. The fingers of his left hand held the sensor line below the connector, keeping its weight off the repaired joint. His right hand worked a portable shade over the instrument head. Above them, mirror 117 waited ten degrees west of its first bloom angle, a silver scratch moving through the red haze.

The canal-side beds had been uncovered before they arrived. Apricot branches stood in ordered rows behind the glass, their buds swollen but closed. Garden crews tested irrigation valves along the upper terrace. Every few minutes a hive keeper leaned close to one of the municipal boxes, listening for the pressure of bees against wood.

Orison prepared bloom by pretending not to hurry.

Senn read the instrument. “Dark baseline holding at point zero four.”

“Within spec.”

“You repaired this joint with seal thread.”

“I repaired it with the correct seal thread.”

“It has three colors.”

“Three correct colors.”

She recorded the baseline. Dael lifted the portable shade, allowing the city lamps to reach the sensor. The reading climbed by a measurable fraction. He lowered the shade again. Light vanished from the instrument without changing the canal temperature.

They repeated the sequence at two depths, then raised the sensor and set it in a cradle on the stone. Water streamed from its black fins. Dael wiped each optical face with a square of lint paper and held it toward the dim sky.

“No film,” he said. “No thermal lag beyond seven seconds. If you hoped for a dishonest sensor, this one will disappoint you.”

“I hoped for a complete account.”

“That costs more.”

The origin trace Senn had opened the previous afternoon had produced a procedural compromise. Policy accounting would not release the debit's source map. Maintenance would permit her to test the instrument path used to credit Orison's early warmth. First Curator Ceryn Ahl had scheduled a technical review in two days, which gave every office time to decide how little to bring.

Senn had chosen the canal test because mirror 117 offered the only named physical adjustment connected to the discrepancy. Dael had agreed because refusing would have turned a tolerance note into evidence of concealment.

Neither had called the arrangement cooperative.

A public chime sounded from the tram bridge. Workers opened the first greenhouse vents. Damp air rolled across the canal, carrying soil heat and the bitter scent of dormant leaves.

Dael checked his wrist slate. “First target in three minutes. Put the sensor on the inner edge.”

“That edge produced yesterday's advance.”

“Yes.”

“You say that without concern.”

“I say it because the canal has not moved since yesterday.”

Senn placed the cradle where the retaining wall turned toward the nearest hive terrace. Dael ran a sighting thread from its center to a brass notch in the stone. The notch bore years of small scratches from prior calibrations. One scratch had been filled with fresh white paste.

“Your offset?” she asked.

“My reference.”

“Which differs from the scheduled reference.”

“By eleven millimeters at this wall.”

“That becomes how much at the edge of the reflection?”

“Wait for the light.”

He did not speak evasively when metal or angle could answer instead. Senn respected the instinct despite the work it required from her.

Mirror 117 crossed its first control point. A narrow bar of reflected Varda light appeared on the upper canal, copper at its margins and white in the center. The bar moved down the water with mechanical smoothness. Greenhouse glass threw fragments across the terrace ceilings. Bees struck the hive doors in sudden waves.

Dael watched the sensor rather than the spectacle. “Now.”

Senn began a continuous record. The light bar touched the far edge of the canal. Temperature held. It advanced across the water, reached the sighting thread, and turned the sensor display from gray to green.

Delivered light matched the mirror telemetry within two tenths of a percent.

She checked the backup photometer. Its value agreed.

Dael moved the portable shade over both instruments. Their optical readings dropped at once while the thermal fins retained a slower trace. He counted seven seconds, lifted the shade, and nodded toward Senn's slate.

“No phantom delivery,” he said.

“Not here.”

“Test every stone in Orison if you like. Light remains irritatingly real.”

Senn followed the reflection as it passed below the hive terraces. Yesterday's warmth had not come from false sensor credit. Orison had received the measured energy. The discrepancy survived contact with water, glass, and an independent device.

She had wanted the result clean. Clean results narrowed questions. This one also removed the cheapest answer.

At the end of the control pass, mirror 117 should have continued east along the canal. Instead, its bright edge paused. The central band narrowed, shifted half a degree darkward, then resumed its scheduled path.

Senn marked the time.

Dael began coiling the cable.

“That was your clinic window.”

“That was an edge correction.”

“You can give it both names.”

“Only one fits the maintenance field.”

The shift had lasted seventeen seconds. On the canal wall, its effect amounted to a patch of light narrower than Senn's hand. At orbital scale, however, the altered edge would travel beyond Orison's terraces. She opened the public angle trace and overlaid Dael's maintenance command. The official path displayed a smooth curve. His command inserted a shallow hook too small for the city summary.

“Show me the receiving geometry,” she said.

“No.”

“You entered the command under Conservatory authority.”

“I entered a correction within my tolerance authority.”

“Then the receiving geometry should survive an audit.”

“The mirror survived it.”

A hive latch rattled above them. The keeper held it with both hands and looked downhill for release permission. Dael raised one finger without taking his eyes from Senn. The scheduled light reached the terrace, and he lowered it. Six doors opened along the row. Bees poured into white brightness.

The correction had not taken enough from Orison to delay a single release.

Senn disconnected the backup photometer and wrapped its leads. “You told me the offset protected a clinic.”

“I told the maintenance ledger.”

“Without a place.”

“Places complicate routine tolerance.”

“People generally do.”

Dael's mouth moved at one corner. “That sounds almost like something I would say.”

They carried the instruments along the canal to the western gauge house. The path narrowed between water and greenhouse wall. Condensation rolled down the glass in clear beads, each briefly lit as the mirror band passed. Inside, apricot buds opened with minute snaps that Senn could hear only when the pumps paused.

At the gauge house, Dael bolted the sensor to a fixed mount and connected it directly to the canal archive. Senn watched his hands. The burned fingers lacked full extension, but he made no allowance visible in the work. He tightened each contact by touch, then asked her to inspect the seals before power entered the unit.

“You want my initials?” she asked.

“I want you unable to claim I changed it after the test.”

“That is almost trust.”

“That is good maintenance practice.”

She initialed the seals.

The second pass tested spectral content. A mirror could deliver the recorded quantity while placing too much energy outside the crop-useful band, forcing greenhouse stones to absorb heat without waking flowers. Dael fitted three filters over the sensor. Senn called readings while he adjusted a shutter wheel.

Blue fraction within tolerance. Red fraction high by a third of a percent. Infrared fraction matched the canal's early thermal gain but remained insufficient to explain five warmed terraces before scheduled bloom.

“Your sensor tells the truth,” Senn said.

“It has poor prospects in administration.”

“Your mirror does too, mostly.”

Dael removed the filters. “There is no mostly in a photon count.”

“There is in an angle trace.”

Before Senn accepted the result, she carried the backup photometer three terraces downstream. Dael followed with the fixed sensor's portable reference plate tucked beneath his arm. They chose a place where the canal bent under an old tram abutment, beyond the greenhouse reflections that might have contaminated the first test.

A maintenance hatch beneath the bridge gave them access to the district fiber. The lock resisted Dael's key until he warmed its face with his palm. Condensation had frozen inside the cylinder.

“You could list this as infrastructure obstruction,” Senn said.

“I could also fix it.”

He removed the lock barrel, dried each pin on his sleeve, and worked graphite into the channel. Senn held the lamp. Two tramloads passed overhead while he reseated the barrel. The bridge translated their wheels into a slow iron thunder.

Inside the hatch, the raw mirror feed arrived before municipal smoothing. Senn connected the photometer, covered it, then opened the shutter on Dael's count. The downstream reading agreed with the first site closely enough that instrument drift could not explain the early canal heat.

“Reverse the test,” she said.

Dael directed the photometer toward greenhouse scatter while she placed the reference plate in the mirror path. If reflected glass had inflated canal delivery, the side reading would climb out of proportion. Instead, both values followed the geometry he had predicted.

A canal worker stopped above them and asked whether the bridge would lose light. Dael answered that the mirror could not tell the bridge from the water. The worker considered this bad design but accepted that it would continue.

Senn ran the calculation twice. “No hidden reflection credit.”

“Would you like the water to sign a witness field?”

“It has better records than some offices.”

They closed the hatch. Dael tested the lock three times, not because the audit required it but because another technician would need it in winter. Senn added the downstream result to the calibration bundle. The extra site cost them forty minutes and removed two more innocent explanations.

The edge correction sat between their slates. Seventeen seconds of documented light had gone somewhere the official district map did not show. Its energy could keep a treatment room above minimum, warm medicine storage, or do nothing useful after atmospheric loss. Without a receiving instrument bundle, Senn could not claim which.

“Give me a tower number,” she said.

“No.”

“A bearing.”

“No.”

“Expected transmission loss.”

“That would give you a bearing.”

“Then you admit there is a receiver.”

“I admit mirrors point at surfaces.”

Senn closed the spectral record before irritation entered her notes. “A clinic that lacks lawful weather standing cannot request a share. You protect it with offsets, the offsets vanish into tolerance, and the schedule continues to show no demand.”

“You have built a sturdy argument from a name I did not give you.”

“You gave me the function.”

“Function does not grant you custody of the place.”

“You think concealment does?”

Dael set the shutter wheel down with care. The gauge-house pumps cycled beneath them, shaking a film of dust from the rafters.

“I think a technical auditor arrives with powers she considers narrow,” he said. “A location enters her origin trace. Policy freezes access. Licensing asks who has been adjusting unauthorized service. Security asks whether residents interfered with Choir property. By the time the audit discovers a person, three offices have discovered an offender.”

“And your alternative lasts how long?”

“Seventeen seconds today.”

“That is not an answer.”

“It warms the medicine cabinet.”

The specificity changed the room. Dael looked toward the canal immediately afterward, as though the water had spoken for him.

Senn let the pump vibration pass through the floor. A medicine cabinet implied storage temperature rather than patient heating. Seventeen seconds of edge light, captured through a receiving pane and banked in dense stone, might cover the cabinet's narrow range. The adjustment remained small enough to hide because the need had been reduced to the minimum an engineer could protect.

“How often?” she asked.

Dael did not answer.

She opened his maintenance history. Similar edge notes appeared irregularly across three mirrors, never enough to breach tolerance. Some protected diagnostic heat. Others cited coolant viscosity or shutter release. Each phrase could belong to machinery. Together they described a pattern of maintenance attention darkward of the public schedule.

The pattern reached back twelve years, but Senn did not say the duration aloud. Naming everything she could infer might make him defend every detail at once.

“Does policy know?” she asked instead.

“Policy knows its instrument totals.”

“That was not my question.”

“It is the answer available here.”

A greenhouse steward knocked on the gauge-house door. One western hive remained below release temperature although its neighbors had opened. She wanted to know whether the test had shaded it.

Dael took his probe and followed her without argument. Senn brought the archive cable.

At the hive, he pressed two fingers to the wall, then checked the lower vent. Cold air leaked around a warped wooden slide. He removed the slide, shaved its swollen edge with a service blade, and seated it again. The internal temperature began to rise.

“Not the mirror,” he told the steward. “Keep this colony closed for six more minutes.”

“That puts them behind the apricot row.”

“Better six minutes behind than a cold release.”

The steward looked to Senn, perhaps expecting audit authority to overrule maintenance. Senn entered the delayed hive into her notes and confirmed the decision. Bloom schedules tolerated local resistance even when public images did not.

While they waited, she walked the terrace boundary with the photometer. The delivered-light field stayed true within the advertised tolerance. Mirror 117's small hook had left no measurable deficit in the crop beds. Whatever Dael had given away, Orison had not been forced to notice.

That fact did not legalize the adjustment. It made its ethics more inconvenient.

Dael asked the keeper for the prior ten release records. The boy produced a grease-stained packet from beneath the hive shelf. Three entries showed delayed internal warmth on canal-cold mornings, but none recorded the warped vent.

“You have been correcting this with release time,” Dael said.

“The colony catches up by second flight.”

“Until it does not.” He showed the keeper where the slide rubbed its guide. “Sand this edge after the bees settle tonight. Seal the lower corner before rain shift. If moisture enters here, the wood swells toward the latch.”

Senn compared the packet with the municipal summary. Every delay had been rounded into normal bloom because no hive missed its daily pollination target.

“Another small discrepancy,” she said.

“One caused by wood.”

“One hidden by successful compensation.”

Dael returned the packet. “You are developing a dangerous interest in maintenance.”

The keeper looked between them. “Should I amend the old records?”

“No,” Senn said. “Add today's finding. Leave the earlier entries as they were observed.” Altering them after the cause became known would make the archive look wiser than the people had been.

At the sixth minute, the keeper opened the hive. Bees emerged slowly, tested the air, and rose toward the nearest flowering wall. Dael watched until their flight stabilized.

“You could file the medicine window as a protected service variance,” Senn said.

“Protected for whom?”

“A medical facility.”

“Facilities require district identifiers.”

“Then give the place an identifier.”

His gaze stayed on the bees. “You are circling the same closed gate.”

“So are you. You simply service its hinges.”

He accepted that without a joke.

They returned to the canal after midday. Orison had entered full bloom shift. White flowers covered the terrace walls, and the first pollen film softened the water's black surface. School groups crossed the high bridges carrying observation boards. Public screens showed the day's light delivery as an unbroken green band.

Senn compared that band with her raw record. The city had received every scheduled lumen-hour within tolerance. It had also received the early heat documented in the hidden balancing account. The instrument path contained no false light, no faulty sensor, and no calculation error broad enough to create the winter debit.

Dael's seventeen-second hook appeared only in the maintenance trace.

At the Conservatory annex desk, she prepared two findings. The first stated that mirror 117 and the canal sensor accurately measured delivered energy; Orison's bloom benefit therefore reflected physical delivery rather than instrument inflation. The second stated that an engineer had used an edge correction for an unlisted medical receiver while keeping the adjustment inside maintenance tolerance.

She turned the slate so Dael could read both.

“You called it medical,” he said.

“You called it a medicine cabinet.”

“I did not authorize a location trace.”

“I have not named a location.”

“You will.”

“When the evidence permits it.”

He read the second finding again. “If you attach my command path, they can reverse the bearing.”

“I am sealing the raw geometry under auditor custody until the technical review.”

“That protects it for two days.”

“It protects it from a routine licensing query. If policy compels release, the request enters the record.”

Dael studied her face with the same attention he had given the sensor seals. “Why?”

“Because the small discrepancy has a patient at the end of it.”

“You do not know that.”

“I know there is medicine.”

After a pause, he touched his thumb to the witness field. His mark did not approve her finding. It confirmed he had read it.

Senn sealed the calibration bundle. On one side of her slate, the unnamed winter debit carried enough energy to alter a city's season. On the other, a mirror's small lie redirected seventeen seconds toward a clinic Dael refused to place on any map she could open.

The first discrepancy had institutional authority and no human name. The second had a human purpose and no lawful authority.

She sent both into the review record without allowing either to cancel the other.

Chapter 3 — Eleven Days of Spring

The first apricot petal struck Senn's sleeve while she was counting hospital windows.

She left it there. Across Orison's middle terrace, bloom light climbed the greenhouse walls in measured bands. Each band woke a different crop height: low strawberries first, then bean frames, then the apricot crowns trained against pale stone. Municipal bees crossed between them as dense brown threads. From the tram bridges, the terraces looked spontaneous. At ground level, every opening flower stood inside a schedule.

Senn marked the hospital's eastern reservoir inlet, checked its instrument number, and moved to the next glass bay. The hidden debit's balancing path entered Orison through eleven allocation blocks. She had printed each block on paper and folded the sheets into her coat by function rather than account number.

Three belonged to hospitals. Four served food production. Two extended school light. One warmed municipal canals. The last carried the phrase urban continuity, which still had no unit attached.

A nurse opened a service door before Senn could knock. Warm damp air escaped around her, followed by the smell of boiled cloth and green leaves.

“You are blocking sterilization exhaust,” the nurse said.

“I need the reservoir timing for this wing.”

“Then stand inside or farther away.”

Senn stepped through.

The hospital grew medicinal plants in narrow beds between its treatment blocks. Blue-veined feverleaf occupied the lower shelves. Above it, climbing filters drew metallic dust from intake air. The eastern reservoir banked light heat during bloom and released it through the dark hours, reducing demand on the municipal heat line.

“Which schedule do you use?” Senn asked.

The nurse pointed to a board above the beds. “Clinical bloom, green column.”

The board listed thirty-three days.

Senn's public forecast listed twenty-two.

She compared start dates. Both schedules began that morning. The hospital version continued eleven days beyond the public bloom turn before shifting to rain.

“Who authorizes the extension?”

“Municipal continuity. We receive the schedule each turn.”

“Is it optional?”

The nurse gave her the look reserved for questions formed in offices. “The feverleaf reaches usable vein density on day twenty-eight. We can buy dried stock from Caldrift if the roads stay open. The respiratory filters need thirty-one days unless you want to change them twice as often.”

“Could you shift those beds under lamps?”

“With whose electricity?”

Senn copied the crop durations and asked for prior-turn usage. The nurse unlocked a cabinet of paper slips, each recording heat released from the reservoir during treatment hours. No single slip named extra spring. Together they assumed it.

A reservoir attendant took Senn below the growing beds to verify the release path. Copper pipes crossed the ceiling in paired runs, one from the bloom-facing wall and one toward treatment rooms. Salt bricks packed between them stored heat without raising air temperature around the medicines. The attendant pulled a sample brick from its cradle and pressed a probe into the center.

“Day twenty-two leaves these at sixty-eight percent charge,” she said. “The longer clinical schedule brings them above ninety before rain shift.”

“What happens at sixty-eight?”

“We preserve surgery and neonatal rooms. Sterilization moves to the municipal line. Plant drying stops. If another ward calls for emergency heat, someone chooses which clean work waits.”

“Has that occurred?”

“Before the extension became dependable, twice in one turn.” She opened a valve no wider than her smallest fingernail. Warm fluid entered the treatment loop with a faint mineral click. “Now our staffing calendar assumes we can sterilize after the public bloom ends. Cut the input without moving staff and the reservoir empties during occupied hours.”

Senn inspected the charge log. Hospital planners had turned an unlisted benefit into shift assignments, linen cycles, and medicine preparation. Replacing its energy would not only require heat. It would require people at different hours.

Before leaving, Senn removed the petal from her sleeve. It had gone translucent in the humid room.

The school terraces began two levels uphill. Children in copper visors carried trays of germinating beans from one glass classroom to another, following the moving light. Their teacher recognized Senn's auditor seal and became careful at once.

“We have not exceeded the education allotment,” he said.

“I am tracing its duration, not alleging excess.”

“That distinction rarely reaches the budget office.”

He showed her the school calendar. During public bloom, morning lessons began early and ended when mirror glare sharpened. The undocumented extension supported eleven longer school days after the public turn changed, allowing children from colder outer wards to attend while their household heat walls drew less power.

“Why not print the longer days on the public forecast?” Senn asked.

“They have always been on our calendar.”

“That does not answer why.”

“It answers everyone who needs to bring a child here.”

The teacher tapped a line labeled seasonal stabilization. No source district appeared. The school received the hours through Orison's municipal total, already condensed before the calendar system touched it.

A girl at the nearest table raised her hand. “Are you taking spring away?”

The teacher began to intervene.

Senn answered first. “I am finding out where it comes from.”

The girl considered the bean sprout in her palm. “From the mirrors.”

“Before the mirrors.”

“From Varda.”

“After Varda.”

The child's patience ran out. “Then from your papers.”

Senn wrote down the school schedule and left the answer standing.

On her way out, a transport clerk stopped her beside the boot warmers. Three darkward-facing school routes used the longer days to combine return trams. Without afternoon light, each route required heated cars two hours earlier, when hospital freight occupied the same track.

“We can move the children,” the clerk said, laying route tiles across a bench. “We cannot move them at the hour the calendar assumes and also save the heat. Choose one.”

“Could lessons begin earlier?”

“Some families share morning heat walls with night crews. Children leave after the wall recovers.”

Senn shifted one tile and blocked a seed delivery. Another route crowded the canal interchange beyond its cold-platform limit. Even the school extension reached into freight, household heating, and safe waiting time.

At midday, she crossed the crop terraces with a municipal agronomist named Pel. He carried a hooked knife for opening irrigation clogs and used it more often than his slate. Every third channel held roots or fallen petals. While Senn asked about light-days, Pel knelt, cleared fibers from a grate, and sent water down another row.

“Twenty-two days gives you first set,” he said. “Thirty-three gives stable set on the upper apricots and brings the bean flowers into the same pollinator window.”

“How much yield depends on the last eleven?”

“Depends on cloud water, hive strength, and night heat.”

“Hold those at expected values.”

“I do not hold weather at expected values. The Almanac does that, badly enough for conversation.”

Senn waited while he cleared another grate.

Pel wiped the knife on his trousers. “Seven percent for apricots in an ordinary turn. Four for climbing beans. The seed nurseries vary from nothing to twelve.”

“Staple effect?”

“Beans, yes. Apricots keep people willing to eat another year.”

“Could Orison surrender the extension next turn?”

“Could your office tell forty thousand households to reduce meals by four percent next turn?”

“The city stores reserves.”

“For failed rain, mold, bridge closure, and every other risk you prefer to count separately. Remove bloom days without changing those risks and you spend the same reserve twice.”

He led her into a seed-start house where trays climbed three stories on rotating racks. Warmth rose through perforated floors. Tiny leaves made the air smell sharp and wet.

“These starts go to six districts,” Pel said. “Not only Orison. Caldrift's late beans begin here because their nurseries cannot hold temperature through dark rain. The coast buys our root stock. Two smaller towns abandoned local seed houses after our schedules became reliable.”

“Reliable through an allocation not shown publicly.”

“You asked what depends on it. Dependence does not wait for honest labels.”

Senn inspected the shipping cards. The final seed-start rotation ended on the thirty-second day. One day of margin followed before rain shift. If the city's bloom stopped at the printed turn, several racks would move under electric lamps. Others would ship immature or be discarded.

A stolen benefit would have been easier to despise if it served only ornamental walls. The apricots were beautiful, but hospital reservoirs, bean yields, school hours, and regional seed stock had grown into the same eleven-day extension.

By late afternoon, pollen coated Senn's cuffs. She sat beside a canal gate and arranged her paper sheets on the warm stone. Public bloom ended after twenty-two days. Internal hospital, crop, and school schedules all continued to day thirty-three. Their instrument demands matched the allocation blocks in the winter debit, including the advance warmth that had woken the hives.

She drew a line through each block as it closed.

The final line, urban continuity, resolved into canal storage, tram-glass heating, and night market lamps. None required eleven full days alone. Together they kept municipal demand smooth after the public turn changed. Orison did not merely receive extra light. Its institutions had been designed around the absence of a visible transition.

Senn called Tovin from the canal wall.

His voice arrived under a crack of fabric. “If this is family, I am holding a kite spar over my head.”

“It is work.”

“Then I am holding two.”

“I need reserve absorption capacity for eleven bloom days removed from Orison.”

The fabric noise stopped.

“What timescale?” he asked.

“Immediate, then staged options.”

“Come to the south hangar.”

“Send the figures.”

“No. You will read the policy capacity, assume it means physical capacity, and accuse me of hiding a decimal.”

“Would I be wrong?”

“South hangar.”

Tovin ended the call.

The shade-kite hangars stood beyond Orison's last greenhouse tier, where the city's pale stone gave way to low service buildings anchored against wind. Senn took a maintenance tram. It passed beneath three collapsed kite frames, each longer than a market street. Their black fabric lay folded in ribbed crates. High-atmosphere shade kites did not block Varda directly. They altered where heat entered the ribbon and softened pressure differences before those differences became storms.

Inside the south hangar, Tovin hung from a safety line halfway up a test frame. He had their mother's narrow face and none of Senn's caution about heights. A crew below him tensioned a triangular panel while load meters blinked along its seams.

“You are late,” he called.

“You provided no appointment time.”

“You inherited that defense from childhood.”

Senn put on an impact hood and waited outside the marked fall zone. Tovin drove a locking pin into the spar, tested it twice, then descended. He smelled of resin and cold metal.

“Show me your eleven,” he said.

She handed him the folded allocation sheets. He read them standing beside the frame. Unlike Dael, Tovin moved quickly through numbers and returned to the physical object whenever a quantity displeased him. At the hospital reservoir block, he looked up toward the kite fabric. At the seed-start demand, he whistled once.

“You proved the link?”

“Every block. The hidden debit funds eleven extra bloom days embedded in these schedules.”

“Not decorative light.”

“Some decorative and commercial bloom appears inside municipal continuity. Most of the fixed dependency sits here.” She tapped the hospital, crop, and school sheets.

Tovin rubbed resin from his thumb. “Policy says reserve can absorb twelve Orison-equivalent bloom days.”

“I read the same capacity.”

“Policy imagines the whole fleet deployed at rated tension, with six hours' warning and no active rain shaping.”

“What does the fleet imagine?”

“Kites do not flatter us.”

He led her beneath the suspended frame. Gray patches marked repairs along three seams. Fine sensing threads crossed the fabric like veins. Each thread measured load and sent a release command if tension approached tearing strength.

“Removing light from Orison does not erase it,” Tovin said. “The Choir can turn mirrors away, but central heat already banked in canals and stone keeps moving. If we reduce the next passes too sharply, starward air cools faster than the darkward return. The pressure edge steepens over the greenhouse belt.”

“You shade the starward intake.”

“With active kites. Six are in overhaul. Four shape next week's rain front. Three carry fabric beyond preferred age because procurement counts folded cloth as if it does not fatigue.”

“And reserve?”

Tovin pointed toward a sealed cradle at the far end of the hangar. A black numeral 6 had been painted on its cover.

“Reserve 6 has not flown under full shear load this cycle. Policy counts it at one hundred percent. I count eighty-two until the test proves otherwise.”

“Eighteen percent uncertainty does not consume the whole capacity.”

“No. The hospital reservoir and crop soil do.”

He set the allocation sheets on a workbench and drew three curves across a dusted glass plate. The first showed reflected energy withdrawn. The second tracked stored heat leaving Orison's stone over several days. The third represented kite capacity needed to keep wind exchange below the shear threshold.

“If you cut all eleven at once,” he said, “the fleet absorbs the transition while Orison releases stored warmth unevenly. Greenhouse districts cool slowly. Open canal wards cool fast. You get cross-city pressure cells before the planetary model sees one clean front.”

“The Almanac can schedule district phases.”

“The Almanac receives roof-temperature summaries every twelve minutes. A torn panel changes the answer in less than one.”

“What staged removal can the reserve carry?”

“Not until I know fabric condition, rain commitments, and which mirrors can spread the angle changes.”

“I need a range.”

“You need permission to write a number. I need the kite not to split.”

Senn heard their old arguments inside the exchange. She had asked for quantities as a child; Tovin had brought her the broken latch or leaking pipe. Each thought the other mistook evidence for temperament.

A crew chief called him to the test rig. The frame would take simulated gust load in five steps. Tovin invited Senn behind the safety screen, perhaps because no explanation of capacity could compete with the sound of fabric under strain.

Pumps drew the panel outward. At twenty percent load, seams straightened. At forty, the entire hangar filled with a low drum note. Sensors painted green lines across the screen. Tovin watched one repaired corner rather than the average.

“Why that seam?” Senn asked.

“Thread response lags.”

“It remains inside tolerance.”

“For now.”

At sixty percent, the corner line turned yellow. Tovin stopped the test. The crew vented pressure, and the fabric settled with a long descending groan.

“Policy would continue to rated load,” he said.

“Would it fail?”

“Unknown. That is why we stop and inspect.”

“Your reserve capacity depends on a fleet full of unknowns.”

“Everything flying contains unknowns. Maintenance decides which ones may enter the sky together.”

They crossed to the panel. A sensing thread had loosened under its clear resin channel. No tear showed in the cloth. The defect only made the crew less certain about where one might begin.

Tovin knelt and marked the channel. “You can write that sudden withdrawal exceeds verified reserve until testing closes. You cannot write that Orison must keep the days.”

“I did not ask for that conclusion.”

“You came here wanting to switch off a theft.”

Senn looked at the black fabric, then at her papers on the bench. “I came wanting to know whether it could be switched off.”

“And?”

“The question had hidden dependencies.”

“Most useful questions do.”

He said it without triumph. That made accepting it easier.

The next morning, Senn returned to the greenhouse terraces before the first school shift. She followed the extra allocation forward rather than backward. The hospital had already placed future feverleaf use into treatment plans. Seed houses had promised trays to other districts. Schools had aligned transport heat with longer light. Hive rotations, canal storage, market cooling, and shade-kite maintenance assumed the same duration.

None of this made the winter debit lawful. Reliance could not turn an imposed burden into a weather share. A trade required a district able to refuse, and the debit named no district at all.

Yet closing the account at once would not restore what had been taken. It would transfer risk through systems built during twenty-two years of dependable spring. Hospitals and staple crops had the strongest claim. Schools and transport carried human costs less easily reduced to temperature floors. Decorative blossom could surrender first, but even that loss would alter pollinator timing and municipal work.

At the upper terrace, Senn found the girl from the school carrying another tray of beans. The child held it toward the light while her teacher adjusted a shade screen. One stem had bent during the climb. Senn steadied the tray while the girl tied the stem to a reed with green thread, then tested the knot by turning the leaves toward the mirror.

“Did you find out?” she asked.

“Some of it.”

“Where spring comes from?”

Senn looked across Orison. Apricot walls shone white. Canal frost had vanished. Bees moved through air warmed by a debit hidden behind infrastructure language. Farther south, the kite hangars kept their black reserve folded under a capacity figure no prudent pilot trusted.

“It comes through mirrors,” Senn said. “It also comes through choices about who receives cold.”

The girl frowned at the bean tray. “That sounds worse.”

“It is more complete.”

Senn walked to the Conservatory with pollen on her coat and the eleven allocation sheets under her arm. By the time she reached her desk, the city's beauty had stopped serving as an argument against the evidence.

It had become part of the evidence: living systems with roots in the hidden transfer, too dependent to cut blindly and too costly to leave unnamed.

Chapter 4 — The Season Table

Ceryn Ahl turned Lorn Dusk gray before Senn had permission to ask what it contained.

The name appeared only on Senn's private slate. On the circular instrument below, the corresponding sector carried no civic label, no population, and no climate floor. A narrow gray band extended darkward from the inhabited ribbon toward two radiator towers. When Ceryn touched the table's rim, the band faded into the infrastructure layer.

“Keep that layer open,” Senn said.

The First Curator looked across the circle. “We have not begun the review.”

“You have begun changing the display.”

“I am removing maintenance noise from a seasonal allocation question.”

Six people stood around the Season Table in the closed central chamber: Ceryn, Senn, Dael, a policy accountant, a crop-risk analyst, and the chamber operator. The doors had sealed behind them. Red light from the pressureworks windows crossed the floor but stopped short of the table, whose projected bands supplied their own color.

Orison glowed green through a broad section of the temperate ribbon. Its eleven added days lay over the city as a second, thinner band. Senn had never seen the benefit rendered so plainly. She had also never seen the gray that balanced it. Public displays clipped the map at the last inhabited districts.

Ceryn addressed the operator. “Restore infrastructure sectors at ten percent intensity.”

Gray returned, pale enough to overlook.

“Full intensity,” Senn said.

The operator waited for Ceryn.

“Twenty,” Ceryn replied.

Senn entered the instruction into her review notes. Ceryn noticed and made no objection.

“Senior Auditor Vale has established that Orison receives physically delivered bloom beyond its published turn,” Ceryn said. “The immediate purpose of this session concerns continuity risk during the open origin trace. We will not adjudicate historical policy.”

“Historical policy authorizes the current debit.”

“That remains your claim to establish.”

“The authorization field says inherited from originating order. Your office restricts that order.”

“Which makes access a separate matter.”

Dael leaned both palms against the table rim. “Light, heat, and restriction still occupy one system.”

Ceryn gave him a measured glance. “Your participation concerns mirror geometry, Engineer Orro.”

“Geometry also refuses separate matters.”

The crop-risk analyst coughed into her hand to hide a response. Ceryn let the interruption pass.

She began with the physical account. At her command, the table lifted Orison's bloom band above the map and divided it by use. Hospital heating showed blue-green. Food production carried a deeper green. Schools and municipal continuity appeared in amber. Decorative and commercial bloom occupied a white outer edge, smaller than Senn had expected but large enough to make the city famous.

On the balancing side, shade tracts darkened. Radiator duty brightened across the gray sectors. Wind-risk tolerance spread as a translucent field over the eastern terraces.

“Remove the winter debit,” Ceryn told the operator.

The gray sectors dimmed. Heat remained in the central ribbon. After nine modeled days, a red shear line formed over Orison and thickened toward the canal districts.

No one at the table treated it as a prediction. It represented a known risk under stated assumptions, useful only if the assumptions remained visible.

Senn opened the model key. “You preserved all eleven bloom days while removing only the balancing debit.”

“Yes.”

“That tests cancellation without correction.”

“It tests the current risk created by your freeze.”

“My freeze allows existing schedules for thirty-six hours and requires named renewal after that. It does not command eleven days of unchanged delivery.”

Ceryn folded her hands. “What replacement schedule did you submit?”

“None. I lack the source categories.”

“Then the operator used the schedule we have.”

The exchange had the polished quality of a corridor floor: no single step looked dangerous, but every direction favored the institution that owned it.

Senn touched the table and restored the gray infrastructure bands. “Show the protected floor for this sector.”

NO HABITATION FLOOR appeared above it.

“Show equipment tolerance.”

A web of blue limits covered the radiator towers, heat walls, coolant lines, and service roads. Domestic warming demand entered the web as a load. Medical reserve appeared as another equipment requirement. School-cycle consumption had been condensed into a periodic service peak.

“Why does a radiator sector have a school-cycle load?” Senn asked.

The policy accountant read from his slate. “Service instruction and dependent shift care.”

“Continuous through every turn?”

“Operations are continuous.”

“Birth records?”

“Not in the instrument bundle.”

“Burial heat?”

“Could reflect furnace maintenance.”

Dael's damaged fingers tightened on the table rim.

Senn shifted her attention to a second gray band. Its load curve displayed domestic heating at night-equivalent intervals. A third carried clinic storage and greenhouse illumination. None had an address ledger attached because the Almanac category did not request one.

“How many people live inside these sectors?” she asked.

Ceryn answered without consulting anyone. “The Conservatory does not maintain resident totals for thermal service precincts.”

“That is not zero.”

“No.”

The single word altered the chamber more than a denial would have. Ceryn never lied about a number once directly asked. Here she protected herself by refusing to pretend the absent total meant an empty place.

“Do people live there?” Senn asked.

“Personnel and dependents remain near darkward infrastructure.”

“Continuously?”

“Yes.”

“For how many years?”

“That question enters the historical policy matter I excluded from this review.”

Senn marked the answer.

The crop-risk analyst, Rema Tal, moved Orison's food band into the center. “If the auditor's freeze causes a lapse, seed starts fail before policy access changes anything. Can we separate hospital and staple use from the outer bloom?”

“We can separate the account,” Dael said. “The atmosphere will be less courteous.”

“Give me the uncourteous version.”

He spread the withdrawal over six mirror passes. The white decorative edge vanished first. Hospital reservoirs retained their input. Crop walls lost upper-tier light while seed houses stayed protected. Pressure differences formed along the city but remained below the red shear line for three days.

“After that?” Rema asked.

“Stored canal heat exits unevenly. Shade support becomes necessary.”

“Available?”

“Not at published reserve.”

Ceryn looked at him. “Maintenance reports thirty-six kites operational.”

“Operational does not mean available for this transition.”

“Your report lists reserve capacity above the modeled requirement.”

“My report lists rated capacity and maintenance state. Someone else condensed them.”

Senn thought of Tovin under the test frame, watching one lagging seam while policy counted the whole panel. The table rendered capacity as a smooth black arc. It did not show a loose sensing thread.

Ceryn asked the operator to save Dael's staged model. “This supports managed exposure during review.”

Senn looked up from her notes. “Define that phrase.”

“Maintain present balancing within verified equipment tolerance while we establish a safe transition.”

“For the gray sectors, present balancing includes imposed winter beyond an inhabited district's protected floor.”

“You have not established district status.”

“You have established continuous personnel, dependents, medical demand, school-cycle load, and domestic heat.”

“Those facts may support a status review. Until one occurs, the allocation remains governed by its existing category.”

“Then managed exposure means continuing the burden while the institution restricts evidence required to challenge it.”

Ceryn's face did not harden. Her attention grew more exact. “It means preventing an unmodeled correction from crossing survival limits elsewhere.”

“Whose survival limit appears for these sectors?”

“Equipment tolerance remains visible.”

“I asked about people.”

“The table cannot render a climate floor without recognized habitation.”

“Recognition requires the archive you will not open.”

The operator kept his hands flat beside the controls. Rema studied the crop band. The policy accountant stopped pretending to annotate.

Ceryn touched the table's outer ring and rotated the entire map. Gray sectors passed beneath her fingers. “You believe category follows fact directly. It does not. Category follows a legal finding based on fact. Without that sequence, every maintenance camp, seasonal crew barracks, and temporary shelter could alter the planetary floor during an active schedule.”

“Does every temporary shelter remain for decades?”

“You do not yet know the duration.”

“Because you restrict it.”

Ceryn let out a breath through her nose. “When I was nineteen, the pre-Choir allocations failed during a fungal famine. Local officials opened stores without coordination. Three districts protected their own seed. Two hospitals lost heat because no one preserved the central balance. My family did not survive the second shortage.”

No appeal entered her voice. She offered the history as context, not absolution.

“I have watched correct local claims destroy a planetary schedule,” she continued. “The Conservatory exists so necessity does not become a contest won by whoever can reach a valve first.”

Senn understood the argument. That made its omission clearer.

“A floor protects necessity from trade,” she said. “You cannot defend planetary coordination by hiding which people have one.”

“I am defending the sequence by which we establish it.”

“Your sequence begins after the burden.”

“Every inherited system begins after something.”

Dael looked from one woman to the other. “Metal does not pause for jurisdiction. Nor do lungs.”

Ceryn turned to him. “Which is why your unauthorized offsets present a separate stability risk.”

“You have not shown instability from them.”

“You concealed receiving geometry from an auditor.”

“I kept a clinic window inside tolerance.”

Rema shifted back from the table. The unnamed place now had a function in the official room, though not a location.

Ceryn said, “Submit the geometry under sealed maintenance review by tomorrow.”

Dael glanced at Senn. She had promised only that a compelled request would enter the record. Ceryn's instruction now stood in the chamber log.

“I will submit a risk statement,” he said.

“That did not answer the instruction.”

“It answered what I can submit without exposing an unrecognized facility to enforcement.”

Ceryn did not threaten him. “Your license terms make that decision reviewable.”

“Then review it.”

The table continued to glow between them, beautifully indifferent to professional ruin.

Senn returned to the gray sectors. “Display district totals.”

The operator brought up forty-one inhabited bands with population, climate floor, traded surplus, and current risk. Gray infrastructure remained outside the total.

“Add these sectors to the denominator as unclassified occupancy,” she said.

“I cannot assign population,” he replied.

“Add their measured thermal demand.”

“That mixes civic totals with equipment units.”

“The existing category already mixes them. Show the result.”

He looked to Ceryn again.

“Permit the comparison,” Ceryn said.

The operator converted domestic heat, clinic reserve, school cycles, and greenhouse illumination into a demand-equivalent band. Gray widened along the darkward edge. Orison's share of discretionary benefit decreased. The planetary total no longer closed under the district view because substantial lived demand existed beyond its civic boundary.

Senn asked him to isolate the largest sector and run a standard winter interruption. The operator hesitated over the controls.

“Standard for equipment or habitation?” he asked.

“That distinction concerns the review. Run both.”

Under equipment rules, the model allowed domestic heat to fall until radiator bearings approached their preferred range. Clinic storage retained a narrow protected line because medicine counted as maintenance supply. School-cycle load disappeared during the interruption. The sector remained blue, indicating acceptable machinery conditions.

The habitation model required assumptions the current file did not contain. Senn entered the measured continuous heat as a floor proxy and marked it provisional. Amber spread through the heat-wall network within six hours. By the second modeled day, the clinic reserve could protect treatment storage or household warming, not both.

“Clear the proxy,” Ceryn said.

“After the output saves.”

“It has no certified population basis.”

“It uses the demand your schedule already supplies.”

“Demand does not establish minimum entitlement.”

“It establishes what goes cold.”

Dael pointed to the radiator load. “Equipment safety improves in the first model because living spaces surrender heat. The table calls that spare tolerance.”

Ceryn examined the two outputs side by side. “Which proves why we need verified status before converting operational loads into Charter floors.”

“Or why excluding status produces a false margin,” Senn said.

Rema asked for the same interruption across the other gray sectors. One showed a greenhouse load large enough to preserve staple starts under equipment rules, though domestic walls could lose heat. Another protected coolant pumps while a periodic medical draw fell below its current pattern. The third lacked enough raw detail to model anything beyond aggregate power.

“Who owns these instrument bundles?” Rema asked.

“Darkward maintenance coordination,” the accountant said.

“Can they certify the uses?”

Ceryn answered, “Maintenance can certify measurement. Civic use requires policy verification.”

“So the people who see the loads cannot name them, while the people allowed to name them do not collect the loads.”

“That separation prevents local operators from assigning legal status through instrumentation.”

“It also prevents this room from seeing what its tolerances permit,” Rema said.

The crop analyst had entered the review to protect Orison's yield. Now she watched a modeled clinic line disappear beneath acceptable blue. Senn did not mistake discomfort for alliance. Rema could recognize the category's harm and still oppose any correction that endangered seed starts.
