The operator saved the interruption outputs inside the restricted session bundle. Export symbols remained red, but each model now had a timestamp and six witnesses.

Rema leaned closer. “How long has the denominator excluded that load?”

The accountant answered, “The current series inherits its precinct definitions.”

“From when?”

“Originating policy.”

The same phrase had become a locked door every office could point toward.

Senn asked the operator to save the comparison. A red restriction symbol appeared.

“Why can I not attach it to the origin trace?”

“Policy-derived category conflict,” he said. “The instrument data can leave this room. The combined civic rendering cannot without First Curator release.”

Ceryn said, “I will release the physical model and the staged-risk comparison. The gray-sector reconstruction remains internal pending legal review.”

“It uses measurements already in my audit bundle.”

“It also assigns civic meaning the current measurements do not possess.”

“Meaning or consequence?”

“Both require authority.”

Senn saved the raw demand conversion on paper, writing each value by hand because the table would not export its combined image. The operator watched, uncertain whether copying visible numbers violated the restriction. Ceryn allowed her to finish.

When Senn reached the last sector, she noticed a narrow gap between its current winter burden and equipment tolerance. The table showed that gap as available capacity. If people occupied the sector, their own heat demand filled much of it. Under the infrastructure category, human use counted against machinery but did not establish a floor. The system recognized bodies as load and rejected them as claimants.

“Does managed exposure have a maximum duration?” she asked.

Ceryn consulted the meeting clock. “Until safe transition parameters are approved or the category changes.”

“That is not a duration.”

“It depends on work not yet complete.”

“Who reviews continuation?”

“My office.”

“Under what legal authority?”

“The originating emergency classification and inherited seasonal powers.”

“Name the classification.”

Ceryn looked toward the policy accountant. He lowered his slate.

“Thermal service precinct,” she said.

The term entered the official transcript.

The accountant requested a recess to consult policy counsel. Ceryn denied it.

“If the category governs current allocation, this room can name its basis without counsel,” she said.

Senn noted the decision. “Does the originating classification authorize exclusion from public forecast notice?”

“I will not interpret unseen language from memory.”

“Then release it.”

“Archive access and interpretation follow separate controls.”

“Which control allows the table to use the result while denying the rule?”

Ceryn looked at the gray bands. “Operational continuity. The Conservatory cannot stop every inherited instruction while its legal file travels upstairs.”

“How long does a legal file travel?” Dael asked.

“That depends on the challenge.”

“Longer than light.”

Ceryn ignored the line. She directed the operator to attach the session transcript to any archive petition Senn filed, but not the restricted models themselves. The concession gave Senn proof that the chamber had discussed the category. It withheld the clearest display of its consequences.

Senn searched current policy from her wrist slate. No public Charter manual returned the category. An index reference pointed to sealed emergency orders below the Conservatory. Access required archival authorization or a written legal basis from the First Curator.

She opened a formal request field.

Ceryn watched her. “You may submit an archive petition after the session.”

“I am submitting a narrower question now.”

“State it.”

Senn wrote while the Season Table held the darkward sectors at twenty percent gray.

Identify the current legal category and active authority under which continuously occupied thermal service precincts are excluded from district totals, climate-floor rendering, and civic forecast notice.

She selected WRITTEN RESPONSE REQUIRED. The field asked for grounds. She attached the First Curator's acknowledgment of continuous personnel and dependents, the measured domestic and clinic loads, and the table's exclusion from district totals.

Ceryn read the request across the shared review display.

“You have combined technical findings with a legal conclusion,” she said.

“I requested the category behind the findings. You may correct my conclusion in writing.”

The policy accountant shifted his weight. A spoken refusal could later become context. A written answer had to name the door and who held its key.

Ceryn touched her seal to the receipt field. “Response due by the end of tomorrow.”

The operator printed a narrow transaction strip and passed it across the table. Senn checked the request number, due time, and attached transcript range before she signed receipt. Ceryn added her own witness mark. No later summary could reduce the exchange to an informal question raised during technical discussion.

The request left Senn's slate and entered the Conservatory record. She folded the paper strip around her handwritten sector values and placed both inside the origin-trace envelope, where removal would require a second seal.

Only then did the chamber operator close the infrastructure layer. Gray sectors vanished from the Season Table, but their measured loads remained on Senn's paper in blue ink, widening the planetary total beyond the map permitted on the screen.

Chapter 5 — Order Nine

Pera Iven put both hands beneath the archive box and refused to let Senn lift it by the cord.

“The cord closes the lid,” she said. “It does not forgive gravity.”

“I was not going to carry it by the cord.”

“You had the look.”

“What look?”

“The efficient one.”

Pera set the box on a felt pad and loosened its brass clasp. Blue dust marked her fingertips. Around them, the emergency archive descended in narrow iron aisles below the Conservatory. Paper occupied the coldest rooms. The ventilation kept mold low, ink stable, and every clerk unhappy after twenty minutes.

Senn had been below for an hour.

The written response from Ceryn's office had arrived that morning. It named the category as THERMAL SERVICE PRECINCT under inherited emergency authority, then cited Order 9 and six renewal instruments. It did not provide the documents. Access remained subject to archive handling rules, which required a clerk to retrieve paper originals because the policy scans were sealed against general export.

Pera had read the response twice and said, “That category sounds like an administrative ghost.”

Now she opened the first box.

Inside lay construction permits, radiator schematics, food allotments, temporary shelter rolls, and weather exceptions from thirty-one years earlier. Every bundle carried a blue thermal-ink label fading toward brown at its handled edges. The paper smelled of mineral sizing, old cold, and the faint oil used on order seals in the lower archive.

The present Choir comprised 240 mirrors, 36 shade kites, and 18 radiator towers. These papers came from the smaller system that had not yet commissioned its final darkward towers, when a temporary camp could still sound temporary to someone reading in Orison.

“Order series runs by emergency domain,” Pera said. “Crop, transit, medical, thermal, civil. The number alone does not give us a shelf because previous archivists believed future people would enjoy puzzles.”

“Ceryn's response cites thermal.”

“Thermal contains nine boxes for that year. Some numbering resets by quarter.”

“Efficient.”

“You have the look again.”

They began with the index cards. Senn read headings while Pera checked embossed seals under a low side lamp. The emergency had covered radiator construction delays, worker housing, coolant rationing, and a pressure-front deviation that threatened two unfinished towers. Temporary camps appeared across four darkward work zones.

The first reference to precincts occurred in a food allocation note. Service precinct population to be provisioned under equipment-support headcount pending Order 9 classification.

“Population,” Senn said.

Pera leaned over her shoulder. “Not equipment load.”

“Before the order.”

“Someone knew workers ate even before policy improved them.”

Senn copied the reference. Pera found a corresponding shelter roll with one hundred eighty-six names. Each had an expected departure date within two years. Family status showed only for medical rationing.

“Construction camps can contain families,” Senn said.

“They can. The question concerns when temporary stopped describing them.”

The next box held the originating meeting papers. A gray ribbon separated drafts from adopted orders. Pera checked every page number before allowing Senn to unfold the packet.

Order 9 began with three pages of engineering necessity. Towers 12 through 18 had to remain staffed through extended winter operation. Construction weather had exceeded its allocation. The Charter's inhabited-district process would take longer than the emergency allowed. Therefore four named zones would receive a temporary thermal service precinct classification.

The fourth page defined the legal effect.

Residents would draw heating, medical support, and food through infrastructure accounts. Precinct occupancy would not enter district climate-floor calculations. Local settlement rights remained deferred because the camps existed solely for completion and maintenance of planetary equipment.

Senn read the phrase twice. “It calls them residents here.”

“And occupancy in the next line,” Pera said. “Then removes both from habitation.”

“The category does not claim no people exist.”

“It claims their presence belongs to the equipment.”

They reached the duration clause.

Authority shall expire upon commissioning of the designated towers or five years from adoption, whichever occurs first. Continuation requires civic-status review under the Charter of Habitation.

Pera placed a paper marker beside it. “There is your legal answer.”

“It expired.”

“Unless a renewal altered the duration.”

“Ceryn cited six.”

“Then someone kept teaching the ghost new dates.”

Senn recorded the exact language, including the review requirement. The original signatures belonged to emergency governors now dead or retired. Ceryn's name did not appear. At the time of adoption, she had not led the Conservatory.

Pera turned the final page over. The reverse carried a routing stamp to the Almanac policy office: OCCUPANCY LOAD TO REMAIN EQUIPMENT-ONLY FOR ALLOCATION PURPOSES.

That instruction explained the Season Table's gray bands. It also predated permanent households, local births, and any evidence Senn could yet prove existed.

“Find the commissioning dates,” Senn said.

Pera consulted the infrastructure register. “Towers 12 through 15 entered service within three years. Sixteen and seventeen in year four. Eighteen missed the five-year limit by seven months.”

“So most precinct authority should have ended before the maximum.”

“If the whichever-comes-first clause survived renewal.”

They requested the first continuation box. The retrieval system responded with a shelf code two levels deeper.

A hand lift carried them below the public archive floors. Frost edged the iron rails. Pera distributed fingerless archive gloves and warned Senn not to breathe directly over thermal ink.

“Does anyone?”

“Senior officials lean closer when a document contradicts them.”

The first renewal had been filed one month before the five-year limit. Its opening report acknowledged permanent repair crews, expanded greenhouses, two births, and a burial ground. A settlement survey had been ordered but not completed. The continuation extended the classification for three years pending civic review.

“Already outside the original purpose,” Senn said.

“Already carrying the facts needed for habitation.”

“Continuous civic heat, address ledger, births or burials.”

Pera counted on blue-stained fingers. “Heat through the infrastructure account. Births and burial here. No ledger yet.”

A reference in the survey annex read: Local address book maintained by precinct keeper; format not certified.

“Ledger,” Senn said.

“Apparently a book becomes an address ledger only after the right office admires its binding.”

They copied the line.

The second renewal followed three years later. It shortened the stated purpose to operational continuity and removed construction language from the header. Its attached survey listed households by heat-wall section but withheld district status until radiator staffing stabilized.

The third cited crop instability and extended the category another three years. The fourth cited darkward maintenance shortages. Each included a recommendation for review. Each postponed that review until after the present weather risk.

A pattern emerged in the margins. Early pages used temporary residents. Later clerks wrote precinct households. Medical allotments changed from shift casualty provision to clinic reserve. School transport appeared under dependent instruction. The category remained unchanged while its contents learned to walk, vote locally, grow food, bury relatives, and repair the walls that heated them.

Senn's feet had gone numb by the time they opened the fifth renewal box.

Pera made her stand on the warming grate while they logged the first four packets back into temporary custody. A temperature tag had shifted from blue to white on one folder, so Pera replaced its felt pad and waited until the paper cooled inside range. No discovery excused damaging the record that carried it. Senn flexed feeling into her toes and copied the custody times while the archive pipes ticked overhead. When the tag darkened again, Pera sealed the earlier box before touching the next clasp.

The file was thicker than the others. Its seal named the severe crop emergency seventeen years earlier. Fungal loss had struck central seed stores. Hospital nutrition demand exceeded reserve. The Conservatory redirected maximum bloom toward seven ribbon cities and increased darkward radiator duty.

Pera read the first page in silence. “This one had a real emergency.”

“They all cite one.”

“This one has mortality forecasts attached.”

Senn examined them. Without expanded bloom, food deficit models crossed hospital thresholds in four districts. The risk did not excuse an unlawful category, but it explained why an administrator might avoid reopening every dependency while crops failed.

The renewal language extended precinct status for the emergency duration plus one turn, subject to full review afterward. An annex acknowledged permanent households and requested a plan for climate-floor recognition once central reserves recovered.

The signature page was missing.

Page numbering moved from eleven to thirteen. Binding holes showed that a sheet had once sat between them. Blue fibers clung to the cord where someone had removed paper without unbinding the packet cleanly.

Pera brought the side lamp close. “Not a scanning omission.”

“Could it be misfiled?”

“Could be. It could also be sleeping inside someone's harmless crop annex.”

“Who had authority to sign?”

“The First Curator, two emergency governors, or a delegated panel if the instrument changed Charter status.”

“Does the routing record name the signatory?”

Pera checked the back sheet. “Received under valid seal. No name.”

Senn looked at the empty interval between pages. She did not yet know who had approved the fifth continuation. Guessing would turn evidence into theater.

Pera did not accept the vacancy until she had tested the packet's assembly marks. Every renewal sheet carried a pinprick code near its lower edge, almost invisible unless light passed through the paper. She arranged pages nine through thirteen over a glass plate and dimmed the room lamp.

Codes formed a sequence of tiny bars. Page eleven ended with a left-facing notch. Page thirteen began with the matching right-facing half. A sheet from the same print run had occupied the gap.

“Could page twelve have contained only a routing cover?” Senn asked.

“Routing covers use coarse paper and no sequence marks. This belonged to the instrument.”

Pera measured the torn blue fibers under a lens. “Removed after binding. Not recent. The exposed ends have browned with the rest of the packet.”

They searched the box lining, then the paired crop-emergency folders. Senn lifted every annex while Pera checked folio numbers. A fungal-loss report held three detached authorization sheets, all for seed transport. A hospital allocation contained a signature page filed backward, but its order code differed.

The shelf register showed that the renewal packet had left the archive four times. Two withdrawals supported later policy migrations. One went to crop accounting. The oldest had no borrower name because the former ledger recorded offices only.

“Can we infer where the page went?” Senn asked.

“We can list where it might have traveled. We cannot select the most satisfying destination.”

“Policy archive?”

“Possible. Crop annex, possible. Misfile, possible. Deliberate removal, also possible.” Pera wrote each in the condition report with equal spacing. “Suspicion needs good shelving.”

“Record missing page twelve,” Senn said. “Include fiber condition and intact sequence marks.”

Pera wrote the note on a separate sheet, signed it, and asked Senn to witness. “Now nobody can put a page back and claim it never traveled.”

“Do you expect that?”

“I expect archives to contain everyone's past confidence. People become imaginative when confidence ages badly.”

The sixth renewal had no ordinary signature packet. It consisted of an inheritance notice generated after the crop emergency ended. Existing classifications would continue while policy review queues cleared. A clerk had marked the precinct file for review, but an automated continuation field had carried the category into the next seasonal cycle.

After that came annual administrative notices rather than formal renewals.

“How many?” Senn asked.

Pera checked the box depth. “Enough to ruin dinner.”

They sampled the first, middle, and current years, then returned for every notice when the wording began to shift. Early forms read REVIEW REQUIRED. Later versions added continuity pending review. Seven years before the present, a policy migration converted the inherited status into active until superseded.

No official had to choose the burden again. The system treated the absence of a superseding order as permission to continue.

Senn laid the notices in chronological order along the felt table. “The current authority cites the originating emergency.”

“Which gave five years.”

“Then formal renewals extended it.”

“Without the fifth signature page.”

“And administrative inheritance carried it after the last stated review.”

Pera tapped the current notice. “This does not renew the legal authority. It only says the database found no replacement.”

“Policy may argue inherited seasonal powers.”

“Policy may argue a cupboard remains locked because no one located the key. That does not turn the cupboard into a law.”

Senn almost smiled. “Put that in the remarks field.”

“I prefer continued employment.”

They moved to the current cross-reference bundle. Ceryn's written response had cited only the order and renewal chain, but Senn wanted every live system attached to the category. Pera requested policy indexes, measurement ledgers, and address-related fields.

The policy index arrived first. Thermal service precinct mapped to equipment occupancy, maintenance staffing, medical reserve, dependent instruction, and local provision. The measurement index attached radiator loads, domestic heat, greenhouse lamps, burial furnace use, and clinic storage.

The address index returned ACCESS RESTRICTED, then offered a set of reference codes.

Pera stared at the screen. “It says addresses exist.”

“It says references exist.”

“Current references. Look at the update dates.”

Each precinct code linked to a live external ledger source. The Conservatory did not ingest names, but it queried household counts whenever it calculated heater demand. One source updated every seven days. Another recorded additions after births. A third adjusted heat-wall occupancy when someone moved.

Pera opened the query history rather than the protected ledgers. The first visible transaction asked how many heated rooms had changed occupancy since the prior schedule. A second requested age bands for clinic-reserve forecasting, stripped of names before it entered the Almanac. The latest transaction sought school-cycle participation by heat-wall section.

“Those are civic questions,” Senn said.

“Asked through an equipment interface.”

“Can we see who designed the interface?”

Pera traced its revision history. The earliest form asked for worker bunks and active shifts. Later revisions replaced bunks with heated rooms, then households. A policy migration had preserved the output label as occupancy units even after the input became local addresses.

Senn compared two consecutive weekly returns. The household total changed by one. A note attributed the change to a new address assignment after a birth record. No name crossed into Conservatory custody, but the added heater demand entered the next winter calculation.

“Can we attach this history?”

“The query language, yes. Local values remain restricted.” Pera printed the field definitions and stamped them as derivative records. “This proves what the system asks, not who answered.”

“It proves the category needs household facts to function.”

“It also proves someone took care to strip the words that would make those facts civic.”

“Equipment-only occupancy with address-ledger references,” Senn said.

“The ghost receives mail.”

They printed the index results because export restrictions blocked the linked data. Each page carried reference codes, update times, and query purposes. The system used address changes to forecast domestic heat while denying that those addresses established habitation.

One code recurred across clinic reserve, school-cycle demand, and burial heat. It belonged to the largest gray sector on the Season Table. Senn entered it into the archive catalog. A bound local ledger appeared as the source, held outside Conservatory custody. The place name remained masked under policy classification.

“Can we unmask it from paper?” she asked.

“Not in this series. External registers sit in correspondence or field survey.”

“Find the most recent survey request.”

Pera searched. The terminal produced a travel authorization draft from eight years earlier, canceled before issue. Another appeared four years later. Both cited the need to verify continuous occupancy at radiator settlements. Neither named the places in the visible header.

A current maintenance route attached to the same codes, scheduled three days from now. Its destination field remained sealed, but the travel office had assigned darkward transit provisions to two Conservatory staff.

Senn recognized Dael's engineer code among them.

The second traveler field stood blank.

“He has a route,” she said.

Pera read the schedule. “You have an origin trace and a written restriction. Does that authorize field verification?”

“It authorizes me to establish whether the debit affects a climate floor.”

“Policy will disagree.”

“They can do it in writing.”

Pera rubbed warmth back into her hands. “I would like to be present when you say that.”

“You have spent seven hours in a basement with me. Do not become reckless.”

They reboxed every document in reverse order. Pera aligned page edges, replaced gray ribbons, and tied each cord without pulling against the lid. The missing signature sheet received a vacancy marker cut to the exact page size. When the packet closed, the blank paper remained visible between pages eleven and thirteen.

Before shelving Order 9, Senn read the duration clause once more. Five years or tower commissioning, whichever came first. Civic review required for continuation. The language contained no power to defer recognition indefinitely because weather remained difficult.

At the upper archive desk, Pera prepared a retrieval report. She listed the originating order, six continuations, administrative inheritance notices, the missing page, and current address references. Her summary avoided conclusions. Precision gave the facts their own pressure.

Senn attached the report to her origin trace and requested access to the external address ledgers. The system denied the request under the same thermal service precinct category she was challenging.

She appealed through field verification. This time the travel office accepted the filing and placed her name in the blank traveler field beside Dael's.

“Destination still masked,” Pera said.

“It will appear when the route opens.”

“Comforting.”

On the desk between them, the current index printout showed weekly household updates feeding an account that claimed equipment-only occupancy. New addresses changed heat demand. Birth-related additions changed reserve forecasts. Local burial use altered furnace schedules. The Conservatory trusted each reference enough to balance weather against it.

Senn folded the printout beside her travel authorization. Order 9 had begun as a five-year exception for construction work. Thirty-one years later, live address-ledger references still entered through its equipment category, carrying households into the calculation without carrying their right to refuse.

Chapter 6 — The City Under Winter

The reflected dawn failed while Senn still had one boot on the tram step.

A white seam had been growing along the eastern cut, narrow enough to mistake for frost on the glass. Dael had watched it through the final descent from the ridge station. When the seam should have opened across Lorn Dusk, it contracted to a pin, trembled, and disappeared.

Nobody on the platform looked up twice.

Porters kept unloading insulated grain bins. Two children dragged a sled of ceramic heat vanes between the rails. A woman in a quilted coat turned the dial on a public light post, extending its lamp by another hour with the practiced annoyance of someone correcting a familiar lie.

Senn stepped down into air that bit through her trouser seams. “The transit schedule marked a dawn window.”

“Transit schedules mark many ambitions,” Dael said.

His breath smoked under the platform lamps. Above them, permanent night remained complete except for a green auroral thread and the red warning points on radiator towers 14 and 15. Their vibration traveled through the rails into Senn's boot soles, too low to hear cleanly. She felt it in her teeth.

A station board clicked from REFLECTED DAWN to MUNICIPAL LIGHT CONSERVATION. No cancellation notice followed. No cause appeared. The board simply revised the present as if no other present had been promised.

Senn took out her slate. “Which mirror carried the window?”

“Not yet.” Dael closed his hand over its edge. His burned fingers did not straighten, even inside thick gloves. “You came to see a place. Let it arrive before you turn it into a fault code.”

She considered telling him that the fault code had arrived first. Instead she put the slate away.

A person waited beyond the freight arch with a lantern hung from a hooked staff. They wore a mechanic's coat patched at both elbows and carried no Conservatory insignia. Gray hair showed beneath a wool cap. Their gaze settled on Senn, moved to Dael, then returned to the clean seams of Senn's audit coat.

“Dael Orro,” they said. “You have taken your time.”

“I brought an auditor. That made every gate slower.”

“Auditors increase in mass near locked doors.”

Senn offered her hand. “Senn Vale.”

“Hesh Dara.” Their mitten touched hers once. “Keeper of the address ledger, because a place excluded from the map has to appoint someone to remember where its doors are.”

Hesh led them under the freight arch before Senn could answer. Lorn Dusk descended through a shallow basalt basin around the two radiator towers. From the ridge, Conservatory diagrams rendered the basin as service roads, heat conduits, and maintenance sheds. At street level, its geometry belonged to use rather than planning. Enclosed bridges joined roofs to preserve warmth. Greenhouse windows glowed violet behind frost shutters. Painted signs advertised vane repair, fermented root cakes, coat mending, and three performances at the coolant-cistern theater.

The failed dawn changed work without stopping it. Shopkeepers clipped portable lamps above counters. A school procession crossed the main lane carrying shared heat bricks under canvas. Mechanics at an open wall recess shifted a household exchanger from reflected-light mode to stored return heat. Nobody behaved as though darkness proved an emergency. Everyone paid for it in small labor.

Senn watched an old man lift two heat bricks from a communal cart. “How often do windows cancel without notice?”

Hesh kept walking. “You have asked for a total before learning what we count.”

“I asked how often.”

“Then you will dislike the answer until you understand the nouns.”

Dael made a quiet sound that could have meant sympathy for either of them.

Their route narrowed between buildings whose outer walls shared a continuous copper pipe at shoulder height. Residents pressed gloved palms to inspection plates as they passed. Every fifth doorway had a ceramic tag showing current wall temperature and the next household's duty shift.

Hesh stopped beside a plate marked 42.6. “Shared heat wall. Return flow comes off tower coolant after the exchanger strips industrial contaminants. Every address accepts a temperature band and a maintenance watch. When a reflected window arrives, domestic demand falls and we bank the excess. When a window disappears, somebody feeds the wall.”

A teenage mechanic crouched below the pipe with a ceramic wrench. Hesh asked, “Loss?”

“Point six across the western run.”

“Valve or seam?”

“Seam near the school coupling. Mara took felt and clamps.”

“Put the clinic branch above domestic reserve until she closes it.”

The mechanic turned the wrench one notch. Heat clicked through the pipe. No central dispatcher authorized the change, yet each doorway tag updated as the local control loop accepted it.

Senn crouched beside him. “Does this meter feed the Conservatory?”

“Tower service does.” He looked to Hesh before continuing. “Household tags feed our hall.”

“Why separate them?”

“Because your ledger calls my kitchen a coolant accessory.”

Hesh thanked him and moved on.

Cold collected in Senn's cuffs. She had expected hardship because the instruments showed hardship. Expectation had not supplied workshops, school duty, theater notices, or the quick resentment of a mechanic who knew precisely how his kitchen entered her accounts. The place required no romance to disprove its category. It only required doors.

At the next junction, three women rolled insulated soil trays from a greenhouse annex. Pale shoots stood in neat rows under low amber lamps. One woman held a tray toward Dael.

“Eyr stock,” she said. “Your aunt's bean line, unless she lied to improve the sale.”

“She would call that calibration.” Dael pinched one leaf between his fingertips. “Too much blue in the nursery light.”

“We lost the blue quarter with dawn.”

He looked toward the dark cut where the reflected seam had vanished. “Use amber for six hours, then half-cycle the violet. Don't chase growth with heat.”

Senn heard the familiarity beneath his instruction. “You have worked here recently.”

Dael released the leaf. “Recently covers useful ground.”

“How much ground?”

Hesh opened a frost-stiff door. “You may interrogate him after tea. Lorn Dusk has endured winter without the additional burden of watching two Conservatory employees quarrel in a lane.”

Warm air touched Senn's face. The address hall occupied a former parts depot with a high arched ceiling. Shelves held bound books instead of tower bearings. A communal stove burned pressed greenhouse waste near the entrance. On the far wall, thousands of names had been painted in narrow white lines, grouped by street and household rather than year.

The room contained more people than Senn expected. A birth clerk wrote beside a steaming kettle. Two couriers sorted maintenance slips. An elderly couple argued over whether a lane number had moved when a bridge closed. At a long table, a child copied names from a household card into a practice book.

Hesh hung the lantern from a ceiling hook. “Coats stay on. The western seam is losing heat.”

Senn approached the shelves. The oldest volumes used rough construction paper. Later books carried leather corners, stitched repairs, and heat-darkened labels. Each spine listed a range of addresses. Several began before the emergency order's five-year limit could have ended.

“Continuous?” she asked.

“From the first camp row.”

“Births and burials?”

“Separate indexes, cross-bound by address.”

“Civic heat records?”

“Wall logs downstairs.”

The Charter tests assembled themselves in her mind: continuous heat, an address ledger, local births or burials. Hesh had named all three without citing law.

Senn set her case on the long table and removed a pair of archive gloves. “I request permission to inspect and copy the ledger sections corresponding to current tower telemetry.”

“No,” Hesh said.

The refusal came without anger. Senn had encountered officials who denied access through delay, procedural ambiguity, or a sudden need to consult absent superiors. Hesh simply stood between her and the shelves.

“I have an open origin trace carrying protected climate-floor implications.”

“You have a Conservatory seal.”

“That seal permits evidentiary collection.”

“From recognized districts.” Hesh took a cup from the stove shelf. “Your permit cannot decide whether it applies here without first deciding the matter under dispute.”

Senn disliked the accuracy more than the refusal. “A witnessed copy would preserve your custody.”

“Custody has preserved itself for three decades.”

Dael accepted a cup from the birth clerk and did not intervene.

“Without documentary comparison,” Senn said, “policy accounting will call my field observations anecdotal.”

“Your office called our addresses equipment labels when we sent them voluntarily.”

“That happened before my trace.”

“Every central visitor believes history began when their file opened.”

The child at the table stopped writing. Hesh noticed and lowered their voice, though none of the force left it.

Senn folded the archive gloves together. “What condition would permit inspection?”

“Inspection has begun. Copying has not.”

“I am looking at spines.”

“You are looking at a room that your maps deny. Try to gather evidence from it.”

Hesh gave Senn a cup. The tea tasted of roasted root and mineral-heavy water. Around them, clerks continued their work. A courier brought three household changes to the table: one family moving across the west bridge, a mechanic taking a room above the vane shop, and a baby assigned the same address as her mother. The birth clerk challenged the bridge number. The elderly couple joined the dispute. Within two minutes the room resolved which door would receive emergency heat if the west bridge failed.

No one consulted the Conservatory.

Before the ink dried, a delivery worker came in with a heater strapped to his back. He had carried it from an empty room on Lantern Row to a family above the market whose youngest child had developed a cough. The transfer needed two address marks: one to show where the heater had left the wall balance, another to assign responsibility for its filters. Lio Marr checked both against a paper street diagram patched with translucent cloth.

Senn asked why the heater did not remain attached to its registered room.

“Rooms do not breathe,” Lio said. “People do.”

The answer exposed another category error, local and quickly corrected. A fixed asset register would have preserved the heater's location while defeating its use. Lorn Dusk kept both facts: which wall owned the machine and which lungs needed it tonight.

Hesh showed Senn the reverse of the transfer slip. Three earlier loans filled the margins, each signed by the receiving household and the duty mechanic. “Our records move because our heat moves. If you copy one page without its relations, your office will call the crossings inconsistent.”

“Are they reconciled at the end of each winter turn?”

“Every six days during ordinary load. Every night during a cancellation.”

Senn examined the signatures without touching the slip. A clean central record would have condensed the heater into domestic demand. Here, its movement joined a child, a filter schedule, two rooms, and the person obliged to bring it back. The detail did not weaken evidentiary value. It explained why a total alone could not carry custody.

Hesh waited until the entries had dried, then took Senn through a rear corridor. Chalk bars covered its plaster from waist height to the ceiling. Some stood alone. Others formed rows with dates and tiny annotations. Fresh marks showed bright white; older ones had sunk into the gray wall beneath hand grease and stove smoke.

“This morning?” Senn asked.

Hesh drew a short bar beside the door. “Cancelled window. Twenty-three minutes promised.”

“Why chalk?”

“Because the public schedule can replace its own record. Chalk requires a person to erase it.”

Several marks carried circles. A few had been crossed in blue pigment.

“Blue means restored within the same turn,” Hesh said. “Circle means a clinic, school, or nursery load depended on the light. A diagonal cut means notice arrived before we committed heat.”

Senn found very few diagonals.

Near the floor, a child's shorter marks clustered in an uneven row. One had a date but no duration. Hesh touched it with two fingers.

“Ressa made those when she could first hold chalk. The window vanished after children had assembled to see it. We call that a broken season.”

“Not every cancellation?”

“No. Weather changes. Machinery fails. A promise breaks when someone who could have warned us chooses not to count the people waiting.”

The distinction resisted Senn's categories because it joined service, notice, and standing in one local term. A broken season did not mean unpleasant weather. It recorded a civic relationship that had failed.

She took out her paper notebook rather than the slate. “May I copy the key for the marks?”

“You may write what I say.”

Hesh dictated the symbols. Senn recorded each definition exactly. At the end, they added, “No total without the associated wall log.”

“Because dependency varies.”

“Because a missed light window can cost a tray of beans, an hour of school, or a body. The chalk does not pretend those carry one unit.”

A radiator pulse passed through the corridor. Dust slipped from the upper plaster. The vibration strengthened, held for five breaths, then receded.

Dael put down his cup. “Tower 15 changed load.”

No alarm sounded. Hesh opened the basement door, and cold coolant odor rose from below. They descended into a low service room where copper returns entered the building through ceramic collars. Four residents already stood at the control rack. The teenage mechanic from the lane called temperatures while another person adjusted branch gates.

“West seam closed,” Mara reported. Frost whitened her sleeves. “Tower return fell one point eight.”

“Dawn cancellation plus industrial step,” Dael said. He leaned over the analog pressure tubes. “You are drawing the clinic reserve early.”

“We drew it when the school coupling fell.” Hesh checked the wall chart. “Can tower service spare two degrees?”

Dael's hand hovered over a sealed Conservatory control plate. Breaking it would place his initials in another maintenance history. Senn recognized the pause from the mirror offset: not uncertainty about what to do, but calculation of how much concealment the action required.

“Do not touch it,” she said.

Four faces turned toward her.

Dael's expression closed. “Then the western wall drops below band in forty minutes.”

“I did not say leave it.” Senn pointed to the manual bypass beside the sealed plate. “Can local authority open that gate?”

Mara nodded. “For equipment protection.”

“Does the rule define occupied heat walls as equipment?”

Hesh gave a humorless laugh. “When cold harms us, we become residents. When warmth reaches us, we become equipment.”

“Open the bypass under local equipment authority,” Senn said. “Record school, clinic, and domestic demand separately. Dael can advise without entering the control.”

Mara opened the gate. Pressure moved through the tubes, sluggish at first, then steady. The wall temperature climbed by tenths. Dael watched the joints for strain and told the crew where to listen for hammer in the return pipe.

Senn wrote the entire operation into her notebook. The same classification that denied habitation granted enough maintenance discretion to prevent immediate freezing. Its contradiction had become a tool people needed to survive.

When the western run stabilized, Hesh brought them to the coolant-cistern theater. The disused tank formed a deep circular room lined with ceramic tiles. Seats descended around a low stage built over old pipe anchors. During heat emergencies, Hesh explained, insulated curtains divided the space into sleeping bays because the buried cistern retained warmth longer than street-level halls.

Tonight, children rehearsed a weather pageant under portable lamps. One child wore a silver mirror mask. Another operated a canvas cloud on a pulley. The actor playing winter had wrapped herself in so many coats that she could barely bend her knees.

A teacher halted the rehearsal as Hesh entered. “We lost the dawn cue.”

“Keep the scene,” Hesh said. “Change the line.”

The mirror child lifted her mask. “Again?”

“Schedules change. Plays remember.”

Senn stood at the upper rail while the children revised their entrance. The winter actor crossed the stage first, dragging strips of white cloth. The cloud followed. Last came the mirror, late and visibly carried by a child whose shoes flashed beneath the costume.

“You wanted proof of a city,” Hesh said beside her. “Cities contain bad theater.”

“It is not bad.”

“You have central standards.”

“We have larger budgets.”

Hesh's mouth shifted by a fraction. “That can imitate quality from a distance.”

After rehearsal, they returned to the address hall. More people had gathered, summoned not by Hesh but by news of the auditor. They brought household cards, burial copies, heater repairs, school attendance books, and clinic-window requests. Senn saw enough documentation to bury any claim of temporary occupancy. She also saw how easily she could reduce it to a perfect Charter checklist and repeat her old mistake in a new form.

She opened her case but left the archive gloves inside.

“My first request was premature,” she said.

A man near the stove asked, “Does that mean you withdraw it?”

“No. It means I asked for your evidence before learning what your evidence protects.”

Hesh sat at the head of the long table. “Better phrasing does not earn a ledger.”

“I know.” Senn placed her notebook before them, open to the chalk key. “Tell me the conditions.”

“Names first. Not totals, not representative cases. You will hear who kept each record, which wall they lived behind, and what happened when the scheduled season broke. After that, you may ask what can leave this room.”

The demand threatened every efficient instinct Senn possessed. Eight thousand residents could not fit into an audit interview. Yet Hesh had not said every resident. They had said the names attached to whatever record Senn hoped to carry away.

Dael leaned against the stove wall, waiting without rescue.

Senn removed the copying lens from her case and set it on the table with its cover closed. “No images tonight.”

Hesh called the birth clerk forward. She introduced herself as Lio Marr, keeper of current household changes and the western bridge register. Beside her, the teenage mechanic gave his name, address, and duty relation to the school coupling. Mara followed with the seam repair, then the clinic branch she had protected.

Senn wrote each name before the associated quantity.

Outside, tower vibration passed through Lorn Dusk's shared walls. The promised dawn did not come back. In the hall, Hesh opened the first household book but kept it on their side of the table.

Senn listened.

Chapter 7 — People Counted as Weather

Senn balanced Hesh's oldest address ledger on two felt blocks while live tower figures crawled across her slate.

The ledger remained on Hesh's side of the table. Senn had earned inspection, not custody, after a night of names and heating histories. At each page turn, Lio Marr recorded the folio number and checked Senn's bare hands for chalk, ink, or oil. Copying lens, stylus camera, and archive film stayed locked in Senn's case.

Dael had gone to sleep on a cot above the vane shop. Senn suspected he had not slept, but his absence removed one source of useful evasion. Across the address hall, dawn-shift clerks ate root cakes while settling household changes. Tower vibration made the tea in Senn's cup quiver.

“Page,” Hesh said.

Senn read the first current household entry aloud. “Western Bridge, heat wall branch C, rooms twelve through fifteen. Six adults, four children. One medical reserve connection. School coupling south.”

Hesh turned the volume without letting her touch it. “Telemetry.”

She opened the Conservatory maintenance feed for the same branch. Tower 15 listed no household demand. Under AUXILIARY EQUIPMENT OCCUPANCY, four decimal loads appeared: baseline thermal retention, daytime cycling, reserve draw, and intermittent instructional operation.

Senn converted each quantity from radiator-return loss into expected interior use. The calculation took less than a minute.

“Baseline covers six adult-equivalent bodies at sleeping temperature,” she said. “Daytime cycling adds four smaller occupancy curves. Reserve draw matches one medical heater. Instructional operation follows your school hours.”

Nobody at the table answered.

She checked the next address. Three adults worked rotating tower shifts, which altered their heat use across the day. Conservatory telemetry described the variation as mobile maintenance demand. A baby born seven months earlier appeared in the hall's household book. The central feed had registered a new low-volume heat source eleven days after the birth, then classified it as sensor drift until a quarterly average absorbed it.

“Why eleven days?” Lio asked.

“The Almanac requires persistence before accepting a new equipment load.”

“So it waited to see whether he would continue existing.”

Senn looked at the baby's name. “That describes the calculation.”

“It does not improve it.”

“No.”

Hesh placed a narrow paper strip beside the book. On it, the child's mother had recorded heater use during his first fever. Senn entered the dates manually. The same rise appeared in tower telemetry as temporary coolant inefficiency.

Each euphemism preserved physical truth. Heat had gone somewhere. Pumps had carried it, walls had retained it, warm bodies had altered return temperatures. The instruments counted every consequence of human presence while policy refused the presence itself.

Senn built a comparison grid with three columns. The first held addresses. The second held local civic loads. The third held Conservatory categories. Hesh would not allow names into the slate, so she used household codes assigned by Lio for the witnessed session.

Western Bridge C became thermal retention, reserve draw, and instructional operation.

Lantern Row 8 became distributed valve protection and nonproductive moisture control. The latter matched cooking steam.

Market Loft 3 became elevated nocturnal leakage. Two elderly sisters lived there and kept their wall warmer because one had damaged lungs.

At the fourth page, Senn stopped converting. “How many addresses feed tower 14?”

“Current or historical?” Hesh asked.

“Current first.”

Lio gave a number from the daily register. Senn queried the tower bundle for independently cycling loads that met domestic heat profiles. The result matched, except for seven entries.

“Seven absent?” Hesh said.

“Seven more in telemetry than the current ledger.”

Lio pulled a bridge-closure sheet. Five households had moved during a pipe repair. Their old branches still carried minimum heat to prevent cracking. One extra load served the theater's stored costumes. The last belonged to a public washroom.

“The feed overcounts occupied addresses,” Senn said.

“By counting empty heated rooms,” Hesh replied.

“Yes. Which proves it cannot distinguish an inhabited room from protected fabric unless policy gives it a civic record.”

“Your office had the civic record.”

Senn enlarged the source field attached to equipment occupancy. An old registry code appeared, followed by a note: LOCAL ADDRESS REFERENCES NONAUTHORITATIVE.

“Who marked them nonauthoritative?”

Hesh drew the book closer. “That question belongs to your house.”

By midday, the comparison grid held fifty addresses. Every clinic reserve matched a named medical branch. Every school load followed actual attendance windows, adjusted for cancelled light. Domestic use resolved to household size within the ordinary variation for insulation, shift work, age, and illness.

One discrepancy remained in the clinic column. The local ledger showed a sterilizer cycle every third morning, yet tower telemetry carried a smooth daily draw. Senn asked Nima Sol's duty assistant to bring the clinic switch book. A man named Per Jalo arrived with a narrow volume, a coil thermometer, and impatience sharpened by a missed meal.

“We bank heat on cycle morning,” he said. “The sterilizer takes it at noon. Your tower sees the bank fill every day because the wall controller limits the rise.”

Senn drew the controller curve on paper. “If I remove household baseline, the stored increment should form three equal steps.”

“It will form two equal steps and one smaller one. Laundry shares the third return.”

She recomputed. The curve separated exactly as he described. What appeared centrally as constant equipment demand contained sterilization, laundry, and a deliberate reserve for clinic instruments that could not freeze between cycles.

“Who authorizes the bank?” Senn asked.

“Clinic keeper and western wall duty.”

“Does tower control receive that authority?”

Per turned the switch book around. Two signatures accompanied each cycle. “Tower control receives the load. Authority remains here because tower control claims we are a tool shed.”

Hesh asked Senn to include the switch book in her comparison grid.

“I can cite its existence,” she said. “You have not permitted a copy.”

“Then inspect every entry in the current turn.”

Per read dates while Lio checked signatures. Senn compared them against twelve live-history points. Eleven aligned within measurement tolerance. The last ran warm by enough to suggest an extra heater.

“That day?” she asked.

Per touched the margin. “A patient could not leave after treatment. We opened recovery bed two.”

Central telemetry had labeled the rise adaptive enclosure stabilization. The phrase could have described a valve housing. It had measured a person too weak to walk home.

Senn added a fourth category to her grid: local decision. Numbers alone showed use, but governance showed habitation. People scheduled scarce heat, assigned duties, witnessed exceptions, and argued about competing needs. Machinery did none of those things without an accountable instruction.

The clinic assistant watched her write. “Will that make us real?”

“You are real without my finding.”

“Will it make your office act as if we are?”

Senn capped the pen before answering. “It will remove one excuse.”

Per accepted neither comfort nor promise from that. He took his thermometer and went back to the clinic.

Next, Senn tested whether central forecasting predicted individuals closely enough to expose them while denying them status. She selected a school branch with Hesh's permission and overlaid five days of load. Arrival peaks showed children bringing cold clothing into warm rooms. Midday dips marked greenhouse practice. A late rise followed an evening class for tower apprentices.

“Does the Conservatory publish these profiles?” Hesh asked.

“Only aggregates outside protected maintenance access.”

“Protected from whom?”

“Competitors, sabotage, household identification.”

“We receive none of that protection because the households officially do not exist.”

The point required no elaboration. Senn reduced the slate to hourly blocks before allowing anyone else to view it. Even here, evidence needed boundaries. Proving surveillance did not authorize more surveillance.

Only after those tests did Senn ask the hall clerks to choose three records she had not seen. She wanted to rule out a curated sample. Hesh chose none of them. Lio asked a courier, the child practicing address entries, and the elderly man who had argued about the bridge number.

The courier brought a workshop household above a ceramic kiln. Its heat signature should have been difficult to separate from industrial use. The ledger tracked sleeping rooms by the hour and subtracted kiln recovery through a manual vane reading. Conservatory telemetry carried the residual as operator-presence compensation, a phrase that acknowledged workers without granting them homes.

The child chose her own building. Nine people occupied four rooms around a narrow stair. Two attended school. One worked nights at tower 15. A greenhouse lamp warmed the landing because an inner room had no window. The central categories reproduced each cycle exactly.

Last came the old man's closed bridge address. When the bridge failed, his household moved for nineteen days but continued heating the original wall to protect pipes. The Almanac had split them into two equipment profiles during the move, then merged the demand after their return. Local records showed one family occupying two civic obligations for a defined interval.

Senn pressed her thumb against the edge of the slate. “The classification has more information about your daily life than many recognized district schedules.”

“Recognized districts call that forecasting,” Hesh said.

“Here it becomes machinery.”

“Here it becomes permission.”

The afternoon light window arrived on schedule. A pale band opened high above the basin, reflected by mirrors too distant to see. Nobody left the hall. Their faces brightened by a few degrees while frost on the upper windows turned translucent. Senn watched the telemetry respond. School lamps stepped down. Heat-wall draw eased. Greenhouse shutters opened. The Almanac recorded reduced equipment occupancy demand.

On paper, Hesh noted: REFLECTED WINDOW RECEIVED, FULL DURATION PENDING.

“Why pending?” Senn asked.

“Because arrival does not guarantee completion.”

The answer sounded severe until the reflected band flickered. Several clerks looked up. Light steadied after two breaths, but no one crossed out the caution.

Senn requested the policy objective associated with the tower bundle. Her origin-trace authority admitted her one layer deeper than it had in Orison. Text appeared above the live feed:

MAINTAIN THERMAL SERVICE FUNCTION. LIMIT EQUIPMENT OCCUPANCY LOSS WITHIN ACCEPTED PRECINCT TOLERANCE. PRIORITIZE REGISTERED HABITATION FLOORS AND CENTRAL CROP CONTINUITY.

Hesh read over her shoulder. “We lose after registered habitation because we do not count as registered habitation.”

“Yes.”

“What does accepted mean?”

“That an authorized order defined the risk tolerance.”

“Accepted by whom?”

Senn searched the field. It linked back to the renewal chain Pera had found, then stopped at the missing signature page. “The current schedule inherited acceptance. I cannot yet identify the last human review.”

“You can identify who did not accept it.” Hesh tapped the address book.

The reflected light strengthened. A warm stripe moved down the wall of names. For several minutes, each painted line carried its own thin shadow.

Senn exported no data. Instead she wrote a formal observation on paper and asked Hesh to read it before she sealed it:

Live Conservatory telemetry for radiator precincts 14 and 15 resolves into continuous domestic heat, medical reserve, educational operation, and locally governed civic infrastructure corresponding to witnessed entries in the Lorn Dusk address ledger. Policy accounting classifies these loads as equipment occupancy despite instrument patterns consistent with human habitation.

Hesh crossed out consistent with.

“The patterns do not resemble habitation,” they said. “They measure it.”

Senn considered evidentiary caution. “Telemetry alone cannot prove names.”

“The ledger supplies names.”

“Which I cannot cite until you release a copy.”

“You are using our refusal to weaken what you have seen.”

“I am stating what another auditor can reproduce without privileged access.”

Hesh pushed the paper back. “Then write two claims. One for what your machine proves. One for what we prove.”

Senn revised:

Telemetry proves continuous human-scale heating, clinic, school, and domestic cycles within a category designated for machinery. Witnessed local records identify the people, addresses, and civic obligations generating those cycles.

Hesh read the sentences twice. “Better.”

The light window completed. Lio drew a blue cross through the morning's cancellation mark but left its chalk bar visible beneath the pigment. Restored within the same turn, Senn remembered. A service could arrive late and still owe an account of the waiting.

Dael entered near evening carrying a pressure gauge and three bowls of stew. “Tower 15's return manifold disagrees with its own maintenance summary.”

“By how much?” Senn asked.

“Enough that I need the histories before I answer. Not enough to interrupt your constitutional court.”

“This is an audit.”

Hesh accepted a bowl. “She has learned that nouns matter. We have not yet reached adjectives.”

Dael set the gauge beside Senn's case. “Did the loads resolve?”

“You knew they would.”

“I knew what some of them served.”

“All fifty matched.”

His eyes moved to the open policy objective. “That language used to say transient operator load.”

“When?”

“Before my license.”

Hesh put down their spoon. “You have seen earlier wording?”

Dael rubbed the scarred joint of his forefinger. “In Eyr training copies. The categories changed as families grew, but never toward residence. Operators became occupancy. Temporary shelters became thermal enclosures. The system learned to describe us more accurately without naming us.”

Senn added his statement to the witness sheet. “Will you authenticate a training copy if Pera finds one?”

“Yes.”

“Under your license?”

“That question has delayed twelve years of honest work.”

Hesh watched him with an expression Senn could not interpret. Eyr origin had shaped his access to Conservatory work, but Hesh did not turn the admission into solidarity. Dael had left. He had helped in secret. Both facts remained present.

After the hall closed to routine traffic, Hesh brought out three proposed ledger pages. They did not choose the oldest or most tragic entries. One covered Western Bridge C, including the school coupling and medical branch. Another covered Market Loft 3, with the sisters' warmer wall band. The third recorded a week of moves during the bridge closure.

“Why these?” Senn asked.

“They show ordinary governance,” Hesh said. “Your institution understands disaster better than life. We will not prove residence by presenting ourselves only at the point of injury.”

The pages contained names. Senn felt the condition before Hesh stated it.

“Witnessed copy,” they said. “Lio operates the lens. You receive sealed images with no permission to detach household names from their loads. Any public filing may use addresses only with our review. The originals remain here. Your chain of custody begins with this table, not with your seal.”

Senn opened her case and slid the copying lens across the wood. “Agreed, subject to one limitation. I cannot give you veto power over an audit finding.”

“Not a veto. A right to identify mistranscription and loss of context.”

“That I can grant.”

Lio cleaned the lens plate with a square of boiled cloth. Hesh positioned the first book on felt supports, pressed each corner flat with ceramic weights, and read the page number aloud. Senn entered date, time, room temperature, witnesses, and seal condition. Dael signed only as observer.

The lens made a soft click.

One page became evidence without leaving the people who had kept it.

They repeated the process twice. On the final image, a shadow from the stove crossed the lower margin. Senn rejected the copy before Hesh could object. Lio repositioned the lamp, and the second exposure caught every entry cleanly.

Senn sealed the three images in her origin-trace packet. The packet asked her to classify the submission: measurement error, unauthorized allocation, archival discrepancy, district-boundary conflict, or other.

None described people accurately counted as weather.

She selected OTHER and opened the free field.

Dael read from across the table. “You are filing tonight?”

“The evidence will not improve by sleeping.”

“The towers might worsen.”

“Then your evidence will join mine.”

Hesh stood behind her, close enough to see each word. Senn began with the Charter definition rather than the winter debit. Continuous civic heat. Address ledger. Local birth and burial records. She cited the live telemetry comparison, the witnessed pages, and the policy objective that subordinated equipment occupancy to registered districts.

At the remedy field, she requested no immediate schedule halt. The choice cost her. Continuing the debit meant continuing an unlawful burden; freezing every transfer without a thermal model could move danger into other homes. She asked instead for provisional protection of climate-floor evidence, a ban on destroying or condensing precinct records, and an expedited determination of habitation status.

The form required a title.

Senn wrote: HABITATION CHALLENGE: THERMAL SERVICE PRECINCTS 14 AND 15.

Hesh touched the page title. “Lorn Dusk.”

“Policy needs the precinct numbers to bind the active schedule.”

“People need the name to know the challenge includes them.”

Senn revised: HABITATION CHALLENGE: LORN DUSK / PRECINCTS 14 AND 15.

She added Eyr and Nacre Fold as related unresolved allocations, not yet evidenced by local records. Dael supplied the instrument bundle identifiers without comment.

The Conservatory seal field pulsed red. Filing from an unrecognized district required a registered civic node, which Lorn Dusk officially lacked. Senn could route through the tower maintenance terminal, but that would reproduce the category under dispute.

Lio pointed toward the public light controller. “It receives schedule notices.”

“Not civic filings,” Senn said.

“Does it have a return channel?”

“For fault acknowledgement.”

Hesh understood first. “Then acknowledge a fault.”

Senn connected her slate to the controller. Its narrow return channel allowed two hundred characters and an instrument attachment. She sent the filing seal as a schedule fault, citing denial of protected habitation review. The system rejected the first attempt because no recognized population could experience the fault.

On the second attempt, she attached the tower telemetry proving human-scale loads. The controller hesitated. Policy logic encountered physical fact, failed to reconcile them, and elevated the packet to her open origin trace.

A receipt appeared.

Hesh read it aloud. “Submission accepted pending standing determination.”

“Circular,” Dael said.

“Recorded,” Senn replied.

She printed the receipt on the hall's fading thermal paper. One copy went into her case. Hesh bound the other behind the three source pages.

Outside, municipal lamps dimmed for the sleep interval. Heat kept moving through the shared wall. On the table, the address ledger remained open beside a filing that could no longer claim nobody lived at its source.

Interlude I — Order Nine

EMERGENCY MEETING RECORD / YEAR 44 AC / PRESSUREWORKS ROOM 3

The ninth meeting began at 04:10 with two radiator foundations open to frost and a construction population scheduled to depart before winter turn.

Present: Deputy Curator for Heat Export; Choir works engineer; Charter counsel; camp logistics officer; Almanac policy clerk; recording secretary.

The works engineer reported that towers 14 through 18 could not accept full load until coolant trenches closed. Temporary camps contained rotating crews only. No civic charter had been issued. Food, heat, medicine, and sanitation traveled through construction accounts.

Charter counsel asked whether any worker intended to remain after commissioning.

The logistics officer answered: “Not under the current contracts.”

Counsel requested language limiting the exemption to five years or completion of the radiator works, whichever came first.

The policy clerk proposed: THERMAL SERVICE PRECINCT. OCCUPANCY LOAD TO BE TREATED AS EQUIPMENT-DEPENDENT FOR PURPOSES OF CLIMATE-FLOOR ALLOCATION.

Question: Would personnel retain workplace safety minima?

Answer: Yes. Equipment protection tolerances exceeded the minimum required for temporary sleeping quarters.

Question: Would births, schools, domestic heat, or local burial authority exist inside a service precinct?

Answer: No such civic functions were planned.

The secretary recorded a pause of seven minutes while tower 16 lost pump pressure. When the meeting resumed, the works engineer recommended immediate adoption. A crop-light transfer waited on confirmed radiator capacity. Delay would reduce seed starts in four ribbon districts.

Order 9 passed without objection at 05:02.

Order review status: review required.

Expiry field: FIVE YEARS.

ATTACHMENT / CAMP SERVICES / YEAR 44 AC

Beds authorized: 860 rotating.

Heat walls: industrial dormitory standard.

Medical provision: first-aid station, no inpatient reserve.

Education: none.

Address ledger: equipment inventory only.

Burial authority: transfer to nearest chartered district.

Margin note in brown ink: Families not permitted under works insurance.

FIRST RENEWAL ABSTRACT / YEAR 49 AC

Tower commissioning remained incomplete at three sites. Permanent maintenance staffing exceeded original projections. Service housing had expanded beyond dormitory rows.

Local submission reported forty-one children receiving instruction in a converted valve store. The submission requested recognition of a school heat cycle.

Policy disposition: instructional load may be entered under operator continuity. No change to precinct category.

A bound address book accompanied the request. Its pages listed doors, household occupants, emergency heat duties, and two local births. The receiving clerk indexed it under equipment location references.

Reviewer's note: Reclassification during active commissioning could interrupt radiator guarantees supporting central crop schedules. Renew for limited continuity. Survey after works completion.

Renewal term: three years.

Renewal review status remained review required.

BURIAL TRANSFER EXCEPTION / YEAR 50 AC

Request originated from precinct 14 after ridge transit closed for eleven days. Family asked to bury a tower mechanic locally rather than retain the body in an unheated freight store.

Charter counsel approved one emergency burial without district implication.

Coordinates entered into the works map.

Name entered into a local wall record.

Central index label: PERSONNEL DISPOSITION / EQUIPMENT SITE.

No habitation review opened.

SECOND RENEWAL EXCERPT / YEAR 52 AC

Commissioning certificate now covered all five towers. Works authority recommended dissolution of the camps.

Maintenance division objected. Trained crews lived adjacent to equipment; moving them to the ribbon would increase response time beyond bearing-failure tolerance. Existing households had built heat walls tied to coolant return.

A representative from precinct 11 submitted apprenticeship rolls. A representative from precincts 14 and 15 submitted school attendance, heater duty, and burial records.

Minutes summarized both submissions as continuity data.

One reviewer wrote: Temporary description no longer accurate.

A second hand answered in blue thermal ink: Operational dependence remains accurate. Category governs service, not settlement form.

Renewal approved pending policy survey.

The pending-action field still read review required.

POLICY SURVEY REQUEST / YEAR 53 AC

Question 1: Do precincts sustain continuous civic heat?

Response omitted. Tower-return telemetry attached.

Question 2: Do precincts maintain address ledgers?

Response: Local equipment-location books exist but lack Conservatory authority.

Question 3: Do births or burials occur locally?

Response: Personnel events have been recorded for logistical continuity.

Survey conclusion: Insufficient evidence of chartered habitation.

Routing note: Do not circulate outside heat-export policy. Reclassification may alter active bloom balancing.

That year, the first expanded crop-light transfer entered the Almanac. Darkward loads accepted longer winter exposure within inherited equipment tolerances.

THIRD AND FOURTH RENEWALS / CONSOLIDATED ABSTRACT

Population language disappeared from the header.

The third renewal cited radiator stability, maintenance response, and central crop continuity. An annex listed domestic heater profiles but called them enclosure retention. Clinic reserve draw appeared as instrument protection. School lamps appeared as operator training load.

The fourth renewal inherited every policy category unchanged. Its reviewer requested updated surveys from three precincts.

Lorn Dusk sent an address total and copies of twelve household pages.

Eyr sent apprenticeship completions, birth entries, and ceramic-vane workshop registrations.

Nacre Fold sent a clinic ledger and local burial coordinates.

The records office separated each submission from the renewal form. Address pages went to equipment indexing. Birth entries went to staffing continuity. Clinic records went to radiator-risk modeling. Burials went to works-site safety.

No single file retained the fact that all four categories described the same families.

Fourth renewal term: two years.

Fourth-renewal disposition preserved review required.

CROP EMERGENCY ANNEX / YEAR 58 AC

Fungal loss crossed three staple reserves. Central greenhouse projections showed failure under ordinary bloom allocation. The Conservatory ordered maximum viable light for seed recovery, hospital food production, and protected nursery stock.

Heat-export modeling required continued darkward precinct tolerance. Immediate recognition of household climate floors would remove capacity from the emergency schedule before alternate shade or radiator repairs could be deployed.

A technical note stated: Service records now indicate permanent households, multigenerational occupancy, local schooling, and independent heat governance. Existing category does not describe settlement conditions.

Below it, an unsigned policy response read: Description and allocation function must be distinguished during emergency continuity. Reclassification deferred until crop reserve recovers.

The fifth renewal authorized the existing category for one additional emergency term.

Its signature sheet carried a First Curator seal. The archived abstract preserved the office, date, and authority but not the signatory name.

Review field: review required upon crop stabilization.

AUTOMATED STATUS NOTICE / YEAR 60 AC

Crop reserves recovered above emergency threshold.

No policy survey had been assigned an active officer. Heat-export schedules depended on the current precinct codes. The Almanac requested disposition before the next bloom turn.

Administrative inheritance selected the latest valid authorization.

A clerk opened the status notice, marked the crop emergency closed, and forwarded radiator maintenance reports to engineering. The precinct review field remained unassigned.

At 18:44, the inheritance service tested whether Order 9 had a superseding instrument.

Result: none located.

It tested whether the originating equipment still operated.

Result: active.

It tested whether dependent schedules referenced the category.

Result: active dependencies found.

The service retained the thermal service precinct classification. A nightly policy copy propagated the result into mirror scheduling, heat-export forecasts, archive indexes, and the next turn's crop plan. No clerk received a warning because inheritance counted as continuity rather than amendment. Local records kept accumulating under the old labels while each dependent schedule made later review appear more disruptive.

In the copied header, one phrase changed without a meeting, witness list, or new signature.

review required became continue until superseded.

Act II — The Cost of Legibility

Chapter 8 — Forty-Six Days

Dael broke the seal on tower 15's history cabinet with Senn watching his hands.

The cabinet had not opened cleanly in nine years. Frost sealed the lower hinge, and the authorization wafer flashed amber because his license allowed remote maintenance review, not local extraction of protected history coils. He warmed the hinge with his palm heater, entered his Conservatory number, and accepted the violation notice.

“You could request emergency access,” Senn said.

“Then Orison would decide whether this counts as an emergency.”

Hesh stood at the service-room door with two Lorn Dusk mechanics. “We decided when the return manifold dropped.”

Dael pulled the cabinet open. Cold metal scraped across the floor. Inside, ceramic memory coils sat in numbered slots from Year 44 onward, each one recording radiator load, inlet temperature, bearing vibration, coolant pressure, and maintenance interventions. Central summaries retained ten years at full resolution. The local coils had kept all thirty-one.

He removed the oldest coil using both hands. Burns tightened the fingers of his left hand when cold reached them. Senn noticed, as she noticed anything that impaired measurement, but said nothing.

A portable reader waited on the workbench. Mara had built its frame from a school-lamp ballast and two ceramic vane brackets. The instrument would offend Conservatory procurement. It also held calibration within half a degree.

“Source?” Senn asked.

“Eyr apprenticeship pattern,” Mara replied. “Dael pretends he invented it whenever central engineers visit.”

“Eyr patterns use three brackets.”

“We had two.”

“Then you improved it.”

Senn connected her slate through an isolation loop. “No edits pass from my device to the coils.”

Dael checked the loop himself. A green telltale lit on the reader. Only then did he seat the first coil.

Tower history opened as a stack of narrow traces. Winter turns appeared in blue, reflected windows in white, industrial heat-export steps in red. Dael had learned to read the shapes before he learned the policy names. At eleven, in Eyr, he could identify a failing coolant pump by the shiver it put through a wall cup. His apprenticeship exam had asked him to convert that knowledge into approved units and forget who drank from the cup.

“Start with baseline,” Senn said.

“Baseline changed.”

“Then show each change.”

He aligned the original Order 9 schedule with current tower duty. Construction years carried irregular demand. Crews arrived and left. Dormitories heated in blocks, then went cold. After the first renewal, the traces settled into household rhythms. Radiator export rose as central bloom transfers expanded. Reflected windows shortened by minutes, then hours, then whole service days.

Dael marked every winter extension beyond the settlement's nominal schedule. Senn created a parallel table and made him justify each boundary.

“That blue shoulder could be weather variance,” she said.

“Not with a red export step twelve minutes earlier.”

“The tower accepted more central heat.”

“And local light dropped to keep the darkward exchange inside shear tolerance.”

“Causal link?”

He enlarged the control sequence. EXPORT REQUEST ACCEPTED appeared first. PRECINCT WINDOW MODIFIED followed. No storm measurement intervened.

Senn entered the day.

They worked through three coils before the room warmed enough to soften the frost on Dael's cuffs. Hesh's mechanics rotated out for wall duty. Mara stayed, checking the reader against analog gauges whenever a trace showed impossible smoothness. Two such sections came from central archive compression; the local coil preserved real vibration beneath the summary.

By the eighth winter turn, the pattern no longer required argument. Extra cold accumulated whenever Orison and the six central cities extended bloom. Some turns gave Lorn Dusk several days back through late reflected windows. Other turns carried the deficit forward under heat-export necessity.

Senn asked, “What averaging interval does policy use?”

“One turn.”

“That conceals carryover.”

“It calls carryover a new baseline.”

“Use rolling six-turn windows.”

Dael did. The first interval averaged nineteen additional winter days. Later windows climbed through twenty-seven, thirty-five, and forty-two. The expanded crop transfer pushed the curve higher. For the most recent twelve turns, Lorn Dusk averaged just above forty-eight.

Hesh brought food at the sixth hour and found none of them eating. They set bowls directly over Senn's notes until she moved the paper.

“What does the number say?” they asked.

“Lorn Dusk alone runs higher than the combined precinct estimate,” Senn said. “We need Eyr and Nacre Fold histories before asserting the burden across all three.”

Dael opened a side index. “We have mirrored service packets.”

“Authorized?”

“No.”

Hesh looked at him. “How did they arrive?”

“Maintenance crews exchange failure patterns. Central summaries remove too much.”

“Does Eyr know you kept them here?”

“Their tower keepers know.”

“That did not answer the question.”

Dael set down his spoon. “Household councils did not authorize evidence transfer. The packets contain tower data, not address records.”

Senn closed the side index. “We can inspect technical history. We cannot use local civic loads without custody.”

“They still answer the duration question,” he said.

“If you authenticate source and transmission.”

His own hidden practices kept turning into procedural work. For years, secrecy had let him act faster. Under Senn's attention, every useful shortcut developed a constituency he had failed to ask.

They opened the Eyr packet first. Tower 11's ceramic-vane signatures felt familiar enough to become smell and texture in Dael's memory: kiln dust, mineral glaze, hot wool drying over wall pipes. The packet carried checksums from three maintenance shops. He named each shop and the keeper who had sealed its section.

Eyr's winter extensions ran slightly below Lorn Dusk's. Nacre Fold's lava-tube insulation reduced domestic loss, but its narrower reflected-light path produced longer outages when mirrors shifted. Senn made separate calculations for duration, temperature depth, and clinic interruptions. She refused to turn unlike harms into one quantity.

“For schedule duration only,” Dael said, “weight the three by inhabited load.”

“Population?”
