“Heat profiles. We do not have authorized population records for two settlements.”

“That repeats the category problem.”

“It avoids inventing people while still measuring where winter landed.”

Senn considered the formula. “Calculate both unweighted and heat-load weighted. Label the limitations.”

The two results differed by less than a day. Dael reran them with uncertain start boundaries, deleted compressed years, and conservative assumptions that treated partial cancellations as ordinary winter. The range tightened around forty-five to forty-seven.

He entered the midpoint in words rather than digits: forty-six additional winter days per turn, average across Lorn Dusk, Eyr, and Nacre Fold.

Hesh read it from behind his shoulder. “People here know winter grew.”

“I know.”

“They will ask why this number matters more than their memory.”

“It does not. It matters to a system that moved the days and pretended they had no destination.”

Senn initialed the calculation. “It also defines the dependency. Removing forty-six days from one side puts the heat somewhere else.”

No one mistook the proof for a remedy.

On the second morning, Dael turned from schedule histories to machinery. Tower 15's vibration trace carried a narrow harmonic at three times shaft frequency. It appeared only under high export. Central maintenance had classified it as sensor resonance.

Mara pressed a metal listening rod against the return pipe and offered him the padded end. A rough pulse struck through the rod every third rotation.

“Bearing cage,” Dael said.

“Which one?” Senn asked.

“Upper secondary, probably. We need a shutdown inspection.”

“When can it stop?”

Dael checked the forward export schedule. “Not during the next bloom step without moving load to tower 14.”

“Can 14 carry it?”

Mara answered first. “Not at listed tolerance.”

They opened tower 14's service history. Coolant pressure had begun falling after each high-load cycle, recovering too slowly for ordinary seal contraction. Dael traced the decline to a flange installed during a rushed overhaul. Its thermal expansion coefficient differed from the pipe collar by a small amount. Thirty cycles had turned that difference into a leak that appeared only when the tower cooled.

“Two towers,” Senn said.

“Here.” Dael opened Eyr's packet. Tower 11 showed vane-control lag under rapid export steps. The ceramic vanes moved correctly once positioned, but an actuator drew increasing current. One hard correction could stall it between angles.

Nacre Fold's packet added tower 17. Its intake screen had collected shell-lime dust from settlement repairs. The pressure loss remained below emergency tolerance because local crews cleaned it twice as often as central maintenance assumed. Recent traces showed cleaning no longer restored full flow.

Senn listed them. “Four radiator towers.”

“Four urgent,” Dael said. “Others need ordinary work.”

“Define urgent.”

“Tower 15 risks bearing damage under sustained high export. Tower 14 loses coolant during recovery. Eleven may fail to angle its vanes during a fast load step. Seventeen cannot reach listed intake flow.”

“Could all four fail together?”

“Anything could. That question does not help.”

“Could one schedule event stress all four together?”

He looked at the central bloom trace. “Yes. A sudden cancellation of the darkward debit would transfer heat and control demand across the same network.”

Senn wrote without triumph. Evidence kept removing easy choices.

A history trace could justify opening machinery, not condemn it. Dael told Mara to request a reduced-load inspection on tower 15. The reply granted fourteen minutes between export steps. Fourteen minutes allowed no bearing removal, but it permitted a listening survey and oil sample if everyone moved before the load fell.

They crossed the covered service bridge in hard-soled boots. Tower vibration strengthened until each handrail buzzed. Beyond the insulated windows, radiator fins rose into blackness, shedding a faint heat shimmer that bent the stars. Frost retreated from the tower base in a perfect arc.

Mara closed the secondary load gate. Dael watched shaft speed descend while Senn called time from the doorway. At half load, he pressed three wax pickups against the bearing housing. The first flattened evenly. A fine repeated ridge marked the second. The third came away with a ridge deep enough to feel through his glove.

“Cage strike confirmed,” he said.

“Time,” Senn called. “Nine minutes.”

Dael opened the oil port. Black lubricant filled the sampling tube, carrying bright metal flecks that turned under his lamp. A sensor-resonance fault could not shed steel.

Mara swore under her breath. “How long?”

“At current high-load frequency, I will not guess.”

Senn stepped closer. “Give a range.”

“Days under continuous maximum export. Several turns under ordinary cycling. Every strike damages the race.”

“Can it be replaced locally?”

“Upper secondary requires a lift cradle. Lorn Dusk's cradle failed certification because a central inspector counted patched cables as automatic rejection.”

“The cables hold above rated load,” Mara said.

“I know. A hearing about procurement will not lift this bearing.”

Four minutes remained. Dael sealed the oil port, pulled the pickups, and signaled for return to load. The gate resisted at three-quarters travel. Mara struck its actuator casing with the heel of her wrench, once, at a point worn pale by earlier blows. The motor caught and opened.

Senn recorded the strike. “Routine method?”

“Approved method failed years ago,” Mara said. “This method works.”

“That answer belongs in the repair request.”

“It has appeared in six.”

Back inside, Dael compared the sample with a preserved tube from the prior winter turn. Metal content had more than doubled. He sealed both under Hesh's local witness stamp and Senn's audit mark.

Tower 14 required a different test. Its coolant loss appeared only during recovery, so the crew could not inspect it between export steps. They routed a low-temperature diagnostic loop through the suspect flange and painted the collar with white frost compound. As pressure rose, a narrow wet line opened along the lower seam. It vanished when the pipe warmed.

Senn crouched beside the collar. “How much per cycle?”

“Too little to trigger inventory variance,” Dael said. “Enough to empty the local reserve after repeated hard recoveries.”

“Central history shows reserve within target.”

“Because crews refill from domestic treatment stock and list it as line flushing.”

Hesh had joined them for the test. “Another false cause.”

Mara wiped the seam. “Another way not to lose a tower while procurement considers a flange.”

Dael asked for the refill slips. Each transfer from domestic treatment reduced contingency water for the shared wall. Mechanical degradation did not remain mechanical in a settlement built around tower return. A leaking flange borrowed from household resilience, then central summaries called both systems adequate.

Eyr sent a live vane diagnostic when its keepers received Senn's sealed inquiry. The actuator on tower 11 took six seconds longer to cross the final angle than its controller reported. Dael recognized the false completion signal: current dropped when the drive encountered resistance, and old logic interpreted the drop as arrival. A ceramic vane could therefore stop short while the schedule believed it had reached position.

Nacre Fold answered with intake photographs. Shell-lime coated the screen supports beyond reach of ordinary cleaning rods. The local crew had improvised brushes from greenhouse fiber, but each pass loosened material that lodged deeper in the radiator channel. Full cleaning required a shutdown and access cage.

Senn aligned all four repair needs against the coming schedule. “Each request failed for a different reason.”

“Lift certification, low inventory loss, controller reporting, shutdown priority,” Dael said.

“What joins them?”

“The category. Central planning expects equipment sites to spend no domestic reserve, train no local crews outside Conservatory licensing, and need no service window beyond predicted machinery tolerance.”

Hesh added, “It expects us to maintain the towers without consuming a season.”

Dael sent urgent work notices before the next load step could begin. He attached oil flecks, flange images, live vane lag, and Nacre Fold's blocked intake. For once, no false cause protected the request. The notices named household consequences beside equipment risks.

Near midday, tower 15 requested a clinic-window adjustment. The service panel displayed the ordinary sequence: reflected light scheduled in twenty minutes, central export step arriving in twelve, local heat wall projected to fall below clinic reserve for eight minutes. Official control would preserve radiator duty and let the local load dip because equipment occupancy remained within accepted tolerance.

Dael reached for the offset field.

“Wait,” Senn said.

A clinic indicator glowed amber on the local rack. Hesh had not yet arrived. Mara stood by the manual gate but did not open it.

Dael entered a mirror-lattice correction of 0.07 percent, enough to advance the reflected edge and warm the clinic branch before export rose. He labeled it sensor phase compensation. That lie fit within maintenance tolerance and would pass routine review.

Senn watched every entry. “How many times?”

“In this history or my career?”

“Begin with this history.”

He queried his initials. The result filled more lines than the display could show.

“Twelve years,” he said. “Not every correction served clinics. Some held schools, nurseries, or domestic walls during tower work.”

“How many records use false causes?”

“Most. An honest cause would have routed the request through a district authority Lorn Dusk does not possess.”

The reflected-light indicator shifted from amber to white. In the clinic branch, reserve temperature stopped falling.

Senn asked, “Did any offset take a recognized district below floor?”

“No.”

“Did you draw only from tolerance?”

“Usually.”

“Usually does not close an audit.”

“Three times I delayed Orison greenhouse edge light by under four minutes. Once I increased tower wear by holding export at an inefficient angle. Twice I used an unassigned maintenance margin that policy would have returned to central crop continuity.”

“Any storm risk?”

“Measured increase below reporting threshold.”

“Not zero.”

“Correct.”

He waited for accusation. Senn asked for the raw entries instead.

Dael opened each flagged correction and named its purpose from his private maintenance marks. A short diagonal meant clinic. Two dots meant school. A hooked line meant a heat wall at pipe-risk temperature. The marks did not appear in central records. He had built a second language inside approved notation, as local ledgers had built civic truth inside equipment references.

Hesh came in during the review and saw the marks. “You never gave us this index.”

“It could identify the adjustments.”

“It could let us verify whom you chose.”

“I chose the lowest temperature against the next available window.”

“You chose.”

Dael felt the room contract around the verb. “Yes.”

“Did Nacre Fold know when you preferred an Eyr clinic?”

“No.”

“Did Lorn Dusk know when you spent tower wear on Orison delay?”

“No.”

Secret protection had spared people. It had also reserved authority to one engineer who could leave whenever his license demanded it. Dael had called that caution because the alternative looked like exposure. Hesh made it look like government without witnesses.

Senn turned the slate toward them. “I will include the falsifications in the habitation case.”

Dael expected fear and found relief mixed through it. “My license number?”

“Yes.”

Hesh asked, “And their purpose?”

“Each entry will name the protected load, the available alternatives, and the harm avoided where the records support it. I will not classify concealment as measurement fraud. The measurements remain accurate. The stated maintenance causes do not.”

“That distinction may not save him,” Hesh said.

“It should not disappear to save me,” Dael replied.

Senn drafted the annex at the workbench. She described his unauthorized offsets, false cause labels, use of tolerance, and failure to obtain local consent. Then she recorded the clinics kept above reserve, school couplings protected, and pipe freezes prevented. Neither column cancelled the other.

Dael signed beneath the statement. Hesh added a witness line noting that local councils had not authorized the hidden allocation choices. Mara authenticated the tower traces.

The filing seal accepted all three signatures. Its receipt included the urgent repair notices, linking concealed heat choices to machinery already spending its remaining margin.

Behind them, the clinic window arrived seven minutes early under Dael's false label. Its light moved across the service-room pipes while Senn attached the truth about it to her case.

Chapter 9 — The Fifth Renewal

Pera found the missing signature sheet inside a fungus quarantine order.

Her message reached Senn before the return tram cleared Lorn Dusk's basin. It contained no greeting, only an archive location and a warning not to request the file digitally. Senn read it twice while tower light receded behind the tram. Dael slept opposite her with one hand wrapped around his tool case.

At the Conservatory, dawn had begun over Orison. Bloom mirrors warmed the pressureworks windows until frost withdrew from the red glass. Senn left her travel case with security, carried the sealed ledger copies under her coat, and descended to the crop-emergency annex.

Pera waited between compact shelves wearing dust sleeves over her uniform. Blue ink marked one cheek. Three archive boxes occupied the floor around her, each labeled FUNGAL CONTAINMENT / SEED TRANSFER.

“You said quarantine order,” Senn whispered.

“I said location.” Pera checked the aisle behind Senn. “The order itself concerns blight barriers. Its attachment bundle concerns emergency bloom authority, which concerns radiator capacity, which apparently concerns where senior officials hide pages they do not want renewal clerks to inherit.”

“Who filed it?”

“A crop secretary seventeen years ago. She indexed by the annex's first instrument, not by every paper inside. Either she was careless in a useful direction or careful in one.”

Pera knelt beside the middle box. Its original string had been replaced by a modern archive band. She held out a custody form.

Senn signed before touching anything. “Show me the sequence.”

“The Order 9 renewal file ends with an attachment stub. CROP-EMERGENCY CAPACITY OPINION, seven pages. The central index preserves pages one through five. Page six appears in the crop annex. Page seven contains the signature field.”

“Authentication?”

“Paper stock matches emergency executive forms from the year. Seal fibers continue across the fold. Blue thermal ink aging fits within two months. I have not tested pressure impression because that would require opening a conservation request.”

“Do that now.”

“It alerts the First Curator.”

“So will my custody entry.”

Pera's mouth tightened. “I had hoped to enjoy six more minutes of being merely nervous.”

She lifted a packet from beneath the quarantine order. Brown spotting marked its edges, residue from the crop fungus samples once stored nearby. Pera had enclosed the pages in clear preservation leaves. Senn read the capacity opinion from the beginning.

The crop emergency had not been rhetorical. A fungal strain had crossed three staple reserves in one rain turn. Infected grain could not be recovered. Nursery stock required maximum clean light to reach transplant size before stored food fell below the public ration floor. The Conservatory modeled alternatives: shade reductions, hospital garden conversion, emergency imports from outer ribbon towns, delayed seed starts, increased radiator export.

Every model lost food.

The selected plan lost least. It extended central bloom, drove darkward heat export near tower tolerance, and preserved the thermal service precinct category so household floors would not constrain the emergency schedule.

Page four listed settlement records received from Lorn Dusk, Eyr, and Nacre Fold. Address books. Schools. Clinic reserves. Burial coordinates. Page five summarized them under one sentence:

Service records now demonstrate permanent households and civic functions inconsistent with temporary construction occupancy.

Senn looked at Pera. “The renewal abstract kept that sentence.”

“Without the attachments.”

“The fifth renewal proceeded anyway.”

Pera turned page six.

A handwritten note crossed the printed recommendation. The script moved in small, controlled angles, blue faded toward brown where fingers had held the sheet.

Recognizing habitation now would compel immediate floor allocation during a crop emergency. Current models cannot meet both obligations without ration failure. Renew for the emergency term. Open a full status review when reserve recovers.

Below the note, page seven carried a seal and signature.

Ceryn Ahl.

The name produced no shock. Its pressure grooves only gave a hand to the decision already outlined by every restriction, inherited phrase, and missing attachment. Blue ink sat deeper where the signature had driven it into the paper grain.

Pera said, “Pressure test?”

“Yes.”

They carried the preservation leaf to the archive bench. Pera placed polarized film above the signature and lowered a cold lamp. Pressure grooves appeared across paper fibers. The pen had paused after Ceryn's family name, then pressed harder into the authorization line.

Pera compared the grooves against an authenticated emergency directive from the same month. Stroke angle, letter joins, pressure cadence, and seal placement aligned.

“She signed it,” Pera said.

“Write the finding as authentication, not accusation.”

“I know my work.”

“I know.” Senn softened her voice. “I am reminding myself.”

They photographed the sheet under dual custody. Pera sealed the image to the origin trace and cross-indexed the physical page under Order 9, crop emergency, habitation records, and First Curator authority. Each new index made future disappearance harder.

Senn asked for the attachment stub from the original renewal file. Pera produced a paper impression rather than the bound volume, preserving the archival rule against moving two linked originals into one room. The stub carried seven tiny punctures where a brass tie had once held the packet. Page seven in the crop annex carried matching holes, including one offset near the lower edge.

“Somebody separated it after signature,” Senn said.

“Before the thermal ink aged fully,” Pera replied. “See the brown transfer around the top hole? The pages rested together long enough for ink residue to mark the stub.”

“Can you date the separation?”

“Within a season, perhaps. Not closely enough to name a person.”

They examined routing stamps. The packet had moved from crop authority to the First Curator's office, then to emergency implementation. A final stamp routed the technical opinion to policy archives. Beside it, in a different hand, someone sent the quarantine order and its attachments to crop containment storage.

“Deliberate concealment?” Senn asked.

“Possible. Also possible that a clerk saw fungus documents and filed by contamination protocol. I will not turn untidy custody into intent.”

“Good.”

Pera frowned. “You could sound less pleased when I resist your theory.”

“I am pleased by resistance that improves evidence.”

“That must make you delightful at meals.”

Senn requested the ration implementation records cited in the capacity opinion. Pera retrieved microfilm from a side drawer and fed it through a hand viewer. The emergency became physical in columns no model summary could soften. Grain stores declined each day. Nursery trays failed fungal screening and went to incineration. Municipal kitchens substituted root paste for two meals, then reduced portions again when a storage vault developed mold.

One district requested permission to mill seed reserve into food. The Conservatory denied it because eating the seed would preserve people for a month and destroy the next planting. Another order redirected hospital greenhouse light to clean staple shoots. Clinic administrators objected, then signed after receiving projections that showed ration collapse reaching their wards.

“Ceryn approved all of these,” Pera said.

Senn followed the seal marks. “She also rejected a proposal to cut outer-town rain below crop floor.”

“That would help her defense.”

“It belongs in the evidence for that reason.”

A page of nursery temperatures showed how close the plan had come to failure. Three nights of inadequate light left seedlings below transplant threshold. Extra bloom on the fourth morning recovered enough growth to continue. The corresponding export authorization cited the thermal service precinct tolerance.

Senn placed the two records side by side: green shoots saved in Orison, households denied a floor darkward. Neither page invalidated the other. Together they documented a choice made under scarcity, then carried far past its stated end.

Pera found the reserve-recovery notice six folders later. It declared the crop emergency closed and released executive controls. No habitation review order accompanied it.

“Was the promised review ever opened under another case number?” Senn asked.

“I searched status, precinct, radiator, occupancy, and emergency succession. Nothing.”

“Search civic heat and address authority.”

Pera did. The index returned local submissions routed to equipment records, the same fragments Senn had seen in the historical chain. It returned no review.

“Then the omission has edges,” Senn said. “A trigger date, an assigned authority, and no action.”

“An omission with excellent filing habits.”

The conservation notice chimed before Pera finished.

FIRST CURATOR ACCESS REQUEST / ANNEX AISLE C.

Pera looked at Senn. “That took four minutes.”

“You were promised six.”

“Institutions continue disappointing me in measurable units.”

Ceryn entered without an escort. She wore no ceremonial coat, only a gray work jacket with the Conservatory seal at the throat. Her hair remained pinned from the overnight schedule session. Fatigue showed around her eyes but did not loosen her posture.

“Has the page been authenticated?” she asked.

Pera answered, “Preliminary pressure, ink, paper, and seal comparison all support authenticity. Full archival report pending.”

“Do not call it preliminary once you have four independent matches.”

Pera glanced at Senn, then corrected the form. “Authenticated.”

Ceryn read the custody sequence. She did not ask to remove the page or challenge the search authority. “Who else has access?”

“Origin-trace principals,” Senn said. “Pera, me, policy archive supervision upon seal, and any habitation proceeding authorized from the challenge.”

“Not Dael?”

“Not until his role requires it.”

“Not Hesh Dara?”

“They receive evidentiary notice because the page references their records. The image remains protected until a proceeding sets access.”

Ceryn rested two fingers on the bench, outside the preservation boundary. “Correct.”

The word angered Senn more than obstruction might have. “You knew.”

“Yes.”

“Permanent households. Schools. Burials.”

“Yes.”

“Then you signed the fifth renewal under a category you knew had become false.”

Ceryn looked toward Pera. “Complete the index. Auditor Vale and I will use the old pump gallery.”

Pera held her ground. “Do you want me absent as archivist or as witness?”

“Neither. I want the page sealed before conversation produces claims about its handling.”

That answer also proved correct. Pera finished the cross-index, locked the sheet into a fire drawer, and gave each woman a custody receipt. Only then did Ceryn lead Senn upstairs.

The old pump gallery overlooked Orison's lower canals. Decommissioned pistons stood between windows, their black iron polished by generations of maintenance hands. Morning light reached the apricot terraces. Hive boxes opened in sequence. From here, spring showed none of its darkward accounting.

Ceryn stopped beside a pressure wheel taller than Senn. “Ask directly.”

“Why did you renew?”

“Because the fungal failure had destroyed two staple strains and contaminated a third. We had seed for one clean planting. If the nursery cycle failed, the next ration schedule fell below survival reserve across the central ribbon.”

“You had other models.”

“Each killed more people.”

“Show me.”

Ceryn went to a dormant service desk and entered an executive archive key. She did not hesitate over the access warning. Crop-emergency simulations opened across the old slate.

One model recognized the service precincts immediately and granted every household a climate floor. Radiator export fell. Central greenhouse temperatures dropped. Seed starts failed across three staple nurseries. Another preserved seed light by cutting hospital gardens and school meals. A third transferred the burden to outer agricultural towns already near rain minimum. None maintained every protected floor because the emergency stock, shade capacity, and radiator performance could not support them simultaneously.

“What did your selected plan prevent?” Senn asked.

“Projected ration collapse in eleven cities. The uncertainty band ran wide. At its median, several hundred thousand people lost staple minimum for at least one winter turn.”

“Projected deaths?”

“I will not give you a clean number from a model that depended on household compliance, fungal spread, and reserve spoilage. We expected mass mortality under the worst branch. We expected severe hunger under the best.”

Ceryn opened ration records. Portions narrowed after the plan began but stayed above survival reserve. Seedling mortality fell. The next crop reached transplant readiness with three days of stock remaining.

“I prevented a famine,” she said. “Not alone. Growers burned infected fields. Clinic gardens surrendered light. Tower crews carried export above preferred load. The plan prevented it.”

“And Lorn Dusk paid.”

“Yes.”

“Without standing.”

“Yes.”

Ceryn never lied about a number once asked. Senn had respected that discipline when she mistook measurement honesty for governing honesty.

“Your note promises a full review when reserve recovers.”

“It should have opened.”

“Who stopped it?”

“I did not assign it.”

“That is not an answer about a machine.”

“No.” Ceryn closed the ration display. “The emergency ended unevenly. Reserves recovered, then a mirror failure reduced bloom capacity. Tower 16 required a rebuild. Two cities had built hospital schedules around the extension. Every turn presented a reason to delay a correction that remained physically dangerous.”

“How long before delay became policy?”

“Immediately, though I did not call it that.”

Outside, canal chimes announced the nursery shift. Workers moved shutters from the lower greenhouse roofs.

Senn asked, “Did you read later household submissions?”

“Summaries.”

“Did they alter your understanding?”

“They increased the scale of the obligation, not its nature.”

“Why preserve the false category rather than create standing with staged implementation?”

Ceryn's gaze followed the mirror line down the canal. “Because the Charter did not clearly separate recognition from immediate floor allocation under an active schedule. Legal counsel predicted injunctions. Central cities would have fought any weather loss. The Conservatory could enforce a deferred review; it could not guarantee an orderly constitutional case while food systems depended on the result.”

“So you denied the districts the standing needed to argue for one.”

“I held a system inside the only authority available to me.”

The sentence came close to turning power into necessity. Senn opened the Lorn Dusk telemetry on her slate. “This system measured infants as new equipment loads. It classified clinic recovery as enclosure stabilization. It used domestic treatment water to conceal a tower leak. Did you know that level of consequence?”

“No.”

“Would it have changed your emergency decision?”

Ceryn took time. “During the first crop turn, perhaps not.”

“And after?”

“Yes.”

Senn believed her. Belief did not repair the years between.

A service clerk arrived at the gallery door with two printed schedules. Ceryn signed one and sent the other back for revised wind limits. Even inside confession, weather continued demanding administration.

When they stood alone again, Ceryn said, “Your habitation challenge will trigger a classification conflict. Rul Fen already represents municipal petitioners who claim immediate standing would invalidate active crop allocations. If the matter opens publicly before correction models exist, every district will defend its own floor through emergency filings.”

“Then build correction models.”

“We are. Quietly.”

“With whose participation?”

“Conservatory engineering, Charter counsel, selected municipal load officers.”

“None from Lorn Dusk, Eyr, or Nacre Fold.”

“Not while their legal status remains contested.”

“Which your renewal ensured.”

Ceryn's face tightened. “Yes.”

The admission left no useful target for Senn's anger. She folded the custody receipt and placed it in her notebook.

Ceryn moved to the next piston. “I can order a staged review. First, authenticate the records internally. Second, model recognition under controlled assumptions. Third, invite local custodians as technical witnesses. If the result supports standing, we bring a complete remedy to the delegates rather than a crisis.”

“How long?”

“Six months.”

“Winter continues under the inherited debit?”

“Reduced where safe.”

“Who decides safe?”

“The Conservatory.”

“Who decides which local custodians qualify?”

“Archive and policy offices.”

“Who has standing during the review?”

Ceryn did not answer at once. “Formally, no district until determination.”

Senn looked through the red glass. Orison's bloom warmed canal stones while workers opened school roofs to the extended morning. Those uses mattered. Hospital gardens mattered. Crop continuity mattered. Ceryn's offer protected all of them from a disorderly fight by asking the harmed settlements to remain evidence instead of participants.

“Hesh permitted three ledger pages to leave Lorn Dusk,” Senn said. “They required names to remain attached because your summaries separated every civic fact until no people survived the filing.”

“I read the challenge.”

“You read an audit abstraction. They should decide how their records enter remedy.”

“A public case could produce an immediate halt.”

“Then we show why an immediate halt creates danger. We do not prevent them from arguing because their argument may prevail.”

Ceryn's voice stayed controlled. “Auditor Vale, authority carries responsibility for outcomes, not only procedure.”

“So does exclusion.”

“I have described the outcome I prevented.”

“And the category continued after that outcome ended.”

A mirror adjustment moved copper light across the gallery. It passed over Ceryn's hands, revealing ink in the creases of one thumb.

Senn took out her slate and opened the habitation challenge. The signature-sheet authentication field waited for an attachment. Adding it would elevate the case beyond a technical classification dispute. It would state that a named First Curator had knowingly preserved a false category during emergency governance.

Ceryn watched without reaching for the device. “If you file now, I cannot contain the proceeding.”

“That should not depend on your containment.”

“I can keep municipal counsel from seeking immediate injunctive relief during a staged review.”

“By keeping the districts outside it.”

“By arriving with a survivable proposal.”

Senn attached the authenticated page. “Standing cannot wait until everyone with standing has agreed the answer.”

She marked the crop models protected but discoverable, preventing their use as public spectacle while preserving them for the case. She included Ceryn's admission that the emergency decision had knowingly deferred habitation recognition. She did not call the famine invented, the model fraudulent, or the prevention meaningless.

The filing seal asked whether the First Curator offered administrative resolution.

Senn selected YES.

It asked whether she accepted.

She selected NO and entered the reason: AFFECTED DISTRICTS REMAIN WITHOUT STANDING IN THE PROPOSED REVIEW.

Ceryn read the line. “You may force a hearing before you have a remedy.”

“Then we will arrive without pretending proof and remedy are the same work.”

Senn pressed her thumb to the seal.

The fifth renewal joined the habitation challenge. Below them, Orison's nursery chimes continued through a spring that Ceryn had once saved. Neither woman looked away from the measured light.

Chapter 10 — The Thermodynamics of Refusal

Senn pulled the winter debit out of the lattice with two fingers and watched Orison turn white.

The simulation translated heat into color. Green held within scheduled range, amber marked stress, white meant the model had exceeded a material or civic limit. The first pale seam appeared beneath the central canal. It widened through the greenhouse terraces before Senn had moved her hand away from the control glass.

“Reset it,” she said.

Dael remained beside the instrument rack. “You asked for cancellation.”

“I asked you to remove an unlawful burden. I did not ask the model to invent heat.”

“It did not.” He tapped the narrow blue bars at the darkward edge. “You stopped exporting it.”

The lattice room occupied a windowless vault below the Conservatory, built when weather calculations still required coolant pipes thicker than a person's waist. Modern slates lined the old pipe channels. Their combined display curved around a waist-high map of the terminator ribbon: six hundred twenty kilometers of farms, cities, radiator fields, canals, and wind corridors reduced to light under glass. Condensation gathered on the dormant pipes overhead and fell at measured intervals into floor drains.

Hesh stood on the far side of the map with both hands resting on their ledger case. They had refused the chair offered by policy staff. They had also refused to let the simulation call Lorn Dusk a service precinct. Pera had entered the population, domestic heat, clinic reserves, school cycles, births, and burials as witnessed provisional data. For the first time, the lattice displayed a narrow inhabited band around towers 14 and 15.

Senn enlarged the white seam. “Which limit?”

“Canal stone storage first,” Dael said. “Then hospital reservoirs. Greenhouse skins begin venting on day four.”

“Those systems receive eleven days they should never have had.”

“Material does not care how its schedule became unlawful.”

Hesh opened the ledger case but did not remove a book. “Show us the days.”

Dael sent the simulation back to its starting state. Orison returned to green. Lorn Dusk glowed dull blue, its color clipped at the bottom of the public legend until Pera's provisional data forced the scale lower. Four radiator towers carried amber points. Thin red threads marked known maintenance restrictions.

He entered Senn's instruction again: cancel all inherited winter transfers at the next schedule boundary, restore every darkward district to its climate floor, preserve current central light allocations until their authorized end. The lattice advanced one day at a time.

On the second day, radiator demand dropped. Heat remained in the atmospheric exchange instead of leaving through the darkward towers. The central ribbon absorbed part of it. Wind carried the rest starward, where shade schedules had already been committed to crop corridors.

By day four, Orison's greenhouse roofs vented in staggered pulses. Their warm air entered the canal corridor and climbed. The lattice drew a white hook above the eastern terraces.

“Why does bloom continue?” Hesh asked.

“Because those orders have separate authority,” Senn said. “They are not written as benefits from the debit.”

“Then your cancellation cancels our cold but protects their flowers.”

“For the remainder of the issued order.”

Hesh looked at her across the map. “Law seems to have excellent brakes when comfort might strike a wall.”

Senn accepted the hit. “I can freeze inheritance. I cannot make an issued mirror angle disappear without a safety finding.”

“Then find one honestly.”

Dael advanced the model. On day six, the hook bent over the city. Air moving darkward met a denser return current above the radiator road. The interface tightened into a narrow yellow plane.

“Here,” he said. “The morning pressure releases have nowhere broad to spread. The east array keeps warming the lower canal. Darkward export has fallen faster than central storage.”

Senn rotated the wind field. The yellow plane looked almost delicate, a translucent fold above the model roofs. “Probability?”

“Still below intervention threshold.”

“Do not protect me with a threshold. Give me the distribution.”

Dael opened the uncertainty bands. Twelve plausible runs fanned around the central line. In five, the fold loosened over uncultivated stone. Four struck the upper greenhouse tiers with damaging gusts. Two drove hail through the hospital reservoir roofs. The final run carried the front south and tore three kite tethers above a school district.

Hesh read each outcome without comment. Their thumb moved along the worn edge of the ledger case.

Day seven brought amber across the hospital storage field. Day eight whitened two school roofs and pushed the wind fold down toward inhabited streets. After nine days, the model labeled the formation a shear storm.

Senn walked around the table until she stood beside Dael. “You tuned this against the cancellation.”

“No.”

“You selected a central demand curve that assumes every discretionary bloom order survives.”

“I selected the orders already issued.”

“Policy could revoke them.”

“Policy can revoke a sentence. Crews still have to turn two hundred forty orbital surfaces without crossing adjacent light corridors. Canal shutters need a cooling sequence. Seed rooms cannot shed stored heat into occupied wards. Kite pilots need acceptable air.”

She pointed to Orison's white core. “How much of this comes from the hidden eleven days?”

“Most of the excess over the turn. Not most of the heat already in stone.”

“Then begin their reduction now.”

“With which mirror?”

The question came flat and practical. Dael opened the Choir schedule. Lines filled the wall from floor to ceiling, each showing an angle, transfer interval, ground track, and fallback position. Mirror 117 carried his small clinic offset in faded amber. Forty-three surfaces contributed directly or indirectly to Orison's current bloom. Moving one changed glare along tram routes or stole a planned window from another district. Moving several together altered the upper-air heating pattern faster than shade kites could answer.

Senn chose a sequence that reduced the eastern terraces first. The simulation accepted her hand entries, then marked three crop corridors below germination floor.

“Those are surplus beds,” she said.

“Two are,” Dael replied. “The third supplies hospital meal reserves.”

She shifted the cut south. A school-heating warning appeared.

“Buildings can close for three days.”

“Children go somewhere when buildings close.”

Hesh's mouth tightened. “That argument has been used against us for twenty-two years.”

Dael looked away from the display. “I know.”

“Do you?” Hesh asked. “You show a school warning here and every hand stops. Our school loses a reflected morning, so the ledger calls the heaters equipment demand.”

“I falsified windows to keep your clinic warm.”

“You kept some people alive without giving us authority to refuse the next loss. I am grateful for the heat. I am not grateful for the silence attached to it.”

The pipes clicked overhead as coolant changed pressure in another vault. Dael rubbed the scarred fingers of his left hand against his coat seam. Senn had seen him use those fingers against hot fabric, testing tension through pain he no longer fully registered. Now he kept them closed.

“We are not measuring gratitude,” Senn said. “Run a simultaneous reduction. Darkward restoration paired with central bloom cancellation at the same thermal rate.”

Dael obeyed. The model dimmed Orison as Lorn Dusk warmed. For half a day, the transfer appeared balanced. Then the atmosphere distinguished light from radiator export. Reduced mirrors cooled surfaces from above; reduced tower duty retained heat in moving air. The two changes met at different heights and times. A shear line formed farther west, weaker at first, then sharpened over the canal road.

Senn slowed the run to hourly steps. “What keeps it from coupling?”

“Shade between the heated ground and the return layer. Distributed, not one large block.”

“Use the reserve fleet.”

“Committed.”

“All thirty-six?”

“Thirty maintain active schedules. Five cover mechanical or storm contingency. One is under recertification.”

“Reserve six,” she said.

Dael gave her a look that belonged more properly to Tovin. “You have read the policy capacity. You have not held the fabric.”

“The reserve exists for instability.”

“It exists on paper at full area. Its last edge test showed unequal draw.”

“Unsafe?”

“Unknown until Tovin completes the frame run.”

Hesh pulled a chair closer and sat at last. “Your system has a hidden city, a false legal category, four failing towers, and a reserve nobody has tested. Which part did you intend to tell us before asking for another winter?”

No one answered quickly enough.

Senn cleared the model and entered only the Charter constraints. Every inhabited district would receive its climate floor. Every issued share above that floor could be altered. No location could carry an unnamed debit. The lattice rejected the run before weather calculation began.

MISSING ALLOCATION AUTHORITY, the wall reported.

“That is the point,” she said.

Dael read the rejection. “The machine cannot distribute a burden without a rule.”

“It distributed one for decades.”

“It had a rule. A wrong one.”

“Then equalize it.”

“By population? Area? Existing use? Available radiator distance?”

Hesh leaned forward. “Not population.”

Senn turned. “I have not proposed a final basis.”

“You entered equality as if a person could tell us what the unit means later. Large districts with little surplus would pay more because they contain more bodies. That gives poverty an arithmetic penalty.”

“Equal thermal contribution, then.”

“From what?”

The question stopped her. A thermal unit taken from a greenhouse above survival heat did not equal one taken from a decorative canal. A cold hour imposed on a clinic did not match a cold hour in an empty tram hall, though both balanced the same account. The ledger could convert each into energy. It could not select which loss remained tolerable.

Dael rebuilt the starting state and highlighted every district's declared climate floor. Broad green bands appeared across the ribbon. Lorn Dusk's provisional band came up hard against measured demand, leaving almost no room between life and scheduled weather. Orison displayed a thick layer above floor.

“Can you give them that now?” Senn asked.

“Not with a switch.”

“That answer preserves the present harm.”

“No. It describes the time constant.”

“People freeze during time constants.”

“People die in shear storms.”

Their voices stayed low, which made the quarrel carry farther in the sealed room. Hesh watched each of them as if checking two instruments that disagreed.

Senn opened the emergency authority panel. “I can issue a corrective freeze on every bloom extension derived from the debit.”

Dael placed his palm over the seal field before she could touch it. “Then maintenance inherits an impossible order. Crews either violate your freeze or watch the storm form.”

“Move your hand.”

“Name the safe first angle.”

“I am not the lattice engineer.”

“Yet you are prepared to command the lattice.”

Hesh struck the ledger case once against the table. The small sound cut through them.

“Enough,” they said. “Dael, take your hand away. Senn, close the authority panel.”

Neither moved.

Hesh rose. “I came because you told us the central city could not claim ignorance once the records opened. I did not come to watch one of you make a threat and the other make physics into a veto.”

Dael withdrew his hand first. Senn closed the panel.

“Lorn Dusk wants the debit cancelled,” Hesh continued. “We have wanted it cancelled since before anyone let us know its administrative name. We will not consent to another turn of imposed cold. We will also not call a storm over children justice because an auditor needs clean hands.”

Senn felt the words settle with more weight than Ceryn's defense had carried. Ceryn had offered staged review while retaining control. Hesh demanded an end to the wrong and denied Senn the innocence of haste.

“What do you consent to?” she asked.

“Recognition now. Authority to see and challenge every schedule touching our floor. A correction we help design. No new debit inherited while people debate.”

“The existing schedule still runs.”

“Then put our name on every hour it takes.”

Dael zoomed out from Orison. Beyond the city, weather shares intersected across the ribbon: rain held for Caldrift, orchard warmth in Nacre districts unrelated to Nacre Fold, industrial shade along western reservoirs, unused preferences, emergency margins, ceremonial dawns, transit glare limits. The hidden transfer had survived partly because policy treated the correction as a choice between Orison and three gray sectors. The larger system contained more levers, though none came free.

“We need room below every declared surplus,” he said. “Not merely Orison's.”

Hesh shook their head. “Declared by whom?”

“Each district.”

“Then wealthy districts will rename comfort as essential before the request arrives.”

Senn studied the bands. “We audit the floor, not the preference. Continuous heat, medical reserve, food germination, schools, domestic survival. Everything above needs its authority shown.”

“Who decides what a school needs?” Hesh asked.

“The district supplies evidence.”

“To an office that called ours equipment.”

Senn removed the Conservatory's default floor values. Half the green bands disappeared, leaving raw measurements and local declarations side by side. “Then the office does not decide alone.”

Dael began a new run without being asked. He restored Lorn Dusk only by a small increment for the first day, paired it with several minor shade changes across unoccupied industrial surfaces, and reduced Orison's decorative canal light after the public bloom interval. The model advanced. No white seam appeared. On day three, however, a western reservoir lost enough evaporation to alter a rain order. He stopped.

“Failure?” Senn asked.

“Consequence.”

“Can it move?”

“If the reservoir district accepts later rain.”

“Would its climate floor hold?”

“Yes.”

Hesh came around the table. “Show the people who would be asked.”

Dael opened the district layer. A municipal council, water cooperative, and two farming associations governed the preference. None had participated in the hidden transfer. All would see less predictable water if they agreed.

“That resembles consent,” Hesh said. “Do not mistake resemblance for completion.”

They worked through the meal chime. Pera sent up paper packets from the archive: Charter floor definitions, dormant challenge procedures, district contact authorities, and the fifth renewal sheet whose signature had changed the nature of Senn's case. No single procedure allowed an unrecognized settlement to negotiate planetary weather. A habitation challenge could establish standing, but policy classification delayed its hearing. An emergency safety order could change mirror schedules, but only to prevent quantified physical danger. Audit protection could release evidence, not allocate seasons.

Senn arranged the papers around the glowing map. “We have treated standing as the reward after a remedy.”

Hesh traced the edge of the habitation form. “Standing must come before you decide which remedy counts.”

“Provisional recognition could invalidate the debit without commanding an immediate physical reversal.”

Dael considered it. “The lattice would still need an interim rule.”

“No inherited increase. Every present burden visible by district and hour. Restoration paced at the fastest rate that stays below safety thresholds. Any delay requires a named finding.”

“Signed by whom?”

“Conservatory engineering, audit, and an affected district representative.”

Hesh's expression did not soften. “One district representative from each side of the transfer.”

“Agreed.”

“Local records define our floor until a hearing reviews them openly.”

Dael enlarged the wind field again. “If a local declaration exceeds physical capacity?”

“Then you show the constraint,” Hesh said. “You do not erase the residents.”

The last simulation began with no dramatic switch. Lorn Dusk's band rose by measured steps. Orison surrendered canal warmth, an unused commercial light rehearsal, and part of a greenhouse overrun. Two other districts contributed small shade margins subject to consent. The model reached day six without whitening. On day seven, a shear warning appeared, broad rather than sharp. Dael changed no values; he marked the place where additional agreements or reserve capacity would be needed.

By nine days, the central ribbon remained below material limits. Lorn Dusk had not yet recovered its whole climate floor. The result satisfied nobody, which made it more credible than the clean alternatives.

Dael printed the thermal cross-section on translucent film. Heat layers showed as brown contours, their handled edges already fading where his thumb pressed the sheet. He laid it over the district schedule and punched registration holes through both pages. The misalignment became physical: restoration rose at the darkward ground while retained atmospheric heat traveled hundreds of meters above it.

“Take these apart in a hearing and someone will claim the schedules balance,” he said.

“Then fasten them,” Senn replied.

He threaded copper wire through the holes. Hesh added a third sheet listing the promised windows Lorn Dusk had lost during the active order. Instead of aligning neatly, the dates crossed Dael's contours at irregular intervals.

“Now nobody gets to present air without people,” they said.

Senn checked the model's source bundle. It still called Orison's canal light an urban continuity requirement. She opened the designation and attached photographs from the bloom terraces: apricot walls, public tram displays, the decorative western reach where no hospital reservoir or seed bed depended on the warmth. The essential portions remained protected. The rest acquired an evidence flag.

A facilities clerk knocked and offered to clear the room for the night shift. Senn requested two more hours under active audit authority. Hesh requested food. Dael requested a replacement coolant cup for the rack, because argument had not exempted the processors from heat. Each demand entered a different office, and each had to be named before anyone acted.

Senn saved the run under no remedy name. Naming it too early would let policy defend the label instead of the work.

“What have we agreed?” Dael asked.

Hesh closed their ledger case. “Not enough.”

“That is accurate but difficult to enter.”

“Enter this: Lorn Dusk refuses continued unrepresented harm. We also refuse an unrepresented correction imposed on somebody else.”

Senn wrote the sentence into the simulation record. Beneath it she added the procedural needs: provisional habitation standing, shared access to floor evidence, visible interim burdens, distributed options, engineering findings attached to any delay.

Dael read over her shoulder. “You have not cancelled the debit.”

“No.” The admission hurt more than the earlier argument. “I have stopped pretending cancellation is one act.”

Hesh took the stylus from her and placed a line before every proposed technical step. “Standing first,” they said.

The three of them looked at the altered sequence. No switch led it. A name did.

Chapter 11 — The Names in the Heat Wall

Hesh lifted the first heat brick before Senn had removed her coat.

Warmth left a dark rectangle on the plaster. Beneath it, someone had written a name in charcoal, then covered the letters when the brick went back into its wall cradle. Hesh carried the dense ceramic block to a handcart and exposed another name.

“Records room is behind the wall,” they said. “Help if you want to enter.”

Senn set her document case beside the stove. The address hall had emptied after the late meal, though voices traveled from the workshop corridor and radiator vibration trembled through the floor. Heat bricks lined the inner wall in stacked iron cradles. During a reflected-light window, surplus warmth charged their ceramic cores. Residents moved them to bedrooms, the school, or the clinic as need changed. Tonight the bricks had to come out before the records door could open.

She gripped one through folded canvas. Its weight drew at her shoulders. “Why build access behind working storage?”

“Because the records need steady temperature,” Hesh replied. “People need the bricks. Space serves twice here.”

The charcoal names emerged one by one: PEL ARU. SIVA NEM. OLAN TIR. Each occupied the plaster directly behind a brick cradle. Some letters had blurred from decades of heat and handling. Others looked recent enough to stain a finger.

Senn placed her brick on the cart. “Deaths?”

“People whose last winter involved this wall.”

“Involved how?”

“That question waits for Nima Sol.”

They cleared three vertical rows. A narrow metal door appeared, its surface patched where repeated heating had warped the hinges. Hesh unlocked it with two keys and made Senn sign a paper entry sheet before crossing the threshold.

Shelves filled the room from floor to pipework. Address ledgers stood upright in oilcloth wrappers. Clinic registers occupied gray boxes. Heating records hung from metal rods by month, their paper strips notched where temperatures crossed warning values. Burial notices, school absence books, radiator incident reports, and requests for reflected dawn had separate shelves. Nothing resembled the compact evidence packet Senn had expected.

“You asked for the full record,” Hesh said.

“I did.”

“This is what full looks like before an archive makes it convenient.”

Nima waited at a folding table near the back. She wore a clinic coat with the cuffs turned twice and had tied her dark hair out of her face using a strip of instrument cloth. Three stacks of files stood before her. A fourth chair held a kettle and cups.

“You brought the auditor,” she said.

“The auditor carried four bricks.”

“Then her causal contribution to the door is established.”

Senn sat opposite her. “You are Nima Sol.”

“Physician, not statistician. That distinction may become important before morning.”

Hesh took the remaining chair. “Begin with Pel.”

Nima drew the first file from the left stack. Pel Aru had repaired condensation traps in tower 15 and lived with his daughter above the tool exchange. During a winter extension twelve years earlier, his district heater allocation fell below the domestic request for six consecutive nights. The local clinic noted numbness in his right foot, then tissue injury. A reflected clinic window arrived two days late. Pel developed fever after debridement and died under antibiotic shortage protocols.

“Could warmer weather have prevented his death?” Senn asked.

“Yes.”

“Can you establish that the transferred winter caused it?”

“No.”

The clean refusal made Senn look up.

Nima folded her hands over the file. “He had vascular disease. He delayed treatment because his shift team lacked a replacement. The clinic's drug order had also been reduced after a transport fault. Cold initiated the injury. Delay worsened it. Supply constrained treatment. If you ask for one cause, the record will not give it.”

“What can it give?”

“An exposure path. Temperature below the requested domestic floor. Duration. Injury consistent with exposure. Treatment conditions altered by withheld light or heating. A comparison with residents of similar age and illness during ordinary schedules.”

Hesh touched the charcoal rubbing attached to Pel's file. “And his name.”

Senn wrote each link on a separate line. “Who certified the temperature instrument?”

“I did not expect that to be your first objection.”

“It is not an objection. If policy can break the path by discrediting one sensor, they will.”

“The heat-wall probe received Conservatory calibration every second turn until nine years ago. After that, Dael arranged comparison checks through tower maintenance. Those lack civic certification.”

“Do you have the comparison sheets?”

Hesh pointed to a rod of folded pages above her head.

Senn fetched them. Dael's initials appeared on the margins, beside two local mechanics and a clinic orderly. The readings tracked a certified tower intake probe within tolerance. Unauthorized evidence, physically sound. She attached the sheets to Pel's chain without pretending the certification problem away.

Siva Nem's file came next. Siva had taught the lower school and used a portable oxygen concentrator after a dust illness. A cancelled dawn window forced the school onto stored power. When domestic heating demand rose that evening, equipment-priority rules protected tower pumps but not household medical devices. Siva's concentrator lost power for forty-three minutes. She recovered, then died six weeks later after repeated respiratory infections.

“Too remote?” Hesh asked.

Nima answered before Senn could. “For individual attribution, probably. For population risk, perhaps not. We retain her because the winter altered the chain. We mark uncertainty rather than erasing her.”

Senn examined the power log. Conservatory telemetry had counted the concentrator draw inside equipment occupancy. The machine existed in the central record. Siva did not.

Olan Tir had fallen from an exterior radiator stair during ice accumulation. The official incident blamed a boot cleat. Local weather records showed that a promised warming interval had been reassigned after the stair crew began work. He died on impact. Nima classified the association as strong but distinguished it from cold-exposure mortality.

“Policy will say you mixed categories,” Senn said.

“I separated them in every table.”

“They will say the headline does not.”

“There is no headline yet.”

Hesh watched Senn sharpen her pencil. “You sound disappointed.”

“I sound cautious.”

“You arrived angry enough to cancel a planetary transfer in one motion. Now the dead require caution.”

Senn set the pencil down. “The dead require proof that survives people who benefit from doubting them.”

“Survives for whom?”

“For a hearing.”

“We survived without one.”

Nima pushed a cup toward each of them. “You can argue over ownership after we keep the records from being dismissed.”

Steam carried the mineral smell of heat-wall water. Senn drank and found it bitter with dried leaf. Her fingers had stiffened after moving the bricks. The room itself stayed cool to protect paper, forcing them to work in coats while warmth waited on the other side of plaster.

They continued through the first shelf. Ressa's grandfather, Mal En, had died after a heater ration forced three households into one room during a respiratory outbreak. A newborn named Iri survived her first cold injury but developed renal complications before her second birthday. Two tower workers suffered coolant burns because an extended radiator run shortened a maintenance cooling interval. One later died from infection. Nima never let Senn merge their paths merely because the winter transfer touched each one.

At midnight, Hesh brought in a tray of grain cakes from the communal stove. Grease marked the file guards despite their care. Senn replaced one sheet and recorded the handling change.

“You make a note when food touches paper,” Hesh said.

“Someone examining the stain later should know it happened tonight.”

“Do you make notes when policy touches people?”

“That is why I am here.”

The answer sounded insufficient even to her.

A narrow blue ledger near the bottom shelf bore the title TENANCY AND HEAT EXCEPTIONS. Senn opened it expecting Lorn Dusk addresses. The first pages listed temporary greenhouse quarters occupied by workers whose names never entered the main address book because their employers claimed rotational housing.

“Tenant growers,” she said.

Hesh leaned over. “You know the form.”

Caldrift used an older version. Eight years earlier, Senn had treated leaseholder records as the legal destination for storm-loss compensation. The field surveys listed families cultivating each parcel, but the payment protocol recognized only title and active lease at disbursement. Twelve households lost their agreements during the delay. Landholders received compensation for crops the departing families had planted.

Nima turned the ledger toward herself. “Your signature?”

“Not here.” Senn kept her hands flat. “In Caldrift.”

“What happened?”

She could have described the appeal. She could have explained that no rule then allowed an auditor to suspend title-based disbursement, that the total crop loss had been measured correctly, that later review changed the forms. Those facts had protected her license. None had returned the farms.

“I approved a reconciliation by parcels,” she said. “The losses belonged to tenant growers. The payment followed the leases after the growers could no longer hold them.”

“How many?” Hesh asked.

“Twelve households lost their land before payment.”

“Did you know their names?”

“Not when I signed.”

“Did the records contain them?”

“Yes.”

Hesh closed the blue ledger. “Then you have seen this trick before.”

“Not with winter.”

“The weather changes. The trick keeps its coat.”

Nima reached into her middle stack and removed a table with no personal identifiers. Age bands occupied one axis. Exposure categories occupied the other. Senn saw why the conversation had turned.

“This summary will travel faster than those shelves,” Nima said. “It must. No delegate will read every clinic note before acting. But once a total leaves this room, people will argue with the total and forget what it summarizes.”

“Then the chain needs an annex.”

“Annexes disappear.”

“Not under protected audit procedure.”

Hesh gave a dry laugh. “A protected procedure issued by the office protecting its own classification.”

Senn drew the blank summary closer. “Attach a reversible index. Every included case gets a local record key. Public material can protect medical details, but authorized review must move from any row back to a person, place, exposure interval, and uncertainty grade.”

“Who holds the key?” Nima asked.

“You do. Hesh holds a witnessed copy. The archive preserves a sealed copy.”

“What happens when Ceryn classifies it?”

“She can restrict medical information. She cannot legally suppress evidence attached to an active habitation challenge if the evidence establishes standing or harm.”

“Can she call it preliminary?”

“Yes.”

“Can she delay it?”

“Yes.”

Hesh's gaze did not leave Senn. “Honest answers improve a poor offer.”

Senn turned the table over. On its blank back she drafted an evidence rule: no aggregate assertion without a countable case register; no case register without names retained by the originating district; uncertainty expressed per causal path; exclusions logged with reasons; additions witnessed; revisions preserved rather than overwritten.

Nima read it twice. “You keep saying count.”

“Eventually someone will demand one.”

“Someone always does. A number looks governable.”

“Then we make the number answerable.”

Hesh stood and took a key from a chain beneath their shirt. “You have seen the selected files. The full death register sits in the second room.”

Senn looked toward the back wall. No door showed between the shelves.

Nima moved two hanging rods aside. A recessed panel carried a keyhole and a strip of dark cloth around its edges. Hesh unlocked it. Cold air moved out, dry and faintly scented with paper preservative.

The second room held no desks. Bound volumes occupied shallow shelves, each spine marked by year. Small cloth packets rested beside some books: a school badge, a cracked heater dial, boot cleats, a child's knitted cuff. The objects did not prove causation. They prevented the records from becoming only clinical abstractions.

Senn stopped before the most recent volume. “How many files have you classified?”

“Enough to know the methods need independent review,” Nima said.

“That was not my question.”

“No aggregate total,” Hesh said. “Not until the complete attribution joins Lorn Dusk, Eyr, and Nacre Fold. If you carry a partial number out, Orison will spend it as if the omissions were certainty.”

Senn nodded. Her impatience did not create entitlement.

They took the current volume to the outer table. Each entry began with an address and name, followed by birth date, occupation, household heat source, incident or illness, medical finding, weather interval, instrument references, and Nima's causal grade. Cross-references led to district heating strips and tower duty logs. Some entries ended EXCLUDE FROM ATTRIBUTION while remaining in the historical record.

“Why retain excluded cases in the working register?” Senn asked.

“To prove we did not select only what favored us.”

A man named Cor Venn had died during an extended winter, but his room stayed above heating floor and his illness showed no cold sensitivity. Excluded. A transport collision occurred on a clear road after a light cancellation. Excluded. A woman missed medicine when a lift battery failed, though district power remained adequate. Uncertain equipment link, excluded pending engineering review.

The exclusions gave the included files harder edges. They also showed the labor behind restraint. Nima had not turned grief into a claim indiscriminately.

A heating strip from the fourteenth year carried a puzzling gap. Temperatures ran in blue ink from midnight to the fifth hour, vanished for ninety minutes, then resumed two degrees higher. The matching clinic register documented three cold injuries during that interval.

“Instrument failure?” Senn asked.

“Wall power failed,” Nima said. “We reconstructed from room thermometers.”

“Where are those readings?”

Hesh brought down a school exercise book. Fourteen households had written times and temperatures across pages meant for geometry. Different hands used different decimal habits. One person rounded to whole degrees. Another recorded the kitchen and sleeping room separately. A third wrote only COLD ENOUGH TO MOVE BABY TO WALL.

Senn arranged the entries by address. “Policy counsel will call this uncalibrated testimony.”

“It is uncalibrated testimony,” Nima replied. “Do not improve its name.”

“We can test it against the recovery curve. Once wall power returned, the certified strip shows how quickly each zone warmed. If the household readings fit the building response, they support the missing interval.”

Hesh found the maintenance diagram. Together they traced shared pipes from the wall exchanger into four residential branches. Senn entered the later temperatures, exterior air, brick distribution, and pipe lengths into a simple decay model. Eleven household readings fell within the broad physical range. Two sat outside it but corresponded to rooms using portable heaters. One lacked a time and could not be placed.

Nima changed no source. She added a note distinguishing contemporary observation from later reconstruction.

“Would you include the injuries?” Hesh asked.

“For exposure association, yes. For any narrower claim, the uncertainty remains high.”

“Three people felt the cold without high uncertainty.”

“The cold is certain,” Senn said. “Its contribution to each later condition needs a grade.”

Hesh copied her wording into the margin, then underlined contribution. “Better than cause.”

Another problem surfaced in the burial register. Two residents shared the name Lina Vey, one born thirty years before the other. A clinic key had attached the younger woman's heating history to the elder woman's respiratory file. Nima swore softly and opened both address books.

Senn followed the error through six references. Correcting it removed one apparent exposure and added a different missed-medicine interval. The likely classification changed. More important, the sealed index now showed how the error entered and who amended it.

“This will be used against the whole record,” Nima said.

“Only if we hide it.” Senn drew one line through the mistaken key, leaving every character readable. “An auditable correction strengthens the method.”

“Central archives say that after mistakes involving paper,” Hesh said. “With people, they prefer a blank field.”

Senn passed them the correction form. “Then sign beside mine.”

Their three signatures crowded the narrow margin. No one mistook the repair for cleanliness.

Toward morning, Senn took a shift reading entries aloud while Hesh checked the address ledger and Nima matched clinic keys. The names changed the cadence of the work. Each forced her mouth to stop before the dates and categories resumed. No line could slide silently into the next.

“Ela Sor,” she read. “Heat-wall address C-seventeen. Greenhouse mechanic. Exposure interval begins on the sixth night after a missed light window.”

Hesh found the household page. “Three residents at that address then. One moved to Eyr the following turn.”

Nima corrected the comparison group. “That move matters. Eyr's winter interval differed by four days.”

Senn logged the revision instead of replacing the old field. Her Caldrift reconciliation had treated departures as administrative noise. Here, movement remained part of the evidence.

Dawn never reached Lorn Dusk directly. The hour announced itself through a change in radiator load and footsteps outside the hall. Someone began returning charged bricks from the night rooms. The metal door would soon need closing so the wall could work again.

Hesh gathered the case keys. “Decision time.”

Senn looked at the shelves, the incomplete regional table, and the provisional index. Accepting the record did not certify every causal claim. Refusing it until central methods made it tidy would repeat the failure that had brought her here.

“I accept the full local record into the habitation audit,” she said. “Clinical conclusions remain subject to independent attribution. Exclusions remain visible. Medical details stay sealed except to authorized reviewers.”

Nima waited. “And the summary?”

“No count leaves its case index. No case index loses the district-held names.”

Hesh placed one key on the table between them. “Write that as a condition, not a promise.”

Senn wrote it on the intake form. Nima signed as originating custodian. Hesh signed as ledger keeper. Senn pressed her audit seal below both.

Outside, residents lifted the heat bricks from the cart. The door narrowed row by row as warm ceramic returned to the cradles. Pel Aru disappeared first, then Siva Nem, then Olan Tir. Their charcoal letters remained behind the working wall, attached to the heat it had held and failed to hold.

Senn carried no total away. She carried the key map, three signatures, and a rule that any future number had to open back into names.

Chapter 12 — Reserve Six

Tovin drove his boot into the lower spar and felt reserve 6 answer with a shiver it should not have had.

“Slack the starward corner,” he called.

Two riggers eased the test lines through their brakes. Black kite fabric sagged across the hangar frame, swallowing the red work lamps in a shape forty meters wide. The membrane never flew whole inside the ground facility. Crews unfolded one cell at a time across an articulated steel rig, pulled it to simulated wind load, and listened for failures too small for instruments to find early.

The shiver came again beneath Tovin's sole. Not a snap. More like a moth striking paper, faint and quick.

“Hold at eighteen percent.”

Cal, the senior rigger at the starward winch, locked her brake. “Gauge says clean.”

“Gauge has no foot.”

“It has calibration.”

“My foot passed recertification last month.”

Laughter moved around the frame without loosening anyone's hands. Tovin crouched on the spar and pressed his palm to the fabric seam. Reserve kites spent most of their lives folded in dry nitrogen, which made policy officials imagine they did not age. Adhesives continued curing. Conductive threads took a set. Micrometeoroid patches stiffened beside untouched film. A machine could rest itself into weakness.

Beyond the hangar doors, Orison's bloom light struck the apron hard enough to sharpen every tether shadow. Flight crews worked beneath open canopies on two active kites, replacing control vanes before the next pressure shift. The air smelled of hot dust and polymer sealant.

“Take it to twenty,” Tovin said.

Cal raised one finger, relayed the command, and turned both winches by a quarter mark. The membrane drew flat. Tovin felt the left diagonal load through the spar. The right side lagged.

A pale line opened near the cell boundary.

“Drop!”

Brakes released. Fabric collapsed into the catch net with a dry rush. One technician slapped the emergency stop after the motion had already ended.

Tovin climbed down, ducked under the rig, and found the line. It measured no longer than his hand. The top coating had separated from its reinforcement, exposing a silver conductive mesh. Under flight load, a tear could follow the mesh across three cells before automatic reefing responded.

Cal lay beside him on the net. “Storage crease?”

“Too straight.”

“Patch edge?”

“No patch in this panel.”

Someone at the hangar door said, “Policy lists full area available.”

Tovin knew the voice. He stayed under the fabric. “Policy can come hold this corner at altitude.”

Senn crouched outside the netting, her auditor's case balanced against one knee. Dust from darkward travel marked the hem of her coat. She looked as though she had slept on paper and found the paper argumentative.

“I need to know what Orison can surrender,” she said.

“You came to a good place. We just surrendered one kite.”

“Temporarily?”

Cal whistled through her teeth and crawled toward the access edge. Tovin followed. Standing upright, he handed Senn a strip of red fault tape.

“Mark the spot,” he said.

She looked at the tape. “I am not certified on membrane work.”

“Neither are most people who quote reserve capacity.”

Senn took the point. She crawled beneath the lowered cell while Tovin directed her to the exposed mesh. Her tape flag went beside his chalk circle, careful not to touch the damaged coating.

“Fabric fault,” she said.

“Good. Now the official record has discovered matter.”

Cal began photographing the seam with a scale strip. The rest of the crew checked adjacent folds. Tovin opened the kite's maintenance history at a standing console. Reserve 6 had passed electrical continuity, gas storage, and control-vane tests. Its last full tension run occurred nineteen months earlier. Policy assumed annual testing, but two emergency deployments of other craft had displaced this one from the frame schedule.

Senn read the delay note. “You signed the postponement.”

“I had one rig and three kites that had actually flown through grit.”

“You also signed full reserve availability six weeks later.”

“The control systems were available. Fabric certification stayed conditional.”

“The Almanac sees a whole kite.”

“The Almanac sees the capacity field policy asks us to fill.”

“You filled it.”

Tovin enlarged the form and pointed to a code beneath the green status mark. “R-six-C. Conditional membrane validation.”

“Does seasonal accounting ingest that code?”

“No.”

“Did you know?”

“I knew scheduling treated reserve numbers as planning values, not launch promises.”

“That means yes.”

He closed the history harder than necessary. “Welcome home, Senn.”

Cal glanced from one sibling to the other. “I can clear the crew.”

“No,” Tovin said. “If auditors only hear maintenance disputes after workers leave, they think equipment repairs itself between sentences.”

Senn set her case on the scarred workbench. “Immediate cancellation produces a shear front over Orison. Distributed shade could slow it. Dael says this reserve may provide part of the response.”

“Dael said may?”

“Yes.”

“Then he has learned manners.”

“What can it provide?”

Tovin looked back at the slack membrane. “Today? A repair problem.”

“After repair.”

“Unknown until full-load test, edge-control test, conductivity scan, vane synchronization, pack cycle, and a flight corridor.”

“How long?”

“If this separation stays local, three days. If the roll shares the defect, we replace cells. Replacement stock needs cutting and cure time.”

“Maximum?”

“Stop asking maintenance for one number when the inspection has not reached the second fold.”

Her jaw set. In childhood, that expression preceded lists. Senn had used lists to prove Tovin took the long irrigation route, borrowed tools without returning them, or ate preserved fruit assigned to winter. He had learned to irritate her by confessing to adjacent offenses she had not measured.

“Caldrift taught us to inspect before rain,” she said.

“Caldrift taught us rain arrived whether the form closed or not.”

Their hometown entered the hangar between them: low stone cisterns, seed roofs, their mother's weather notes pinned with kitchen knives when wind found the shutters. Senn had left for accounting. Tovin had left for machines that could be reached with both hands. Each believed the other had chosen distance.

A rigger called from the net. “Second separation, same diagonal.”

Tovin went back under. This fault ran along an internal conductor eight meters from the first. He pressed a light through the film and saw tiny clouding around the adhesive bond.

“Bad cure batch,” Cal said beside him.

“Or storage solvent.”

“Panel numbers?”

They read the edge marks. Both cells came from the same manufacturing roll, installed after a hail puncture six years earlier. Four other cells shared that roll.

Tovin ordered all six isolated. The crew clipped colored tabs along their boundaries and moved support nets before releasing the seams. Every step reduced the kite's usable area.

Senn watched without interrupting until the cell map appeared on the console. “How much shade remains if those six are reefed?”

“Enough for local glare control. Not enough to spread the whole Orison heat plume.”

“Can active kites contribute?”

“They already contribute to other schedules.”

“Above district floors?”

“Some. I pilot fabric, not constitutional distinctions.”

“You schedule maintenance against municipal priorities.”

“I receive priorities.”

“And decide which machine flies.”

Tovin pulled off his gloves. Sweat had collected beneath the cuffs. “Say what you came to accuse me of.”

“I came to ask what Orison can surrender.”

“Ask Orison. The city built eleven extra days into every dependency it could name. Now you want workers to remove them while everyone who enjoyed the light calls the consequences technical.”

“I know the consequences are physical.”

“You know after Dael made you run the model.”
