The hurt crossed her face before control returned. Tovin regretted the sentence and remained angry enough not to retrieve it.

Senn opened her case. Inside lay thermal cross-sections wired to district records. The pages looked used rather than presented. “This is what immediate cancellation does. I refused to believe safety could justify delay until I saw the layers separate.”

“Safety does justify delay.”

“Not silence.”

“No. But exposure changes demand before crews can change machines. Public challenge freezes signatures. Districts protect their allocations. Pilots receive schedules with no legal path through them.”

“You are afraid the Conservatory will ground the reserve fleet.”

“I am afraid you will prove the whole system unlawful before anyone has authority to keep it alive.”

“An unlawful system is already failing people.”

“So is a torn kite.”

Her palm flattened over the wired records. “What would you have me do? Leave Lorn Dusk below floor because our forms hid a conditional code?”

“I would have you bring the people who turn the winches into the room before issuing the correction.”

“They do not have standing.”

“Neither do those crews when policy closes. We get liability.”

Cal came to the bench holding a cut sample from the faulty seam. “You both keep using workers as if we are not within speaking distance.”

Tovin looked at her.

She placed the sample under a magnifier. “Give us a staged demand curve. We can tell you which parts are impossible. Give us an emergency shutdown and we will tell you it is unsafe, because it is.”

Senn nodded once. “Will you participate under audit protection?”

“Will participation suspend my license?”

“Not for disclosing an existing condition.”

“That sounded narrower than I hoped.”

“It is as broad as my authority.”

Cal smiled without humor. “Runs in the family.”

The inspection continued. Two of the remaining four suspect cells showed early clouding but no open separation. Tovin cut power to their conductive mesh and ordered tensile coupons from every seam. Repair stock covered three full cells. The other three required material from a retired kite panel stored across the city.

He called the depot. Its manager refused release because retired membrane remained emergency inventory under municipal seal.

“Tell them active reserve repair,” Tovin said.

“They want an incident number.”

“The incident is in front of me.”

“System wants digits.”

Senn reached for the terminal. Tovin blocked her with an elbow.

“Maintenance finding first,” he said. “Your seal after.”

Together they built the record. Cal described the audible vibration and coating separation. Tovin classified flight consequence and isolated the affected roll. Senn attached the difference between listed planning capacity and tested deployable area. When the form asked whether public safety faced immediate risk, none of the offered answers fit. No would keep the depot closed. Yes could ground the fleet.

“Conditional risk under schedule change,” Senn said.

“No field.”

“Add a statement.”

“The system ignores remarks for release authority.”

She opened the form definition. “Then we challenge the field.”

“A field challenge takes five days.”

Tovin selected YES and constrained it manually: RISK ARISES ONLY IF RESERVE SIX IS ASSIGNED ABOVE CURRENT TESTED AREA. NO ACTIVE SCHEDULE RELIES ON THAT AREA. MATERIAL REQUESTED TO RESTORE CONTINGENCY.

Senn read it. “Accurate.”

“It may still trigger fleet review.”

“I will attach an audit finding against automatic grounding.”

They both sealed the incident. The depot released the retired panel within seven minutes.

No triumph followed. A tug had to cross Orison during bloom traffic, the material needed contamination inspection, and the crew faced a night cure if they wanted the next tension test on time. Tovin assigned shifts. Two riggers objected to the hours. Cal revised the sequence so each seam team received a rest interval. The repair lengthened by four hours and became less likely to fail through fatigue.

Before cutting the retired panel, Cal rolled a strip into the inspection hood. Ultraviolet lamps revealed three old crease lines and a repair stamp from a kite scrapped after lightning damage. The depot manifest had labeled the whole roll reusable.

“Not this end,” she said.

Tovin measured past the last fluorescent trace. Clean material remained for two full cells and most of a third. They could splice the final section, but every splice added a tension discontinuity.

Senn watched Cal mark the cutting path with weighted cord. “Can another reserve donate a panel?”

“Then another reserve loses certification,” Tovin said.

“What about new stock?”

“Seven days from the fabric works if their coating line has no crop-film priority.”

“Could we certify reduced area now and add the final cell later?”

Cal answered. “Yes, if policy stops calling reduced area a failure. We would reef the damaged corner, rebalance the vane table, and fly at sixty-eight percent.”

Tovin opened the control model. Reefing changed more than area. One edge would carry higher flutter, while the guidance vanes spent extra power keeping the shortened shape aligned. He entered the measured clean cells rather than the nominal panel. Battery reserve fell below storm standard but remained above ordinary deployment threshold.

“Useful for staged shade,” he said. “Not a storm intercept.”

Senn wrote the distinction into her notes. “What restores storm capacity?”

“New third cell, full cure, another frame pull, then a pack cycle. No shortcuts.”

The crew trimmed the usable fabric with heated blades. Polymer fumes entered the hood extraction and left a faint sweet odor in the work bay. Tovin helped align the first seam. Conductive threads had to overlap within a finger's width without touching the reinforcement braid. Cal rejected their first placement because one corner creased under its own weight.

They lifted, cleaned, and aligned it again.

Senn held the far edge under instruction. Her hands stayed rigid until Tovin told her the membrane needed support, not restraint.

“You cannot order it flat,” he said.

“I noticed.”

“Let it find the frame marks.”

Material relaxed between their gloves. The silver traces settled over the alignment grid. Cal lowered the bonding bar and began the timed cure. For twelve minutes nobody could improve the seam by touching it.

Senn studied the schedule. “This is what I need for bloom reduction.”

“A shift roster?”

“A sequence that identifies resistance instead of hiding it inside a capacity value.”

“Flowers do not have seam teams.”

“Canal shutters do. Greenhouse operators do. Hospital reservoirs do.”

Tovin took a clean slate and opened Orison's current bloom demand. He rarely saw it as a civic whole. His screen usually received only the shade response: glare caps, high-air temperature ceilings, emergency margins. Senn separated demands into protected floors, operational dependencies, and discretionary uses. Decorative canal warmth occupied more capacity than Tovin expected. Commercial night bloom occupied less, but its timing forced three kite transitions.

“If you remove this block,” he said, “kite 12 can hold one angle instead of turning twice. That gives us a wider response margin.”

“Crop effect?”

“None from shade. Ask the greenhouse side about stored heat.”

They called a bloom engineer named Varo, who objected before the graph finished loading. The commercial block supported flower contracts and evening tram traffic. Senn asked for floor evidence. Varo supplied none. Tovin asked what equipment required the heat. Two canal pumps needed freeze protection, but they could draw from stored water if the decorative lights ended gradually.

“Gradually means what?” Senn asked.

Varo wanted six days. Tovin tested four. Pump load spiked on the second night. Five kept the system inside ordinary duty.

They moved to the western apricot walls. Removing light too fast risked bud loss, which would reduce fruit set rather than threaten staple supply. Varo called that distinction politically naive. Senn called it relevant. Tovin entered a two-stage reduction and watched kite transitions simplify.

Hours passed in specific surrender. A school bridge could lose evening glare while keeping classroom heat. The central tram hall could accept colder glass if crews treated condensation before the change. Two nursery blocks needed their full start, then could shorten extension light. Hospital reservoirs remained untouched. Seed storage retained its floor.

None of these decisions repaired Lorn Dusk by itself. Together they created room in the shade schedule for a slower thermal correction.

At the meal break, Tovin found a message from their mother. Caldrift expected late rain, and she wanted to know whether Senn planned to visit before the cistern festival. No mention of planetary injustice, torn fabric, or public schedules. Their mother assumed weather work remained difficult and meals still happened.

He showed the note to Senn.

“She asks you because you answer,” Senn said.

“She asks me because you turn travel into an ethical allocation problem.”

“Tell her after the hearing.”

“No hearing has been scheduled.”

“It will be.”

Tovin leaned against the bench. “That certainty scares me more than your doubts.”

Her expression changed, not into apology. Senn rarely used apology where a correction could be made. “Then I will say this precisely. I intend to release the challenge if policy blocks it. I do not know what municipal demand will do afterward. I need your model before that happens.”

“You want a safe emergency shutdown.”

“I want to know whether one exists.”

“It does not.”

“You have not modeled every sequence.”

“I know the fleet. Shutdown sends demand discontinuities into machines already carrying active weather. Safe work comes from staged change, spare fabric, crews with sleep, and districts accepting outcomes they did not request.”

Senn closed Varo's feed. “Model the staged bloom reduction, then.”

“Under whose authority?”

“Technical contingency planning. No schedule change yet.”

“Ceryn can classify it.”

“Not the maintenance inputs. Keep copies with the crews.”

Cal returned with the depot panel wrapped around a clean tube. “We already do.”

Tovin looked from the replacement fabric to Senn's wired evidence. Exposure had arrived regardless of whether workers wanted it. His choice concerned whether the first public demand would meet a blank capacity field or an honest sequence.

He created a new model and entered five days for decorative canal reduction, staged apricot cuts, protected hospital and seed loads, simplified kite transitions, and reserve 6 at only the area its repaired cells could prove.

“I will run this,” he said. “I will put crew hours, cure times, weather corridors, and every conditional reserve in the output.”

Senn waited for the rest.

Tovin gave it to her. “I will not promise a safe emergency shutdown. Do not quote a range as one.”

Behind them, the riggers unrolled replacement membrane beneath the lowered kite. Light passed through the thin material in a copper stain. Nobody called it ready.

Chapter 13 — The Public Forecast

The tram glass promised perfect bloom while Nima Sol excluded a dead child from the attribution.

Gold letters traveled across the window behind her: ORISON BLOOM CONTINUES AT FULL BRIGHTNESS. CANAL WARMING ON SCHEDULE. POLLINATOR CONDITIONS EXCELLENT. The words passed over her clinic coat and vanished into the frame.

Nima drew a line through case NF-044. “No defensible weather link.”

Hesh turned the Nacre Fold register around. “The medicine transport missed its light corridor.”

“Because the carrier launched late,” Nima said. “Weather narrowed the margin. It did not create the delay.”

“The child still waited in cold.”

“She remains in the death record. She does not enter this claim.”

Senn watched Hesh press two fingers to the page. They had argued for restraint in Lorn Dusk, but another district's child made exclusion harder. Pera sat at the end of the archive table, entering the reason in both the sealed case index and public methodological annex.

“Do not write unrelated,” Hesh said.

Pera looked up. “I wrote insufficient causal contribution under stated standard.”

“Good.”

The four of them had worked since the first public chime in a borrowed records room above Orison's east tram station. Nima had refused the Conservatory clinic because its access system logged patient keys before the audit seal engaged. Hesh distrusted every room whose locks answered to Ceryn. Pera found a municipal lease archive with paper blinds, a working heat plate, and a view of three forecast screens nobody could switch off from inside.

Eyr's files occupied the left table. Nacre Fold's occupied the right. Lorn Dusk's case map hung on the wall, names protected beneath folded paper strips but local keys visible. The regional table remained blank in its final field.

Senn opened the next Nacre file. “NF-045. Mara Pell. Age sixty-two at death. Shell-lime worker.”

Nima found the clinic extract. “Chronic lung scarring. Two cold admissions before the disputed interval, four during it.”

Hesh read the heating record. “Domestic rooms below local request on eleven nights. Clinic light shortened twice.”

“Comparison?” Senn asked.

Pera passed across a chart. Residents with equivalent lung scores in recognized districts showed fewer admissions after adjustment for age, prior illness, and household size. The sample stayed small. Confidence bands overlapped at their edges.

“Associated,” Nima said. “Not individually attributable.”

“Then she contributes to the population estimate but not the high-specificity case set.”

“Correct.”

Senn entered the grade. Every distinction slowed the work and strengthened the result. They were not counting bodies. They were estimating excess deaths against a world that had never run the same twenty-two years without transferred winter. Causal proof had to be assembled from comparisons, exposure timing, clinic records, instrument histories, and known pathways. No alternate Meral waited nearby as a control.

A knock sounded. Pera covered the key map before opening the door.

The station clerk delivered four cups and a notice that forecast maintenance would brighten the west screen at midday. “Municipal publicity wants the colors consistent across the canal.”

“Can we ask them to dim it?” Hesh said.

“During perfect bloom?” The clerk looked alarmed by the phrase alone. “Not without a visual continuity finding.”

Pera accepted the cups. “We are conducting one.”

After the door closed, the glass announced eleven additional public garden events. Senn could see one below the station: workers tying pale ribbons along the canal rail, children carrying paper flowers, vendors unfolding shade awnings against the light whose perfection needed advertising.

Nima moved to the Eyr files. Their clinic records used ceramic tags that had been photographed and indexed by Dael's former apprenticeship office. Several years lacked complete domestic temperatures because tower instruments recorded only intake and exhaust. Local heat-vane positions supplied a proxy. Each vane bent at a known temperature range, and apprentices had logged their morning angles as part of training.

“Counsel will call this folklore,” Pera said.

“Counsel can reproduce the calibration,” Senn replied.

They compared vane angles with years containing certified probes. The relationship held within a range broad enough for exposure bands, not precise household values. Nima lowered the grade for seven cases. Hesh objected to two. They reopened both.

One concerned a tower mechanic named Es Dar who died after working a prolonged exterior shift. His supervisor's log showed standard duration. His partner's household note claimed the shift ran three hours longer because the relief tram lost its light window.

“Transport archive?” Senn asked.

Pera searched the municipal copies. “Eyr does not legally operate a civic tram.”

“It has moved workers for thirty years,” Hesh said.

“Officially, tower 11 operates a maintenance carriage.”

The equipment schedule recorded a stalled carriage and delayed relief. Human shift records omitted the delay because payroll closed against standard hours.

Nima revised the exposure path. “Strong association. Infection after cold injury remains multifactorial.”

Senn attached both categories. The same classification trick recurred at every scale. A tram became a carriage. A household became equipment occupancy. An extended shift became standard because wages recognized no extension. Measurements survived while names lost their civic connections.

At midday, the west screen brightened. Golden light filled the room, bleaching blue ink until Pera lowered the paper blinds. Heat still entered through the glass.

“Public forecast says the day holds within ideal pedestrian range,” Pera said.

Hesh touched the warming table. “Does it say for which pedestrian?”

No one answered. They returned to work.

A harder dispute waited in the nutrition files. Nacre Fold had recorded low-weight births after three winters when greenhouse light failed during fungal outbreaks. The local report attributed the entire change to reduced food variety. Nima separated maternal cold exposure, infection, household crowding, and ration composition, then found the records could not distinguish them reliably.

“Exclude the birth outcomes,” she said.

Hesh paced to the window. Beyond the blind, projected apricot petals slid across the tram glass. “If a consequence spreads across a household, your method loses it because no clinic line can hold the whole path.”

“My method loses claims it cannot defend.”

“Policy created the missing records.”

“That does not let medicine create certainty in reply.”

Senn opened the ration ledgers instead of resolving the argument. Deliveries to Nacre Fold fell during the same intervals, but total food mass stayed above emergency floor. Variety narrowed sharply. Protein cultures arrived late. A central analyst might call the difference discomfort. Birth records suggested more, though comparison years included a separate greenhouse fungus.

“Keep the data in the contextual annex,” Senn said. “Do not use it in the mortality estimate. State that exclusion may undercount diffuse effects.”

Nima agreed. Hesh made her add who would review the question later. Pera created a research limitation section rather than burying it below methods.

Next came baseline selection. Conservatory tables compared darkward residents with all of Meral, whose age distribution and medical access differed. Nima had built matched groups from cold-edge districts, but recognized districts received more reliable light. Rul Fen would claim no true comparator existed.

“He will be right,” Senn said.

Pera stared at her. “That is inconvenient.”

“Write it.”

They ran three baselines: planetary, cold-edge recognized districts, and each settlement's own ordinary-schedule years. Planetary comparison produced the largest excess. Internal comparison produced the smallest, partly because no year escaped the inherited burden entirely. The matched district approach fell between them.

Nima chose the internal result as the conservative floor for cold-linked mortality and used matched districts only where medical access could be adjusted. Her pencil moved several cases out of the high-specificity set.

Hesh read every removal. “A lower estimate will become the truth people repeat.”

“A higher estimate with weak assumptions will become the flaw they repeat,” Nima said.

Senn added the full range to the methods display. “The central figure must never travel without the uncertainty interval and exclusion rule.”

“Numbers shed luggage,” Hesh replied.

“Then we fasten it.” Pera created a display object in which copying the central field automatically copied years, range, method version, and annex link. A person could still retype the figure alone, but the official record would resist effortless separation.

The forecast screen changed from bloom conditions to a smiling map of school garden openings. One highlighted terrace stood above a canal heated partly by the concealed transfer. Children released paper-winged pollinators into air warm enough for municipal bees.

Pera lowered the blind another finger's width. “Would showing this beside the report count as editorializing?”

“Yes,” Senn said.

“Pity.”

A technical objection arrived from the Conservatory archive before Senn had transmitted anything. Automated validation rejected Lorn Dusk's oldest burial years because local date notation preceded the current civic calendar format. Pera swore, then pulled two Order 9 supply manifests whose dates used both systems. The manifests mapped local winter turns to Conservatory years.

“Emergency clerks accidentally authenticated the people their order denied,” she said.

Conversion shifted four exposure intervals by one day. Nima reran the affected cases. One medicine delay no longer overlapped a cancelled window and moved to exclusion. Another cold interval lengthened.

Senn made Pera preserve the rejected import, the date bridge, and the corrected run. “No invisible cleaning.”

“I have started hearing that sentence in my sleep.”

“Good archival training.”

“Poor sleep training.”

The first complete model run finished after the meal hour. Nima stood behind Senn as the slate processed district exposure intervals, baseline mortality, age and illness adjustments, specific high-confidence pathways, and uncertainty from missing measurements. The result appeared as a range.

Its center exceeded the figure local advocates had used in private. Hesh stared at it without satisfaction.

“Run exclusions as zero contribution,” Senn said.

“They already are,” Nima replied.

“Run every uncertain instrument year at the warm boundary.”

Pera saved the primary result and labeled the next test CONSERVATIVE EXPOSURE ASSUMPTION. Eyr's temperature bands shifted. Nacre Fold's comparison weakened. The center fell, but not enough to change the scale of the finding.

“Now remove accident pathways,” Hesh said.

Nima did. Radiator falls, coolant injuries, transport failures, and maintenance incidents left the model. Only cold exposure, delayed medicine tied directly to withheld service, respiratory risk, and documented crop-light nutrition effects remained. The range narrowed.

Senn read the central estimate. “We need independent replication.”

“You asked me to complete the attribution,” Nima said.

“I asked you to make it defensible. One model on one slate is not enough.”

Pera opened the sealed methods package. “The Conservatory health office can replicate.”

“Ceryn controls its classification.”

“Three recognized district clinics have mortality analysts. Give each a blinded bundle.”

Hesh looked at the name index under its paper covers. “Blinded from people?”

“Blinded from district and policy category,” Nima said. “They receive exposure bands, comparison data, outcomes, and method. Names remain attached here. We test whether knowing darkward origin changes judgment.”

Senn authorized three protected review copies. Pera generated random case keys while Hesh witnessed the map between temporary keys and local records. The transmission would take less than an hour. Replies could take days.

A message from the Conservatory arrived before any reviewer answered. First Curator Ceryn Ahl required Senn's presence at the Season Table with all preliminary health findings. Her order cited planetary stability and restricted policy material.

“She knows,” Pera said.

“She knows records moved under the challenge,” Senn replied. “Not the result unless the room leaks.”

Hesh examined the air vent.

“That was not metaphorical,” Pera said.

They checked seals. No breach appeared. Senn suspected inference rather than spying. Ceryn knew which records existed and what twenty-two years of winter could contain. She had signed the renewal while permanent households remained.

“Finish the annex before we go,” Senn said.

Pera bound the methods, exclusions, instrument limitations, comparison definitions, alternate runs, and reversible case index into one digital package. Medical details stayed encrypted behind district-held keys. Hesh signed custody. Nima signed clinical method. Senn signed audit intake.

The complete table still waited for independent review.

At the Conservatory, perfect bloom covered the exterior walls in projected apricot branches. Visitors stood beneath them for pictures while Senn's group entered through the staff door. The old pressureworks windows cast red bars across the Season Table chamber.

Ceryn waited with two policy counsel and Rul Fen, counsel for the central cities. No health officer attended.

“You brought a district claimant into a restricted technical review,” Ceryn said.

“Hesh holds source custody.”

“They lack recognized district status.”

“The active challenge concerns that denial. Excluding them would contaminate the evidence chain.”

Rul folded his hands. “Orison's forecast has already issued. Any suggestion of mass casualty attribution could alter demand before engineering has a stable alternative.”

Nima put her case on the table. “Then listen carefully enough not to suggest what we have not concluded.”

Ceryn's attention settled on her. “Doctor Sol, has your review produced a number?”

“A provisional central estimate pending independent replication.”

“Give it to me.”

“No,” Senn said.

Ceryn did not raise her voice. “You have no authority to withhold evidence from the First Curator during a planetary risk review.”

“I have authority to protect preliminary audit material from policy use before method and source conditions attach. You asked for a number. The evidence is not one field.”

Rul opened a city dependency map. Hospitals, schools, crop starts, transit, and municipal heat glowed across seven central districts. “Public release without remedy planning could cause councils to refuse transfers. Forecast schedules rely on reciprocal demand. The atmosphere will respond before procedure does.”

Hesh stood behind an unlit section of the table. “Your concern is that named cities may refuse burdens once they see who paid them.”

“My concern includes that,” Rul said. “It also includes clinics losing heat because frightened councils lock every discretionary share.”

The honesty prevented easy dismissal.

Ceryn requested the method package. Senn transmitted the protected header, which displayed scope and review state but not patient keys. The First Curator read the causal standards, alternate assumptions, and pending replication notices.

“This falls under sensitive infrastructure mortality analysis,” Ceryn said. “Policy rule sixteen permits temporary classification.”

“For a specific threat,” Pera said.

One counsel turned toward her. “Archive clerks do not interpret policy at the Table.”

“This one does when a rule changes the custody status of her annex.” Pera pointed to the citation. “Rule sixteen covers information that identifies vulnerable infrastructure. The public table can redact clinic locations and instrument weaknesses. It does not authorize suppression of aggregate findings or habitation evidence.”

Ceryn looked at the junior clerk for a long second. “Who asked you to prepare a public annex?”

“The audit form did.”

“Which form?”

“Habitation challenge procedure eight. Evidence affecting standing must carry a reviewable basis.”

Senn recognized the narrow smile Pera used when fear had found something exact to hold.

Ceryn changed approach. “Then I classify the complete package pending redaction review.”

“Classification would include the annex,” Pera said. “A preservation copy has already entered the protected archive queue under challenge custody.”

“You moved it before policy review?”

“I preserved it before policy alteration. Those are different verbs.”

The counsel beside Ceryn began drafting an order.

Senn's slate signaled the first independent response. A recognized district clinic had replicated Nima's method within the stated range. The reviewer challenged one comparison band but found the central estimate supported. Two minutes later, the second response arrived with a lower estimate under different respiratory assumptions. Its uncertainty still included Nima's result.

Everyone at the table saw the alerts.

“Do not open those into this meeting,” Ceryn said.

Senn opened them. Protected audit procedure required review findings to join the package when received. She logged the critiques, reran the respiratory sensitivity, and watched the central estimate move by less than one percent.

The third reviewer requested raw code and flagged possible duplicate matching between an Eyr clinic key and transport record. Pera traced the keys. Two sources described one person. Nima removed the duplicate pathway, though the model had already prevented two deaths from being assigned. The correction altered weighting, not the case count.

Hours tightened around a single field. Outside the chamber, forecast chimes announced another ideal bloom interval. Inside, Ceryn's counsel completed a temporary classification order. Pera's archive queue reported preservation complete.

The third replication arrived.

Nima read it first. She checked the conservative model, the district ranges, and the specific-case annex. Then she wrote the defensible attribution in the final field: 317 excess deaths across twenty-two years.

Hesh did not look at the figure. “Open the names.”

Pera unfolded the covers over the local key maps. Rows of names did not appear on the public layer, but every case resolved to district custody. Hesh tested three at random. One opened into a burial record and heat-wall strip. Another led to a Nacre clinic file with shell-lime exposure separated from winter contribution. The third reached an excluded Eyr case, proving the denominator retained decisions against the claim.

Ceryn read the result. Control left her face only in the stillness around her mouth.

“This report remains classified until stability planning closes,” she said.

“No,” Senn replied.

Rul pushed back his chair. “Auditor, municipal demand will react within minutes.”

“It should react to evidence.”

“It may react by refusing every transfer, lawful or not.”

“Then engineering needs to know the reaction is coming.”

Ceryn placed her seal against the order. “I am directing you.”

Senn opened habitation challenge procedure eight. The protected route allowed an auditor to release evidence jointly establishing unrecognized habitation and material harm when policy action would otherwise prevent timely review. It required source custody, independent method replication, privacy redaction, stated uncertainty, and personal acceptance of disciplinary review.

Every condition now had a mark beside it.

She joined the habitation challenge to the attribution package. The address ledgers established continuous civic heat, births, burials, and households. The report established consequences associated with treating those households as infrastructure. Pera attached the complete public annex and preservation checksum. Nima approved the medical redactions. Hesh approved district custody language.

“Once you seal that,” Ceryn said, “you cannot control what the cities do with it.”

“I could not control what they did without it.”

Senn pressed her thumb to the protected release.

Across Orison, the public forecast vanished from tram glass and water towers. For one breath, the screens held only their own pale reflection. Then the habitation challenge appeared beside the attribution. The green bloom band contracted to an upper corner while the names filled the remaining glass.

Chapter 14 — A Necessary Shadow

The first refusal reached Tovin while reserve 6 hung half packed above the launch cradle.

A municipal seal flashed across the hangar console, canceling West Canal’s acceptance of the next shade interval. Two more notices followed before he finished reading it. Upper Orison withdrew a heat export. Apricot Ward locked its reflected-light allotment pending legal review. Each council cited the newly released attribution and declared that no local weather share would move until the Conservatory proved where it went.

Above the console, black membrane descended in careful folds between Cal’s gloved hands. The repaired kite had passed a reduced-area tension test at dawn. Its damaged corner remained reefed, its vane table had been rebuilt for the shorter edge, and its battery margin allowed one ordinary deployment. Policy still displayed a green reserve symbol as if those conditions could be folded away with the fabric.

“Stop the pack,” Tovin called.

Winches halted. Cal held the membrane off the cradle while two riggers secured the suspended fold with padded clamps.

Senn came through the hangar door carrying no coat and three active slates. Public forecast light, stripped of its bloom colors, made her face look gray. “How many?”

“Three refusals.”

“Seven.” She laid the slates on his console. “Four went to scheduling rather than fleet control.”

Tovin opened the next mirror sequence. Its orderly bands broke apart as each canceled transfer propagated. A shade interval over West Canal had balanced mirror 82’s hospital corridor. Without that shade, the schedule shortened the corridor to contain surface heat. Apricot Ward’s locked light forced mirror 117 to hold a starward angle longer, warming air above the eastern terraces. Upper Orison’s withdrawn export left the resulting heat nowhere to go before a darkward current reached the city.

“How long since release?” he asked.

“Thirty-eight minutes.”

“Fast work.”

“Fear has good clerks.”

A pressure alarm sounded at the meteorology desk. Cal passed the hanging fold to another rigger and crossed to the wind display. Two air masses appeared as pale sheets, one rising from Orison’s heated stone, another sliding starward from the night ribbon. Their predicted margins touched above the eastern greenhouses.

Tovin enlarged the shear layer. “That seam did not exist on the morning run.”

“It existed as dispersed risk,” Senn said. “The refusals have concentrated it.”

“Which district moved first?”

“Lower Glass. They canceled a cold transfer after the report named Nacre Fold. The others copied the action without copying its destination.”

The distinction mattered physically and nowhere else. Lower Glass had rejected a transfer connected to the concealed burden. West Canal had rejected lawful shade exchanged with another recognized district. Municipal offices now treated every movement of cold as suspected theft. Demand did not know the difference between caution and panic once it reached the mirror queue.

Cal traced the forming seam with one blunt fingernail. “When does it tighten?”

“Run current commitments,” Tovin said.

Senn linked her audit feed. The lattice advanced across the afternoon. Heat gathered over the canal stone, lifted through the greenhouse terraces, and met the darkward flow after the meal chime. Yellow spread along the contact. By evening, a red hook curved toward three school roofs and the eastern staple beds.

No one in the hangar mistook the color for weather. Red meant crews securing glass, clinics preparing for impact injuries, canal gates lowering before wind drove water over their margins. It meant people under roofs built for the gust band they had been promised.

Tovin stopped the run at the first warning. “We can blunt the warm side with shade.”

Senn looked up at the suspended kite. “At sixty-eight percent.”

“Sixty-seven after pack allowance. Do not improve my machine while I am standing beside it.”

“Can it reach the eastern corridor?”

“Not under the published plan.” He opened the flight paths. “Corridor three crosses mirror control space during the hospital turn. Corridor four takes us around, but the seam closes before arrival.”

“Then move the hospital turn.”

A scheduler at the neighboring desk answered without invitation. “South Clinic refuses transfer changes. Their council locked all light after the release.”

Senn gathered the seven notices into one column. “The clinic did not refuse its own light.”

“They refused any adjustment touching it.”

“That is not the same authority.”

“It gives the same command to my desk.”

Tovin watched the red hook deepen. An argument over authority could outlive the roofs. “Who controls corridor three?”

“Mirror crews 82, 117, and 204,” the scheduler said. “Separate municipal contracts.”

“Put their leads on voice.”

Only crew 82 answered. Its lead, Iven Marr, appeared against a service-room wall patterned with old burn marks. “We received a hold order.”

“You also received a shear warning.”

“Municipal counsel says no transfers until destination review.”

Tovin sent the flight overlay. “I need ninety seconds of angle clearance to lift reserve 6 through your corridor. Your hospital delivery stays whole. Arrival shifts by four minutes.”

“Four minutes enters our variance record.”

“So enter it.”

“Under whose order?”

“Mine.”

Iven’s expression did not change. “Fleet coordination cannot alter a mirror contract.”

“Correct. You will alter it under immediate equipment protection.”

“Counsel excluded demand disputes from that clause.”

“This is a kite crossing, not a demand dispute.”

Senn leaned toward the voice pickup. “The protected audit release suspends no safety authority. I will sign that finding.”

Iven glanced away, likely toward workers listening outside his camera. “Your last signature created the hold.”

The words found Senn cleanly. Her shoulders drew back, but she did not defend the release. “Yes. The evidence changed demand. Current safety law still applies.”

Tovin waited. He knew the difference between giving a crew lead facts and offering her a place to hide. Iven needed a reason she could repeat with her workers’ names attached.

“Your mirror keeps its destination,” he said. “You accept a four-minute timing variance so a certified reserve craft can cross. I log the lost minutes against fleet emergency use, not against South Clinic. If the council wants to call that an unlawful transfer, they can explain which district received the four minutes.”

“None receives them?”

“The sky does.”

Iven read the overlay again. “Crew 82 accepts temporary delay for corridor clearance. Send the exact clock.”

One band opened in the schedule. It did not reach launch altitude.

Crew 117 refused voice twice, then returned text demanding written proof that its adjustment would not recreate the winter debit. Senn drafted a narrow finding while Tovin calculated the crossing. Mirror 117 had to rotate starward by less than a degree, reducing light on an ornamental apricot wall. The saved heat entered no darkward account. It simply did not enter Orison.

“Call the wall operator,” Tovin said.

Varo answered from a greenhouse control walk. Behind him, workers watched public screens carrying the attribution range and names of the three settlements. “We have a full-light commitment.”

“You have decorative bloom.”

“I have eight thousand visitors expected this afternoon and a council order against unscheduled shade.”

“Then expect them in coats.”

Varo’s mouth tightened. “Easy to say from a hangar.”

Tovin turned the camera toward reserve 6. Riggers stood beneath its imperfect fold, holding repaired fabric above the cradle because he had stopped the pack. “My crew spent the night making policy’s green symbol capable of flight. I am asking yours to close one tram wall for six minutes.”

“Public reaction is already severe.”

“Public wind will be worse.”

Senn slid her finding onto the channel. It stated that the proposed shade created no debit, reduced central heat input, and touched no protected service. Varo read each clause.

“Who answers when visitors say Orison darkened because the death count came out?” he asked.

“I do,” Senn said.

Tovin shook his head. “We all do. Your crew records the closure. Mine records the launch. Audit records why. Nobody calls the six minutes normal bloom.”

Varo looked behind him. A worker in a condensation apron spoke beyond pickup range. Another pointed toward the wall controls.

“Western apricot crew accepts six minutes of temporary shade,” Varo said. “We keep heat strips on the upper bridge.”

“That gives us mirror 117,” the scheduler reported.

Two clearances still left a narrow collision at altitude. Mirror 204 served a seed-start district south of the canal. Its crew lead, Mara En, answered at once and refused before Tovin finished the request.

“Our council has locked every concession,” she said. “They think the Conservatory will use voluntary movement as proof that the old allocation can continue.”

“So do not volunteer a transfer. Accept local shade.”

“On seed roofs.”

“On service glass between the roofs. Eleven minutes, moving strip, no crop cell loses more than forty seconds.”

Mara pulled the proposed track apart. “Your shortened kite carries edge flutter into our mirror wake.”

At last, a useful objection. Tovin opened reserve 6’s measured vane response and removed the nominal certification curve. “Flutter peaks here. We cross below it if your mirror holds two tenths darkward after the strip passes.”

“That points light toward Orison.”

“Toward a canal surface already under shutter.”

“Show temperature.”

Cal fed him the live canal probes. Stored warmth remained high, but closed shutters would reflect most of the brief addition. Tovin modeled the remainder. Surface air gained less heat than the kite removed from the rising plume.

Mara studied both curves. “My crew accepts temporary shade if your pilot holds below the wake ceiling and canal shutters confirm closed.”

“Agreed.”

“I want your name on the flutter assumption.”

“You have it.”

Three crews had accepted visible inconvenience without surrendering weather to an unnamed place. The opened path ran from the launch apron to the eastern heat plume in a thin blue curve.

Tovin keyed the cradle sequence. “Resume pack. Flight configuration.”

Clamps released in order. Cal guided the repaired membrane into its canister while riggers checked each conductive trace at the fold. The earlier delay had cost seven minutes. Beyond the doors, ground vanes shifted toward the city, showing the first pull from the darkward current.

Senn followed him to the equipment rack. “You are flying?”

“I certified the reduced vane table.”

“You coordinated all night.”

“I slept during cure.”

“For how long?”

“Long enough to remain legally disappointing.”

Cal threw him a harness. “Ninety-two minutes. I logged it.”

Senn looked ready to turn fatigue into a prohibition. Tovin tightened the chest straps before she could find the correct field. “Cal takes guidance. I handle deployment and municipal calls.”

“She has had less sleep than you.”

“I had four hours,” Cal said. “Unlike him, I did not spend two of them arguing with a fabric depot.”

That ended the audit. They climbed into the control cabin beside the cradle, close enough to reach the manual cutoffs but never close to the high air. Kite pilots flew pressure and tension through instruments. Heroics at altitude belonged to memorial plaques and bad staffing.

Launch fans lifted the packed craft into the first current. Tovin felt its load through the control grips as the tether paid out. Reduced area made the canister rise unevenly. Cal corrected with two guidance vanes, keeping battery draw inside the measured margin.

“Crew 82 window in forty seconds,” the scheduler said.

Mirror light narrowed on the apron. A bright column shifted across the hangar roof, paused, then opened a dim lane through the eastern sky. Tovin guided the canister into it. Four minutes vanished from a hospital schedule without vanishing from its account.

Crew 117 cut the apricot wall. The city’s projected blossoms went dark along one tram route. Real leaves behind the glass lost their shine, and pedestrians below stopped to look up. Tovin had no audio from the street, only heat readings dropping by fractions as reserve 6 climbed.

“Approaching seed corridor,” Cal said.

Canal shutters confirmed closed. Mirror 204 held its angle while Mara’s crew laid the moving strip of shade between crop roofs. Tovin kept below the wake ceiling. The canister shuddered once as the shortened edge met disturbed air.

Cal’s hand closed around the reef control. “Flutter rising.”

“Within curve.”

“Barely.”

“Two tenths darkward now.”

Mara adjusted the mirror. Light struck the shuttered canal in a hard white patch, then slid away. The shudder eased. Reserve 6 cleared the last service roof with battery margin intact.

Behind its passage, Mara’s crew restored light cell by cell instead of swinging the whole mirror at once. Seed technicians confirmed each roof before the next strip brightened. One service pane remained shaded because a worker had found condensation under its frame and refused to certify heat until the seal received inspection. Tovin marked the delay as maintenance, not weather surrender. Eleven minutes grew to thirteen, but the extra time stayed attached to a pane, a defect, and the person who had seen it.

Ahead, the shear front had become visible in the instruments as a pressure ridge. Warm air rose on its starward side. Cold flow pressed beneath it from darkward, sharpening the boundary with every minute of unchanged demand.

Tovin released the first membrane cell.

Black film opened against copper haze. Tension climbed along the repaired seams. Cal fed vane corrections into the shortened edge while he watched the old manufacturing roll take load. One cell, then another, spread above Orison. The reefed corner left the shape asymmetrical, more working sail than policy diagram.

“Seam temperatures stable,” Cal said.

“Open to certified area.”

Fabric extended across the high current. Its shadow touched the eastern terraces as a faint gray movement, too diffuse to have an edge from the ground. Heat input fell. The rising plume weakened but did not flatten.

A new alarm sounded from West Canal. Its council had ordered shutters reopened to preserve the scheduled pedestrian warmth canceled during launch.

Senn answered before Tovin could. “Keep them closed under immediate shear protection.”

A municipal officer appeared on the channel. “Auditor Vale, your release is under disciplinary review. You cannot assign our district a consequence while the challenged schedule remains disputed.”

Tovin held the kite angle. “This consequence stays in West Canal. Nothing moves darkward.”

“Our public forecast promised full bloom.”

“The forecast changed when your council withdrew shade.”

“We rejected complicity in an unlawful allocation.”

“Then reject it accurately,” Senn said. “The old debit remains absent. Your shutters now prevent local heat from entering a shear front.”

Silence lasted through three pressure updates. Tovin imagined officials reading the attribution on one screen and a weather warning on another, searching for a choice that carried no stain.

“What duration?” the officer asked.

Senn looked at him.

Tovin ran the stabilized field forward. Reserve 6 could hold this angle for seven hours before battery limits required descent. The immediate shadow blunted the front, yet the next mirror schedule would rebuild the imbalance unless councils released selected commitments. He entered the three crew concessions, locked the concealed transfer at zero, and allowed only locally accepted shade. The model found a narrow path through the turn.

“Nine hours today,” he said. “Reduced intervals afterward. Full schedule review within nine days.”

“You can guarantee resolution?”

“No. I can buy nine days for people to build one.”

The officer authorized closed shutters through the evening chime.

Across the field display, yellow pulled back from the school roofs. Red disappeared last, shrinking to a thin point above an empty service yard before the warning cleared. Hospital light arrived four minutes late and at full quantity. Seed roofs kept their crop exposure. The apricot wall stayed dark.

Cal eased reserve 6 toward its holding track. “Pressure ridge dispersing.”

Tovin did not trust a single indicator. He checked roof gusts, canal margins, hospital reservoirs, greenhouse floor temperatures, and the darkward loads that had once been used as an invisible balancing field. Lorn Dusk, Eyr, and Nacre Fold showed no renewed debit. Their cold did not deepen to rescue Orison from its refusals.

Senn remained on the municipal channel as district notices changed from absolute holds to conditional review. Councils had seen that refusal could aim weather at their own roofs. None withdrew its objection to the concealed system. Two asked for proof that their proposed adjustments stayed local. A third demanded crew signatures before accepting shade.

“Send them the launch record,” Tovin said.

“It is not complete.”

“It is true enough to work from. Add corrections when Cal lands the kite.”

Senn transmitted the live record with its conditional certification, named crews, altered minutes, and destinations. No clean capacity figure survived the package. Every shadow touched a place.

By the meal chime, Orison’s public screens carried a revised municipal notice. UNSCHEDULED COOLING IN EFFECT. PROTECTED SERVICES MAINTAINED. The announcement avoided cause, then an audit attachment supplied it. People left canal tables carrying cups in both hands. Tram passengers buttoned coats beneath dark apricot glass. Greenhouse crews lowered interior curtains to hold soil warmth.

Tovin kept reserve 6 aloft until the pressure field separated into ordinary currents. Its repaired seams held. Battery reserve narrowed exactly as the test had predicted, leaving no generous margin for anyone to convert into future policy.

After descent began, Senn stood beside the cabin window and watched shade travel across the terraces. “You were right about what exposure would do to demand.”

He loosened one aching hand from the grip. “That does not make you wrong to release it.”

“No.”

“Try to enjoy agreement before we ruin it.”

Her mouth shifted, almost a smile. Then she checked the settlement loads again.

Evening had not arrived, but Orison’s stones stopped releasing their stored warmth. Breath showed near the tram platforms. Canal frost formed under bridge shadows where public crews spread grit and posted warning lamps. Clinics reported ordinary attendance. Roof teams stood down without opening an injury register.

When the revised forecast named the interval, it used the plain phrase cold afternoon. No district received a debit for it. No municipal total hid where the lost warmth stayed.

Tovin watched people cross the chilled bridges slowly, irritated and alive. Orison spent its first unscheduled cold afternoon, and nobody died.

Act III — Standing in the Storm

Chapter 15 — No Innocent Correction

Senn moved Lorn Dusk eight hundred kilometers starward and watched Orison drown in warm rain.

The model rendered the change without drama. Blue cloud bands thickened over the central terraces. Canal margins flashed amber for overflow. A shear warning opened above the eastern greenhouses, followed by a hospital reservoir alert and nine crop-temperature deviations. Lorn Dusk’s district band, newly inserted for the test, warmed toward its climate floor.

Dael stopped the simulation before the first roof failures appeared. “You cannot move a city with a stylus.”

“I moved its burden.”

“You moved it as if distance had no unit.”

Across the table, Hesh Dara kept both hands around a cup that had gone cold. They had arrived from the darkward transfer station two hours earlier and refused the Conservatory’s offer of a heated guest coat. Chalk dust still marked one cuff. “Run it to nine days.”

Dael glanced at Senn.

“Run it,” she said.

The lattice advanced. On the fourth day, Orison’s canal pumps exceeded drainage capacity. By day six, warm air from the bloom terraces struck a darkward flow redirected around Lorn Dusk. The collision tightened into a moving seam. Day nine painted a red crescent through Orison’s school roofs and staple beds.

Hesh studied the crescent. “How many people receive their proper floor in this version?”

“All eighteen thousand four hundred twenty across the three settlements,” Senn said.

“How many central districts fall below theirs?”

“None.”

Dael reached into the display and opened the risk layers. “A floor does not protect anyone from a roof coming off. This model preserves annual minimums while concentrating the transition into one weather front.”

Senn closed the shear overlay. Beneath it, the transfer looked almost fair. Every inhabited band retained enough heat, light, rain, and wind tolerance to meet the Charter threshold. The figures balanced. Her old Caldrift reconciliation had balanced too, while leases passed out of the hands of people the payment meant to save.

“Then geography constrains the remedy,” she said.

“Geography has constrained every remedy since the first mirror,” Dael replied.

Hesh set the cup beside the control rail. “You asked us to help build a correction. We did not agree to admire the reasons you cannot make one.”

They worked in a borrowed forecasting room below the Season Table. Its ceiling carried old pressure pipes and newer light conduits, a history of climate work added without removing what came before. Through the high interior window, Senn could see clerks preparing the planetary hearing docket. Names entered one column. Districts entered another. Lorn Dusk, Eyr, and Nacre Fold appeared only in the evidence index.

She opened the first remedy model again. “Population share. Every district accepts transition burden according to registered inhabitants. No district falls below its floor. Larger districts take more because more people benefit from planetary stability.”

Hesh came around the table. “Show the shares without district names.”

Senn hid the labels. Pale bars rose from the surface, each proportional to population. Orison carried the highest absolute burden. Three other central cities followed. Near the darkward edge, two bars stood taller than their wealth or infrastructure suggested.

“Put the names back,” Hesh said.

The labels returned. One tall bar belonged to a dense foundry district whose residents lived six to a room near public heat ducts. Another belonged to a rain-poor settlement that had taken in families displaced by a reservoir failure. Lorn Dusk’s bar remained smaller, though not small.

“You have made crowded poverty expensive,” Hesh said. “A district gets punished for keeping households alive closely enough to share walls.”

“The proportion follows people, not wealth.”

“That distinction comforts the people who own wealth.”

Senn expanded the foundry district’s account. Its climate floor consumed nearly all scheduled weather. Only a narrow margin of rain preference and high-light timing remained available for trade. Her population model took three times that margin. The deficit did not appear as a floor violation because she had allowed storm-risk tolerance to carry it.

Dael tapped the amber wind figure. “Those roofs use ceramic pins rated for the current gust band. Add this much variance and maintenance crews start choosing which blocks receive replacements.”

“I can cap the storm component.”

“Then the burden moves into heat.”

“Cap that too.”

“Then you have stopped using population.”

Hesh looked through the interior window at the clerks. “Lorn Dusk has eight thousand one hundred forty residents because families stayed after the construction work ended. Under this model, the proof of habitation becomes a larger invoice.”

Senn deleted the population weighting. The bars fell flat.

No innocent correction, she wrote on the paper beside her slate. She did not enter the phrase into the model. Models preferred labels that implied completion.

For the second test, she divided the required transition equally among every district recognized by the current schedule, then added the three settlements as if standing had already been granted. Each received the same quantity of cold acceptance, shade, rain delay, and light reduction. The distribution looked clean enough for a public diagram.

Dael gave it forty seconds.

Eyr breached its wind-shear floor first.

Senn frowned. “Its total allocation remains above minimum.”

“Open the direction.”

She rotated the weather field. Eyr sat near tower 11 where the darkward outflow narrowed against a ridge. A small shade acceptance there cooled air entering the passage. The same quantity over Orison diffused across canals and heat-storing stone. At Eyr, it accelerated a current aimed at the ceramic-vane quarter.

Dael drew two equal circles on the table. One covered Orison’s western terraces. The other covered Eyr’s ridge. “Equal quantities do unequal work.”

Hesh leaned closer. “Can the share arrive as light instead?”

“Not during that turn. Mirror geometry gives Eyr a narrow reflected window. Shift it and the clinic loses morning heat.”

“What about rain delay?”

“The settlement stores little water outside insulated cisterns. Delay costs more there than in a canal city.”

Senn changed the burden’s composition, trading shade for a later high-light pulse. Tower 11’s service deck overheated. She tried cloud water. Ice loading increased on the heat vanes. Each equal exchange met a piece of ground that made equality false.

“Orison can take this quantity,” she said.

“Orison can take four times that quantity in diffuse cloud before any floor approaches,” Dael answered. “Nacre Fold can accept more cold than Eyr because the lava tube buffers temperature, but less wind variance at the western mouth. Caldrift can delay rain during one seed stage and not another. Equal division ignores the machinery and the soil.”

“Population ignores poverty. Equality ignores geography.” Hesh counted the rejected premises on two fingers. “What does your law leave us?”

“Rank,” Senn said. “Which we are not using.”

“Historic benefit,” Dael offered.

That model charged Orison and the six central cities according to the bloom days they had inherited. Its moral clarity lasted until Senn opened the hospital dependencies. Extra bloom had entered operating schedules, nursery cycles, public heat storage, school meals, seed starts, and municipal work shifts. Removing every inherited benefit according to its age cut into protected functions even when the city retained enough aggregate climate.

Rul Fen’s hearing submission had listed the dependencies in careful categories. Senn distrusted his purpose and respected the numbers. Orison’s hospitals warmed sterilization reservoirs during bloom because the light arrived reliably. Staple growers timed fungal controls to a dry interval inside the eleven added days. Public kitchens drew on those harvests. A benefit acquired unlawfully could still become part of someone’s survival floor.

Hesh read the hospital column. “How much of this existed before the transfer?”

“Most of the reservoirs. Not the timing.”

“So inherited convenience entered protected use.”

“In part.”

“Lorn Dusk’s children entered equipment occupancy.” Hesh’s voice stayed level. “Orison’s convenient timing entered medical necessity. Your categories learn tenderness in one direction.”

Senn did not defend them. “Then we separate the floor from everything above it.”

Dael pulled the physical control rail toward him. “We already protect floors.”

“We protect them after selecting a burden base. Reverse it. Calculate each district’s floor first, using actual habitation and local geography. Lock it. What remains can carry correction.”

The room’s ventilation clicked into its low cycle. Clerks beyond the window rolled the hearing docket into a blue case. Hesh read Senn’s note upside down.

“Everything above minimum?” they asked.

“Not simply minimum. Some uses cannot drop at the same time even if each annual total permits it. Hospital reserves, staple seed starts, safe wind bands, radiator cooling. We define the operational floor for the turn, not a yearly average.”

Dael’s damaged fingers rested on the rail. “Then identify the movable remainder by service type. Rain timing, decorative light, commercial bloom, optional school extensions, excess reservoir warmth, storm tolerance above the safe band.”

“Discretionary surplus,” Senn said.

Hesh’s expression tightened. “Discretionary to whom?”

“A fair objection.” Senn removed the label. “Name it yourself.”

They considered the exposed accounts. “The portion a district can surrender without sending another person below floor. But the district must see the calculation and challenge it.”

“That takes longer.”

“So did counting our dead.”

Dael entered a temporary field name: SURPLUS SUBJECT TO LOCAL REVIEW. “The machine needs a shorter column.”

“It can endure inconvenience,” Hesh said.

Senn restored her original label with a review flag attached. The term mattered less than who controlled its boundary. She set the Almanac to calculate floors from measured civic loads rather than official district status. Gray sectors appeared as provisional habitation bands. For the first time in their workroom model, Lorn Dusk, Eyr, and Nacre Fold received locked minima before any surplus allocation began.

The first run failed because Orison’s declared floor included decorative greenhouse walls along three tram routes.

Hesh pointed at the light allotment. “Do patients receive care in the apricot glass?”

“No.”

“Do staple crops grow there?”

“Tourists walk there.”

“Tourists can walk in coats.”

Senn reclassified the walls. Orison’s discretionary margin widened by almost one bloom day.

A second failure came from Eyr. Its apprenticeship kilns appeared as industrial surplus, but Dael objected before the model completed.

“The ceramic vanes keep tower housings intact,” he said. “Close those kilns and the repair stock vanishes in six weeks.”

“Could production slow?” Senn asked.

“By a fifth. More than that breaks the replacement cadence.”

Hesh requested the apprenticeship schedule. Half the kiln time trained workers, yet those workers supplied the only local repair pool permitted near tower 11. They protected the training hours and cut ornamental ceramic production instead.

Nacre Fold offered cold tolerance but little safe wind variance. Caldrift offered rain timing after its current germination window. The foundry district contributed commercial heat storage while retaining domestic walls. Each place required a different translation from surplus into physical weather.

By the sixth run, the model no longer produced equal bars. It produced narrow colored segments, uneven in quantity and unlike in kind. Orison carried a broad green reduction. Caldrift carried a blue rain delay. Nacre Fold accepted a measured temperature dip. Two canal districts gave up low-priority cloud water. Lorn Dusk contributed nothing during the first transition interval because its existing schedule did not reach the climate floor.

Dael advanced the simulation through nine days. No shear crescent formed.

“Twenty,” Senn said.

The model crossed the end of the current bloom turn. Four radiator towers remained under repair assumptions, so the available heat-export margin narrowed. Orison’s reduction increased during the next interval. Hospital reservoirs stayed protected. Staple beds retained their dry treatment window. Tram-wall flowers lost light.

Hesh watched the district bands instead of the central graphs. “Lorn Dusk crosses floor on day three.”

“By a narrow margin,” Dael said.

“Keep going.”

At day twenty-seven, a failed-tower contingency pushed Eyr toward its wind limit. Dael distributed the excess across five districts with compatible geography. Each accepted a change smaller than ordinary forecast tolerance. The combined adjustment held Eyr above floor without recreating a sacrificial sink.

Senn checked the hidden assumptions. Population no longer set the burden. Political rank contributed nothing. Historic benefit affected the schedule only where it had created movable surplus. Local physical constraints shaped the form of each concession. Every climate floor stayed locked.

“What did we smuggle in?” she asked.

Dael opened the maintenance layer. “Perfect telemetry.”

“Remove it.”

He introduced three delayed sensors and one false radiator reading. The distribution controller hesitated, then widened safety margins. Orison lost more discretionary light to preserve the uncertain districts. No floor broke.

Hesh tapped the review flag. “Central staff still decide which use counts as optional.”

“Then district auditors must sign the boundary,” Senn said.

“We do not have district auditors. We do not have districts.”

The hearing docket moved past the window in a clerk’s arms.

Senn looked at the gray labels beneath the provisional bands. “The correction assumes standing before procedure grants it.”

“Good,” Hesh said. “Make them argue against the assumption where everyone can hear.”

They ran the model again with hostile choices. Orison protected commercial bloom as if it served staple crops. A central city understated canal storage. Nacre Fold overclaimed wind vulnerability. Lorn Dusk, given the opportunity, marked its entire reflected-dawn schedule non-discretionary.

Dael audited each claim against physical measurements. Greenhouse species records separated food from display. Canal temperatures exposed stored heat. Wind monitors constrained Nacre Fold’s request. Lorn Dusk’s claim survived because every scheduled dawn remained below the Charter floor once missed windows entered the accounting.

“Fairness needs instruments,” Dael said.

“Instruments need standing,” Hesh replied.

Senn added both requirements to the remedy note. The model could not grant either. It could show what followed if delegates did.

Before they attempted the full run, Dael printed the proposed surplus boundaries on transparent sheets and laid them over the physical weather map. The model had made concessions look divisible. Metal and air still had to perform them. A rain delay in Caldrift changed reservoir pressure at two downstream gates. Less decorative light in Orison cooled tram glass that normally released stored heat after dusk. Nacre Fold’s colder interval reduced demand on one radiator but increased condensation near its clinic intake.

Dael marked every secondary effect with a grease pencil. “A concession that creates an emergency elsewhere does not belong in the surplus column.”

Hesh picked up the Caldrift sheet. “Who checks consequences outside the district offering it?”

“Conservatory engineers,” Senn said.

“Only them?”

She heard the trap before Dael answered. The same institution that had certified an empty service precinct could not retain sole authority over what counted as harmless. “An affected district signs too. If Caldrift moves rain and the downstream canal district receives the change, both review the transfer.”

“That gives a city power to refuse every inconvenience,” Dael said.

“Not if refusal must cite a floor or physical safety limit. Preference alone cannot block a verified share.”

Hesh turned the sheet toward the light. “Then every refusal needs a visible reason. So does every acceptance.”

They tested the rule against Orison’s tram walls. A municipal planner had classified their stored warmth as pedestrian safety because frost sometimes formed on the upper bridges. Dael pulled maintenance records. The bridges already carried embedded heat strips sized for the coldest ordinary winter. Bloom storage reduced operating cost but did not protect the floor.

Senn moved the wall allotment into surplus. “The city may prefer flowers to electrical draw. Preference does not become necessity because accounting favors it.”

“Unless the electrical draw pushes another service down,” Dael said.

They followed the power demand through pumping, hospital reserve, and greenhouse ventilation. Even at the coldest modeled interval, existing capacity held with a narrow margin. The wall light remained movable.

A harder claim came from a school district. Its additional bloom hours supported evening lessons for children whose parents worked central greenhouse shifts. Removing them would not threaten heat or food, yet the loss would fall chiefly on families with the least control over work schedules.

Hesh refused Senn’s first classification. “Not a climate floor. Not disposable either.”

“The remedy cannot preserve every good.”

“No. It can refuse to hide which people lose one.”

Senn separated physical protection from civic priority. The school hours entered discretionary surplus because surrender would not breach habitation. A burden note required Orison’s delegates to defend how they distributed the lost time within the city. The weather model could keep bodies safe. It could not decide whose evening mattered.

Dael drew a box around the note. “That distinction belongs in the hearing. Otherwise they will call every disputed comfort a technical flaw.”

By midafternoon, the paper map carried a web of signatures that did not yet exist: offering district, receiving district, engineer, local auditor. Hesh added one more line beneath each transfer for the people who maintained the relevant walls, canals, kites, or radiators. “Officials promise weather. Crews discover whether the promise has bolts.”

The requirement slowed their model almost to uselessness. It also made every concession legible. No burden could disappear into a planetary total before touching a named place.

Late on the third day, they reached the first full two-turn run. The forecasting room had accumulated cups, ration wrappers, paper objections, and one ceramic bearing sample Dael used to keep district names from sliding off the table. Orison’s public bloom projection glowed through the high window in cheerful green. Outside, people crossed the hall beneath eleven promised days they had built their lives around.

Senn began the run at current conditions. Reserve 6 held the temporary shade line Tovin had bought. The unlawful debit remained absent. Darkward settlements rose toward protected service. Central districts surrendered different pieces of weather according to verified discretionary surplus.

The first turn ended colder across Orison. Hospital and staple-crop floors held. Three public gardens missed flowering. Canal cafés lost four warm evenings. Schools shortened two light-extended recreation periods while retaining meal growth and classroom heat. The shear field stayed below warning.

During the second turn, four radiator repairs restored export capacity in stages. Orison’s remaining added bloom contracted day by day. Eyr’s clinic kept its morning window. Nacre Fold accepted one colder storage interval inside the lava tube. Caldrift shifted discretionary rain after seed establishment. No district crossed its floor.

The final green segment disappeared from Orison’s account.

“Count it,” Hesh said.

Senn counted the retired days against the original benefit. “Eleven.”

“Duration?”

“Two turns.”

Dael checked the shear plot, then the maintenance tolerances. “Physically survivable under the repair schedule. Uncomfortable. Sensitive to bad data. Not innocent.”

Hesh took the stylus. Beside the result, they wrote: NO FLOOR MAY ENTER THE TRADE.

Senn sealed the model for hearing disclosure. On the table, Orison’s bloom band ended eleven days earlier than the schedule glowing beyond the window. Every other district still held a visible floor beneath it.

Chapter 16 — The Hearing of Weather

Oren Pell tested the speaking lamps by asking each one to recognize a district that did not officially exist.

“Lorn Dusk,” he said into the first bronze grille.

NO REGISTERED DELEGATION appeared on the chamber band.

“Eyr.”

The display answered: NO REGISTERED DELEGATION.

“Nacre Fold.”

For the third name, the band returned NO REGISTERED DELEGATION.

Pell walked the inner curve of the delegates’ chamber, tried the remaining lamps, and received the same answer eight times. He was seventy and moved with the economy of someone accustomed to canals, councils, and mechanisms that failed by repeating their instructions. At the ninth lamp he turned toward the public benches.

“The recording system works,” he said. “Its answer does not settle the question before us.”

A clerk entered that sentence by hand.

Senn sat at the technical table with Dael on one side and three sealed model cases on the other. Hesh waited two rows behind them in the evidence section, not among the delegates. Their address ledger rested across their knees. The chamber’s circular floor resembled the Season Table stripped of weather light. Forty-one desks faced inward around a shallow well. Above them, pressureworks pipes crossed beneath a glass roof filmed against Varda’s glare.

Ceryn Ahl stood when the opening chime ended. Her gray formal coat carried no Conservatory insignia except a narrow copper thread at the cuff. She had declined the First Curator’s elevated desk and taken a witness station level with the others.

“Chair Pell,” she began, “the Conservatory renews its request that the initial proceeding address technical continuity. Habitation status raises Charter questions beyond climate administration. The current instability requires a bounded review of physical capacity before broader claims enter the schedule.”

Pell adjusted the paper docket rather than looking at her. “Your request classifies the cause as broader and the consequence as technical.”

“It separates competencies.”

“It separates the people who received weather from the people whose weather funded it.”

A murmur moved through the upper gallery. Pell waited until it stopped without striking the chime.

Ceryn kept her hands flat on the witness rail. “If every constitutional claim changes live climate orders before engineering review, Meral will not retain a stable schedule long enough to decide the claim.”

“Agreed,” Pell said. “No order changes by the opening of this hearing. Your proposed classification remains denied.”

The clerk wrote again. Senn watched Ceryn accept the ruling with one measured breath. She did not object twice to decisions she expected to challenge later through detail.

Pell faced the ring. “This hearing concerns habitation, inherited emergency authority, physical remedy, and the policy categories supplied to the Almanac. Technical evidence will be heard as evidence. It will not define the boundary of what the delegates may consider.”

At the central-cities table, Rul Fen rearranged five folders into a straight line. He wore a grower’s green pin beside his counsel seal, a choice calculated to place soil inside procedure. His clients occupied seven desks around the ring, including Orison’s. None looked pleased by Pell’s ruling.

Fen rose. “Before evidence, a question of notice.”

“State it.”

“The submission published thirty-one hours ago includes a remedy model that assigns prospective burden to registered districts. My clients received no opportunity to verify the underlying discretionary calculations.”

Senn opened her copy of the filing. “The model is illustrative, not operative.”

Fen turned toward her. “Auditor Vale, you have not been recognized.”

Pell’s mouth tightened by less than a smile. “Counsel, you asked the room a factual question in the direction of the technical table.”

“I asked the chair.”

“The chair heard an answer. Continue.”

Fen’s folders remained perfectly aligned. “Illustrative burdens can move markets, alter municipal demand, and induce districts to refuse scheduled transfers. We experienced all three after the attribution release. Calling a public model non-operative does not make it harmless.”

Senn thought of reserve 6 holding a shear front while Orison spent its cold afternoon. Fen had earned that point.

Pell ordered the remedy cases held sealed until the dependency record had been heard. Then he recognized Fen for the central cities’ preliminary evidence.

A clerk rolled a projection screen into the well. Fen did not begin with flowers or cafés. He displayed hospital sterilization reservoirs across Orison, Vey Canal, Senra, and four smaller central districts. Each line showed the heat accumulated during inherited bloom intervals and the services scheduled around that accumulation.

“Would the chair prefer quantities by city or service?” Fen asked.

“Service first. Cities after.”

Fen called a municipal systems analyst to authenticate the records. She established that eleven hospitals used bloom-timed reservoir warming, though only four depended on it for more than operating economy. Six staple-crop cooperatives scheduled fungal treatment during dry bloom extensions. Twenty-three schools aligned meal-greenhouse cycles with the added light. Canal pumps, tram glass, hatcheries, public kitchens, and seed banks had incorporated the pattern at different levels.

Senn marked each distinction in the margin: dependence, convenience, historical adaptation, protected floor. Fen’s strategy required the chamber to hear them as one inherited body.

“Can those systems be changed?” he asked the analyst.

“Yes.”

“Without interruption?”

“No.”

“Without expense?”

“No.”

“Without any increased risk?”

“No climate schedule changes without increased risk somewhere.”

Fen let that answer remain.

By midday, the hearing had acquired numbers but no direction. Rul Fen proved that inherited bloom reached farther than decorative terraces. He traced hospital timing into staff shifts, crop windows into public kitchens, and school greenhouses into meal contracts. Orison had not merely enjoyed stolen warmth. It had built civic systems around reliable receipt.

Hesh sat without writing. The address ledger stayed closed beneath their hands.

During a document change, Senn leaned back. “He is making dependence look like authority.”

Dael kept his eyes on a tower-demand graph. “He is proving dependence. Do not make his evidence weaker because you dislike where he carries it.”

“I know.”

“Your pen disagrees.”

She looked down. Three hard strokes cut beneath the word inherited. Senn turned the page.

Fen called an Orison hospital engineer. The woman described reservoir retrofits made fourteen years earlier, after bloom extensions had become predictable enough to justify removing a backup heater. Reinstalling equivalent capacity would take one turn if parts arrived, longer if the radiator repair program competed for alloys.

“Did your hospital know the bloom interval came from a winter debit?” Fen asked.

“No.”

“Did you know any inhabited settlement carried additional cold?”

“No.”

“Would you have approved the retrofit if you had known?”

The engineer looked toward Ceryn, then Senn, then the public gallery. “I would have asked whether the transfer had consent. I cannot tell you what the board would have done.”

Fen thanked her without trying to improve the answer.

Ceryn questioned the witness next. “If the interval ended tomorrow, could the hospital protect sterilization capacity?”

“For nine days, using reserve electrical heat and postponed non-urgent procedures.”

“After nine?”

“We would require supplemental power or restored bloom.”

“Could the city supply that power without changing another weather service?”

“Not at current demand.”

Ceryn sat. She had placed the danger into the record without defending the source.

Pell invited questions from delegates. A canal district representative asked how many listed systems would actually cross a climate floor if the inherited bloom ended over the staged interval Senn had filed. Fen objected that the remedy remained sealed. Pell allowed a question about dependency but not the unpublished allocation.
