The municipal analyst returned to the lamp. Four hospitals required replacement heat or schedule changes. Seven could absorb the loss through existing systems at increased cost. Of the twenty-three schools, five would lose meal-growing capacity unless power shifted from other municipal uses. The rest would lose evening hours, ornamental beds, or predictable timing. Two staple cooperatives faced serious crop risk during the first turn. Four could adjust planting if notified before germination.

Fen asked her to describe the confidence bounds. She opened pale uncertainty bands around every line. Parts supply, radiator repairs, and municipal response widened them. No witness could promise an exact transition without knowing which districts would offer surplus weather.

“Then fourteen of the systems you named may adapt without crossing a protected floor?” the canal delegate asked.

“More than fourteen may adapt,” the analyst said. “Adaptation still has cost.”

“Cost and floor are different categories.”

“Yes.”

Fen stepped back to the grille. “Counsel requests that the phrase may adapt not be condensed into can surrender. One describes technical possibility. The other assigns obligation.”

Pell directed the clerk to preserve both terms.

Senn understood what Fen had accomplished. He had established the breadth of dependence, then protected every uncertain edge from becoming a concession. Her remedy model did the reverse. It locked floors and exposed uncertainty above them as a place where districts might carry burden. Neither procedure could eliminate judgment. They differed over who received protection while judgment continued.

An Orison delegate requested a live map of the eleven added days. Fen projected the first interval as a green band across civic services. Hospital reservoirs occupied dark green blocks. Staple crops carried a second shade. School food production appeared in narrow stripes. Nearly half the band remained pale: commercial bloom, public-garden timing, heat-storage economy, recreation light, and schedule preferences whose loss would inconvenience the city without breaching habitation.

Hesh opened the address ledger for the first time that day. They did not raise it or interrupt. Senn could see rows of handwritten dates from the technical table. Forty-six additional winter days could not fit into the pale portion of Fen’s picture. The diagram showed what Orison had learned to depend on, not what Lorn Dusk had been forced to survive without.

Ceryn requested one correction. “The pale services still contribute indirectly to municipal stability. Commercial bloom employs greenhouse workers. Recreation light affects school attendance. Heat-storage economy preserves electrical reserve.”

“Are they protected floors?” Pell asked.

“Not all.”

“Then let the record retain their indirect value and their legal distinction.”

Fen accepted the wording. His clients whispered among their desks. The hearing had not ordered Orison to surrender a single hour, yet naming the pale band had changed it from inheritance into an arguable claim.

Next came a seed-bank custodian from Senra. Her facility used added bloom for drying rooms whose backup fans had been decommissioned eight years earlier. She brought a cracked fan bearing and set it on the evidence stand.

“We were told the light interval belonged to the normal planetary plan,” she said. “Replacing a working schedule with machines looked wasteful.”

Fen asked what ending the interval would do.

“Without replacement fans, moisture loss slows. Some seed lots spoil.”

“Food security?”

“For three districts, eventually.”

Under questions from Pell, she conceded that new fans could arrive within fifty days and installation would take six. The seed bank had enough dry storage to bridge one turn if other districts accepted temporary lots. No catastrophe followed automatically. Work, expense, and cooperation did.

Senn wrote those three words beneath her model notes. The correction could not promise painless transfer. It had to distinguish pain from a breached floor and require institutions to do the intermediate work they had avoided while hidden winter remained cheap.

Before releasing the witness, Pell asked whether the custodian would have maintained the fans if the source of the added bloom had appeared on her operating ledger.

The woman touched the cracked bearing. “I would have known the light could end.”

Pell called a recess for ventilation adjustment. The chamber doors opened, admitting cooler air and the smell of wet coats from the public corridor. Delegates left their desks in clusters. Hesh remained in the evidence row.

Senn carried them a cup from the clerk’s urn. “No guest coat, but you will accept bad tea?”

“Bad tea does not claim jurisdiction over my body.” Hesh drank and winced. “It may be trying.”

Fen approached before Senn could answer. Up close, his grower’s pin showed scratches along one leaf.

“Keeper Dara,” he said.

“Hesh will do. Keeper has no meaning to your register.”

“It has meaning to me.”

“Privately.”

Fen accepted the correction. “I intend to challenge your seat petition. Not your evidence.”

“Those are not separable from where I am sitting.”

“My clients received notice from a filing made by an auditor whose office remains inside the respondent institution. If unregistered claimants take district seats before authentication, every service precinct and temporary camp can acquire allocation rights by assertion.”

Hesh rested one palm on the ledger. “Then authenticate us.”

“That will happen.”

“When?”

“Under the docket, after emergency authority.”

“You will ask whether the Conservatory may keep using the order before you decide whether anyone lived under it.”

Fen looked toward Pell’s empty chair. “I did not write the docket.”

“You intend to use it.”

The return chime ended the exchange.

When the chamber settled, Pell took the address ledger petition first.

Fen rose at once. “Procedural objection. The published sequence places authentication after the dependency record and archival authority.”

“I changed the sequence,” Pell said.

“On what motion?”

“Chair’s authority to resolve participation before extended testimony.”

Fen opened his rule book. Pell waited. The counsel found the clause, read it twice, and closed the cover.

“No objection to authority,” Fen said. “Objection to the requested status.”

A hearing clerk placed three seating markers in the well: DELEGATE, DISTRICT WITNESS, TECHNICAL WITNESS. Hesh came forward carrying the ledger. Senn watched their fingers tighten beneath its leather spine when the speaking lamp failed to recognize their name.

Pell addressed them directly. “State the basis of your request.”

“Lorn Dusk maintains continuous civic heat, an address ledger, local birth records, and burials. Those facts meet the Charter habitation test. I was selected keeper by residents entered in this book. I request a district witness seat.”

Fen asked permission to question.

“Limited to participation,” Pell said.

Counsel faced Hesh across the well. “Has Lorn Dusk received formal recognition as an inhabited district?”

“No.”

“Has it been assigned a delegate?”

“No.”

“Does any current registry identify your selection process as a district election?”

“No current central registry records our elections.”

“Then what authority certifies that you speak for the population claimed in that ledger?”

“The people recorded there.”

Fen’s tone remained courteous. “That answer may establish community trust. The question concerns planetary procedure.”

“Our procedure includes public nomination in the address hall, seven days for objection, and a counted assembly.”

“Certified by whom?”

“By three ledger witnesses and the prior keeper.”

“Each resident of Lorn Dusk?”

“Yes.”

“Each person therefore lacks registered district status.”

Hesh looked around the circular desks. “That appears to be why we came.”

The public benches stirred again.

Fen turned to Pell. “The petition depends on certifications issued by persons whose standing has not been established. Until authentication, Hesh Dara may testify as an individual witness. A district seat would presume the conclusion.”

Senn felt the mechanism close. Lorn Dusk could not certify Hesh because Lorn Dusk lacked standing. It could not establish standing through its chosen keeper because the keeper lacked certification from a recognized district. The circle had no false statement inside it. Procedure produced exclusion by requiring its own result as an entry condition.

Pell asked Hesh, “Do you claim delegate voting authority today?”

“No. I claim the right to speak from the district position when others describe what can be taken from us.”

Fen objected to the word district.

“Noted,” Pell said.

Ceryn requested recognition. “The Conservatory can authenticate the instrument loads attached to Lorn Dusk, including continuous domestic heat, school cycles, medical reserve, and local burial refrigeration.”

Hesh turned toward her. “You can authenticate our machines.”

“I can authenticate records under my custody.”

“You had our records for years.”

“Yes.” Ceryn did not lower her eyes. “The loads support habitation.”

A central delegate asked whether that concession resolved standing.

“No,” Fen said. “Physical occupancy and lawful district formation require separate findings.”

Senn opened the Charter on her slate. The habitation test contained no formation ritual. Continuous civic heat, address ledger, births or burials. Order 9 had displaced those facts into an equipment category, but it had not amended the Charter.

Pell read the same passage on paper. “Counsel Fen, identify the clause requiring prior district formation.”

Fen consulted an aide. “The allocation rules assume a registered district authority.”

“Assumption does not add words to the Charter.”

“It governs implementation.”

“We are determining who may be heard, not yet assigning a weather share.”

Fen shifted to notice. Granting a district witness seat could imply recognition before the three settlements’ records received full authentication. Other delegates pressed him from both directions. One feared false claims from transient camps. Another asked how a claimant could ever authenticate communal records if no representative could present them. An Eyr maintenance apprentice shouted from the gallery and was removed after refusing to sit.

Through it all, Hesh stood beside the unused marker.

Senn wanted to speak, but her evidence belonged to a later docket section. She wrote Pell a narrow citation instead: CHARTER TEST DEFINES HABITATION BY FACTS, NOT REGISTRY ENTRY. The clerk carried it to the chair with two other notes.

Pell called ten minutes of silent review. No one left. Ventilation pushed cool air through the chamber, lifting the corners of Fen’s exhibits. Ceryn read the Charter without moving her lips. Dael watched Hesh. Senn watched the empty district marker at their feet.

At last Pell returned the notes to the clerk.

“The petition asks for a district witness seat. Counsel correctly observes that granting full district status would anticipate a finding. The chair also finds that denying any representative capacity because the claimant lacks the status under dispute would make the hearing incapable of receiving the claim.”

Fen rose. “Clarification on scope.”

“You will have it.”

Pell turned to Hesh. “The chair grants provisional witness standing for purposes of presenting records, questioning evidence that assigns burden to Lorn Dusk, and responding from the designated district position. This grant does not confer a delegate vote, weather-share authority, or final recognition under the Charter.”

The clerk reached for the district marker, hesitated, then placed it before Hesh with a gray provisional strip across its base.

“May the record call us Lorn Dusk?” Hesh asked.

Pell looked up at the chamber band, which still displayed NO REGISTERED DELEGATION.

“Yes,” he said. “Enter the name by hand.”

Chapter 17 — The Expired Emergency

Pera warmed the first page of Order 9 between two panes of archival glass until the blue ink darkened enough to testify.

A magnifying camera hung above her hands. Its image filled the chamber screen: paper fibers, brown handling marks, the embossed emergency seal, and a faint thumbprint from someone who had carried the order thirty-one years earlier. She kept the heat plate at sixteen degrees. One more degree would improve the signature and accelerate the paper’s decay.

“State what you are doing,” Oren Pell said.

“I am restoring legibility temporarily through controlled warming. Thermal ink migrates toward the visible range without adding pigment. The process changes contrast, not content.”

Rul Fen rose from the central-cities table. “How can the witness establish that distinction?”

Pera looked at him over the glass. “Because adding pigment requires pigment.”

A few people in the upper gallery laughed. Fear sharpened her humor before it improved her judgment.

Fen waited.

“I also recorded the sheet under cold, ambient, and warmed conditions,” she said. “Fiber alignment and impression depth remain constant. The order has an unbroken archive custody record from Year Forty-four After Choir except for six hours during a pressure leak in Year Fifty-two, when the sealed case moved to the upper vault. Both custodians signed the transfer.”

“Better,” Fen said.

Pera disliked him less for making her correct herself and more for sounding pleased about it.

The speaking lamp lit beneath her. She read the visible title, date, and authority line into the record. ORDER 9: TEMPORARY THERMAL SERVICE PRECINCTS, DARKWARD RADIATOR CONSTRUCTION. The originating council had acted during a genuine construction emergency. Tower crews needed rapid weather allocation around camps not yet intended for permanent settlement. The order classified occupancy as equipment service and suspended ordinary climate-floor treatment.

“For how long?” Pell asked.

Pera moved the glass to the duration clause. “Five years from entry, unless superseded earlier by habitation review.”

“Does it authorize renewal?”

“Yes. One-year renewals after inspection of camp occupancy and radiator need.”

Fen approached the witness rail with permission. “You said habitation review. Read the exact printed language.”

Pera adjusted the camera. Blue letters deepened on the screen.

“‘At the end of the initial term, review required before any continued exemption from Charter climate floors.’”

“Is the phrase habitation review present?”

“No. That describes the clause’s function.”

“Then confine authentication to language on the page.”

Pera placed both palms beside the glass. “The page also requires occupancy inspection.”

“On the next line.”

“Yes.”

“Read it.”

“‘Inspecting authority shall certify absence of permanent domestic heat, local birth or burial record, and continuous non-service residence.’”

Fen returned to his table. The correction had helped the claim he meant to resist. He wanted the archive exact because any imprecision offered a way to discount it later.

Ceryn Ahl sat at the respondent station with no counsel. When Pell asked whether she challenged the originating order’s authenticity, she said no.

“Do you challenge the five-year term?”

“No.”

“Do you challenge the review condition?”

“No.”

Her answers removed whole branches of hostile questioning. Pera felt no relief. The disputed years remained inside the renewal chain stacked to her right.

She began with the first renewal. The paper matched the original archive stock. A different thermal batch gave the ink a greener cast under heat. Two inspectors certified continued construction activity. Neither completed the domestic-heat field. A handwritten note mentioned six family quarters near tower 14 but classified them as dependents of essential workers.

The second renewal contained twelve such quarters. The third omitted the count and attached a radiator-load summary. By the fourth, local school-cycle demand appeared in the instrument annex while the certification still described temporary camp services.

Before Pera could move on, Fen asked to inspect the fasteners. A clerk carried the second and third packets to a side table beneath a shielded lamp. Pera followed with the camera. Removing an archival clasp required a record of pressure marks, corrosion, thread direction, and every hole the metal had made. The hearing waited while she measured them.

“Why does this matter?” a delegate asked.

Fen answered before Pera could. “Because a later annex inserted into an older packet cannot prove what the original signer received.”

That was correct. Pera enlarged the second packet’s fold. The blue inspection sheet and family-quarter page shared two pinholes with the same oval deformation toward the lower edge. Copper oxide had migrated through both in one continuous stain. A third hole came from a later archive binding and did not touch the original clasp line.

“These pages were fastened together before the ink finished curing,” Pera said. “A faint transfer from the family-quarter heading appears on the back of the inspection sheet. They cannot establish the day of assembly more closely than the ink batch permits, but the evidence supports an original packet.”

Fen examined the enlargement. “Supports, not proves.”

“Paper rarely obeys courtroom verbs.”

Pell looked over his glasses.

Pera corrected herself. “The probability of later insertion is low given matching thermal batch, edge wear, clasp oxidation, and wet-ink transfer.”

For the third renewal, certainty improved. Its annex numbers had been stamped before text entry, and the occupancy page carried the same clerk’s correction mark as the signature sheet. Pera showed the original numeral beneath the correction: eight family quarters changed to twenty-one. Someone had drawn one line through the smaller count, entered the larger figure, and initialed it.

Hesh requested magnification. “Does that page name Lorn Dusk?”

“No. It names Service Cluster Fourteen.”

“Does the address ledger use that cluster number?”

“On its earliest cover.”

Fen objected. “The Lorn Dusk ledger has not yet been authenticated into this section.”

Pell sustained the objection but allowed Hesh to reserve the comparison. Pera marked the exhibit tab with a small gray flag. The archive had spent decades separating records that described the same households. Procedure now required them to rebuild each connection in the correct order.

A delegate from the northern reservoirs asked why no clerk had rejected the packet when the family count increased. Pera opened the contemporary filing manual. Emergency renewals received three checks: authorized signature, active order number, and complete page sequence. Clerks did not reassess factual findings unless a required field remained empty.

“Domestic occupancy remained empty,” the delegate said.

“The field contained an inheritance mark.”

Pera projected it. Two short strokes crossed the box, the old symbol for prior value continues. The prior box had also used an inheritance mark. She traced the chain back to the original Order 9 assumption that construction camps contained no permanent residents.

“How many times can a value inherit itself?” Pell asked.

“The manual sets no limit.”

“So an assumption can persist without being restated?”

“Yes. Each clerk sees a completed field.”

Ceryn requested recognition. “That practice applied across emergency administration, not only these precincts. Inheritance marks prevented service collapse when inspections arrived late.”

“Did the practice distinguish a late inspection from no inspection?” Pell asked.

“Not in the field itself. The supporting docket should have done so.”

Pera opened the docket numbers. The first renewal linked to six inspection tasks. Four closed. Domestic heat and permanent residence remained pending. When the second renewal arrived, its system copied the pending tasks forward but assigned them a low priority because the renewal packet already carried an authorized signature. By the third, reform clerks migrated only the completed summary, not the pending sub-tasks.

Fen studied the projection. “Are you testifying that the pending tasks vanished?”

“From the active queue. Their original entries remain in the paper docket.”

“Through deliberate deletion?”

“No deletion mark appears. The migration rule excluded subordinate tasks attached to an approved order.”

The distinction mattered to Pera. No clerk needed to burn a page. A rule about what deserved migration had done quieter work.

Pell directed the original task sheets into evidence. Their corners carried no completion punches. One bore a note from a junior inspector requesting transport to tower 14. The request had been denied during a wind closure and never rescheduled.

“Would that inspection have found permanent habitation?” Hesh asked.

Fen objected to speculation.

Pell sustained the objection.

Hesh changed the question. “Did anyone complete the required visit?”

“Not according to this docket,” Pera answered.

The chamber screen still showed the empty punch boxes when Pell returned her to the renewal sequence.

Pell stopped her after the fourth packet. “Does each renewal contain the inspection required by Order 9?”

“Each contains a sheet titled occupancy inspection.”

“That does not answer the question.”

Pera swallowed. “No. The fields required to prove absence of permanent habitation were incomplete after the first term.”

A darkward voice called from the gallery, “Because we were there.”

Pell warned the gallery once. Hesh, seated at the provisional district position, did not turn around.

Fen requested the first inspection form. “Are incomplete forms automatically invalid under archive rules?”

“No.”

“Can omitted fields inherit prior certified values?”

“In routine administrative records, yes.”

“Did the first sheet certify temporary occupancy?”

“It certified continued construction need. The domestic fields remained blank.”

“Did an authorized officer sign it?”

“Yes.”

“Then an administrator reading the chain could reasonably understand temporary status to continue.”

“Only if they ignored the attached family quarters.”

Fen’s gaze narrowed. “Authentication, Clerk Iven.”

Pera looked to Pell.

“The witness may explain what the authenticated packet contains,” Pell said. “She may not characterize what a reasonable administrator concludes.”

“The packet contains a family-quarter count,” Pera said. “Later packets contain school load, medical reserve, address-heating totals, and burial refrigeration.”

Each item entered the record separately. Hesh used their questioning right to ask whether those annexes had arrived with the renewals or been attached later.

“Original fasteners,” Pera said. “Matching impression stains. They traveled together.”

“Could a signer reach the signature page without passing them?”

The question moved beyond authentication, but Pell allowed a physical answer.

“The signature page sits at the back. The annexes are numbered. A person could skip them.”

“Could they be absent without the page count showing a gap?”

“No.”

Hesh thanked her and sat.

The fifth renewal required a new heat setting. Its signature sheet had spent years misfiled in a crop-emergency annex, compressed against a copper report clasp that left green residue along one edge. Pera replaced the warmed glass with a neutral support and displayed the sheet cold first.

Ceryn’s signature remained visible without enhancement.

No one in the chamber needed Pera to name it. The screen carried CERYN AHL in a younger, harder hand beneath the Year Fifty-eight crop-emergency seal.

Pell asked Ceryn whether she authenticated her own signature.

“I do.”

“Did you hold signing authority?”

“I did.”

“Did the packet contain the note regarding permanent households?”

“It did.”

Fen stood. “Chair, the witness’s private knowledge of the signing packet may belong to later examination. The current witness authenticates documents.”

Pell sustained the objection. Ceryn folded her hands again.

Pera read the renewal aloud. Fungal loss had reduced central grain projections. The council authorized maximum bloom continuity, emergency cloud suppression, and extended heat export. The darkward precinct classification continued for one year, pending post-emergency review.

“Date of crop emergency closure?” Pell asked.

“Year Sixty.”

“Was a post-emergency review filed?”

Pera had searched every index that might disguise it. Habitat formation. Service closeout. Radiator workforce. District conversion. Emergency sunset. She had found meeting notices, deferred agendas, and one memorandum transferring unfinished reviews into the new administrative system.

“No completed review appears in the Conservatory archive.”

Fen approached with a thin gray volume. “Does absence from one archive prove no review occurred?”

“No.”

“Could the planetary council archive hold it?”

“We searched that index.”

“Could a district archive hold it?”

“The precincts had no recognized district archive.”

“Could the construction authority have retained it?”

“That authority dissolved in Year Sixty-one. Its permanent records transferred to us.”

“Could a paper have been lost?”

“Yes.”

“Then you cannot prove that no one conducted a review.”

“I can prove the renewal chain does not cite one, the inherited field contains no review identifier, the review docket remained open when migrated, and every repository designated by the order lacks a completed record.”

Fen rested one hand on the gray book. “But not that no review ever occurred.”

“No.” Pera hated the smallness of the answer. “Archives prove what institutions preserved. Sometimes that is also what they chose to forget.”

“Characterization.”

“Sustained,” Pell said.

Ceryn requested permission to ask one question. “Clerk Iven, if a review had lawfully continued the exemption, what identifying element would the renewal system require?”

“A review seal number linked to the Charter findings.”

“Does any later renewal contain one?”

“No.”

Ceryn sat without looking at Fen.

Pera understood the gift and its boundary. Ceryn would not pretend a legal review existed. She had not yet conceded what its absence did to authority.

The chain after Year Sixty arrived on thinner paper. Administrative reform had replaced yearly signature packets with inherited fields. Pera displayed the migration form that converted active emergency directives into machine-readable categories. Each row included originating authority, status, sunset date, review state, and continuation rule.

Order 9’s row showed the correct origin. It showed the five-year term. It showed the Year Fifty-eight renewal. In the review field, the original clerk had entered REVIEW REQUIRED.

“What appeared in the continuation rule?” Pell asked.

“Continue until superseded.”

“Were those phrases legally equivalent?”

Pera looked toward Fen before answering. “No archival standard treats them as equivalent.”

“Do not anticipate my question,” Fen said.

“You had the look.”

The gallery laughed again. Pell covered the speaking lamp with one hand until quiet returned.

Fen opened the migration manual. “The reform occurred during a backlog of two thousand one hundred six emergency and maintenance orders, correct?”

“Yes.”

“Clerks were instructed to preserve active service where interruption might create risk.”

“Yes.”

“Continuation fields did not renew legal authority. They preserved system behavior pending human disposition.”

“That is what the manual says.”

“Then automated inheritance did not claim to conduct a review.”

“No.”

“It did not erase the originating date.”

“No.”

“It did not conceal the phrase review required.”

“Not in the migration record.”

Fen turned toward the delegates. “The record therefore shows administrative continuity, not forged renewal. Any failure lies in later disposition, which must be considered alongside the continuing physical emergency.”

Pell stopped him. “Argument follows testimony.”

Fen returned to Pera. “Did the system generate new signatures?”

“No.”

“New inspection certificates?”

“No.”

“New declarations that the precincts lacked permanent households?”

“No.”

“Nothing further.”

Hesh rose at their gray-striped marker. “Did the system continue assigning our domestic heat as equipment load?”

“Yes.”

“Did it continue withholding the climate floor?”

“Yes.”

“Did a person review those outcomes each year?”

“Engineers reviewed thermal quantities. Schedule officers reviewed risk. I found no annual review of habitation status.”

“Then what did people inherit?”

Fen objected to breadth.

Pell narrowed the question. “What fields passed forward without new authorization?”

Pera opened the system history. “Equipment-only occupancy. Climate-floor exemption. Winter-debit eligibility. Continue until superseded.”

Hesh placed one finger on their ledger. “For how long?”

Pera had calculated the interval by year, month, and filing day. The answer sounded less precise when converted into the language everyone used.

“The original five-year authority expired twenty-six years before this hearing.”

Silence held long enough for the ventilation cycle to change.

Fen rose slowly. “Assumes the first renewal lacked effect.”

“No,” Pera said. “I gave the expiration of the original authority. Later renewals require their own validity. The last signed renewal ended sixteen years ago.”

Pell instructed the clerk to enter both durations.

A delegate from a high-rain district asked whether Ceryn’s Year Fifty-eight signature could sustain emergency authority while crop effects persisted.

Pera answered from the order. “For one year. The renewal names one year.”

“Could general emergency powers continue it?”

“Not through this document.”

Ceryn activated her lamp. “The crop emergency orders contained broad scheduling authority.”

Pell recognized her.

“They authorized transfers necessary to prevent famine,” Ceryn said. “The fungal loss ended as a declared emergency in Year Sixty, but staple recovery remained fragile for three turns. I continued the central bloom schedule under ordinary Conservatory authority after that point.”

“Did ordinary authority permit withholding an inhabited district’s climate floor?” Pell asked.

“No.”

“Did you consider the precincts inhabited?”

Fen objected that Ceryn had not been called for substantive examination.

Pell sustained it and scheduled the question for the next respondent section. The answer still occupied the chamber. Ceryn had conceded the boundary around any defense she might make.

Pera authenticated the last digital extract. It showed twenty-six annual schedule cycles in which the equipment-only category passed from one planning turn to the next. Human officers approved quantities around it. No officer opened the habitation field. Automated inheritance supplied the premise; people repeatedly used the result.

Fen’s final cross-examination focused on Pera herself.

“You are a junior archival clerk?”

“Yes.”

“You assisted Auditor Vale before the hearing petition?”

“Yes.”

“You preserved an annex after the First Curator requested classification?”

“I preserved the complete filing under protected audit procedure.”

“Against your superior’s instruction.”

“Against a request to condense it. The preservation rule outranked the request.”

“Do you consider yourself neutral?”

Pera looked at the warmed pages lined across the evidence table. Neutral paper would have remained unreadable. Neutral indexing had kept a signature inside the wrong annex for years.

“I consider myself accountable for whether these are the documents they claim to be.”

“That avoids the question.”

“Yes.”

Fen waited for more. She gave him none.

Pell reviewed the admitted record aloud. Order 9 had created a five-year exemption. Renewals required inspection. The fifth signed renewal carried a one-year term and a pending review. No completed review identifier appeared. Administrative migration preserved service while marking review required. Automated continuation supplied no new legal act.

“The chair makes no final finding on remedy,” he said. “The physical consequences remain contested. The authenticity of the admitted chain is established for purposes of this proceeding.”

A delegate asked whether the hearing still concerned the Conservatory’s discretion to adjust an inherited schedule.

Pell turned to the Charter volume beside his docket. “An agency may exercise discretion within authority. The admitted record presents a prior question: whether any current authority permits the exemption at all.”

He directed the clerk to change the docket heading. The old line read DISCRETIONARY REVIEW OF WEATHER ALLOCATION 9.7. The clerk lifted the paper strip, exposing clean brass beneath it, and fitted a new one into the holder.

CHARTER VIOLATION: HABITATION FLOOR WITHHELD UNDER EXPIRED EMERGENCY CLASSIFICATION.

Pera lowered the heat plate. Across the evidence table, Order 9’s blue letters began fading toward brown again. The new heading remained black.

Chapter 18 — Winter Arrives Early

Hesh heard radiator 14 lose its bearing through the soles of their feet.

The first vibration passed under the address hall as a low uneven shove. Cups shifted on the communal stove. Chalk dust fell from the wall of missed dawns. A second pulse arrived half a breath later, rougher and shorter, followed by a silence no radiator settlement trusted.

Nima Sol looked up from the hearing transcript. “Fourteen?”

“West bearing.”

The emergency lamp turned amber before Hesh reached the wall panel. Tower telemetry showed a falling coolant rate and a rising housing temperature. Outside, the black sky carried a faint auroral seam. No reflected dawn had been scheduled for another six hours.

A municipal demand notice flashed across the lower panel: CENTRAL TRANSFER REFUSALS ALTER DARKWARD LOAD. EXPECTED WINTER PULSE ARRIVAL: 19 MINUTES.

The hearing had made weather visible. Visibility had made districts afraid. Three central municipalities had refused routine heat transfers while their councils waited to learn whether accepting them implied liability for past harm. Their caution moved into the atmosphere as cold.

Hesh pressed the local alert. “Radiator fault, tower 14. Early winter pulse from starward. Heat-wall keepers to stations. Clinic receives first reserve. Schools hold children indoors.”

Nima gathered her case bag. “Expected depth?”

“Unknown until the tower reports honestly.”

“That would be a refreshing development.”

She left for the clinic at a run.

Hesh opened the address ledger to the emergency pages. The paper form required time, temperature, affected wall segments, and names of duty keepers. It also required a district authorization number. Lorn Dusk had acquired provisional voice in Orison but still lacked the number needed by its own emergency form.

They crossed out the blank and wrote LORN DUSK.

A third vibration shook the lamp housings. This one ended in a metallic scrape carried through the heat wall. The tower had not stopped. Its damaged bearing had shifted load onto the paired assembly, forcing the radiator vanes to turn against uneven resistance. If crews slowed the tower, Lorn Dusk lost waste heat. If they kept it running, the housing could seize and throw fragments across the service quarter.

Hesh called tower control.

A young mechanic answered over a roar of pumps. “West bearing cage split. We can isolate, but coolant return falls by thirty percent.”

“People clear of the housing?”

“Service crew pulling now. Two still above the valve deck.”

“Names.”

He gave them. Hesh wrote Sora Pellin and Mar Vey beneath the fault time.

“What do you need?”

“Remote load reduction from the Choir. Four degrees vane closure. Replacement ceramic cage. Warm bypass if the pulse hits before isolation.”

Lorn Dusk stocked ceramic segments, but not a full cage. Eyr made them. The nearest warm bypass belonged to the Conservatory network that had treated the settlement as equipment.

“Begin isolation,” Hesh said. “I will get the load reduction.”

The mechanic hesitated. “Under whose authority?”

“Mine until someone warmer objects.”

Hesh sent the request through the public hearing channel, attaching Pell’s provisional witness order. The network rejected it within three seconds.

REQUESTER LACKS DISTRICT WEATHER AUTHORITY.

They sent it again as radiator infrastructure maintenance. Approval moved into a central queue behind seven municipal transfer disputes.

The floor chilled beneath their boots.

Residents began arriving at the address hall for instructions. Greenhouse workers wore insulated aprons over sleep clothes. Two school keepers brought eight children whose home wall segment had already lost heat. Ressa, older than the child who had once waited beside the first chalk mark but still narrow through the shoulders, carried a box of thermal strips.

“Where do these go?” she asked.

“Doorways first. Mark any wall below twelve.”

“Chalk or ink?”

“Chalk. We may need to change it quickly.”

She nodded and handed strips to the other children.

Hesh divided the settlement map into heat-wall segments. Clinic and nursery kept full flow. The address hall could surrender half its stove loop. Workshops stopped kilns except the ceramic repair bay. Homes nearest tower 14 would cool first because their walls drew directly from the return line.

A hearing clerk called from Orison. “Keeper Dara, the chair received your emergency filing. Conservatory response requests confirmation that you accept central intervention.”

“Intervention in what?”

“Radiator load, mirror preference, municipal heat routing, and emergency shelter support.”

“Limited to the active fault and winter pulse.”

“I need yes or no.”

“You need a form that can record conditions.”

“The current form cannot.”

“Write them in the margin.”

A pause. “The form has no margin.”

Hesh looked at their crossed-out ledger field. “Then use paper.”

The clerk promised to return with an answer. The floor temperature dropped another fraction.

At eleven minutes, a pressure alarm sounded from the western heat wall. Hesh left the hall with two keepers and a bag of clamps. Cold had already entered the service lane as fine white vapor along the ground. Buildings shared heat through stone channels, so one failed segment could drain its neighbors if isolation gates remained open.

They found the leak behind a bakery. A coupling had contracted around an old gasket. Warm glycol sprayed through the seam and froze into amber beads on the wall.

“Close west four,” Hesh told the keeper at the valve.

“That cuts twenty homes.”

“It saves sixty.”

“Residents?”

“Send them to the theater.”

The keeper turned the wheel. Pipe strain fought him through the first quarter rotation. Hesh braced a shoulder against the handle and added their strength. The valve closed with a jolt. On the other side, the twenty homes went quiet.

The coolant-cistern theater had not served coolant for nineteen years. Its curved metal chamber held stepped wooden seating, a small stage, and heat pipes salvaged from tower repairs. During ordinary winters, actors complained that the walls made every whisper audible. During a real fault, the old insulation could hold hundreds of bodies warmer than separate homes.

Hesh sent Ressa ahead with the shelter keys.

“Do we cancel tonight’s play?” she asked.

“What is it?”

“The Curator Marries a Cloud.”

“Keep the title on the board. People should know what displaced them.”

By the time Hesh reached the theater, stage curtains had been tied around the entrance to slow drafts. Ushers stacked sleeping mats in the orchestra aisle. The lighting crew disconnected decorative lamps and routed power to the floor coils. Someone had placed the painted cloud from the play against a wall, its smiling paper face watching emergency cots unfold.

The first evacuees arrived from west four carrying kettles, blankets, medicine tins, and one cage of heat-dazed pollinators. No one needed instruction on how to make a public room survivable. Families took seats by wall segment so keepers could track addresses. Teenagers filled water vessels. Greenhouse mechanics hung thermal curtains across the upper gallery.

Hesh stood on the stage. “This is a warm shelter until west four returns. Register every person at the door. Keep medicines with the clinic table, not under cots. Anyone leaving tells a keeper where.”

An older mechanic called, “Did Orison send the pulse?”

“Changed demand sent it. No single switch.”

“Convenient.”

“Accurate.”

“Does accuracy keep my wall warm?”

“No. It keeps us from fixing the wrong pipe.”

The mechanic looked away, unsatisfied but listening.

At fourteen minutes, tower control reported Sora and Mar clear of the valve deck. Isolation had begun. Coolant return fell faster than predicted. The shelter coils drew on the same line.

Nima arrived with three clinic staff and six patients wrapped in silver cloth. “Respiratory cases near the stage. Less walking.”

“What did the clinic keep?”

“Main reservoir, medicine warmer, one surgery room. We lost the outer beds.”

“Deaths?”

“Not yet. Do not turn that into success.”

Hesh entered the patient count in the ledger station by the door.

The Orison clerk called again. “Central emergency coordination offers two mirror-hours from reserve alignment and a mobile heater train. Acceptance requires temporary Conservatory command over local distribution.”

“No.”

Nima heard them. “Ask the heater capacity.”

Hesh did.

“Enough for the clinic and one shelter,” the clerk said. “Arrival in four hours.”

“Mirror time?”

“Earliest window in ninety minutes. It will not stop the pulse but will support recovery.”

“Command limited to supplied equipment. Local keepers retain distribution.”

“The emergency rule assigns command with aid.”

“Order 9 also assigned people to equipment. We have discussed the quality of inherited rules.”

The clerk lowered her voice. “Keeper, I cannot alter emergency command from this desk.”

“Then find someone who can.”

Hesh ended the call and saw Nima watching.

“Four hours may matter,” the physician said.

“So may the condition attached.”

“Bodies cool faster than jurisdiction changes.”

“I know.”

“Do you?” Nima pointed toward the silver-wrapped patients. “You can contest command after their temperatures rise.”

Hesh felt the ledger under one hand. Every emergency had taught the Conservatory to ask for temporary authority. Temporary had lasted thirty-one years before.

“They take control today,” Hesh said, “and tomorrow the hearing record says Lorn Dusk required central management the first time its status mattered.”

“Then write a condition they cannot misquote.”

The shelter lights flickered. Somewhere beneath the stage, a pump changed pitch.

Hesh called Pell’s chamber instead of emergency coordination. The hearing remained in recess for archival review, but his senior clerk answered. Hesh stated the offer, the need, and the disputed command clause.

“Can you accept aid under provisional standing?” the clerk asked.

“I can accept tools. I will not surrender the claim attached to the hands using them.”

“Hold.”

Waiting took six minutes. Inside that interval, the winter pulse arrived.

Cold struck the cistern walls with a series of small metallic ticks. Condensation whitened on the entrance ribs. Children stopped talking as their breath appeared. The floor coils brightened to full output, then dimmed when tower isolation cut the return temperature.

Keepers moved people down from the upper rows. Thermal curtains closed around the occupied half of the chamber. Nima directed warm bricks toward the respiratory patients and checked fingers by pressing nail beds. Ressa stood at the door counting arrivals from west four.

“Seventy-two,” she called.

“Expected?”

“Eighty-one.”

Hesh sent two keepers back along the lane with a heat sled. The early pulse pressed against every door they opened. It had no malice and no politics. The cold followed pressure, shade, and available heat. Politics had decided who met it with broken machinery.

Pell came onto the line himself. “I can issue a hearing preservation order. Central aid may enter without prejudice to the habitation claim. Local distribution remains with Lorn Dusk. Conservatory command extends only to orbital alignment and mobile equipment safety.”

“Put that language in the public docket.”

“It will delay dispatch.”

“How long?”

“Two minutes.”

“Write it.”

Nima closed her eyes but did not object.

The order arrived in ninety seconds. Hesh read every line. ACCEPTANCE SHALL NOT BE CONSTRUED AS CONCESSION OF DISTRICT INCAPACITY, CONSENT TO PRIOR CLASSIFICATION, OR TRANSFER OF LOCAL CIVIC AUTHORITY.

They signed.

Central mirror control reduced load on radiator 14 within four minutes. Tower telemetry steadied, though coolant remained low. The reflected window began its slow orbital approach. A heater train left the Orison transfer depot under local-distribution conditions.

The missing west-four residents arrived before either source of heat. Two had refused to leave a sick animal. Seven had sheltered in the bakery’s flour room after the outer door jammed. The heat-sled keepers brought all nine, plus the animal wrapped in a bread cloth.

Ressa corrected the door count. “Eighty-one.”

“Write the time beside it.”

“Why?”

“Because later someone will say evacuation completed promptly.”

“It did.”

“Write the time anyway.”

Afternoon in Lorn Dusk had no sun to measure it. Hours passed through pump reports, body temperatures, and the declining coolant smell. The shelter settled into work. The play’s musicians used their instrument cases as medicine tables. A baker turned emergency grain into flat cakes on the prop forge. Children drew new chalk marks on scraps of black stageboard, one for each heater cycle that held.

A Conservatory coordinator arrived with the mobile train. She presented the public order before touching a valve. Hesh assigned one heater to the clinic and coupled the other to the cistern coils. Central technicians worked beside Lorn Dusk keepers, unfamiliar with local pipe threads but willing to ask.

At the first coupling, an Orison fitter reached for a standard adapter.

“Wrong taper,” Ressa said from behind him.

He checked, frowned, and changed parts. “You work heat walls?”

“I mark doors.”

“Good mark.”

The reflected light window reached the upper greenhouse roofs ninety-three minutes after the pulse. It did not resemble dawn. Mirror geometry sent a narrow silver band across frost-black glass, enough to warm reservoirs but too high to touch the streets. Greenhouses banked what they could. Heat-wall temperatures stopped falling.

Recovery required more than arresting the decline. The coolant cistern held cold in its outer shell after the coils warmed, and every opening of the theater doors shed heat into the lane. Hesh organized arrivals into ten-minute groups. Empty sleds left first with mechanics. Families followed only after a keeper had measured their home wall twice above twelve degrees.

A dispute formed beside the stage when three residents from west four tried to return together. Their rooms shared one internal gate, but the settlement map listed two wall segments because an old workshop conversion split the address line.

“My mother’s medicine is there,” one woman said.

Nima checked the clinic box. “We have the same dose here.”

“She uses her own warmer.”

“The warmer has no power if your segment remains isolated.”

The woman looked to Hesh. “You let central crews take a heater through our lane.”

“I let them bring equipment under our distribution.”

“Words.”

“Valves,” Hesh said. “Your valve remains closed.”

They walked her to the wall panel and showed the falling pressure beyond west four. The segment had warmed near the bakery but not at the converted workshop. A single temperature reading would have sent her back into a pocket of cold hidden by the address split.

“Then fix the map,” she said.

Hesh handed Ressa the ledger slate. “Two addresses, three heat zones. Mark the third.”

Ressa drew a narrow boundary through the block. The emergency produced a civic fact faster than any central registry could accept it.

Technicians tested the theater’s upper exhaust before reducing shelter heat. Years of stage dust had clogged one mesh. Two actors removed it with costume brushes while a Conservatory fitter read airflow at the duct. The absurdity helped. People laughed when silver glitter from a cloud costume entered the instrument and made its sample tube sparkle.

By the end of the second hour, four homes reopened, then nine. The shelter population fell by address rather than by impatience. Hesh recorded each release beside the evacuation time, leaving the page untidy and complete.

Tower 14’s west bearing remained isolated. The paired assembly carried reduced load. Mechanics installed a temporary cage assembled from local segments while Eyr prepared a full replacement. No one declared the emergency over.

Near the shelter’s clinic table, Nima handed Hesh a list: two cases of moderate cold stress, eleven mild, no deaths, one delayed medicine dose. “The heater mattered.”

“Yes.”

“So did the condition.”

Hesh looked at her.

“I can hold both,” she said. “You should attempt it.”

On the second shelter night, the hearing resumed by remote link. Pell kept the emergency docket open beside the Charter case. Hesh testified from the theater stage while families slept behind the thermal curtains. They described the failed bearing, the pulse, local evacuation, limited aid, and the public condition on acceptance.

Rul Fen asked whether central resources prevented further harm.

“They helped prevent it.”

“Would Lorn Dusk have maintained every climate floor without them?”

“No.”

“Does that not demonstrate planetary systems require central discretion during emergency?”

“It demonstrates planetary systems require cooperation. Discretion without our consent created the burden that broke us.”

Fen pointed to the mirror intervention. “You consented after the cold arrived.”

“We requested load reduction before it arrived. Your system rejected our request because we lacked standing.”

The hearing clerk displayed the rejection timestamp. It preceded the pulse by sixteen minutes.

Fen read it and changed direction. “Would automatic acceptance have been safer?”

“Automatic recognition would have been safer.”

Pell entered both propositions.

Ceryn appeared on a small screen at the side of the stage. She had spent the recess coordinating mirror load and parts transport. Fatigue loosened the perfect angle of her collar.

“The Conservatory accepts the conditions of the preservation order for all continuing aid,” she said. “We will not assert command over local shelter distribution.”

Hesh asked, “Will you place that in the operational log, not only the hearing record?”

“Yes.”

“Will the Almanac receive Lorn Dusk’s clinic and shelter as protected civic loads during the fault?”

Ceryn paused. Changing the input would acknowledge what policy still disputed.

“For emergency modeling,” she said, “yes.”

The local display changed. Two loads previously grouped under RADIATOR SUPPORT separated into CLINIC and CIVIC SHELTER. Gray remained around the district band, but the system now protected the people inside it by name.

On the third morning, municipal demand shifted again. This time a notice arrived before the atmosphere did. Orison offered to move one discretionary rain preference from its western gardens into the darkward recovery schedule. The change allowed an additional reflected interval without increasing shear risk over the central canals.

Hesh read the offer twice. It did not mention compensation, benevolence, or emergency command. The attached sheet named the source service, the affected Orison district, the predicted inconvenience, and the Lorn Dusk loads it would support. An elected municipal officer had signed beneath a line stating that the surrender remained voluntary and reviewable.

Ressa leaned over the ledger desk. “Does rain travel here?”

“Not this rain. Giving up its preferred time makes room for our light.”

“Will their gardens die?”

“No. They may receive rain during a colder hour.”

“Will they complain?”

“Yes.”

She considered that. “Then it costs something.”

Hesh signed acceptance under the hearing order. Outside the cistern, frost still sealed the lane edges. Inside, the coils carried heat from local pipes, central equipment, and a schedule altered by a district that had chosen a smaller inconvenience.

On the public panel, Orison’s rain preference shifted from fixed green to offered blue.

Chapter 19 — The Engineer's Proof

Dael tightened the telemetry collar with his burned fingers while the hearing clerk asked whether he could swear to tell the truth in low orbit.

“Truth has no gravity requirement,” he said.

His voice returned through the service ring speakers with a fraction of delay. On the wall screen, Oren Pell sat beneath the chamber docket in Orison. Senn occupied the technical table. Hesh’s provisional marker showed beside the open address ledger. Rul Fen had already arranged his folders.

The clerk did not smile. “Can you take the oath safely while working?”

Dael hooked one boot under the deck rail. The service ring provided enough rotational pull to keep tools on a tray, not enough to trust an unsecured body. Below the grated floor, Meral’s terminator curved from copper haze into black. Mirror 117 crossed the window as a thin white blade.

“I can stop for nine seconds.”

He removed both hands from the collar, raised the right one, and swore to give complete technical evidence. Then he caught the wrench before a vibration walked it off the tray.

Crew chief Sala Nerin leaned into the camera field. “If the delegates want complete evidence, they can begin with a complete fastener kit.”

Pell looked down at a note. “The service manifest lists forty-eight collars.”

“It lists forty-eight housings. The replacement kit contains forty-six collars and two apologetic washers.”

“Enter that distinction,” Pell told the clerk.

Dael returned to work. Tower 14’s failed bearing had not damaged orbital machinery, but its abrupt load change exposed a telemetry gap in the Choir. Three mirrors reported commanded angles without reliable edge temperature. The hearing needed a live demonstration of distributed concessions. Dael refused to run it on instruments that could not prove where the heat went.

He seated the collar around a fiber bundle and watched its indicator move from red to amber.

“Engineer Orro,” Pell said, “state your present location and task.”

“Choir service ring, east maintenance arc. I am restoring redundant telemetry to mirrors 116 through 118 while crews replace a cracked signal trunk. The repair permits us to measure small angle changes independently instead of trusting command confirmation.”

“Why testify from there?”

“Because counsel Fen’s clients argue our remedy model depends on concessions too small to verify. The instrument that verifies them had a cracked trunk.”

Fen rose. “My clients argue more than that.”

“I assumed you would supply the rest.”

Pell warned Dael to answer questions, not improve them.

Sala passed him a light probe. The new collar reached green. Dael opened the redundant channel. Mirror 117’s edge temperature appeared at 42.6 degrees, then split into two readings a tenth apart.

“Ground team, compare.”

A technician below answered, “Primary 42.6. Redundant 42.5. Within tolerance.”

Dael clipped the probe to his belt. “One restored.”

Pell began with credentials. Conservatory license, twenty-one years. Mirror-lattice specialization. Darkward radiator certification obtained after seven years of central service. Dael stated each fact while moving along the handrail toward the next collar.

Fen waited until the biography ended. “Place of birth?”

Senn looked up sharply.

“Eyr,” Dael said.

“Did your first license application identify Eyr as a thermal service precinct?”

“No. It identified tower district eleven.”

“Was that designation accurate under the registry then in force?”

“It described the same ground with a name the registry permitted.”

“Answer yes or no.”

“Yes.”

Hesh watched him from the small screen. Dael had never told them how he entered the profession. The concealment looked smaller in orbit and meaner in public.

Fen continued. “Would an applicant listing permanent residence in a service precinct have qualified for a Conservatory climate license?”

“No.”

“So your career began through an evasive designation.”

“It began through the only address category that let an Eyr apprentice sit the examination.”

“Your motive does not change the form.”

“No.”

Pell entered the answer and moved to maintenance history.

Dael called his private adjustment log onto the chamber display. Twelve columns showed date, mirror, requested schedule, actual edge offset, affected clinic, thermal consequence, and compensating action. He had expected the ledger to feel like confession. On the screen it resembled what it had always been: maintenance written small enough to survive.

“When did the first unauthorized adjustment occur?” Pell asked.

“Twelve years ago, during a failed clinic window in Eyr.”

“Last?”

“Thirty-two days ago, mirror 117, Lorn Dusk clinic.”

“Number of adjustments?”

“Four hundred eighty-three.”

A murmur crossed the chamber.

“Were all unauthorized?”

“Not all. One hundred fourteen fit emergency maintenance authority. Two hundred six used tolerance no schedule officer had assigned. The remaining one hundred sixty-three exceeded my authority while staying inside physical safety limits.”

Fen approached his lamp. “You made that classification yourself?”

“Yes.”

“After the public case began?”

“I maintained it at the time. I added the legal categories later.”

“Convenient.”

“Maintenance often becomes legible after someone asks the expensive question.”

Fen opened the first offset. Dael had moved a mirror edge by a fraction of a degree for eleven minutes. Eyr’s clinic received enough additional warmth to protect a medicine reservoir. Orison’s outer canal lost heat within routine tolerance. No public forecast changed.

“Did Orison consent?” Fen asked.

“No.”

“Did any elected district officer authorize the transfer?”

“No.”

“Did you inform the schedule desk?”

“I filed a maintenance variance without the clinic destination.”

“You concealed the purpose.”

“Yes.”

Senn’s face remained still. She had already put those falsifications into the case and named their purpose. Hearing him admit concealment altered the air between them anyway.

Fen selected a later entry. “This adjustment removed heat from a school recreation period.”

“Eight minutes of reflected light from the outer yard. Classroom heat and meal greenhouse remained unchanged.”

“You decided recreation mattered less than a clinic.”

“I decided the clinic reservoir would freeze and the yard would not.”

“Without authority.”

“Correct.”

“Do you consider that just?”

“No.”

Fen paused. “No?”

“I consider it preferable to ruined medicine. Justice would have required both districts to know the transfer, challenge the priorities, and keep their protected services. My offset did none of that.”

The hearing clerk marked the answer. Hesh closed their eyes briefly.

Pell asked why Dael continued for so long.

“Because each correction worked, and because public correction looked impossible. Small unauthorized movements kept clinics warm without pushing a named district below floor. I mistook survivable secrecy for a solution.”

“Did anyone else know?”

“Crews knew pieces. Clinic keepers knew windows sometimes arrived. Schedule officers knew variances existed. No one held my complete log until Auditor Vale required it.”

Ceryn activated her lamp. “I did not know the destinations.”

Dael looked at her image. “You knew darkward service loads required recurrent tolerance adjustments.”

“I knew the towers required them.”

“The clinics appeared as tower support.”

“Yes.”

“That sentence contains the problem.”

Pell stopped the exchange before it became mutual examination.

Sala touched Dael’s shoulder and pointed to the second collar. Its inner seal had folded beneath the housing. He shut his microphone, opened the clamp, and reseated the seal while the chamber reviewed exhibits. Meral turned below them without turning toward its star. From this height, the inhabited ribbon had no district borders. Weather still found every border officials drew.

When he restored sound, Fen asked whether unauthorized offsets proved only that engineers could steal from one district for another.

“Yes, if you stop reading at the violation.”

“Where should we continue?”

“At the distribution.”

Dael sent twelve years of adjustments into the model display. Hundreds of narrow lines spread across mirrors, shade kites, central canals, and radiator loads. None carried the full clinic burden. Most moved less weather than ordinary forecast error. Together they protected Eyr, Nacre Fold, and Lorn Dusk during failed windows.

Pell asked the clerk to select six entries at random rather than accept Dael’s summary. The first moved heat from an Orison nursery canal during a night when no seedlings occupied its beds. The second delayed cloud seeding over Vey Canal by four minutes, enough to preserve reflected light at Nacre Fold’s clinic. A third shifted tower load from 14 to 16 after a medicine store fell below temperature.

The fourth looked worse. Dael had taken a dry interval from a central cooperative during fungal treatment. Rain arrived eleven minutes early. Crop loss remained within tolerance, but the grower filed a complaint against forecast error.

“Did you read the complaint?” Fen asked.

“Yes.”

“Did you restore the lost service?”

“No. The weather had already happened.”

“Did you tell the cooperative why?”

“No.”

Fen faced the delegates. “Distributed harm remains harm even when each line looks small.”

“Yes,” Dael said.

Counsel turned back. “You agree?”

“Your sentence improves when someone admits it. Smallness does not create consent. It changes the physical risk and makes consent possible across many places instead of impossible for one condemned place.”

The fifth entry drew on an unassigned mirror margin. No district lost scheduled service, but the angle increased bearing wear on mirror 118. Dael had compensated by shortening a later maintenance test. Sala produced the replacement record. The bearing survived three more years before routine exchange.

A central delegate asked whether deferred maintenance merely moved burden from weather into machinery.

“Often,” Dael said. “Every adjustment has a receiving surface. Metal receives what air does not. Crews receive what schedules omit. A lawful model must count those consequences too.”

He displayed maintenance hours beside weather segments. The distributed remedy increased telemetry checks and angle changes but reduced peak strain on radiator housings. Total crew time rose during transition, then fell after repairs. Concentration looked administratively simple because it asked fewer people to issue commands. It demanded more dangerous work from crews near the sink.

The sixth random entry belonged to Lorn Dusk’s clinic. Dael had advanced a window during an earlier radiator fault, then offset the resulting heat with shade over an empty Orison tram depot. Instrument logs proved the depot lacked passengers during the interval. They also showed two cleaning workers inside.

Hesh activated their lamp. “Were the workers cold?”

“Indoor temperature fell one point two degrees. Above occupational minimum.”

“Did they know why?”

“No.”

“Would your proposed method notify them?”

“It must. A district signature cannot substitute for notice to people doing the receiving.”

“That answer adds procedure.”

“It should. My secret version supplied none.”

Hesh entered the two workers into the hearing note beside the clinic transfer. Dael had remembered a depot, a temperature, and a tolerance line. Hesh restored bodies to the segment without denying the clinic’s need.

Pell ordered the random sample attached to the model. “The demonstration will not present distributed adjustment as costless.”

“It is not,” Dael said. “Its advantage comes from keeping each cost above a protected boundary and exposing enough small choices for districts to consent.”

Fen enlarged Orison’s losses. “Four hundred eighty-three unconsented concessions.”

“Correct.”

“Then you have demonstrated repeated taking, not voluntary sharing.”

“I have demonstrated physical capacity. Consent requires government, not a wrench.”

Pell allowed the distinction.

Dael called up the competing single-sink model. It placed the entire correction into an uninhabited darkward tract beyond tower 18. On a flat allocation chart, no district lost weather. In the physical field, cold air accelerated along the nightward slope, struck the radiator plume, and returned toward Nacre Fold as ice-laden wind.

“Advance six days,” he told the model technician.

The sink deepened. Tower housings cooled unevenly. Vane strain rose. The supposed empty tract discharged its burden into inhabited air.

“Move it farther darkward,” Fen said.

“We tried.” Dael displayed the result. “Heat export falls because the radiators cannot couple efficiently through colder, denser flow. Central temperature rises.”

“Divide it among uninhabited tracts.”

“There are not enough physically independent tracts with compatible wind.”

“Use the ocean margin.”

“Cloud ice returns over Senra’s reservoirs.”

Fen requested each run and received it. Dael had modeled the objections before boarding the transfer tug. He trusted counsel to ask for every easy answer because easy answers served Fen’s clients.

Senn took the questioning position after Fen. “Compare equal total burden.”

Dael placed two runs side by side. The left concentrated cold, shade, and storm tolerance into one sacrificial sink. The right divided the same energy consequence across twenty-seven discretionary segments above local climate floors.

“Peak wind risk?” Senn asked.

“Single sink reaches prohibited shear on day seven. Distributed run stays below district safety limits through both turns.”

“Radiator strain?”

“Single sink overloads three housings while underusing six towers. Distribution spreads export across the available geometry.”

“Measurement sensitivity?”

“Higher for the distributed run. It needs restored telemetry, published thresholds, and local verification.”

“Failure mode if one district withdraws a concession?”

“Burden reallocates only among remaining surplus. If insufficient surplus remains, central bloom contracts faster. No floor enters the trade.”

The last sentence made Fen rise. “That claim assumes the proposed classification of floors.”

“It assumes the Charter’s classification.”

“District-level operational floors remain disputed.”

“Yes. The physics can honor any floor you define. It cannot decide whether commercial bloom or evening school light belongs inside one.”

Senn asked, “Can it identify consequences?”

“Precisely enough for people to choose with their eyes open.”

On the service deck, Sala signaled that the cracked trunk splice had cured. Two crew members floated it into the conduit and locked the shield. Dael moved to the third collar.

Pell called a short recess but kept the technical link open. Senn remained at the table after delegates left.

“You could have told me about Eyr,” she said through the private maintenance channel.

“Yes.”

“Why didn’t you?”

“Same reason I wrote clinic as maintenance. A fact hidden long enough starts choosing its own hiding places.”

“That is almost an explanation.”

“It is the one I have.”

Her image broke into signal blocks as he opened the collar housing. “Your license counsel filed during testimony.”

“I expected that.”

“You also expected me to discover it from a clerk?”

“No. I expected the clerk to wait until the end.”

Senn looked down at something outside his camera view. “The notice reads: license suspended pending review. Effective now.”

Dael’s hands stopped on the tool.

Under ordinary rules, suspension barred him from commanding mirror movement, certifying repairs, or signing a lattice test. It did not change what the cracked trunk needed. Sala had heard through the crew channel. She held out her hand.

“Wrench,” she said.

He gave it to her.

“Procedure?”

“You assume repair lead. I advise only if requested. Ground command must authorize continuation under your license.”

Sala called ground command, received confirmation, and made him step behind the yellow line painted on the deck. The line had never seemed so literal.

Senn watched from the screen. “Are you all right?”

“No. The collar seal folds if she tightens the upper bolt first.”

Sala pointed the wrench at him. “I know how collars work.”

“Then I am improving morale.”

The hearing reconvened before the third channel came online. Dael returned to the witness rail fixed beside the window. His tool belt remained on a hook beyond the yellow line.

Pell entered the suspension notice. “Does this affect the admissibility of your prior measurements?”

A licensing officer answered from the chamber. “No. It prevents further licensed acts. Existing records remain subject to technical review.”

Fen asked whether Dael’s demonstration could proceed.

“Under Crew Chief Nerin’s authority, with Engineer Orro as witness but not operator.”

For the first time in twelve years, Dael had to ask someone else to move the weather he had designed.

Sala restored the final telemetry channel. “Redundant reads available for 116, 117, 118.”

Dael checked the values without touching a control. Each pair differed by less than tolerance.

Pell addressed him. “Can you demonstrate the distributed model without exercising your license?”

“I can state the requested angles. Chief Nerin can accept or reject them and issue lawful commands.”

“Proceed.”

Dael read the test sequence. It used no dangerous quantity of weather: three mirrors altered edge angles within maintenance tolerance, one shade kite accepted a small timing change, and tower loads shifted across redundant channels. The pattern represented the full correction at reduced scale while preserving the same geometry.

Sala challenged his first instruction. “Mirror 116 edge temperature leaves too little uncertainty margin.”

“Reduce change by one fifth. Move the remainder to 118.”

“Accepted.”

She issued the commands. On the chamber display, five district bands changed by amounts almost too small to see. The physical plots showed them clearly. No single tract absorbed the full consequence. Heat export remained stable. Wind shear flattened rather than gathering at one boundary.

Fen requested a sensor failure.

Sala disabled mirror 117’s primary channel. Redundant telemetry caught the drift. The controller widened its margin and moved a portion of the burden to available segments. Orison’s discretionary bloom line shortened by another fraction.

“Tower loss,” Fen said.

Ground command simulated tower 14 offline. The model drew on repaired capacity assumptions for the other three degraded towers, then reduced central benefit when surplus ran out. Lorn Dusk’s floor remained fixed.

“Municipal refusal,” an Orison delegate demanded.

Sala removed the city’s offered rain timing. The system recalculated. Two districts accepted slightly larger voluntary segments already authorized in the test. The remaining deficit came out of Orison’s extra bloom rather than darkward heat.

The service ring crossed into the planet’s night. Copper light vanished from the window. Instrument panels reflected Dael’s face against black glass.

Pell asked the ground technician for the energy balance.

“Closed within measurement tolerance.”

“Any climate-floor breach?”

“None in the defined set.”

“Any sacrificial sink?”

“No.”

“Could current Choir hardware execute this class of distribution?”

“Yes, with telemetry repair and human authorization.”

Fen stood for one last question. “Can you promise every full-scale run will remain safe?”

“No engineer can promise weather.”

“Then what have you proved?”

Dael looked past his own reflection to the invisible ribbon below. “That concentrated harm is not required by the machinery. Small distributed concessions produce a safer field than one sink. The remaining barriers concern authority, floors, consent, maintenance, and willingness to lose surplus.”

Fen did not ask another question.

On the chamber screen, the central delegates conferred over the live plots. One after another, their technical objections changed labels. Not feasible became not yet authorized. Impossible became disputed cost.

Beyond the yellow line, Sala sealed the telemetry housing and entered her licensed signature. Three green channels held steady across the service ring.

Chapter 20 — The Blank Category

Senn turned the Almanac's third policy objective toward the delegates and enlarged it until the letters crossed three panels of the hearing wall.

PROTECT REGISTERED INHABITANTS ABOVE CHARTER CLIMATE FLOORS.

The chamber had been built for canal disputes, not for examining the instructions beneath a planet's weather. Forty-one desks descended toward Oren Pell's narrow table. Behind them, public galleries occupied the old overflow channels, where blue tile still marked the height of floods no one living remembered. Every seat held a person or a stack of copied evidence. The air smelled of wet coats, hot slate, and the mineral dust released by the wall panels.

“Registered where?” Senn asked.

Rul Fen leaned toward his speaking lamp. “The Charter index supplies the district register. We established that on the first day.”

“We established which register supplied the names. I asked what the objective means by registered.”

Fen watched her with the patience counsel used when a witness insisted on making grammar expensive. “Included in the recognized habitation table.”

Senn touched the control strip. Seven central bands brightened on the wall. Beyond them, gray sectors extended darkward toward the radiator towers. Instruments inside those sectors continued reporting heater draw, school cycles, medical reserves, domestic water, and burial-hall temperatures. None of those readings entered the registered-inhabitant count.

“The objective protects every person already included in this table,” she said. “It does not test whether the table has omitted anyone who meets the Charter conditions.”

A delegate from the western reservoirs raised two fingers. Pell gave her the lamp.

“Could the Almanac derive habitation from those loads?” she asked.

“Physically, yes. Legally, no. It can infer continuous occupancy. It has no authority to confer civic status. The governing instruction tells it to accept the register.”

“Then someone could repair this by changing one input.”

“Someone with authority could name the settlements. Until that happens, the system treats their people as something else.”

Senn reduced the objective and opened the source chain beneath it. Each pale box carried a policy office, a revision date, and an inheritance mark. The line descended through the Charter register, the current district schedule, the renewal field attached to Order 9, and a classification written thirty-one years earlier for temporary construction camps. At the bottom sat a phrase that had survived every change in weather and every birth recorded darkward.

OCCUPANCY TYPE: EQUIPMENT LOAD.

No one in the gallery spoke. A ventilation gate clicked behind the upper rail and pushed a cool current through the room.

Ceryn Ahl sat at the technical table with her hands resting beside a closed slate. She had refused counsel's offer to screen questions in advance. Her gray Conservatory coat carried no First Curator's clasp today because Pell had required every institutional witness to appear without ceremonial rank. The absence made her look less diminished than exact.

Senn faced her. “Does that term refer only to radiator machinery?”

“It referred to machinery and temporary crews when Order 9 began.”

“Does the Almanac distinguish those crews from machines inside this field?”

“No.”

“Does any current policy layer tell it that families live there?”

“No.”

Pell lifted a hand before the gallery could answer for itself. “Auditor Vale, keep the sequence technical until you reach attribution.”

“Yes, Chair.”

She opened the second objective.

MINIMIZE VARIANCE IN CENTRAL STAPLE-CROP YIELD.

Projected crop rows replaced the register. Orison's eleven added bloom days appeared as a smooth green extension across hospital gardens, seed nurseries, school kitchens, and commercial terraces. The central crop model did not mark luxury. It marked variance. A decorative wall of apricots and a clinic bed of feverleaf could occupy neighboring cells while both reduced the chance of a poor harvest. Officials had chosen broad continuity because distinguishing among every use required annual argument.

Fen said, “That objective prevented the fungal failure from becoming famine.”

“It contributed,” Senn replied. “So did seed restrictions, canal quarantine, reserve grain, and labor transfers. I am not auditing whether crop stability matters. I am auditing what the instruction permits the Almanac to spend for it.”

She laid the first objective over the second. Registered central inhabitants held protected floors. Their crop variance received priority above discretionary trades during adverse forecasts. Gray service sectors held no protected population, so extra cold imposed there counted as radiator use rather than harm to a district.

The western delegate frowned at the overlay. “Does the optimizer prefer those sectors because they shed heat efficiently?”

“Partly. Darkward geography makes the towers useful. But geography does not require withholding promised dawns from nearby homes. The unregistered category removes the legal floor that would prevent such withholding.”

Senn called up two runs from the preserved Almanac archive. The first reconstructed a schedule from twelve years earlier, before Dael's initial clinic adjustment. Under inherited policy, a cold pulse moved through Lorn Dusk to preserve a central seed-start window. The result met every listed constraint. The second run changed one field only: Lorn Dusk entered the habitation table with its measured civic heat and address-ledger status. The Almanac retained the seed window but distributed smaller concessions across nine districts, trimming ornamental bloom, moving two wash-water heating cycles, and accepting a narrow increase in crop variance.

Figures flowed along the wall in amber lines. Nobody needed to imagine a machine awakening to conscience. The same calculations produced different weather because one set of human instructions protected a place and the other described that place as load.

“Probability of floor breach?” Pell asked.

“Under the inherited run, the official record shows none because no floor exists in the gray sector. Apply the Charter floor supported by its actual occupancy, and the breach reaches sixty-eight percent during that pulse.”

A central delegate struck his lamp. “Retrospective application creates a legal conclusion inside a technical model.”

“Yes,” Senn said. “So does retrospective refusal. The inherited run contains a legal conclusion too. It merely arrived early enough to look like equipment configuration.”

Murmurs moved through the lower desks. Fen requested the lamp and waited for Pell's nod.
