“You have shown one alternate classification,” he said. “You have not shown that the Almanac selected the winter debit because of that classification. Central yield could still dominate under any lawful model.”

Senn had expected the objection. She changed the wall from two schedules to a matrix of sixty-four. Pera had recovered the policy snapshots; Dael had supplied clean instrument bundles before his license suspension removed his access. Each row altered one governing term while preserving weather inputs. Every column carried the same forecast field from the disputed turn.

“Runs one through sixteen remove crop-variance priority,” Senn said. “They produce unacceptable central failures and do not represent current law. Runs seventeen through thirty-two preserve crop priority but cap radiator duty. The burden shifts, yet unregistered settlement demand still receives no floor. Runs thirty-three through forty-eight add the three settlements as inhabited districts. Every one protects their minimum before allocating surplus. Runs forty-nine through sixty-four retain the inherited register and delete Dael Orro's unauthorized offsets.”

On the last set, clinic temperatures dropped first.

A soft sound came from the gallery, not a cry, only several people drawing breath at once.

Fen studied the matrix. “Your selection of protected minimums comes from the remedy model now before us.”

“The numerical floors come from existing Charter calculations used for every recognized district. I applied the same method to measured populations and services.”

“Those populations remain disputed for standing purposes.”

“The instruments counted their heat for twenty-two years. The address records counted their households longer. Dispute the legal conclusion if you choose. Do not call the inputs unavailable.”

Ceryn opened her slate at last. A small reflection from its surface moved across her cheek.

Senn shifted to the third objective.

PRESERVE SYSTEM STABILITY WITH MINIMUM DEPARTURE FROM INHERITED SCHEDULES.

That sentence carried the hearing's most respectable danger. Stability kept mirrors from chasing each new demand until wind shear exceeded control. Inheritance preserved hospital routines, planting calendars, canal storage, crew rotations, and public trust in announced seasons. It also gave old mistakes momentum.

Senn displayed the weighting record. During the crop emergency, departure penalties had tripled. Five years later, after the fungal threat ended, a review notice opened and closed without action. Automated inheritance carried the emergency weights into the next schedule. Later curators adjusted individual crops and storm limits, but nobody returned the category table to review.

“Who approved continued inheritance?” Pell asked.

Ceryn answered before Senn could identify the chain. “My office did, through annual schedule confirmation.”

“Did those confirmations display Order 9?”

“No. They displayed deviations from the prior year. The precinct category did not register as a deviation.”

Fen turned toward her. “First Curator, confine your answer to the record requested.”

Ceryn did not look at him. “The distinction matters. We built a review process that showed change and concealed continuance.”

Senn let the statement settle into the official transcript. Then she opened the annual confirmation screen. It contained thousands of weather weights, each marked changed, held, or inherited. Order 9 appeared only if a reviewer expanded the darkward infrastructure branch, then opened occupancy assumptions, then requested originating authority. The public schedule showed none of those layers.

A delegate from Caldrift asked, “How often did anyone reach that field?”

“Archive access shows six openings in seventeen years,” Senn said. “Three by maintenance engineers tracing load mismatches. Two by policy clerks during software migration. One by me.”

“Did the clerks flag it?”

“One filed a terminology question. The response classified the wording as legacy-compatible.”

Dry laughter broke from the upper gallery. Pell tapped his table once, though his own mouth had tightened.

Senn enlarged the terminology response. Pera's annotation sat beside it in the hearing copy: COMPATIBLE WITH WHAT?

She did not read the annotation aloud. Its presence did enough work.

The audit moved into the physical chain. Instrument readings entered local aggregators every twenty seconds. The aggregators sorted demand by civic status before sending it to the Almanac. Registered clinic heat carried protected priority. Clinic heat inside a service precinct entered continuous operational demand, no different in law from coolant pumps or bearing housings. The optimizer did not erase the clinic. It received the clinic under a field that permitted interruption.

Senn showed a winter morning from Nacre Fold. A medicine cabinet heater requested six more lumen-hours. The local controller labeled the request clinical. The precinct aggregator relabeled the whole bundle as equipment load. A central seed nursery requested the same amount to prevent germination delay. Its district field preserved the request as protected crop continuity.

Both requests reached the optimizer. Only one arrived with people attached.

“Can local technicians alter that label?” the western delegate asked.

“Not without policy credentials. They can adjust timing within tolerance, which explains the small compensations in maintenance records. They cannot establish a climate floor.”

“Could the Almanac alert on the mismatch between school-cycle data and infrastructure status?”

“It could, if governors instructed it to test category coherence. No such policy objective exists in the versions we audited.”

Fen rose rather than use his lamp. “Now we approach invention. An optimizer can test unlimited inconsistencies if given unlimited authority. Government requires settled inputs.”

“Government also requires responsibility for settling them.” Senn closed the Nacre trace. “I am not proposing unlimited authority. I am identifying a blank review duty. Current policy asks whether measurements satisfy the categories. It never asks whether the categories still describe the measured world.”

“That remains a human judgment.”

“Yes.”

The single word checked him more effectively than dispute. Fen remained standing for a breath, then sat.

Pell asked Senn to show how the blank review duty behaved under a live forecast rather than a historical one. A clerk loaded the next nine-day weather field, scrubbed of the negotiating group's proposed correction. Amber wind lines gathered starward of Orison. Darkward radiators carried enough spare capacity on paper, though four tower histories now made that paper doubtful.

Senn ran the inherited policy first. The schedule preserved every central hospital reserve and held crop variation under the preferred margin. It also cancelled two reflected-dawn windows near tower 15, reduced Eyr's school heat for eleven hours, and advanced a Nacre Fold radiator cycle into the settlement's local sleep period. Each action appeared on the result panel as infrastructure management.

“Stop there,” Pell said. “If those same actions occurred in a registered district, what language would appear?”

“Protected-service breach, protected civic-heat breach, and domestic exposure warning.”

“Can you display that without changing allocation?”

Senn copied the physical result into a recognized-district shell. Red warnings covered the three darkward bands. The underlying temperatures did not move by a fraction. Only the labels changed, yet those labels opened mandatory review, local notice, and a prohibition against final authorization.

A delegate who had defended inherited scheduling through four days of hearings removed her glasses. “So the warning system works.”

“For communities admitted to its scope.”

“Could staff have seen these red fields at any time?”

“Only by performing the same reclassification I just performed. Routine operators lacked authority to do it in a live schedule.”

The delegate replaced her glasses with care. “Then access protected the category from the evidence.”

“Access helped. More precisely, procedure separated the people who could see the measurements from those allowed to question the label.”

Ceryn requested the lamp. “That separation had a safety purpose. Schedule operators cannot improvise constitutional status while mirrors are moving.”

Senn nodded. “Agreed. A control-room technician should not rewrite habitation law during a storm. The missing safeguard belongs above operations: periodic category review tied to evidence that contradicts inherited status.”

“What review interval?” asked the reservoir delegate.

“Recommendation falls outside this conclusion. The audit can establish a trigger. Continuous domestic heat, active address records, or local birth and burial data would each have forced human review here.”

Fen examined the red wall. “You are describing information available across offices that did not share records.”

“I am describing information deliberately assigned to different offices. Fragmentation did not happen in the atmosphere. Governors built it into administrative practice.”

Pell told the clerk to preserve both runs. On the evidence wall, identical temperatures remained side by side. One version showed routine gray maintenance. The other refused authorization in red.

Pell called a short recess so clerks could cool the wall panels. Delegates left their desks in clusters, but the galleries stayed full. Senn drank from a metal cup behind the witness rail. Her hand had begun to ache from controlling the display. Dael's matrix waited in a locked evidence partition, precise enough to survive the engineer who had made it unavailable to himself by telling the truth.

Ceryn crossed the floor while the lamps remained dark.

“You left out the famine projection,” she said.

“It belongs to the reason for the weighting, not the operation of the category.”

“Fen will say you separated motive from mechanism because motive weakens your claim.”

“My claim concerns mechanism.”

“Mechanisms inherit motives.”

Senn set down the cup. “They inherit categories. Motives have to be supplied again by living people.”

Ceryn looked toward the gray sectors still faintly visible on the cooling wall. “You think that distinction absolves the system and condemns us.”

“It assigns action where action occurred.”

“That can amount to the same public effect.”

“Not for the replacement. If we blame calculation, we replace a tool. If we name the governing choice, we have to change how authority reviews itself.”

The clerk called the recess to an end. Ceryn returned to her table without answering.

Senn resumed with a blind test. Pera had stripped district names from twelve historical bundles and asked current policy staff to classify each from its measurements alone. Every reviewer identified Lorn Dusk, Eyr, and Nacre Fold as inhabited. They found continuous household heat, civic water, schools, clinics, births, burials, and local maintenance governance. When shown the inherited legal labels afterward, five reviewers requested immediate correction. Two asked for central-risk modeling first. None defended equipment-only occupancy as an accurate description.

“Did you coerce those answers by mentioning this hearing?” Fen asked.

“The test instructions said the bundles concerned a category-coherence audit. Reviewers did not receive the district names or disputed history.”

“How many staff participated?”

“Seven. Every currently qualified policy classifier who had not worked on the hearing record.”

He examined the signed forms and declined another question.

Senn returned the wall to the three objectives. Protect registered inhabitants. Minimize central crop variance. Preserve stability through inherited schedules. Read separately, each sounded defensible. Combined with the obsolete precinct category, they directed the Almanac to hold central abundance steady by spending cold where law claimed no civic life required protection.

No sabotage appeared in the code review. No hidden optimization term preferred suffering. No measurement failure concealed the homes. Human classifications had produced the result, and annual confirmations had continued it.

Pell folded his hands. “Auditor Vale, state your conclusion without recommendation.”

“The Almanac performed the task its governors assigned. It protected the registered table, accepted precinct occupancy as machinery demand, reduced central crop variance, and resisted changes to inherited schedules. The resulting harm did not arise from a failure to optimize. It arose from what government chose to count.”

“Can the system independently correct the error now that contradictory measurements have been shown?”

“No. It can flag a mismatch if instructed. It cannot grant standing, repeal an order, or decide that a legal category has expired.”

Pell turned to Ceryn. “First Curator, do you dispute that limit?”

Ceryn's face held the composure she had brought to famine briefings, classification fights, and the publication of names attached to preventable deaths. She looked first at the policy objectives, then at the gray sectors, and finally toward the gallery where Hesh sat with the address ledger closed across their knees.

“I do not dispute it,” she said.

“Then what correction can the Almanac make under its present authority?”

Ceryn placed both palms on the table. “None that reaches the category itself. The Almanac cannot correct a category its governors refuse to name.”

Chapter 21 — Forty-One Seats

“Standing first,” said the Eyr observer from behind a rail where no delegate's lamp had been installed.

The phrase crossed the chamber without amplification. It reached Senn because the morning galleries had gone quiet after Pell placed the recognition question on the board. Forty-one delegate desks faced him in descending arcs. Three additional tables stood at floor level for counsel, technical witnesses, and communities the hearing had permitted to speak without granting them votes. Eyr's observer occupied none of those. Her authorization covered documentary attendance only.

Rul Fen looked over his shoulder. “Chair, if gallery interventions become argument, procedure has dissolved.”

Oren Pell adjusted the copper clip holding his pages. “Procedure heard the intervention. Procedure will survive.”

A few delegates smiled. The Eyr observer did not.

Pell returned to the board, where two proposed sequences waited in plain black type. The central cities' sequence placed a complete remedy schedule before recognition. A district would receive standing only after the Conservatory certified that its inclusion could be implemented without forcing another district below floor. The darkward petition placed recognition first and required all later allocation to obey it.

Between those sequences lay the old trap. If Lorn Dusk, Eyr, and Nacre Fold remained unrecognized until a safe schedule existed, their losses could continue funding the safety test. If recognition took full operational effect before a transition had been built, the next inherited schedule could fail authorization while mirrors, crops, canals, and clinics still required instructions.

Senn had spent the night drawing the trap as two circles and crossing both out.

Fen spoke from the central counsel table. “No one contests that the habitation evidence deserves final determination. My clients contest an order that grants fiscal and climatic claims before this body understands their distributional effect.”

Hesh sat three places from him at the provisional witness table. They wore the dark work coat they had used in the Lorn Dusk address hall. Their hands rested on a bound copy of the Charter, not on the address ledger itself. That book would come later, if the chamber allowed its keeper more than witness standing.

“Your clients understood the effect well enough to receive eleven days,” Hesh said.

Fen inclined his head. “They received schedules authorized by the Conservatory. Growers built contracts, hospitals built heat reserves, and schools built calendars around them.”

“Lorn Dusk built graves around the same schedules.”

Pell raised one finger. “Neither counsel nor witness will repeat the case already admitted. We are deciding sequence.”

Senn waited for the speaking lamp at the technical table to turn green. It stayed dark while delegates entered questions into the queue. The chamber clerk projected them along one edge of the board: Could temporary recognition carry temporary floors? Who would bear emergency authority? Did immediate standing create compensation liability? Could three districts veto a planetary schedule? What happened if recognition and implementation diverged?

Most questions confused a right with the machinery for honoring it. The confusion had protected the blank category for years.

A delegate from Caldrift received the first lamp. “I favor recognition. I also represent growers whose rain turn can be ruined by a half-day shear shift. If we declare three new floors this morning and no legal schedule can close tonight, what governs?”

Fen answered before Pell redirected the question. “That uncertainty supports the central sequence.”

“It supports separating two decisions,” Senn said.

Her lamp had not lit. Pell looked toward the clerk, then pressed his own control. Green appeared at Senn's table.

“Explain the separation,” he said.

She carried two transparent sheets to the floor display. One showed a district register. The other showed the planetary allocation model. When she overlaid them, the recognized bands aligned with protected climate floors, weather shares, notices, appeals, and voting privileges. The administration had treated that stack as one indivisible object because established districts had received every layer together.

“Recognition answers whether a community meets the Charter's habitation test,” Senn said. “Allocation answers how the Conservatory meets obligations under physical constraints. The first question depends on continuous civic heat, an address ledger, and local births or burials. The second depends on forecasts, surplus, infrastructure, and transition time. We can answer the first without pretending the second has become easy.”

The Caldrift delegate leaned toward her lamp. “What changes immediately?”

“The three settlements receive non-fungible climate floors in every schedule authorized after recognition. Their measured minimum demand can no longer enter as discretionary infrastructure load. Existing physical transfers enter staged review rather than disappearing at the instant of declaration.”

Fen stood. “That phrasing conceals the contradiction. If the floor takes effect, any continued winter debit violates it.”

“Correct.”

“Yet you propose continued transfers during staging.”

“I propose no schedule may place them below floor. Burden above every district's floor can move while inherited dependencies are retired.”

“You have not proved sufficient surplus exists on every forecast.”

“Dael Orro proved distributed concessions outperform a sacrificial sink. The remedy group still has to convert that proof into operating rules. Lack of final rules does not turn inhabited settlements into machinery.”

Fen's fingers tightened around the desk edge. “If the model fails, the central cities carry duties no one can perform.”

“They already carry duties the Charter imposes. Administrative delay does not suspend them. It changes how openly we manage the failure risk.”

Pell asked the clerk to split the board. Under RECOGNITION, he placed habitation finding, immediate floor, district notice, appeal rights, and access to policy audits. Under IMPLEMENTATION, he placed burden formula, tower repairs, bloom retirement, emergency reserve, and schedule dates.

Hesh studied the list. “You omitted participation in writing the second column.”

The clerk's stylus paused.

Pell said, “Witness Dara, your present status permits testimony and submission of language through a delegate sponsor.”

“That means people who received the benefit can edit the remedy at a lamp, while we pass paper through someone willing to carry it.”

“Your objection has been recorded.”

“Recording an inequality does not remove it.”

Several gallery benches answered with the scrape of shoes. Pell waited until the movement stopped. His face showed no irritation, but he placed Hesh's objection beneath the implementation column rather than in the general transcript margin.

Senn returned to her seat. Pera had left a folded note beside the water cup. The note listed the Charter sections that separated habitation from weather-share allocation. At the bottom she had written: THEY ARE PRETENDING THE ORDER OF FORMS CHANGES THE ORDER OF FACTS.

Senn turned the page over before Fen could see it.

The morning narrowed into clause work. Delegates who agreed on harm divided over verbs. “Recognized” sounded final to some and merely administrative to others. “Immediate” prompted questions about the next mirror cycle. “Shall possess a climate floor” invited argument over whether the floor existed before the finding or arose from it. Hesh insisted the text say “has possessed,” because the Charter test had been met for decades. Fen objected that retrospective phrasing decided liability beyond the hearing's mandate.

Pell ordered a practical test. He gave each side the same hypothetical schedule: a newly discovered district met every habitation condition, its present temperature stood two degrees above floor, and the next forecast threatened to lower it by three. Clerks had to authorize or reject the schedule under their preferred sequence.

Fen's team added an interim protection order pending complete remedy certification. Hesh read it twice, then pointed to the expiry line. “Seven days, renewable by the Conservatory. Our floor becomes a favor issued a week at a time.”

“The order prevents the predicted breach,” Fen said.

“It prevents this breach. It leaves the office that ignored us in charge of deciding whether we remain protected.”

The darkward draft recognized the district immediately and prohibited the schedule. A central delegate asked what weather replaced it. Hesh answered that the question belonged to allocation, which satisfied the law and frightened everyone responsible for keeping a mirror plan open.

Senn drew a third version. Recognition took effect upon the habitation finding. A temporary operating schedule could continue only if it held the new district above its measured floor, published every concession, and expired when the negotiated allocation began. Emergency authority could move discretionary services but could not cross any floor.

“Who certifies the measured minimum?” asked Caldrift.

“Existing Charter staff, using the method already applied to your district,” Senn said. “The local representative receives the calculation and may challenge any input.”

Fen tapped the hypothetical. “Your solution assumes enough discretionary room exists.”

“No. It requires the temporary schedule to expose a shortfall if room does not exist. Then reserve authority, voluntary concessions, or an emergency delegate session must close it. Hidden extraction no longer qualifies as a solution.”

Pell placed Senn's version between the competing drafts. It did not settle the language. It changed the dispute from whether recognition could wait to who carried responsibility when a lawful floor made the old schedule impossible.

A canal delegate proposed “shall be treated henceforth as having possessed.” Even Pell removed his glasses at that.

“Language cannot walk both directions,” he said.

By midday, six drafts lay crossed through on the board. Senn ate a seed cake at the witness table while listening to two reservoir delegates dispute whether an unenforced right could be called immediate. Hesh refused food from the chamber service tray and shared a packet of dried mushrooms with the Eyr observer across the rail.

Ceryn arrived after the recess carrying no staff. Her testimony from the policy audit had ended, but Pell had summoned her to answer implementation questions. She read the split board and asked, “Who holds emergency authority between recognition and the first compliant schedule?”

Fen gestured toward her. “The Conservatory, unless this hearing plans to command mirrors by paragraph.”

Ceryn ignored the flourish. “Current reserve authority permits temporary departures from district preferences, not from climate floors. Once these floors enter the register, my office cannot authorize a forecast that crosses them.”

The Caldrift delegate said, “Does that make immediate recognition impossible?”

“No. It makes preparation urgent.”

Fen looked at her sharply. “You testified yesterday that present authority cannot correct the category.”

“It cannot. The delegates can.”

“And then your office may lack a valid schedule.”

“For a limited interval, yes.” Ceryn folded her hands. “Government sometimes creates an obligation before administration has perfected compliance. That does not recommend casual timing. It does recommend honesty about which difficulty belongs to law and which belongs to engineering.”

Senn heard the shift because Ceryn did not soften it. She had defended delay as famine prevention. Now she described delay as an administrative problem rather than a reason to deny recognition.

A northern district delegate asked, “What preparation can begin before a vote?”

Ceryn named forecast isolation, reserve verification, tower access, and publication of inherited categories. “The Conservatory can perform technical work under audit authority. It cannot assign the darkward communities a floor until you recognize them.”

Hesh spoke without a lamp. “Nor should it assign our floor without us.”

Pell glanced toward them. “That point returns us to participation.”

“Then let it return.”

The chair called a closed count of delegate positions, not a vote on any language. Clerks distributed thin white slips with four choices: recognition before complete remedy; remedy before recognition; simultaneous action only; undecided. Each delegate pressed a district seal and folded the slip once.

Senn watched the bowl move through forty-one seats. Some decisions showed in posture. The Orison delegate held his slip until the clerk waited. Caldrift sealed quickly. Two northern districts exchanged no glance, disciplined enough to avoid the appearance of coordination. Fen wrote notes without looking up.

Pell and three clerks counted behind a low screen. No totals appeared publicly. The chair read the result alone, conferred with the procedural clerk, and ordered a second count. The galleries remained silent enough for Senn to hear warm water ticking through the old canal pipes under the floor.

At last Pell came back to his table. “A sufficient bloc exists to introduce recognition language. Positions on final text remain unsettled. This count authorizes drafting time only.”

A release of breath moved around the chamber. Hesh did not relax.

Fen asked, “Will the chair identify the sponsors?”

“When they attach their names to language.”

“Then counsel cannot test whether the bloc survives specific obligations.”

“That is why we will write the obligations.”

The board cleared. Pell requested draft clauses from any delegate by the second morning bell. Three lamps lit before he finished the sentence. Caldrift offered the first sponsorship. The western reservoirs joined. A small high-light district whose glassworks depended on stable winter accepted recognition if the floor method used existing Charter calculations rather than a newly negotiated minimum.

Senn marked each condition on paper. Agreement had not formed around generosity. It formed where narrow interests could survive a distinction: standing now, staged allocation after, no district beneath floor. That made the bloc less beautiful and more durable.

The Orison delegate requested a separate clause preserving hospitals and seed-start functions during bloom retirement. Hesh said the request belonged in implementation, not recognition. For once, Fen agreed.

“Then we have discovered a shared filing system,” Pell said.

Even Hesh smiled briefly.

During the afternoon, Senn worked with Pera at a side table to trace every immediate consequence of entry in the habitation register. Climate-floor protection came first. District notice followed. Audit access followed notice. Voting rights required a certification schedule under a different Charter article, which meant full delegate status could not appear by simple hearing order. Compensation claims entered ordinary review and did not arise automatically from recognition.

Hesh read the list over Senn's shoulder. “They will use voting delay to keep us outside the room that decides the remedy.”

“Yes.”

“You say that as if you have already found their clause.”

“Fen's clerk requested the certification article four minutes ago.”

Hesh looked toward the counsel table. “Can the hearing grant us a working place without inventing a delegate?”

“Pell can appoint technical participants to a negotiating group. They receive the same documents and speaking access, but no vote in the chamber.”

“That sounds like provisional witness standing with a better chair.”

“It can be, unless the order grants co-drafting authority and direct objections on the record.”

“Can Pell do that?”

“The procedure does not forbid it.”

Hesh's thumb moved along the worn edge of the Charter. “Your profession has an alarming fondness for gaps.”

“Only the useful ones.”

“What would Fen call it?”

“Improper delegation of legislative preparation.”

“Then he has probably drafted the objection.”

Across the room, Fen's clerk requested the negotiating-group rule.

Hesh gave Senn a flat look. “Your predictive gifts grow oppressive.”

They wrote a participation order in eleven lines. Recognized settlements would each name one representative to the implementation group upon adoption of the preliminary finding. Before that finding, Pell could appoint Hesh as a party witness with direct drafting access because Lorn Dusk's evidence formed the proceeding's subject. Every technical model would be shared in the same form and at the same time with all group members. Objections could enter the clause record without a delegate sponsor.

Pera read the draft and crossed out “consultative.”

“Consultative means invited to speak after everyone decides,” she said.

Hesh replaced it with “negotiating.”

Fen challenged the order before ink dried. He argued that a witness could not negotiate planetary obligations. Caldrift replied that central counsel already negotiated without holding a delegate seat. A reservoir delegate observed that the hearing had relied on Lorn Dusk records while denying their keeper equal access to remedy documents. Ceryn, when Pell asked for operational effect, said the Conservatory preferred one authorized representative rather than receiving contradictory submissions through sponsors.

“Preference does not settle authority,” Fen said.

“No,” Ceryn replied. “But your objection asked whether equal document access would obstruct administration. It would not.”

The day darkened only by civic convention. Outside the chamber's high windows, Varda's fixed light remained copper and low, but interior panels cooled toward evening blue. Clerks carried draft pages between desks. Delegates began leaving for district calls, each departure reducing the chamber's certainty.

Senn found Tovin waiting in the outer corridor with a maintenance dispatch tucked into his coat. He had come to report reserve readiness to Ceryn and refused to enter the hearing while anyone might ask him a constitutional question.

“How many?” he asked.

“Enough to write.”

“That sounds unlike enough to win.”

“It differs.”

“Does Hesh get a lamp?”

“Not yet.”

Tovin looked through the open doors toward the witness table. “Then you have enough people to write about them.”

Senn folded her position notes. “We are fixing that.”

“Good. Fix the tower parts next. Language has terrible thermal conductivity.”

He left for the service stairs before she could tell him that Dael would have approved the complaint.

On the third morning, Pell reconvened with nine proposed recognition clauses and five implementation reservations. No text held a majority because no text had been called. He forbade delegates from describing the closed position count as an outcome. The white slips authorized work, nothing more.

He read the procedural schedule aloud. Final recognition language would close at the second evening bell. The implementation group would meet through the following six days, with technical drafts preserved for public audit. Formal debate would resume after both sets of language could be placed side by side.

Fen raised his lamp. “And the chair's participation order?”

Pell took the eleven-line page from the clerk. “Adopted under my authority to structure evidentiary remedy conferences. Objections are preserved for review.”

He looked toward Hesh.

“Keeper Dara, you will receive all working models, direct speaking access, co-drafting authority, and a recorded objection channel. You will not cast a delegate vote under this order.”

Hesh stood. “Name the place accurately.”

Pell considered the page, crossed out Pera's rejected word where a clerk had restored it, and wrote the replacement in his own hand.

“The final language session begins at the second bell,” he said. “Hesh Dara will hold a negotiating seat.”

Interlude II — Maintenance Below the Map

CHOIR MAINTENANCE RECORD / YEAR 63 AC / TURN 2 / MIRROR 117 EAST EDGE
Filed by: D. Orro, lattice engineer
Condition: Reflected clinic window under target by 0.08 percent following canal haze correction.
Action: East edge held 4.7 seconds past standard release.
Variance: Within instrument tolerance. No district schedule affected.
Disposition: Close.

LOCAL COPY / LORN DUSK HEAT DESK / SAME WINDOW
Window arrived short, then strengthened during final four seconds. Treatment room 2 retained sterilizer heat. Six appointments completed. Conservatory notice: none.

Keeper's margin: Ask tower crew who held the edge.

Crew reply, unsigned: Nobody held anything. The mirror happened to remember us.

CHOIR MAINTENANCE RECORD / YEAR 64 AC / TURN 1 / TOWER 11, EYR
Filed by: M. Aven, ceramic-vane apprentice
Return-air temperature rose 0.6 degrees during bearing purge. Automatic schedule called for venting to exterior. Local crew delayed vent by ninety seconds and routed purge heat through apprenticeship hall intake.

Reason: Twenty-three trainees occupying hall during lamp failure.

Central review: Service occupancy does not qualify for civic-heat priority. Future purge delays require tower supervisor approval.

Supervisor note: Approval granted retrospectively. Replace “trainees” with “maintenance personnel” in transmitted copy.

Local archive retained original wording.

NACRE FOLD CLINIC / YEAR 65 AC / TURN 4 / SHELL-LIME CORRIDOR
Light cart battery fell below surgical reserve after two promised windows were cancelled. Technician I. Sorel redirected reflective inspection panels from lava-tube seam survey for thirty-eight minutes. Corridor temperature remained below comfort minimum. Pharmacy cabinet reached safe range.

Materials borrowed: four shell-lime panels, eight clamps, two hand lamps.

Materials returned: all, with one clamp bent.

Administrative classification: Equipment protection.

Patient note attached by clinic staff: Medicine does not become equipment merely because a cabinet holds it.

Attachment omitted from central summary.

CONSERVATORY LOAD RECONCILIATION / YEAR 66 AC / QUARTER 3
Analyst query: Repeated positive offsets appear at darkward radiator feeds immediately before medical reserve peaks. Aggregate effect remains below 0.2 percent. Are local sensors drifting under cold conditions?

Engineering response, D. Orro: Sensors agree within calibration. Offsets arise from timing corrections needed to prevent thermal shock during irregular demand.

Analyst query: Define irregular demand.

Engineering response: Loads not represented in the inherited schedule.

Policy annotation: Accept explanation. Do not expand occupancy category in maintenance workflow.

ORISON CANAL CREW 8 / YEAR 67 AC / BLOOM TURN / NIGHT SHIFT HANDOVER
Outgoing water exceeded storage target by 0.3 degrees. Crew could have dumped heat through western fins before morning hive release. Operator Kes Tal held two fins closed for seven minutes after receiving a tower-balance request from an unknown darkward feed.

Day supervisor: The hold advanced canal warmth. Why accept an unauthenticated request?

Kes Tal: Feed carried a Conservatory maintenance key.

Day supervisor: Which engineer?

Kes Tal: Key record expired before review.

Corrective action: None. Apricot terraces opened normally. Canal account absorbed the difference.

Private handover line, preserved on paper: Someone needed seven minutes of our warm water more than we needed an exact morning.

EYR TOWER 11 / YEAR 68 AC / TURN 2 / CERAMIC VANE INSPECTION
Three cracked vanes removed. Standard procedure required closing the south service gallery. Crew kept one intake open because the adjacent school heat wall shared the gallery return.

Risk accepted: Ice accumulation on inspection rail.

Mitigation: Two mechanics worked tethered; one apprentice cleared frost every twelve minutes.

Central instruction: Shut intake and relocate any temporary occupants.

Local response: Occupants number 184. Relocation distance exceeds safe heated transit during current window.

Central disposition: Gallery may remain open under equipment-continuity exception.

Eyr copyist's note: The children completed arithmetic while the equipment-continuity exception warmed their hands.

NACRE FOLD MUNICIPAL WORKSHOP / YEAR 69 AC / TURN 1
A radiator command requested rapid draw at the same hour as fungal cultures in the clinic required stable light. Workshop crews polished two unfinished wall panels and placed them opposite the clinic shaft. No request entered the Conservatory queue because service precincts lacked a civic preference channel.

Measured gain: 1.9 lumen-hours at cabinet level.

Measured cost: seam survey delayed one shift; three workers reassigned; shell dust entered the west ventilation screen.

Cleaning completed before evening.

Forecast effect: none visible at district scale.

CONSERVATORY CENTRAL CONTROL / YEAR 70 AC / SHEAR WATCH
Mirror operator R. Im lowered Orison commercial bloom by 0.04 percent to reduce a narrow wind edge. The resulting spare angle traveled darkward through automatic balancing and strengthened two service windows.

Supervisor: Commercial allocation remained inside tolerance. No report needed.

R. Im: Which services received the spare angle?

Supervisor: Tower auxiliaries.

R. Im: Telemetry lists domestic water rise and a school-cycle start.

Supervisor: Those fields enter as auxiliary load.

R. Im: Then auxiliary load has morning classes.

Supervisor: Close the line, Operator.

Line closed. Personal shift book retained the exchange.

LORN DUSK CLINIC / YEAR 71 AC / WINTER TURN / TREATMENT ROOM 2
Promised light delayed by forty-two minutes. Local heat desk requested a mirror-edge correction through the maintenance channel. Request denied: no active fault.

At minute fourteen, tower 15 crew began a coolant recirculation test six hours early. Waste heat entered the shared wall behind treatment rooms 1 through 3.

At minute twenty-nine, the test paused without completing its diagnostic cycle.

At minute forty-two, reflected light arrived.

Tower report: Test timing selected to match crew availability.

Clinic report: Eleven patients remained warm.

Keeper's note: Both statements can be accurate. Only one explains the hour.

CROSS-SYSTEM VARIANCE REVIEW / YEAR 72 AC / INTERNAL WORKING PAPER
Sample: 436 sub-tolerance deviations across five turns.

Findings: Darkward crews delayed radiator venting during civic-heat drops. Central mirror operators trimmed discretionary bloom during shear margins. Canal workers retained small quantities of heat after unidentified service requests. Nacre Fold staff used survey reflectors to protect medicine. Eyr mechanics routed purge warmth through occupied halls. No common instruction linked the actions.

Estimated combined effect: insufficient to alter planetary crop yield; sufficient to prevent thirty-one recorded local floor excursions, if habitation floors are applied retrospectively.

Reviewer comment: “Floor excursion” inappropriate for unregistered precincts.

Analyst revision: sufficient to prevent thirty-one measured temperature events below the minimum used in comparable districts.

Disposition: Working paper not advanced. Deviations remain within maintenance tolerance.

CHOIR SERVICE RING / YEAR 73 AC / MIRROR 83 ACTUATOR REPLACEMENT
A replacement actuator arrived with a slower release curve than specification. Ring crew compensated by advancing command start. That advance created a fractional light excess over central glassworks.

Ground control offered two corrections: shade the excess locally or pass the angle into darkward balancing.

Ring crew selected darkward balancing after reviewing tower demand.

Reason entered: Lower system wear.

Crew voice note: Tower demand included domestic heaters, though the screen refused that name. We chose the lower wear. We also chose the houses.

Voice note remained in the service copy and did not enter policy accounting.

NACRE FOLD / YEAR 74 AC / MEDICINE TRANSIT WINDOW
Delivery sled required a heated guide line through the lava-tube east bend. Scheduled line activation overlapped radiator draw. A central technician divided the activation into three pulses, each too small to trigger manual policy review.

Sled arrived nine minutes late. Insulin cultures remained viable. Radiator output stayed inside tolerance.

Maintenance audit: Pulse fragmentation increased operator workload and reduced diagnostic clarity.

Technician response: A single authorized pulse would have been clearer. The district had no channel to request one.

Audit recommendation: Consider formal service-request path.

Policy response: Defer pending precinct review.

No precinct review opened.

CONSERVATORY / YEAR 75 AC / COMPILED CREW NOTE FOR HEARING EVIDENCE
Twelve years of records show no master plan and no coordinated concealment among the crews. Small acts arose independently wherever the formal schedule omitted a clinic, a school, a medicine transit, a heat wall, or a household morning. Engineers used tolerance. Operators traded fractions. Mechanics renamed people as equipment when accurate language would have blocked the work.

The adjustments did not repair the governing category. They kept specific rooms warm and shifted specific risks into margins other districts could spare. Their scale remained too small, their authority too narrow, and their record too scattered to serve as a lawful allocation system.

Final crew finding: Planetary stability already depended on unacknowledged sharing.

Act IV — The Weather That Belongs to Everyone

Chapter 22 — The Shared Winter

Dael pressed a heated probe against the model tower's return pipe and waited for the frost to release its grip on the sensor collar.

The negotiating room had acquired machinery because conversation alone kept producing impossible weather. A waist-high rig occupied the center: four radiator loops made from narrow copper tube, three mirror lamps mounted on jointed arms, a shade membrane, two water trays, and forty-one ceramic tiles representing district demand. Tovin had built it from training parts and rejected valves. Every leak left a dark circle on the old pressureworks floor.

“Your tower is wetting the Charter,” Pera said.

A drop fell from the return pipe onto a stack of clause drafts.

“Then move the Charter,” Dael said.

“It has survived worse handling.”

She lifted the pages. Hesh kept one hand on the Lorn Dusk tile while Senn adjusted floor values at the control slate. Rul Fen stood opposite them with three central technical advisers. Ceryn occupied the end of the table by Pell's procedural clerk, present as administrator of any rule the delegates might later authorize. She had brought no Conservatory insignia, but every operator still looked toward her when the rig made an unfamiliar sound.

Dael warmed the collar until it turned. The sensor beneath showed a slow response, exactly the fault he wanted. “Run inherited demand.”

Senn started the pumps.

Light strengthened over the seven central tiles. Heat entered the water trays, moved through the copper loops, and reached the radiator branches. Lorn Dusk, Eyr, and Nacre Fold appeared as gray service pieces beyond the district arc. The rig followed current policy. When central crop demand rose, one valve pushed more cold through the gray branches. A rim of frost formed around Hesh's tile.

Fen pointed to the pressure gauge. “Stable.”

“For whom?” Hesh asked.

“For the assembly.”

Dael tapped the delayed sensor. “And only because this instrument reports enough spare draw. Tower 15 no longer has it. Three others lie in similar directions.”

He closed the faulty branch by hand. Pressure climbed along the central loop. One water tray began to steam. Tovin reached past him and opened the shade membrane before the safety pin released.

“Nine seconds,” Tovin said. “On the real field, you get days. The principle remains discourteous.”

Dael reset the rig. His suspended license prohibited him from issuing a live Choir command, yet no rule prevented him from making a tabletop leak in front of constitutional negotiators. He had decided to enjoy the distinction.

Senn cleared the inherited weights. “We have five requirements agreed in principle. No district crosses its climate floor. Burden draws only from discretionary surplus. Inherited categories become visible. Physical repairs enter the schedule rather than a maintenance annex. Orison's excess bloom retires in stages.”

“Agreed by whom?” Fen asked.

“Everyone at this table has accepted at least four.”

“That phrasing avoids my question.”

“It records your position accurately.”

Dael had watched them spend two hours on whether a principle could be accepted provisionally. Copper behaved better. It leaked, bent, hardened, and failed without reserving a procedural objection.

He moved the three darkward tiles into the district arc. Each gained a red line at its measured minimum. Then he placed transparent caps over all forty-one tiles. The caps represented protected floors. Weather above them could move. Weather below them could not enter negotiation.

“Start with low demand,” he said.

The pumps turned. Senn entered the coming forecast. Mirror lamps sent pale circles across the model. Under floor protection, the old winter debit could not open. Excess heat backed into central water. Dael reduced ornamental bloom in Orison, shifted a glassworks heating cycle, delayed two canal-wash periods, and lowered an uncommitted rain preference from Caldrift. Each change released a fraction. Together they kept pressure below the warning line.

Fen's adviser checked the control slate. “You selected concessions manually.”

“Yes.”

“The planetary schedule contains thousands.”

“The Almanac can rank them once policy defines what may move. Today we define the rule.”

“What happens when ranked surplus does not close the balance?”

“Reserve action, voluntary calls, or delegate review. Hidden extraction no longer sits at the bottom as an automatic answer.”

Hesh rotated their tile. “Show the floor during tower failure.”

Dael disabled the Lorn Dusk branch. Cold demand shifted toward Eyr and Nacre Fold. Their caps stopped the transfer before either minimum broke. The rig then drew small fractions from every tile with available surplus. Some gave light, others accepted colder water, deferred cloud seeding, or increased wind-risk tolerance within local limits.

On the board, no single concession looked heroic. That mattered. His twelve years of covert adjustments had taught the same lesson at an unlawful scale. Stability came from many margins, not from choosing a place that could not object.

“What do you call the call order?” Pell's clerk asked.

“Surplus cascade,” said a central adviser.

Hesh's mouth tightened. “That sounds like water falling on whoever lives downhill.”

“Distributed reserve ladder,” Pera offered.

Tovin shook his head. “A ladder implies someone gets to the top.”

Fen said, “Planetary continuity schedule.”

“That could mean anything written inside this building,” Senn replied.

Dael watched frost retreat from the protected tiles. The model did not promise equal winters. Eyr's geography still demanded longer cold. Orison still held more greenhouse stone, hospital storage, and flexible commercial light. Nacre Fold could not surrender medicine-transit windows merely because another district had surrendered ornamental bloom. The rule shared only what places could lose without crossing the line that made habitation possible.

“Shared winter protocol,” he said.

The room stopped trying names.

Hesh looked at him. “Winter has been shared with us for years.”

“Imposed on you,” Dael said. “I am naming the rule that prevents one district from carrying the whole correction. If the phrase conceals that history, reject it.”

They considered the protected tiles. “Keep it for the draft. The clauses must say what sharing permits.”

Pera wrote the title at the top of a clean page.

Fen requested a definition. Senn began with legal structure, but Dael interrupted before the language hardened around abstraction.

“First rule,” he said. “Every schedule loads the climate floors before any preference. Not after optimization. Not as a warning operators can override. The minimum enters as a fixed physical demand.”

Ceryn asked, “Who certifies a floor change after population or infrastructure shifts?”

“Charter method, local data, independent audit.”

“Frequency?”

“Each turn, with emergency review for material change.”

Hesh added, “Local reviewers receive the raw instruments, not a district summary.”

Pera entered it in the margin.

“Second rule,” Dael continued. “Only discretionary burden moves. The system publishes what each district holds above floor by service class. No undifferentiated surplus total. A clinic reserve cannot hide beside commercial blossom and become tradeable by arithmetic.”

Fen objected to publishing exact hospital reserves. Ceryn agreed on security grounds. Senn proposed bands, methods, and sealed figures available to authorized auditors. Hesh demanded those auditors include the affected districts. The clerk opened a bracket around the whole clause.

Dael let them argue while he changed the rig's service classes. Red caps protected hospital heat, staple-crop survival, civic water, shelter warmth, and minimum school light. Amber rings marked preferences that could move under defined conditions. Green rings showed voluntarily offered shares. The colors turned the negotiating table into a shallow, uneven city.

“Third rule,” he said when the bracketed language stopped growing. “Every inherited category appears in the public policy register with originating authority, last human review, and current measured contradiction. No label continues merely because the next schedule copies the last.”

Ceryn read the proposed trigger. “Measured contradiction requires a threshold. One domestic heater in an industrial tract cannot open constitutional review.”

“Continuous civic heat, an address ledger, or local births and burials already supply the habitation test,” Senn said. “Any one should open review. The full test decides status.”

Fen wanted two indicators. Hesh refused. “Lorn Dusk had all three and still disappeared. Do not design the next silence around a community that can preserve only one record.”

The two-indicator proposal remained bracketed, marked disputed.

Tovin crouched beside the radiator loops. “You have policy, but the model still assumes metal we do not possess.”

Dael handed him the probe. “Fourth rule.”

They opened the repair schedule. Tower 15 needed a bearing cradle and two coolant seals. Tower 14 required return-pipe reinforcement where cyclic cold had embrittled a weld. Eyr's tower 11 needed ceramic vane replacement across the south bank. The Nacre branch relied on a control manifold whose slow sensor could conceal pressure rise for six minutes.

“Four towers,” Dael said. “Urgent work enters the correction schedule as capacity restoration, not optional maintenance. Until each repair clears load test, the allocation uses degraded capacity.”

A central adviser asked what that cost.

“Crew hours, ceramic vanes, two crop-alloy shipments, one mirror window for inspection, and reduced radiator draw during each shutdown.”

“Which districts surrender the inspection light?”

“Determine it through discretionary ranking. Do not preassign the answer darkward.”

Tovin placed a black marker beside reserve shade-kite 6 on the board. Its fabric fault had been repaired after the shear deployment, though the kite remained outside ordinary fleet assumptions. “List reserve 6 as transitional storm protection, not permanent capacity. If you make one emergency flight carry a constitutional promise, someone will eventually fly torn cloth.”

The clerk recorded a use limit and mandatory inspection interval.

By evening, condensation covered the room's lower windows. Dael flexed his burned fingers beside a warm pipe. The rig had run thirty-seven scenarios. Nine failed floors because an operator had loaded preferences before minimums. Six exceeded wind limits when tower degradation entered late. Four required reserve action. Eighteen closed through distributed discretionary cuts.

One failure deserved more than a mark in the reject pile. A simulated ice fracture took tower 14 offline while a high-light shear front prevented reserve 6 from opening to its full span. The automatic ranking exhausted commercial bloom, flexible furnace heat, and rain preferences. It still lacked enough darkward draw to hold the central wind edge.

Fen pointed to Lorn Dusk's workshop margin. “That tile remains above floor.”

“By a narrow amount,” Dael said. “It can surrender part.”

Hesh moved the workshop ring down one increment. The pressure gauge eased but stayed amber.

Eyr offered ceramic-kiln heat in the model. Nacre Fold had no further safe light above medicine transit and shelter demand. Orison's protected seed-start block held a large reserve under its current classification.

Senn opened the block. “Half these trays grow export flowers under a municipal seed license.”

Fen's adviser objected. “The trays share heat loops with staple starts. Operators cannot separate them during the current turn.”

Tovin bent over the water tray and followed its tubing. “Not at the distribution header. They can isolate two greenhouse rows at the local manifolds.”

“Those manifolds do not report to the Conservatory.”

“Then the schedule needs a verified local action, not a fictional indivisible block.”

Dael replaced the single seed tile with three smaller pieces. Staple starts kept their cap. Hospital cultivars retained theirs. Export flowers entered discretionary ranking. The new concession closed half the remaining gap.

Ceryn asked the room to find the rest without touching a floor. Nobody answered quickly. The rig's pump clicked against the amber pressure. In live control, that sound would have come from styluses, alarms, and operators waiting for authority while wind continued moving.

Pera reviewed district offers from the prior emergency. “Three canal cities authorized wash-water delay during the early winter pulse. Their permission expired, but the equipment path still exists.”

“Permission cannot be assumed,” Hesh said.

“Then issue a call with the exact quantity.”

The clerk wrote a simulated public request. Two canal districts accepted delay in the exercise. A third refused because its water stores stood below preferred range. Their combined offer left a small deficit.

Dael reduced Lorn Dusk's workshop ring again and stopped before the red cap. “No more there.”

Fen studied Orison's school extensions. “Move one evening block.”

“Which block?” Senn asked.

“Any one that closes the balance.”

“That answer recreates an aggregate. Name the service.”

The Orison adviser selected adult greenhouse training scheduled during the extended bloom evening. Moving it into rain turn released the final fraction. The pressure needle dropped from amber to green.

Hesh looked around the table. “That took twelve people, local plumbing knowledge, consent calls, and service-level records the current system does not keep together.”

“Which means the operating rules need time limits for escalation,” Dael said. “Automation ranks the clear concessions. Local operators verify separations. Public calls gather voluntary shares. If the gap remains, human authority meets before the forecast closes.”

Ceryn added response windows measured against atmospheric lead time. A slow crop adjustment allowed two days. A developing shear front might allow hours. Nothing permitted silence to convert into consent.

The exercise left wet fingerprints on every district tile. It also established why shortfall procedure could not live in an annex. Hard forecasts would test the constitutional rule precisely when secrecy looked most efficient.

Fen reviewed the failures. “Your success rate does not support adoption.”

“It supports writing the order correctly.”

“You cannot legislate weather into compliance.”

“No. We can stop authorizing the runs that purchase compliance from a hidden district.”

Senn moved the failed sheets into a separate pile. “The protocol needs a shortfall procedure. A failed run becomes public. The Conservatory issues a voluntary call, applies valid reserves, and reconvenes authorized reviewers. It cannot solve the gap by lowering an unrepresented floor.”

“Who bears service interruption while review occurs?” Fen asked.

“Preferences pause in reverse priority,” Dael said. “Commercial and ornamental schedules first. Flexible industrial cycles next. Protected services continue. If those cuts remain insufficient, emergency allocation comes back to human authority.”

Ceryn examined the wording. “Publish the uncertainty range with the call. Otherwise districts will either surrender too much from fear or withhold because they distrust the number.”

Pera added uncertainty, source instruments, and update intervals.

The fifth rule concerned Orison. Its eleven extra bloom days could not vanish without throwing hospital gardens, seed starts, schools, and commercial greenhouses into the same bucket the group had spent all day separating. Senn divided the benefit by service. Protected functions retained enough light to stay above floor. Commercial blossom lost first. Decorative terraces followed. Flexible school extensions moved to rain-turn hours. Hospitals kept targeted exceptions subject to audit.

Dael entered retirement across two turns. The first removed six excess days from the general bloom block while preserving named protected exceptions. The second removed the remaining five. No darkward winter debit would finance the transition. Temporary balance came from distributed surplus, reserve action, and restored tower capacity.

Fen read the schedule twice. “Orison's growers cannot replant a greenhouse economy in two turns.”

“They can change crop timing in two turns,” said Ceryn. “Contracts and municipal supports require separate work. Climate law cannot guarantee every business model built on concealed allocation.”

The room noticed her use of concealed. She did not retract it.

Fen's strongest objection came from uncertainty. If a fungal flare or shear front struck during retirement, emergency need could consume all discretionary room. He wanted authority to suspend the second-stage removal.

Hesh wanted any suspension to require consent from communities carrying added winter risk.

Dael reran the worst forecast with the second stage delayed. Orison kept five days longer. No district crossed floor if all preference cuts took effect. Yet operators could preserve central comfort by classifying more functions as protected. The danger had moved from hidden geography into service definitions.

“Every exception needs a named service, measured floor effect, duration, and review date,” Senn said. “No general urban continuity field.”

Ceryn added, “No inheritance beyond one turn without delegate authorization.”

Pell's clerk drew a box around the language.

Near midnight, they transferred the operational rules from wet pages to a formal draft. Dael read each clause against the rig before allowing it forward. Load every climate floor first. Rank only published discretionary shares. Expose inherited categories and contradictions. Repair degraded capacity under public milestones. Retire Orison's eleven extra days over two turns. Use reserve 6 only within inspected limits. Publish failed schedules and send true shortfalls back to human decision.

The protocol ran again under the room's hard white lamps. Before Senn started it, Dael tightened the wet return collar and marked its delayed sensor for replacement in the public repair annex.

This time, Senn let the Almanac rank concessions under the new order. The lamps dimmed unevenly. Orison's commercial terraces lost the largest share. Caldrift accepted a narrower rain preference. Two glass districts moved furnace cycles. Lorn Dusk surrendered a small workshop-light margin above its newly loaded floor. Hesh watched that tile closely and did not object. Sharing included the right to contribute after protection, not compulsory innocence.

Pressure steadied below the warning line. Tower repairs reduced the burden in later runs. By the second modeled turn, Orison's extra bloom block had retired and no gray sector remained available as a sink.

Fen signed the technical draft as reviewed, not accepted. Ceryn signed for administrative feasibility with six reservations. Tovin signed the reserve and repair annex. Senn signed the floor method.

Dael had no valid license with which to sign an engineering certification. He wrote his name under WITNESS TO MODEL CONSTRUCTION. The demotion hurt less on paper than it had in the service ring.

Pera slid the main draft toward Hesh. “Negotiating representative.”

Hesh read without haste. They checked the floor-loading order, the four towers, the two-turn bloom schedule, and the shortfall publication clause. Their finger stopped at the audit section, where local reviewers received reports but central offices retained credential authority.

“This mechanism can work,” they said. “Provisionally, Lorn Dusk can accept it.”

Fen exhaled through his nose. The clerk reached for the draft.

Hesh kept their hand on the page. “No. Not yet. It needs enforcement language and equal audit power.”

Chapter 23 — Lorn Dusk Speaks

Hesh set the address ledger on the hearing rail and loosened the two straps that had kept it closed from Lorn Dusk to Orison.

The book opened with a wooden sound. Its spine had been repaired four times, first with tower canvas, then with greenhouse cord, then with a strip of coolant hose cut thin enough to fold. Bound pages spread beneath the chamber lamps. Blue thermal ink had browned along the handled edges. Chalk dust remained in the gutter despite Nima's attempt to brush it clean before the journey.

Forty-one delegates looked down from their desks. Behind Hesh, the public galleries held central growers, Conservatory workers, darkward observers, reporters, and families who had watched the hearing through delayed projection until seats opened. The Eyr observer now sat at the negotiating table. Nacre Fold had sent a clinic administrator with three boxes of local records. Neither held a delegate lamp.

Oren Pell leaned toward Hesh. “State your name and authority for the record.”

“Hesh Dara. Elected keeper of Lorn Dusk's addresses, heating failures, births, burials, and promised light.”

“Do you speak for the settlement?”

“I carry authority to present our habitation claim and negotiate correction terms. I do not carry authority to surrender standing.”

Rul Fen shifted a page at counsel's table. Hesh heard it because the chamber had narrowed around the book.

Pell said, “Proceed.”

Hesh turned to the first marked leaf. “The Charter asks three things. Continuous civic heat. An address ledger. Births or burials recorded locally. Counsel has spent nine days discussing whether the test can apply to a place called a service precinct. I will show that Lorn Dusk met every condition before several people in this room took their current offices.”

They placed a transparent strip over the page so the wall camera could read without touching the ink. The earliest surviving entries came from Year 45 AC, one year after Order 9. Construction bunks still carried numbers instead of street names. Beside bunk 6, a mechanic had recorded the birth of Tera Voss during a radiator shutdown. Beside bunk 19, a heater transfer moved from an empty crew bay to a household with two infants.

“The founding order described temporary crews,” Hesh said. “Temporary crews do not usually assign a child's address before assigning her a middle name.”

A few people in the gallery laughed, then stopped when the next line appeared.

Tera Voss, age eight months, respiratory fever, buried locally.

Hesh did not turn toward the gallery. “The burial ground began that winter. It remains in use.”

Fen requested the speaking lamp. “No party disputes that families later occupied the precinct. The legal question concerns recognized civic administration.”

“This book records administration.”

“It records local practice.”

“The Charter does not require a marble office. It requires addresses.”

Fen opened his copy of the statute. “Maintains an address ledger under civic authority.”

Hesh had expected the emphasis. They lifted a narrow packet from the book's back cover. Every third year, Lorn Dusk elected a keeper by marked heater token. The precinct supervisor witnessed early selections because central law barred residents from forming a district council. Later elections used the communal stove register and signatures from every heat-wall block.

“Civic authority can grow where central authority refuses it,” Hesh said. “We assigned water duty, fire response, shelter access, heater priority, school rooms, and burial ground. The Conservatory accepted our load reports whenever it needed radiator work. It rejected the same organization when we asked for weather standing.”

A delegate from the northern mills asked whether the elections had ever received Conservatory certification.

“No.”

“Then how do we verify the records beyond your own custody?”

Hesh turned three pages. “Tower shift books list each keeper as the local contact. Clinic medicine receipts carry the same names. Central maintenance messages address my predecessor as precinct civic coordinator when they need workers moved. Pera Iven authenticated the paper, ink, and transmission seals. You may distrust our title. Your own offices relied on it.”

Pera sat behind the archive table, hands folded over a stack of certifications. She gave no sign beyond lifting the top sheet for the clerk.

Hesh moved to the heat record. Continuous civic heat did not look continuous on paper. It looked like a fight against interruption. Every shared wall had a daily line for intake, loss, repair, and occupied rooms. Tower vibration loosened mortar. Failed light windows froze condensation inside pipes. Families moved between blocks when radiator draw sharpened winter.

They showed a five-year span without a missing day.

“Service equipment did not require this distribution,” Hesh said. “People did. The wall sent heat first to the clinic, then infant rooms, then the school shelter, then households by interior temperature. Workshops accepted the remaining share. When supply fell, the keeper recorded who moved and who stayed.”

Fen asked, “Did the system ever fall below what you now claim as a climate floor?”

“Often.”

“Then continuous civic heat may not have been sustained under the Charter definition.”

Hesh looked at him. “A recognized district does not lose standing when a storm breaks a heater. It receives emergency protection because people live there. You propose that our injuries prove our absence.”

The words reached the chamber without force. Fen lowered his eyes to the statute.

Hesh opened the birth and burial index. Nima had helped prepare it, but she had refused to condense the names into a total for this testimony. A total already existed elsewhere in the public record. Today the names needed to perform a different task. They established continuity across generations.

“Ilven Dara,” Hesh read. “Born in bunk row 4. Later assigned 4 Cooling Lane when residents named the roads. Died at tower 15, bearing accident, age thirty-two. Mara Olt, born in the clinic annex, became a school mechanic. Still living. Oth Pell, no relation to the chair, arrived as a six-month contract worker and remained forty years. Buried under the east heat wall because the ground beyond it could not be cut during winter.”

Pell's expression did not change at the shared surname.

Name after name crossed the wall with an address, a duration, and an event no equipment register could contain. Hesh did not read all 8,140 current residents. Across Lorn Dusk, Eyr, and Nacre Fold, the evidence covered 18,420 people: 8,140 here, 6,030 in Eyr, and 4,250 in Nacre Fold. They read enough to show streets replacing bunks, apprentices replacing founders, children inheriting repaired rooms, and dead people remaining part of local geography.

A delegate from Orison asked to compare one household against central records. The clerk selected Mara Olt without warning. Pera opened Conservatory archives on a second panel. A tower requisition listed Mara at age fourteen as a dependent receiving shared-wall heat. An apprenticeship transfer listed her at seventeen. A maintenance injury report named her as the mechanic responsible for school ventilation. Three systems had tracked one life while the district register carried none of it.

“Could those records belong to different people?” the delegate asked.

Hesh supplied Mara's local address changes. Bunk annex 3 became 8 Vane Street after residents named the road. She later moved to the school block, then into her mother's room after the older woman lost mobility. Every date aligned with central heater demand and work assignments.

Pera overlaid the records. “Instrument and paper continuity support one individual.”

The delegate chose a second name, Oth Pell. His six-month contract appeared in Year 46. Central personnel files ended at contract expiry. The local book continued through his partnership registration, two children, tower retirement, stove duty, and burial. Heater telemetry followed his household across thirty-nine years but never restored his name to the civic index.

“Central records did not know he stayed,” Fen said.

“Central weather records knew his room consumed heat every day,” Hesh replied. “The office knew enough to charge the load. It chose not to ask who required it.”

Pell allowed a third comparison. This time Hesh chose Ressa. Her birth appeared locally. School-cycle demand, clinic vaccinations, and shelter occupancy appeared centrally under equipment service. The public schedule that missed her promised dawn carried no field where her name could have entered.

Three lives made the administrative split visible: a worker followed while useful, an elder counted only through heat, and a child translated into infrastructure from birth.

The western reservoir delegate asked, “Why did Lorn Dusk retain these records when the central register refused them?”

“So a heater crew could find people in darkness. So medicine could arrive. So families could prove who belonged to a room after evacuation. So the dead did not vanish when a supervisor's contract ended.”

“Did you maintain them for a future standing case?”

“My predecessors expected review after five years. Then after the first renewal. Then after the second. Hope becomes a filing practice if denied long enough.”

Hesh disliked the sentence as soon as it left them. It sounded prepared, though it had not been. They put both hands on the ledger until its rough cover corrected their balance.

Senn watched from the technical table, still enough to avoid turning witness into encouragement. Dael sat beside her under the hearing permission that allowed technical attendance despite his suspended license. His damaged fingers rested near the protocol model, not touching its controls.

Pell admitted the ledger packet as habitation evidence. Fen reserved objections to retrospective effect. Three delegates requested copies of the keeper election records. The clerk marked the requests and moved to remedy questions.

A central canal representative spoke first. “If this body recognizes continuous habitation, what compensation does Lorn Dusk seek?”

Hesh closed the book halfway. “Standing.”

“That concerns status. I asked about remedy for past injury.”

“Status determines who sits in the room when remedy gets divided.”

“Compensation could begin before delegate certification. Heating grants, clinic support, tower investment, household payments.”

“Money sent to a precinct still calls us a precinct.”

The representative frowned. “Surely material repair matters more than terminology.”

“Then give us authority over the repair and see whether terminology still looks small.”

Hesh had watched central offices use compensation in two ways. Sometimes it returned what could be returned. More often it purchased an ending. A payment form named a completed incident, a recipient, and a release. Weather harm did not end when the form closed. The next winter schedule still needed someone empowered to challenge its categories.

They opened the ledger to the page where the early winter pulse had turned the coolant-cistern theater into a warm shelter. Names filled two columns beside borrowed heat bricks and clinic checks.

“Lorn Dusk will accept repair funds,” Hesh said. “We will accept clinic heat, tower parts, burial support, and payments owed to households. We will not accept any package that leaves standing undecided or assigns a central office sole control over distribution. Compensation without standing converts us from residents into claimants. Claimants can be paid and dismissed.”

Fen said, “Full standing also creates obligations. Audit costs. Surplus declarations. Emergency concessions. Participation in planetary shortfall.”

“Yes.”

“You do not seek exemption from shared burden?”

“No. We seek protection of the same floor and authority under the same rules.”

A glass-district delegate turned on her lamp. “Your negotiating draft permits Lorn Dusk to surrender workshop light above floor. Some residents may ask why a harmed community should contribute at all.”

“They have asked.”

“What did you answer?”

“That standing does not mean weather becomes free. It means nobody can take our minimum by naming us equipment. Above that minimum, we live on the same planet as everyone else.”

The gallery behind Hesh shifted. Not every darkward observer accepted the answer. An Eyr mechanic folded his arms. The Nacre administrator watched the protocol pages rather than Hesh.

Pell invited questions on staging. This portion carried greater danger than Fen's objections because it divided the settlements themselves. Some Lorn Dusk heat-wall blocks wanted immediate cancellation of every winter debit, regardless of central consequences. They had buried people while Orison's apricots opened early. Asking them to wait through two turns sounded like another official version of later.

Hesh lifted the authority packet from the ledger. “My mandate permits staged correction under four conditions. Floors take effect immediately. The inherited debit loses automatic authority. Every transitional burden appears publicly. Affected communities participate in enforcement.”

Fen said, “Why accept staging at all?”

“Because Dael Orro showed what immediate cancellation would do.”

Dael lowered his gaze to the tabletop.

“Nine days to dangerous central heat,” Hesh continued. “A sharpened shear field over Orison. Hospital and crop systems built around light they should never have received in this quantity, but people now depend on them. If we demand a switch and another district becomes the place where unmanaged harm lands, we have learned only how to move the sink.”

The phrase entered Hesh's mouth from twelve years of maintenance notes and one wet negotiating table. They said it more plainly.

“No new sink.”

The clerk wrote the words on the remedy board.

A Lorn Dusk voice called from the upper gallery. “What about the old one?”

Pell's hand moved toward order, but Hesh turned first. Ressa stood beside Nima at the rail, older now than the child who had waited by a chalk mark, young enough that anger still lifted her onto her toes.

“The old sink receives a floor, repairs, standing, and a record that cannot be hidden,” Hesh said. “It does not receive permission to choose another.”

“Two turns still means more winter.”

“Yes.”

“Whose promise pays for that?”

“Every district's discretionary share, including ours where we have one. Orison retires the eleven added days. The towers regain capacity. If the plan asks Lorn Dusk to cross floor, we stop it.”

“Who stops it?”

That question reached the unfinished part of the protocol. Hesh turned back to the delegates.

“Under the current draft, central auditors certify floor calculations and publish reports. Local reviewers may comment. Comment cannot stop a mirror.”

Ceryn requested the lamp. “Operational authority cannot fragment across forty-one local vetoes during a live event.”

“Neither can it remain whole inside the office that continued Order 9.”

“I did not propose that it should.”

“You proposed credentialed local access under central standards.”

“Because raw control access carries planetary risk.”

Hesh rested a finger on the early winter shelter page. “We are discussing audit power, not mirror command. If a schedule varies a climate floor, local auditors must see the raw inputs, challenge the calculation, and withhold consent before authorization.”

Fen stood. “That creates a veto over planetary allocation.”

“It creates a prohibition against taking a non-tradeable minimum.”

“Emergency forecasts may not permit consent from every affected district.”

“Then write an emergency rule narrow enough to prove its need afterward.”

Senn switched on her lamp. “A climate floor cannot be varied as ordinary allocation. If catastrophe requires a temporary breach to prevent greater loss, the action needs recorded necessity, equal notice, time limit, and automatic review. The affected district must retain authority to challenge continuation.”

Hesh heard the word temporary and thought of Order 9. “No inheritance.”

Pera said from the archive table, “The system can block renewal unless local consent is attached.”

Ceryn considered that. “For a planned variance, yes. For emergency action, local consent may be impossible before execution. Continuation beyond the immediate safety interval could require it.”

“How long?” asked Pell.

Dael answered. “Tie the interval to the physical event. A shear intervention might require hours. A tower fracture might require days. Set an absolute ceiling and demand a named engineering basis for anything shorter.”
