“Pulse authorized,” Hesh said.

Mirror angle shifted. Light climbed off the chalk wall and narrowed across the ceiling. Warmth entered the service corridor. Frost released from the thread in a white crumble. The mechanics turned the valve one quarter, then another. Pressure rose in the school line.

“Full flow,” Marek called.

Tovin restored the angle. The hall brightened again. Pera's ledger added two minutes to the extension automatically because the variance had interrupted delivered light.

No one applauded. They kept counting.

Ressa sat on the floor beneath the old chalk bars. The white piece rested across her knees. Hesh joined her while the final minutes passed.

“Was Orison frightened?” she asked.

“Yes.”

“Were we?”

“Yes.”

“Did that make it fair?”

Hesh looked at the old marks. Fair had been used too often for arithmetic that distributed harm neatly and left authority untouched.

“It made this a shared winter,” they said. “Fair depends on what we do when the next line fails.”

The extension clock reached zero. The local clerk checked total delivered duration against the primary light sensor, not the central schedule. Eighty-three minutes had arrived. Intensity varied within the consented range. Clinic heat stayed above floor. The school beds recovered. Every interruption carried a visible record.

Reflected dawn began its scheduled withdrawal. The rectangle on the wall softened at the edges.

Ressa stood. She did not wipe away any of the older bars. Hesh had not asked her to. She placed the chalk beside the newest empty space and drew one short line, then wrote the date and duration beneath it in the careful hand of someone who expected another person to verify the claim.

Beside the missed promises, she added the kept dawn.

Chapter 28 — The Conservatory of Broken Seasons

Senn failed the first exercise because she trusted the central timestamp.

The local auditors let her complete the entire reconstruction before pointing to the wall clock. It ran seven minutes ahead of Conservatory standard, not from neglect but by district agreement. Lorn Dusk used the advance on tower-service mornings so school heat checks finished before radiator duty changed.

“You copied the timestamp without asking whose time it kept,” Ressa said.

Senn looked from her slate to the apprentice weather clerk at the front bench. Nine months had made Ressa taller and no less direct. A blue training cord crossed her gray coat. Chalk dust marked one cuff.

“I authenticated the instrument,” Senn said.

“You authenticated that it keeps time consistently.”

“Not that the displayed time governs the promise.”

Ressa nodded. “Now fix the variance.”

Dry amusement moved through the coolant-cistern theater. Central Conservatory staff occupied the curved lower rows where Lorn Dusk residents had once slept during the early winter pulse. Local auditors stood at the old stage beside portable forecast screens. The disused coolant pipes above them carried no fluid, but their metal still gave the room a faint bitter smell. Heat bricks lined the aisles beneath coats and tool cases.

The first Conservatory training session held outside Orison had begun with central staff receiving correction from an apprentice. Hesh had arranged the order deliberately.

Senn reopened the exercise. A dawn window had arrived according to central time and run six minutes short according to the local service clock. Under the old account it would have passed as a one-minute overdelivery. Under the district's governing schedule it counted as thirteen minutes missing.

She changed the reference clock, attached the local agreement, and entered the interrupted promise into the practice ledger.

“What triggers next?” asked Marek, who had exchanged greenhouse tools for an audit slate without losing the posture of a mechanic examining a bad valve.

“Source review,” Senn said. “Local notice, mirror instruction, receiving sensor, and any variance consent.”

“Who can close it?”

“Not the office that issued the schedule. One local auditor and one independent member must agree on disposition.”

“And if they disagree?”

“The entry remains open.”

Marek accepted the answer. Ressa did not. “What happens to the next window while it stays open?”

Senn checked the new rule set. “The district keeps its floor. Discretionary trade from the disputed source pauses.”

“Now you may sit down.”

Senn returned to the second row beside Dael. His blue engineer's patch had been restored that morning, new thread brighter than the worn fabric around it. The licensing board had returned his authority under review rules that separated unauthorized protective work from concealment while still recording both. For two years, every schedule he signed would receive an independent technical check. Clinic adjustments now required public variance entries and district consent.

Before the next exercise, the maintenance representative asked Dael to demonstrate the review rule. A practice tower schedule appeared on the stage, built from his old clinic offsets. It offered three extra minutes of mirror edge to a medical reservoir while keeping all displayed tolerances green.

“What would you have done twelve years ago?” asked the representative.

“Entered a maintenance correction and left the district blank,” Dael said.

A central trainee looked uncomfortable. “Because the clinic lacked standing.”

“Because naming it could have triggered cancellation. I told myself a small hidden adjustment protected more than a failed challenge.”

“What do you do now?”

Dael opened the source layer. He named the receiving clinic, requested its measured reserve draw, and sent the proposed shift to the district ledger. The practice council refused because the reservoir already held enough heat. His offset disappeared.

The representative changed one input. Clinic reserve fell below its margin after a sterilization cycle. Dael submitted the same adjustment again. This time the district consented, but the independent checker found that its mirror edge would raise wind load over a neighboring greenhouse.

“Can you reduce the greenhouse share?” the trainee asked.

“Not without asking its district.”

“That takes time.”

“Yes.”

“What if the clinic cannot wait?”

“Emergency authority can act inside a protected trigger. My engineering license does not carry that authority by itself.”

Dael moved the case into emergency review. The audit council approved a shorter edge shift and added a later light window for the greenhouse. Every step stayed visible: clinic need, neighboring consequence, district responses, reserve authority, and final delivery.

Hesh asked what happened if no independent checker answered before the service boundary.

“The clinic floor governs,” Dael said. “The emergency action proceeds under a named authority. Review stays open afterward.”

“So secrecy has merely become paperwork?” one central mechanic asked.

“No. Paperwork can still hide things. The difference comes from who receives the record and who may refuse the next step.”

Pera attached his old anonymous offset beside the practice case. Both had warmed a clinic. Only one let the affected districts contest the cost. The exercise did not excuse twelve years of hidden corrections, but it gave the restored license a procedure sturdier than trust in Dael's intentions.

He leaned toward Senn after the panel dismissed him. “Seven minutes seems generous. I expected them to catch you on the chalk dates.”

“I read the chalk dates.”

“You read the ones at eye level.”

She looked toward the theater wall. Old marks continued behind the stage curtain and down to the floor where children had begun recording them. “You knew?”

“I failed yesterday.”

At the stage, Hesh called the next group. Three policy officers from Orison climbed the steps with an Eyr apprentice and a Nacre Fold clinic clerk. Their exercise involved a category conflict rather than timing. Pera had built it from an actual archive error found during the ninth-month review.

Pera now led the category archive. She sat beneath a portable shelf of Order 9 records, sorting questions into changes, disputes, and historical annotations. Her new title, Keeper of Policy Inheritance, had made her laugh for almost a full minute when Pell announced it. She had accepted only after adding a rule that no keeper could approve an inherited category alone.

The exercise displayed a heat draw labeled SCHOOL AUXILIARY. Central officers treated it as protected classroom warmth. The Eyr apprentice asked for the source meter. The Nacre Fold clerk requested the address. Pera refused both until they identified which office had assigned the label.

“Why does authorship matter if the load is real?” one officer asked.

“Because a real load can carry a false purpose,” Pera said. “We spent thirty-one years proving that.”

They found the assigning office in an emergency crop annex. SCHOOL AUXILIARY referred to flower-show lamps in a courtyard rented by a school, not classroom heat. The load stayed real. Its claim on the floor disappeared.

Pera made them preserve the old label in the archive, add the corrected category, name the assigning office, and notify the school before changing its schedule. Erasure earned no credit. Neither did exposure without repair.

Senn watched central habits meet local resistance in small procedural collisions. Staff asked for standard forms. Lorn Dusk auditors asked what the forms omitted. Engineers wanted common time. Clerks wanted the authority attached to each clock. No one opposed standards in principle. They opposed letting a standard hide the place where weather landed.

At midday, the theater doors opened for the implementation council session. Training benches became public seats. Portable screens shifted into a semicircle around the stage. Hesh moved from instructor's station to the permanent audit council position bearing Lorn Dusk's district seal.

The council had seven standing members: three district auditors, two planetary appointees, one maintenance representative, and one rotating public clerk. Hesh held no ceremonial role. They could open records, suspend a disputed discretionary trade, and require local consent evidence before a variance closed.

Rul Fen sat in the public counsel row with a thicker stack than he had carried to the vote. Nine months of shared winter had generated claims over market losses, seed timing, rain preferences, repair delays, and one central district's allegation that floor calculations favored darkward heat walls. The new institution had not reduced his workload.

Ceryn entered last.

She wore the First Curator's blue mantle for the final time. No staff procession accompanied her. She carried one testimony slate and the original fifth-renewal signature page inside a clear archival sleeve.

Pell opened the session remotely from Orison. His image occupied a narrow screen rather than the center of the room. “The council will receive the First Curator's resignation, effective upon appointment of the interim office. Implementation testimony remains subject to summons and audit. Curator Ahl, do you affirm both conditions?”

“I do,” Ceryn said.

“Does resignation conclude any pending review?”

“No.”

“Does it remove your obligation to authenticate decisions made under your seal?”

“No.”

Pell recorded the answers. “The office accepts the resignation.”

No applause followed. Ceryn removed the blue mantle and folded it across the back of the empty curator's chair. Beneath it she wore the same plain coat from the vote.

Hesh addressed her from the council. “Your testimony concerns the first implementation shortfall and two later reserve declarations. Do you remain available?”

“Yes.”

“Then take the witness seat.”

Ceryn sat without looking at the mantle.

Senn had imagined this transition more often than she admitted. In some versions, Ceryn left the institution and carried its blame with her. In others, she retained command because no one else understood the Choir's interlocking risks. Neither outcome would have changed enough. Stepping down removed concentrated authority. Remaining for testimony kept expertise and responsibility available to the people reviewing its use.

The council began with the 2.4-million-lumen-hour shortfall. Ceryn authenticated her reserve declaration, expiry, source forecasts, and knowledge at the time. Hesh asked why her office had used emergency authority before all local auditors reached the roster.

“Protected windows would have entered contingency before the full appointments arrived,” Ceryn said. “The amendment permitted remote concurrence.”

“Permission does not establish best practice.”

“No. Best practice would have seated the council before the first run.”

“Why did you not?”

“I prioritized schedule release over institutional readiness.”

“Again.”

Ceryn accepted the word. “Again. This time the authority remained public, limited, and independently concurred. That difference does not remove the planning failure.”

Hesh entered the answer without comment.

The next declaration concerned a shear front during the fourth shared-winter interval. Central shade had increased beyond forecast while Eyr surrendered kiln light. Ceryn had authorized reserve 6 for fifty minutes. Tovin's maintenance record showed seventy-four percent tension on one panel, above its preferred limit but within emergency tolerance.

The maintenance representative asked why the office had not reduced Orison's rail-platform warming first.

Ceryn opened the branch. “Wet-switch risk crossed the rail safety floor under that option.”

“Whose instrument supplied the risk?”

“Orison transit authority.”

“Independent check?”

“None during the interval.”

The council marked the decision technically supported but procedurally incomplete. Future transit claims required a second sensor before they could override an offered discretionary share.

Rul objected that duplicating sensors during a storm could delay action. Hesh accepted the objection into review and left the requirement active.

The hearing continued through lunch. No one treated Ceryn as a villain or an oracle. She answered exact questions and asked for records when memory might misstate a number. Once she corrected Hesh on a kite-angle conversion. Twice she conceded that the office's source choice had favored central infrastructure. Her testimony improved the rules that limited her former position.

During a recess, Senn found her beside the old coolant manifold, reading the district's chalk record.

“Did you know they kept all of them?” Ceryn asked.

“Yes.”

“I saw photographs. They did not show how low the first marks are.”

“Children made some.”

Ceryn crouched to read a date near the floor. Thirty-one years separated it from the newest kept-dawn entry. “My daughter used to mark growth on a pantry wall. I had forgotten how near the floor such records begin.”

Senn said nothing. Ceryn's famine history explained the choices she feared. It could not stand in for the people those choices chilled.

After a moment, Ceryn rose. “The interim curator asked whether you would direct central audit.”

“I declined.”

“I expected you would.”

“Did you recommend me?”

“Yes.”

“That may have contributed.”

A small movement touched Ceryn's mouth. “Then my influence has finally become useful through opposition.”

Senn looked toward the theater doors. Beyond them, Lorn Dusk's market corridor carried greenhouse lamps, tower vibration, and the voices of trainees comparing variance forms. “Pell offered a roving weather-standing post.”

“With what authority?”

“Cross-district origin traces. Temporary suspension of disputed discretionary trades. No power to alter a floor or close a local record alone.”

“You will spend most of your life in transit.”

“I already spend it following transfers.”

Ceryn considered the answer. “Accept it. Central audit will become clean again if no one keeps bringing it untidy places.”

Senn had reached the same conclusion during the morning exercise, when a local clock defeated an authenticated timestamp. She entered her acceptance on the appointment slate. The roving post began after the current training cycle.

The appointment terms required each district to choose its own records liaison before any local visit began. Senn could request instruments, open an origin trace, and pause a disputed surplus transfer for one service interval. Beyond that, local or planetary authority had to act. The limits pleased her. An auditor able to impose a remedy alone would become another curator with a different coat. She signed beneath the restrictions before signing beneath the title.

Dael found her before the recess ended. He had seen the appointment enter the public roster.

“How far does roving include?” he asked.

“Every district under the Charter.”

“That sounds poorly designed.”

“You designed an installation at thirty percent radiator duty.”

“Under supervision.”

“So will I.”

His restored license allowed him to lead Choir field reviews again, though another engineer would sign the check for two years. Their assignments would cross at towers, mirror stations, district halls, and disputed pipes. Neither promised to arrange it.

Dael touched the bright edge of his license patch. “Eyr wants its pressure rigs audited before the next repair cycle.”

“I have an opening in nineteen days.”

“Terrible news for the rigs.”

Pera called them back before Senn could answer.

The final training exercise used no fabricated record. Hesh had selected a variance from Nacre Fold's previous rain turn. A reflected window arrived at full intensity but thirty-four minutes late because a neighboring clinic requested priority light during a sterilization failure. Nacre Fold's council had consented to the delay. The window later arrived in full. No floor had been crossed.

Central staff classified it as schedule met with authorized timing variance.

The Nacre Fold clerk objected. “The promise did not arrive when posted.”

“It arrived under an agreed revision,” said an Orison auditor.

“Agreed after the original schedule.”

“Before the service boundary.”

“Residents waiting under the first notice still experienced an interruption.”

The dispute moved through the theater. Some local auditors wanted every deviation called a failure. Engineers argued that lawful adjustments kept the system alive. Rul warned that an adverse label could produce compensation claims even when districts consented. Pera asked whether classification existed to describe experience, assign fault, or trigger review.

“Those are different jobs,” Senn said.

Hesh looked toward her. “Then give them different fields.”

Senn opened the formal weather ledger. Its existing categories allowed delivered, failed, cancelled, emergency substituted, and consented variance. None captured a promise interrupted and later kept. The old system had treated the original notice as superseded, leaving only the successful revision. Local chalk records kept both.

Ressa carried the wall-clock exercise slate to the stage. “If you replace the first schedule, the person who waited becomes wrong.”

Pera added a new record layer without closing the consented variance. ORIGINAL PROMISE remained beside REVISED AGREEMENT. Delivery attached to the revision. The initial interruption stayed visible.

“What do we call the condition?” asked the maintenance representative.

Hesh did not answer immediately. The phrase belonged to Lorn Dusk before it belonged to policy.

“A broken season,” they said. “Not because the weather broke. Because the promise did.”

Rul raised his counsel light. “Does the term assign liability?”

“No,” Pera said. “It triggers review.”

“Does consent close it?”

“Consent can resolve the variance. It cannot erase the interruption.”

Ceryn spoke from the witness seat. “Add source, affected service, notice time, consent time, floor margin, and final delivery. Require review only when the original promise and delivered condition differ beyond posted tolerance.”

The Nacre Fold clerk asked for local description to remain attached. Central staff asked for a standard code so records could be compared across districts. Both entered the form.

Senn tested the category against old cases. It captured Lorn Dusk's missed dawns, Orison's altered bloom, Caldrift's moved rain preference, and the lawful clinic-priority delay without treating them as identical harms. Each record carried its own consequence and authority.

Pera asked the archive whether any active category already used the proposed term. None did. She asked Hesh for permission to retain the local words in the formal ledger.

“Retain where they came from,” Hesh said.

The origin field received LORN DUSK ADDRESS RECORD. The definition followed: PROMISE INTERRUPTED; ORIGINAL NOTICE RETAINED; VARIANCE, CONSENT, CONSEQUENCE, AND FINAL DELIVERY SUBJECT TO REVIEW. A district could now challenge the change without denying that later delivery had occurred under agreement.

Senn authenticated the test entry. Hesh added the local council seal. A planetary auditor signed the independent field. Pera released the category into the policy archive with no inherited authority and a mandatory review date.

On the portable screen, the Nacre Fold schedule kept both times. Its clinic priority remained lawful. Its delayed window remained delivered. The interruption no longer vanished between them.

Pera's new entry read: BROKEN SEASON. PROMISE INTERRUPTED. REVIEW REQUIRED. A date followed, attached to authorities who would have to answer when it arrived.

Epilogue — An Uneven Morning

Ressa posted Lorn Dusk's dawn seventeen minutes later than Orison's and waited for someone to object.

The address-hall forecast took the schedule without warning color. Amber light traced the local window across the wall slate: start at 06:43 district time, duration eighty-one minutes, clinic margin two degrees, tower 15 duty twenty-four percent. A smaller line showed Orison's canal bloom beginning at 06:26.

Three years earlier, any difference from the central schedule would have arrived as cancellation, tolerance, or no notice at all. Now the variance field named Nacre Fold clinic as the receiving priority. Its sterilization reservoir had cooled after a pump seal failed. Eleven districts had reviewed the light shift. Lorn Dusk's council had consented before the service boundary.

Ressa checked the seals again.

“You have already checked them twice,” Hesh said from the ledger desk.

“The first check found the missing tower reading.”

“The second found nothing.”

“That does not prove the third lacks ambition.”

Hesh returned to a burial entry, leaving her to the schedule. They had stopped supervising every motion of her apprenticeship, which meant they now expected her errors to have names.

Outside the hall, radiator hum traveled through frozen paving. Families moved toward the greenhouse corridor carrying work lamps and heat bricks. No one gathered beneath the windows to wait. A posted delay with an identified cause changed the morning's work: school opened twelve minutes later, bread ovens held their second trays, clinic staff extended the lamp bank over infant beds.

Ressa released the public notice.

LORN DUSK DAWN DELAYED 17 MINUTES BY AGREEMENT. RECEIVING PRIORITY: NACRE FOLD CLINIC STERILIZATION. FULL LOCAL WINDOW RETAINED. REVIEW RECORD 88-14.

A market mechanic stopped to read it. “Why does Orison get its light first?”

“It does not receive our seventeen minutes. Its canal schedule sits on another mirror branch.”

“Then why show it?”

“Because the forecast used to imply every district shared Orison's morning.”

He studied the two start times. “Looks uneven.”

“It is.”

The mechanic grunted and went to work.

At 06:26, the comparison line brightened. Orison's bloom began far starward, invisible from Lorn Dusk. Ressa received its source confirmation anyway: canal gates opened, hive boxes held, cold rain risk low. She attached it to the forecast without changing the local clock.

Nacre Fold reported its first sterilization cycle complete at 06:31. The pump seal held. Clinic reservoir temperature climbed above margin. Under the consent agreement, those results triggered release of Lorn Dusk's mirror path.

A Choir instruction arrived with a six-minute uncertainty range.

Ressa called the route operator. “Your range crosses our posted tolerance.”

“High wind over Eyr,” the operator said. “We can narrow by taking two minutes from the end.”

“No.”

“Total heat still clears floor.”

“The consent retained full duration.”

“Then start may run five minutes late.”

“Enter that possibility in the variance.”

The operator did. Ressa published the changed estimate before anyone saw the sky alter. Local review remained open.

At 06:46, three minutes behind the agreed time, reflected light touched the upper greenhouse roofs. The receiving sensor confirmed intensity. Ressa started the duration clock from physical arrival rather than central release.

Warmth entered the address hall slowly. It reached the chalk wall as a narrow silver block, leaving the lower bars in shadow. Children on their way to school put their palms inside it. One complained that Orison had already held twenty minutes of morning. Another answered that Orison had colder rain forecast after bloom.

Neither child treated weather as equal. They treated it as something with records.

The route operator extended the closing time by three minutes. Ressa watched the full eighty-one minutes arrive. Nacre Fold's clinic record stayed attached beside it. Lorn Dusk's greenhouse soil held above margin. No tower warning appeared.

A notice from Orison arrived during the final quarter hour. Its canal ward had complained that the comparison forecast made central bloom look favored. The civic clerk requested removal of the side-by-side start times from darkward displays.

Ressa opened the request for Hesh. “They say the times encourage false comparison.”

“Do they dispute either time?”

“No.”

“Do the schedules share a source constraint?”

“Yes. The clinic priority changed both route margins.”

Ressa denied the request and attached the reason. Comparison did not claim equal weather. It showed that different mornings came from one allocation field. Orison could appeal to the audit council if its clerk preferred a cleaner screen.

When the light withdrew, she printed the variance summary on paper. Hesh authenticated the council consent. A remote auditor in Eyr verified the route. Ressa signed the local delivery line with her apprentice mark.

“Closed?” Hesh asked.

“Delivered and reviewable,” she said. “The start missed agreement by three minutes.”

“Then not closed.”

Ressa left the variance open for the monthly route audit.

She took a piece of chalk from the ledger drawer. The old wall still carried missed windows from before her apprenticeship, bars whose notices had never arrived and whose consequences remained clearly visible in family ledgers. Beside them stood later marks with dates, durations, consent numbers, and repairs. No coat of paint separated one era from another.

Ressa drew today's line without covering the earlier promises. Beneath it she wrote the agreed time, the actual time, the clinic priority, and the full duration received.

At the end she added two words: KEPT LIGHT.
